Update of the risk assessment on emerging and novel brominated flame retardants in food
Authors: EFSA Panel on Contaminants in the Food Chain (CONTAM), Helle Katrine Knutsen, Agneta Åkesson, Vasileios Bampidis, Margherita Bignami, Laurent Bodin, James Kevin Chipman, Gisela Degen, Antonio Hernández‐Jerez, Tim Hofer, Christer Hogstrand, Stefano Landi, Jean‐Charles Leblanc, Kyriaki Machera, Evangelia Ntzani, Isabelle P Oswald, Guido Rychen, Salomon Sand, Katharina Vejdovszky, Barbara Viviani, Emilio Benfenati, Diane Benford, Andy Hart, Martin Rose, Henri Schroeder, Christiane Vleminckx, Martine Vrijheid, Edoardo Carnesecchi, Violetta Costanzo, Andrea Gissi, Eirini Kouloura, Luisa Ramos Bordajandi, Francesca Riolo, Christer Hogstrand
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
AbstractThe European Commission asked EFSA to update its 2012 risk assessment on Emerging and Novel brominated flame retardants (BFRs) in food. Since the previous Opinion, further information was collected for a total of 27 BFRs. The CONTAM Panel concluded that TDBPP, DBPNG and TBNPA are considered genotoxic and carcinogenic. Based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic. EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic. Insufficient or absent data precluded the Panel to conclude on the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs. Reproductive/developmental toxicity and/or neurotoxicity/neurodevelopmental effects were reported for DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO. Reference Points were identified for TDBPP, DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO. No health‐based guidance values could be derived for any of the BFRs. Occurrence data were submitted to EFSA for eight BFRs (BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, BTBPE, DBDPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX). Dietary exposure could be estimated only for BTBPE and HBB, due to the lack of quantitative data for the other BFRs. However, no risk characterisation could be performed for these BFRs due to the lack of a Reference Point. Using the limited available information on dietary exposure estimates reported in the scientific literature, risk characterisation could be performed for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and DBDPE, and the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure does not raise a health concern. The Panel considered that the level of certainty was 90% or higher for most of the reported conclusions. Very limited or no information on hazard or exposure were available for HBCYD, TBCO, DBHCTD, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, OBTMPI, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 or TTBNPP. Recommendations were formulated to address data gaps, including the need for access to original data, and for the collection of biomonitoring data, occurrence data in food, genotoxicity information and toxicological studies to support the identification of a Reference Point.Keywords: BFRs, brominated flame retardants, food, human exposure, occurrence, risk assessment, toxicology

SUMMARY

Brominated flame retardants (BFRs) are chemicals used in a wide variety of consumer/commercial products to improve their resistance to fire. Concern has been raised because of the occurrence of several chemical compounds from the group of BFRs in the environment, food and in humans. This has led to bans on the production and use of certain formulations.

The European Commission asked the European Food Safety Authority (EFSA) to update its 2010–2012 risk assessments on the different families of BFRs, i.e. hexabromocyclododecanes (HBCDDs), polybrominated diphenyl ethers (PBDEs), tetrabromobisphenol A (TBBPA) and its derivatives, brominated phenols and their derivatives, and novel and emerging BFRs. The CONTAM Panel is updating the risk assessments of different classes of BFRs in a series of separate Opinions.

The similarities in chemical properties and effects seen in the previous EFSA assessments for the different BFR families warrant the consideration of a mixture approach. The Panel on Contaminants in the Food Chain (CONTAM Panel) will evaluate the appropriateness of applying a mixture approach in an additional Opinion once the risk assessment for each BFR family has been updated. It will be based on the EFSA Guidance on harmonised methodologies for human health, animal health and ecological risk assessment of combined exposure to multiple chemicals published in 2019 and 2021.

The first four Opinions in the current series updated the risk assessments of HBCDDs in food, PBDEs in food, TBBPA and its derivatives in food and brominated phenols and their derivatives in food. This fifth Opinion updates the risk assessment of emerging and novel BFRs in food previously performed by EFSA and published in 2012.

The draft Scientific Opinion underwent a targeted consultation from 28 April to 18 May 2026. The comments received were taken into account when finalising the Scientific Opinion and are presented and addressed in Annex F.

As requested in the Terms of Reference by the EC, the current assessment considered the six BFRs assessed in the 2012 Opinion and included in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU:

tris(2,3‐dibromopropyl) phosphate (TDBPP),

N,N′‐Ethylenebis(tetrabromophthalimide) (EBTEBPI),

N,N′‐Ethylenebis(tetrabromophthalimide) (EBTEBPI),

Hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD),

Hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD),

Dibromoneopentyl glycol (DBNPG).

Dibromoneopentyl glycol (DBNPG).

The previous Opinion made a distinction between ‘emerging’ and ‘novel’ BFRs, but for the current update, the CONTAM Panel decided not to make this distinction.

The present assessment takes into account the occurrence data in food and biological samples submitted to EFSA after the publication of its previous Opinion on ‘Emerging and Novel’ BFRs in food, as well as the newly available scientific information of relevance to hazard identification and characterisation.

Eight of the BFRs assessed (EBTEBPI, BEH‐TEBP, DBNPG, DBDPE, TDBP‐TAZTO, PBB‐Acr, TTBNPP, TTBP‐TAZ) hold a registered status according to Regulation (EC) No 1907/2006 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 18 December 2006 concerning the Registration, Evaluation, Authorisation and Restriction of Chemicals (REACH Regulation). The studies and information reported in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s), was considered by the CONTAM Panel to inform the hazard identification and characterisation of the BFRs under assessment, when relevant, noting that the Panel did not have access to the original data.

The CONTAM Panel also used the OECD QSAR Toolbox and VEGA QSAR models to generate predictions on Ames test results to inform on the mutagenic potential, to provide insights on the toxicological mode of action as regards genotoxicity and to identify potential for read‐across by generating evidence for analogue identification and structural and mechanistic similarity. The OECD QSAR Toolbox was additionally used to capture experimental data present in databases to complement the literature search performed.

Overall, the information available on the toxicity of the BFRs included in the Terms of Reference varied greatly across compounds, but was in general scarce. Evidence of toxicity for reproduction or development in rodents was identified for DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and DBDPE. Evidence of neurotoxicity or developmental neurotoxicity in rodents was identified for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO. Studies in zebrafish larvae indicated potential for developmental neurotoxicity for DBE‐DBCH.

Regarding genotoxicity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that:

−Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.

Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.

− TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).

TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).

− DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).

DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).

−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.

In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.

−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.

In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.

− BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

−QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

−Insufficient (TTBNPP) or no data (HBCYD, EH‐TBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, OBTMPI) were identified to allow an assessment of the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs.

Insufficient (TTBNPP) or no data (HBCYD, EH‐TBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, OBTMPI) were identified to allow an assessment of the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs.

The CONTAM Panel identified a Reference Point for TDBPP, DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO:

−For TDBPP, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 0.073 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on combined incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenoma and carcinoma in male rats.

For TDBPP, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 0.073 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on combined incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenoma and carcinoma in male rats.

−For DBNPG, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.14 mg/kg bw per day for the technical product FR‐1138 (purity 78.6%), corresponding to 0.9 mg/kg bw per day for DBNPG was identified, based on increased incidence of mammary gland fibroadenoma, adenoma or carcinoma in female rats.

For DBNPG, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.14 mg/kg bw per day for the technical product FR‐1138 (purity 78.6%), corresponding to 0.9 mg/kg bw per day for DBNPG was identified, based on increased incidence of mammary gland fibroadenoma, adenoma or carcinoma in female rats.

−For BEH‐TEBP, a Reference Point corresponding to lowest‐observed‐adverse‐effect level (LOAEL) of 1 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.

For BEH‐TEBP, a Reference Point corresponding to lowest‐observed‐adverse‐effect level (LOAEL) of 1 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.

−For EH‐TBB, a Reference Point corresponding to LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.

For EH‐TBB, a Reference Point corresponding to LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.

−For DBDPE, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day was identified based on a neurobehavioural endpoint, an increase in total wheel running activity.

For DBDPE, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day was identified based on a neurobehavioural endpoint, an increase in total wheel running activity.

−For TDBP‐TAZTO, a Reference Point corresponding to a LOAEL of 5 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on depressive related behaviour in the forced swim test and the tail suspension test in rodents.

For TDBP‐TAZTO, a Reference Point corresponding to a LOAEL of 5 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on depressive related behaviour in the forced swim test and the tail suspension test in rodents.

Due to limited or complete lack of data on hazard identification and characterisation, the CONTAM Panel could not identify a Reference Point for the remaining BFRs included in the Terms of Reference (HBCYD, EBTEBPI, BTBPE, DBE‐DBCH, DBHCTD, HBB, HCTBPH, OBTMPI, PBB‐Acr, PBEB, PBT, TBNPA, TBCO, TBX, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP, TTBP‐TAZ).

No health‐based guidance values (HBGV) could be derived for any of the BFRs included in the Terms of Reference.

Occurrence data in human milk and blood/serum in European countries were identified in the scientific literature for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE, BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX and OBTMPI. The number of studies was limited and characterised by low detection frequencies, and relied on samples collected around a decade ago or more. No data on human samples in European countries was identified for the other BFRs included in the Terms of Reference.

In the previous 2012 Opinion, occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA for three of the BFRs included in the Terms of Reference (BTBPE, DBDPE and HBB) by two countries only (Ireland and the UK) covering the years 2006–2008. For the current update, occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA for two out of the six BFRs covered in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for six out of the remaining 21 BFRs covered in the 2012 Opinion (BTBPE, DBDPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX). These data were provided by six European Countries (France, Ireland, Norway, Austria, Germany, Portugal), from samples collected between 2010 and 2025. Most results for HBB were provided by Portugal (68%) and France (28%) between 2010 and 2017. France was the main data provider for BTBPE (90%) between 2014 and 2017.

A total of 2833 analytical results were submitted to EFSA as follows: HBB (n = 2004, 82% left‐censored), BTBPE (n = 630, 78% left‐censored), DBDPE (n = 76, 96% left‐censored), BEH‐TEBP (n = 21, 100% left‐censored), EH‐TBB (n = 21, 100% left‐censored), PBEB (n = 34, 100% left‐censored), PBT (n = 34), TBX (n = 13, 100% left‐censored).

Only the data availability for HBB and BTBPE was considered appropriate to perform a dietary exposure assessment. For DBDPE, 66 samples on ‘Fish and seafood’ were available but the only three positive samples were from 2010, thus not representative of the recent situation. In addition, insufficient data were available on other food categories. Thus, the CONTAM Panel deemed it not appropriate to perform an exposure assessment for DBDPE. The limited and left‐censored data available for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, PBEB, PBT and TBX were not further considered.

For BTBPE, mean LB dietary exposure ranged from 0.001 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Adults’, ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.006 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Infants’ and ‘Toddlers’, while mean UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.006 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.104 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’, across surveys. The P95 LB dietary exposure ranged from 0.002 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.014 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Other children', while the P95 UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.011 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Verly Elderly’ to 0.328 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’, across surveys. The raw primary commodities that contributed most to the mean lower bound dietary exposure belong to the ‘Milk and dairy products’ (47% to 88%) and ‘Meat and meat products’ (9%–40%) food categories.

For HBB, mean LB dietary exposure ranged from 0.04 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Adults’, ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.25 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Toddlers’, while mean UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.05 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.3 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Toddlers’, across surveys. The P95 LB dietary exposure ranged from 0.06 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ to 0.61ng/kg bw per day in ‘Other children’, while P95 UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.09 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ to 0.67 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Other children', across surveys. The food category that mostly contributed to the dietary exposure was ‘Milk’ (87%–98%) across all age groups.

In the scientific literature limited data on dietary exposure estimates in European countries were identified for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE, BTBPE, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT and TBX, covering one or two European countries. No information on potential dietary exposure in European countries was identified for the remaining BFRs (TDBPP, EBTEBPI, HBCYD, DBNPG, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, DBHCTD, HCTBPH, PBB‐Acr, TBNPA, DBDP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP and TTBP‐TAZ).

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the scientific literature to make robust exposure estimates, but an indication of mean and/or high level (P95) potential exposure was possible for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE, BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBT and TBX.

Risk characterisation could be performed for two out of the six BFRs covered by Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for one out of the remaining 21 BFRs assessed in the 2012 Opinion (DBDPE), making use of the limited information on dietary exposure estimates from one (EH‐TBB, DBDPE) or two (BEH‐TEBP) European countries reported in the scientific literature. The margin of exposure (MOE) approach was applied to assess possible health concerns.

For BEH‐TEBP, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 1500 would be sufficient to conclude that no health concern is raised for non‐neoplastic effects. This consideration was based on the default uncertainty factor of 100 (i.e. factor 10 for the extrapolation from experimental animals to humans and a factor 10 for inter‐individual human variability), an additional factor of 3 to allow for the absence of a NOAEL and the necessity of using a LOAEL, and an additional factor of 5 to account for deficiencies in the database, in particular in relation to inconsistencies between studies of non‐standard design, most studies being on mixtures rather than on the pure substance, and some studies only being available in summary form, and considering the worst‐case approach of attributing the effects solely to BEH‐TEBP. Based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 1 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects. The Panel could not set a Reference Point for neoplastic effects, since no carcinogenicity data are available. In addition, it was not possible to conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP.

For EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 1500 would be sufficient to conclude that no health concern is raised for non‐neoplastic effects. This consideration was based on the default uncertainty factor of 100 (i.e. factor 10 for the extrapolation from experimental animals to humans and a factor 10 for inter‐individual human variability), an additional factor of 3 to allow for the absence of a NOAEL and the necessity of using a LOAEL, and an additional factor of 5 to account for deficiencies in the database, in particular in relation to inconsistencies between studies of non‐standard design, all but one of the studies being on mixtures rather than on the pure substance, no experimental data on genotoxicity, and some studies only being available in summary form, and considering the approach of attributing the effects solely to EH‐TBB. Based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to EH‐TBB does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects. The CONTAM Panel could not set a Reference Point for neoplastic effects, since no carcinogenicity data are available. In addition, it was not possible to conclude on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB.

For DBDPE, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 300 would be sufficient to conclude that no health concern is raised for the critical effect. This consideration was based on the default uncertainty factor of 100 (i.e. factor 10 for the extrapolation from experimental animals to humans and a factor 10 for inter‐individual human variability), and an additional factor of 3 to account for deficiencies in the database, in particular in relation to the lack of long‐term toxicity studies, and the fact that the Reference Point comes from a study showing mild effects on a sensitive endpoint, at much lower doses than those at which effects were seen in other studies. Considering the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day based on the increase of total wheel running activity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to DBDPE does not raise a health concern.

No risk characterisation could be performed for any of the remaining BFRs included in the Terms of Reference. The CONTAM Panel noted that:

−For TDBPP, DBNPG and TDBP‐TAZTO, although a Reference Point could be identified, risk characterisation was not possible due to the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

For TDBPP, DBNPG and TDBP‐TAZTO, although a Reference Point could be identified, risk characterisation was not possible due to the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

−For BTBPE and HBB, although an exposure assessment could be performed based on the occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA, risk characterisation was not possible due to the lack of sufficient toxicological data to identify a Reference Point.

For BTBPE and HBB, although an exposure assessment could be performed based on the occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA, risk characterisation was not possible due to the lack of sufficient toxicological data to identify a Reference Point.

−Due to the lack of a Reference Point and limited information on potential dietary exposure, risk characterisation was not possible for DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT and TBX.

Due to the lack of a Reference Point and limited information on potential dietary exposure, risk characterisation was not possible for DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT and TBX.

−Due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure, risk characterisation was not possible for EBTEBPI, HBCYD, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, DBHCTD, HCTBPH, PBB‐Acr, TBNPA, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP, TTBP‐TAZ and OBTMPI.

Due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure, risk characterisation was not possible for EBTEBPI, HBCYD, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, DBHCTD, HCTBPH, PBB‐Acr, TBNPA, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP, TTBP‐TAZ and OBTMPI.

Risk characterisation was not performed for breastfed infants for any of the BFRs for which a Reference Point could be identified, because of the highly uncertain exposure estimates.

Uncertainties affecting the assessment were identified and their overall impact was quantified in those cases where the available data were sufficient to conclude on genotoxic potential, hazard characterisation or risk characterisation. The CONTAM Panel assessed the level of certainty (probability) to be 90% or more for all the reported conclusions, except for the risk characterisation for DBDPE, where the certainty of no health concern was considered to fall within the range of 66%–90%.

The CONTAM Panel made the following recommendations:

−Occurrence data in food, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no occurrence data were submitted to EFSA. Priority should be given to: (i) BFRs for which a Reference Point is available and/or for those that the Panel has concluded to be genotoxic: TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, DBE‐DBCH, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBNPA, (ii) BFRs that have been detected in human samples but lack a Reference Point or conclusion on genotoxicity: BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX, OBTMPI.

Occurrence data in food, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no occurrence data were submitted to EFSA. Priority should be given to: (i) BFRs for which a Reference Point is available and/or for those that the Panel has concluded to be genotoxic: TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, DBE‐DBCH, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBNPA, (ii) BFRs that have been detected in human samples but lack a Reference Point or conclusion on genotoxicity: BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX, OBTMPI.

−Biomonitoring data, obtained using appropriate analytical methods should be generated. Priority should be given to TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, HBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, DBE‐DBCH and TBNPA, as these show potential for bioaccumulation, or have an identified Reference Point, or are concluded to be genotoxic.

Biomonitoring data, obtained using appropriate analytical methods should be generated. Priority should be given to TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, HBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, DBE‐DBCH and TBNPA, as these show potential for bioaccumulation, or have an identified Reference Point, or are concluded to be genotoxic.

−Genotoxicity data are needed, most importantly for the BFRs with inconclusive (BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX) or absent (EH‐TBB) genotoxicity information and that are known to occur in food.

Genotoxicity data are needed, most importantly for the BFRs with inconclusive (BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX) or absent (EH‐TBB) genotoxicity information and that are known to occur in food.

−Toxicological studies that would enable the identification of a Reference Point are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no studies were identified. Priority should be given to the BFRs for which evidence of potential dietary exposure was identified and/or they have been detected in human samples (BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX, OBTMPI).

Toxicological studies that would enable the identification of a Reference Point are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no studies were identified. Priority should be given to the BFRs for which evidence of potential dietary exposure was identified and/or they have been detected in human samples (BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX, OBTMPI).

−In the context of the One Substance One Assessment, and to ensure efficient and harmonised risk assessments, there is a need to further enable access to the original data underpinning regulatory submissions across relevant authorities.

In the context of the One Substance One Assessment, and to ensure efficient and harmonised risk assessments, there is a need to further enable access to the original data underpinning regulatory submissions across relevant authorities.

INTRODUCTION

Background and Terms of Reference as provided by the requestor

BACKGROUND

Brominated flame retardants (BFRs) are anthropogenic chemicals, which are added to a wide variety of consumer/commercial products in order to improve their fire resistance. The major classes of BFRs are brominated bisphenols, diphenyl ethers, cyclododecanes, phenols, biphenyl derivatives and the emerging and novel BFRs.

Concern has been raised because of the occurrence of several chemical compounds from the group of BFRs in the environment, including feed and food, and in humans. This has led to bans on the production and use of certain formulations of polybrominated diphenyl ethers (PBDEs).

Between September 2010 and September 2012, the Scientific Panel on Contaminants in the Food of EFSA adopted six scientific Opinions on different classes of brominated flame retardants. 1 Because in its Opinion EFSA highlighted several data gaps, hampering the consumer risk assessment for these substances, by means of Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU on the monitoring of traces of brominated flame retardants in food, Member States were recommended to collect in 2014 and 2015 occurrence data for specific substances in specific foodstuffs.

The newly available occurrence data would enable an updated consumer exposure assessment. Furthermore, since the publication of the EFSA scientific Opinions between 2010 and 2012, new scientific information has become available, therefore it would be necessary to verify whether an update of these scientific Opinions would be appropriate, including an update of the consumer risk assessment.

Interpretation of the Terms of Reference

Following the request from the European Commission (EC), the CONTAM Panel will update its 2010–2012 risk assessments on the different families of BFRs: hexabromocyclododecanes (HBCDDs), polybrominated diphenyl ethers (PBDEs), tetrabromobisphenol A (TBBPA) and its derivatives, brominated phenols and their derivatives and Emerging and Novel brominated flame retardants (BFRs) (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2011a, 2011b, 2011c, 2012a, 2012b).

The first four Opinions in the series updated the risk assessments of HBCDDs in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2021), PBDEs in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2024a), TBBPA and its derivatives in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2024b), and brominated phenols and their derivatives in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2024c). This fifth Opinion updates the risk assessment of Emerging and Novel BFRs in food previously performed by EFSA (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The previous Opinion defined ‘emerging’ and ‘novel’ BFRs as follows:

− ‘Emerging BFRs’ were defined as chemicals which are applied as flame retardants and that have been identified in any environmental compartment, in wildlife, in food or in humans. It should be noted that the definition for the emerging BFRs, used in that Opinion, did not imply that there was evidence for an increasing trend in the concentration of these BFRs in the environment, in food or in human samples.

‘Emerging BFRs’ were defined as chemicals which are applied as flame retardants and that have been identified in any environmental compartment, in wildlife, in food or in humans. It should be noted that the definition for the emerging BFRs, used in that Opinion, did not imply that there was evidence for an increasing trend in the concentration of these BFRs in the environment, in food or in human samples.

− ‘Novel BFRs’ were defined as chemicals applied as flame retardants, with confirmed presence in materials and/or goods in concentrations above 0.1%, but not identified in environmental samples, wildlife, food or humans.

‘Novel BFRs’ were defined as chemicals applied as flame retardants, with confirmed presence in materials and/or goods in concentrations above 0.1%, but not identified in environmental samples, wildlife, food or humans.

−occurrence data in food and/or human milk,

The use of terms such as ‘emerging’, ‘novel’ and ‘legacy’, and inconsistent use of abbreviations for BFRs, even within the same published article, can lead to confusion. For this reason, Bergman et al. published a paper on ‘A novel abbreviation standard for organobromine, organochlorine and organophosphorus flame retardants and some characteristics of the chemicals’ (Bergman et al., 2012). This paper described a systematic way to abbreviate names of BFRs and also listed abbreviations that had been used historically. One particularly confusing example is for hexabromocyclododecanes (HBCDDs) and hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD) where both are sometimes referred to as HBCD. In this Opinion, the abbreviations based on Bergman et al. (2012) are used.

The similarities in chemical properties and effects seen in the previous EFSA assessments for the different BFR families warrant the consideration of a mixture approach. The CONTAM Panel will evaluate the appropriateness of applying a mixture approach in an additional Opinion once the risk assessment for each BFR family has been updated. It will be based on the EFSA Guidance on harmonised methodologies for human health, animal health and ecological risk assessment of combined exposure to multiple chemicals (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2019).

Polybrominated dibenzo‐p‐dioxins and furans (PBDD/Fs) are sometimes formed as byproducts in the manufacture of organo‐bromine compounds such as BFRs, or can be formed when they degrade. No direct evidence of their formation was found in the literature that was identified for the BFRs assessed in this Opinion.

Previous assessments

In 2012, the EFSA CONTAM Panel published its first risk assessment on Emerging and Novel BFRs in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) in which information on 17 Emerging and 10 Novel BFRs was reported. Due to the limited information identified at that time for occurrence, exposure and toxicity of the BFRs considered, the Panel could not perform a risk characterisation for any of these BFRs. Instead, the Panel made an attempt to identify those BFRs that could be a potential health concern and should be prioritised for future investigations, based on the available experimental data on behaviour in the environment, occurrence in food and toxicity.

The CONTAM Panel identified convincing evidence for genotoxicity and carcinogenicity of TDBPP and DBNPG and recommended further surveillance of their occurrence in the environment and in food. In addition, based on the limited experimental data on environmental behaviour, the Panel concluded that BTBPE and HBB could raise concerns for bioaccumulation (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The Panel additionally performed a modelling exercise selecting two environmental characteristics, overall persistence (Pov) and the potential for bioaccumulation, to predict the potential presence of the Emerging and Novel BFRs in food. Among the 27 BFRs, the modelling exercise predicted the following BFRs to have both a high overall persistence (Pov > 500 days) and a high potential for bioaccumulation: BTBPE, DBHCTD, EBTEBPI, HBB, HBCYD, HCTBPH, PBT, PBB‐Acr, PBEB and TBX (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Several bodies have performed hazard assessments for some of the BFRs considered in this Opinion, and these are briefly described below.

For TDBPP, and as noted in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) in its monograph on the Re‐evaluation of Some Organic Chemicals, Hydrazine and Hydrogen Peroxide classified TDBPP as probably carcinogenic to humans (Group 2A) (IARC, 1999). In December 2021, the National Toxicology Program (NTP) released its 15th Report on Carcinogens, 2 where TDBPP is mentioned as reasonably anticipated to be a human carcinogen based on sufficient evidence of carcinogenicity from studies in experimental animals. The NTP based their conclusions on observations from studies reported in the IARC monograph (IARC, 1999).

Regarding DBNPG, the Danish Environmental Protection Agency, Centre for Integrated Environment and Toxicology (CETOX), published a report in 2000 based on data submitted to the United States‐Environment Protection Agency (US‐EPA), concluding that DBNPG should be considered potential carcinogenic (Simonsen et al., 2000). As noted in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), IARC published a monograph on Evaluation of Carcinogenic Risks to Humans (IARC, 2000). IARC classified DBNPG as possibly carcinogenic to humans (Group 2B). In 2018, ECHA's Committee for Risk Assessment (RAC) adopted its opinion 3 on DBNPG based on the proposal submitted in December 2016, by the Norwegian Competent authority 4 for harmonised classification and labelling (CLH) as mutagenic and carcinogenic. In brief, ECHA's RAC considered that DBNPG should be classified as a germ cell mutagen, Cat. 1B, based on positive results for somatic cell mutagenicity from in vitro/in vivo studies and evidence from the reproductive toxicity studies that DBNPG reaches the (female) germ cells. In addition, ECHA's RAC considered DBNPG as ‘carcinogenic, Cat. 1B’ based on the evidence that DBNPG‐induced dose‐dependent multi‐site tumours in two species, rats and mice, with tumours of human relevance, in an OECD TG 453‐study conducted by the NTP under GLP conditions (NTP, 1996).

In 2020, ECHA's RAC adopted its opinion 5 on TBNPA based on the proposal submitted in June 2019, by the Norwegian Competent authority for CLH as mutagenic and carcinogenic. ECHA's RAC classified TBNPA as a ‘germ cell mutagen Category 2, H341: Suspected of causing genetic defects’ based on read‐across from DBNPG, a known germ cell mutagen. Additionally, ECHA's RAC classified TBNPA as ‘carcinogen category 1B, Carc 1B, H350: May cause cancer’ based on a detailed read‐across analysis from DBNPG, which was recognised as a multi‐site and multi‐species carcinogen (ECHA's RAC opinion as Carc. 1B), despite the absence of epidemiological and animal data for TBNPA (more details available in Section 3.3.12).

For the rest of BFRs included in the Terms of Reference of the current Opinion, no other risk or hazard assessments by any international body were identified.

Legislation

In this Opinion, where reference is made to European legislation (Regulations, Directives, Recommendations, Decisions), the reference should be understood as relating to the most recent amendment at the time of publication of this Opinion, unless otherwise stated.

In order to protect public health, Article 2 of Council Regulation (EEC) No 315/93 6 of 8 February 1993 laying down Community procedures for contaminants in food stipulates that, where necessary, maximum tolerances for specific contaminants shall be established. The BFRs considered in this Opinion are not regulated under any specific legislation within the EU for food or feed, e.g. Commission Regulation (EU) 2023/915 7 of 25 April 2023 and Council Directive 2002/32/EC. 8

Regarding Regulation (EC) No 1907/2006 9 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 18 December 2006 concerning the Registration, Evaluation, Authorisation and Restriction of Chemicals (REACH Regulation), the 27 BFRs under the Terms of Reference have different statuses under REACH, which reflects the current use of the compounds in the European Economic Area (EEA) (Table 2). Registered status refers to substances/mixtures that hold a valid registration by one or more companies and can be placed on the EEA market. Pre‐registered status refers to substances/mixtures indicated, in 2009, as being intended to be registered by at least one company in the EEA. Notified classification and labelling (C&L) refers to substances either manufactured or imported in small amounts as substances/mixtures or in articles to the EU. Under REACH, a ‘persistent, bioaccumulative and toxic’ (PBT) or ‘very persistent and very bioaccumulative’ (vPvB) assessment is required for all substances for which a chemical safety assessment is carried out. A chemical safety assessment is required for substances manufactured or imported in amounts of 10 tonnes or more per year, unless exemptions apply. Substances that persist for long periods of time in the environment and have a high potential to accumulate in biota are characterised as PBT/vPvB and are considered of specific concern because their long‐term effects are rarely predictable. Substances of very high concern (SVHC) are identified substances, proposed by a Member State or ECHA, at the request of the European Commission, with the following hazard properties: a. Substances meeting the criteria for classification as carcinogenic, mutagenic or toxic for reproduction (CMR) category 1A or 1B in accordance with Regulation (EC) No 1272/2008 on classification, labelling and packaging of chemical substances; b. Substances which are PBT/vPvB according to REACH Annex XIII. c. Substances on a case‐by‐case basis, that cause an equivalent level of concern as CMR or PBT/vPvB substances.

None of the BFRs considered in this Opinion is listed under the restricted substances included in Annex II of the Directive 2011/65/EU 10 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 8 June 2011 on the restriction of the use of certain hazardous substances in electrical and electronic equipment (recast).

Six out of the 27 BFRs considered in this Opinion are listed in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU, 11 on the monitoring of traces of BFRs in food. According to this regulation, the six BFRs listed should be monitored in fish and other seafood, meat and meat products (including edible offal), animal and vegetable fats and oils, milk and dairy products, eggs and egg products and food for infants and small children. Moreover, their determination should be carried out with analytical methods with a limit of quantification of 1 ng/g wet weight (ww) or lower.

DATA AND METHODOLOGIES

The recent updates of the EFSA risk assessments on BFRs, including the current one on Emerging and Novel BFRs, were developed applying a structured methodological approach, which involved developing a priori the protocol or strategy of the full risk assessments and performing each step of the risk assessment in line with the protocol or strategy and documenting the process. The protocol in Annex A of this Opinion contains the method that was used for all the steps of the risk assessment process, including any subsequent refinements/changes made.

The CONTAM Panel used its previous risk assessment on Emerging and Novel BFRs in food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) as a starting point for drafting the current Opinion.

The draft Scientific Opinion underwent a targeted consultation from 28 April to 18 May 2026. The comments received were taken into account when finalising the Scientific Opinion and are presented and addressed in Annex F.

Supporting information for the assessment

Information on physicochemical properties, production and industrial use, environmental fate and levels, analytical methods, previous assessments and legislation was gathered from the previous EFSA Opinion on Emerging and Novel BFRs in Food (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), assessments by international bodies (by checking the original websites of the relevant organisations) and from current EU legislation. Literature searches were also conducted to identify new information in reviews and other peer‐reviewed publications, also in the fields of previously reported occurrence data and exposure assessments, food processing and non‐dietary exposure. The information was summarised in a narrative way based on expert knowledge and judgement, and does not aim to be comprehensive.

Details of the literature search performed are shown in Appendix A. Search strings were run in the databases indicated. The literature searches were performed in April 2024. The outcome of the searches was saved in separate EndNote files and an automatic duplicate detection run. The selection for relevance based on title and abstract and full text was done by EFSA staff and EFSA CONTAM Working Group members.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Information relevant for the sections under hazard identification and characterisation was identified by the literature searches shown in Appendix A and assessments by international bodies. The literature searches were performed in April 2024, September 2024, May 2025 and December 2025. The outcome of the searches was saved in separate EndNote files and an automatic duplicate detection run.

The selection of the scientific papers for inclusion or exclusion was based on consideration of the extent to which the study was relevant to the assessment or on general study quality considerations (e.g. sufficient details on the methodology, performance and outcome of the study, on dosing, substance studied and route of administration and on statistical description of the results). Limitations in the information used are documented in this Scientific Opinion. Studies on levels in human samples were limited to European countries as studies from other countries might not reflect the same pattern of usage of BFRs.

The CONTAM Panel also used the OECD QSAR Toolbox v4.8 and VEGA v1.2.4 for all the BFR substances subject to this Opinion. General information about VEGA 21 and OECD QSAR toolbox 22 can be found at their respective websites (see footnotes). The objectives were the following:

to capture experimental data present in databases of the OECD QSAR Toolbox to complement the literature search performed.

to capture experimental data present in databases of the OECD QSAR Toolbox to complement the literature search performed.

to generate predictions on Ames test results to inform on the mutagenic potential. The CONTAM Panel noted that this was useful also for substances for which experimental data are available because the comparison between VEGA QSAR predictions and experimental data provided additional reassurance on the validity of the QSAR predictions as well as an additional line of evidence. Predictions for other more complex endpoints (both genotoxic and non‐genotoxic) were not considered in this Opinion since QSAR models did not predict these with sufficient reliability for the substances considered.

to generate predictions on Ames test results to inform on the mutagenic potential. The CONTAM Panel noted that this was useful also for substances for which experimental data are available because the comparison between VEGA QSAR predictions and experimental data provided additional reassurance on the validity of the QSAR predictions as well as an additional line of evidence. Predictions for other more complex endpoints (both genotoxic and non‐genotoxic) were not considered in this Opinion since QSAR models did not predict these with sufficient reliability for the substances considered.

to provide insights on the toxicological mode of action as regards genotoxicity (OECD QSAR toolbox profiler). The results from these profilers were considered further in this Opinion to provide insight on the mode of action when there were positive experimental results or, in absence of experimental results, when there were valid positive QSAR results. They were not considered further to conclude on the endpoint in the absence of other valid lines of evidence.

to provide insights on the toxicological mode of action as regards genotoxicity (OECD QSAR toolbox profiler). The results from these profilers were considered further in this Opinion to provide insight on the mode of action when there were positive experimental results or, in absence of experimental results, when there were valid positive QSAR results. They were not considered further to conclude on the endpoint in the absence of other valid lines of evidence.

to identify potential for read‐across by generating evidence for analogue identification and structural and mechanistic similarity, also using the QSAR toolbox profilers.

to identify potential for read‐across by generating evidence for analogue identification and structural and mechanistic similarity, also using the QSAR toolbox profilers.

The analysis included the production of QSAR predictions using VEGA software. VEGA automatically assigns a reliability score to each prediction (see Annex B). These reliability scores were then reviewed. The CONTAM Panel considered that the predictions for negative Ames test should have at least ‘GOOD reliability’ to be further evaluated, whereas predictions for positive Ames test should have at least ‘MODERATE reliability’. A higher reliability requirement was applied to negative predictions since ruling out every possible mechanism of mutagenicity requires high confidence in the QSAR result, which can be achieved when the training or test set of the QSAR model contains substances highly similar to the target, resulting in predictions with ‘GOOD reliability’. On the other hand, for positive predictions, it may be sufficient for the model to identify substances with moderate overall similarity to the target, as long as they share the specific fragment linked to mutagenicity. This can provide the assessor with sufficient confidence to use the QSAR result for further consideration. Annex B include the full reports as produced by VEGA and QSAR Toolbox used in this Opinion.

Benchmark dose (BMD) analysis was carried out according to the EFSA Scientific Committee guidance on BMD modelling (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022) using the Bayesian BMD Modelling web‐app (https://zenodo.org/record/7334435#.Y5osYXbMLD4) available at the EFSA R4EU platform (https://efsa.openanalytics.eu/).

As indicated in the protocol for the risk assessment (Annex A), an evaluation of the inherent uncertainties in the risk assessment was performed, and it was based on the Guidance on uncertainty analysis in scientific assessments of the EFSA Scientific Committee (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2018) and the guidance on communication of uncertainty in scientific assessments (EFSA, 2019a). The aim of the uncertainty analysis was to identify uncertainties affecting the assessment and quantify their combined impact on the main conclusions. Probabilities assessed by the uncertainty analysis were reported as % certainty for the more probable outcome, following EFSA's guidance on communication of uncertainty (EFSA, 2019a). An uncertainty analysis was only performed when a conclusion could be drawn, e.g. on hazard characterisation. Details can be found in each specific sub‐section of this assessment.

Occurrence data submitted to EFSA

Data collection

Following a mandate from the European Commission, a call for annual collection of chemical contaminant occurrence data in food was issued by EFSA in December 2010. Since then, data have been submitted every year by a deadline agreed with the EFSA Scientific Network on Chemical Monitoring Data collection. 23

The data submission to EFSA follows the requirements of the EFSA Guidance on Standard Sample Description 2 (EFSA, 2013). Occurrence data are managed following the EFSA standard operational procedures (SOPs) on ‘Data collection and validation' and on ‘Data analysis of food consumption and occurrence data’.

Data validation and analysis

Following EFSA's Technical Report on handling of occurrence data for dietary exposure assessment (EFSA, 2021) to guarantee an appropriate quality of the data used in the exposure assessment, the initial dataset was carefully evaluated by applying several data cleaning and validation steps. Special attention was paid to the identification of duplicates and to the accuracy of different parameters, such as ‘Sampling strategy’, ‘Sampling year’, ‘Sampling country’, ‘Analytical method’, Expression of result (e.g. fat weight), ‘Reporting unit’, ‘Limit of detection/quantification' and the codification of the analysed food under FoodEx2 classification (EFSA, 2011a, 2011b, 2015).

Left‐censored data were treated using the substitution method as recommended in the ‘Principles and Methods for the Risk Assessment of Chemicals in Food’ (WHO/IPCS, 2009, updated in 2020). This is the same method as indicated in the EFSA scientific report ‘Management of left‐censored data in dietary exposure assessment of chemical substances’ (EFSA, 2010). The guidance suggests that the lower bound (LB) and upper bound (UB) approach should be used for chemicals likely to be present in the food (e.g. naturally occurring contaminants, nutrients and mycotoxins). The LB is obtained by assigning a value of zero (minimum possible value) to all samples reported as lower than the LOD (< LOD) or LOQ (< LOQ). The UB is obtained by assigning the numerical value of LOD to values reported as < LOD and LOQ to values reported as < LOQ (maximum possible value), depending on whether LOD or LOQ is reported by the laboratory.

A mean LB and UB occurrence value was then calculated at each level of the FoodEx2 classification.

Mean occurrence values for specific food categories calculated on less than five analytical results were not used in the dietary exposure assessment. However, these analytical results were included in the calculation of averages for categories at higher levels of the FoodEx2 classification if at least five samples were available at the higher level. If at FoodEx2 Level 1 there were not at least 5 samples, the food category was not included in the assessment and this exclusion was discussed in the uncertainty section.

Specific food subcategories with 100% left‐censored results were included with mean LB and UB concentration of the closest parent FoodEx2 categories for which quantified results were available if there was no reason to exclude the presence of the compound in the subcategory.

Similarly, specific food subcategories for which there were no occurrence data available were attributed with the concentration of the parent FoodEx2 category where contamination could not be excluded, e.g. the mean concentration of BTBPE for ‘Mammals meat’ was attributed to ‘Pig fresh meat’, subcategory for which there were no specific occurrence data.

Analytical methods

The analytical methods for some of the BFRs may be associated with uncertainties due to the fact that analytical standards in some cases may not be readily available. As a consequence, some studies have relied on surrogate response factors and specific recovery of the analyte was not measured (Papachlimitzou et al., 2012). This introduces some uncertainty in the reported concentrations and hinders comparison across datasets.

Food consumption data

Food consumption data from the EFSA Comprehensive European Food Consumption Database (Comprehensive Database) were used to perform the dietary exposure assessment. This database contains national data on food consumption at the individual level, which are the most complete and detailed data currently available in the EU.

The food consumption data gathered in the Comprehensive Database were collected using repeated 24 or 48‐h dietary recalls or dietary records covering 1 to 9 days per individual. Owing to the differences in the methods used for data collection, direct country‐to‐country comparisons of the exposure estimates should be avoided. Details of how the Comprehensive Database is used to assess the dietary exposure to food chemicals are published in a 2011 EFSA Guidance (EFSA, 2011b).

In addition, in 2019, EFSA developed the Raw Primary Commodity (RPC) model (EFSA, 2019b). The RPC model disaggregates the food consumption data for composite foods (foods consisting of multiple components) and RPC derivatives (single‐component foods which have been physically changed by processing) reported in the Comprehensive Database into their corresponding quantities of RPCs. As the occurrence data submitted to EFSA on the novel and emerging BFRs assessed in this Opinion covered mainly raw primary commodities the RPC database was deemed the most appropriate consumption database to be used. The RPC Consumption Database is based on the Comprehensive Database version published on the 31 March 2018, and contains 39 dietary surveys from 22 different countries, covering a total of 73,029 subjects. A chronic dietary exposure assessment is relevant in the context of the Terms of Reference. For such an assessment, surveys in which food consumption data were collected over only 1 day or subjects involved only for 1 day, are not considered appropriate. Exclusion of these surveys and subjects resulted in a total of 33 dietary surveys carried out in 18 Member States covering 61,336 individuals available for the chronic dietary exposure assessment.

Table 3 provides an overview of the population groups and countries included in the dietary exposure assessment. Surveys for special population groups, i.e. lactating women and pregnant women, one survey each, are not included in the table as they are not the focus of the assessment and of limited representativeness; however the dietary exposure estimates of these two surveys will be compared to those of the general adult population.

Population groups and countries included in the chronic dietary exposure assessment.

According to the EFSA Scientific Committee Guidance on the risk assessment of substances present in food intended for infants under 16 weeks of age, the exposure assessment for these infants should be carried out separately from that for older infants, following the procedure described in the guidance (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2017). Based on this guidance, infants under 16 weeks of age should be excluded from the dietary exposure estimation of the infants age group. However, due to uncertainty in the reported individual ages of infants in the Comprehensive Database, the cut‐off age was based on a validated existing age group in this database corresponding to 12 weeks of age. Thus, food consumption data of infants between 12 and 16 weeks of age were also included in the exposure assessment. As the number of children within this age range in the database is limited, it is not expected that this will have affected the exposure estimate for infants of 16 weeks up to 12 months of age. Annex C (Table C.1) provides details on the dietary surveys included in the dietary exposure assessment.

To assess the dietary exposure of breastfed infants, a scenario considering a median age of 3 months was selected, equivalent to a body weight of about 6.1 kg, with an estimated average daily human milk consumption of 800 mL and a high consumption of 1200 mL. A mean fat content in human milk of 3.5% was considered for the estimations.

Food classification

Consumption and occurrence data were codified according to the FoodEx2 classification system (EFSA, 2011a, 2011b). Since 2018, all consumption records in the Comprehensive Database as well as all occurrence data submitted to EFSA have been codified according to the FoodEx2 classification system (EFSA, 2015). The FoodEx2 classification system consists of a large number of standardised basic food items aggregated into broader food categories in a hierarchical parent–child relationship. Additional descriptors, called facets, are used to provide additional information about the codified foods (e.g. information on food processing and packaging material).

Exposure assessment

Due to the limited occurrence data on BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX (all left‐censored) and DBDPE (see Section 3.1.1), the CONTAM Panel deemed it not possible to perform an exposure assessment for these BFRs. An exposure assessment was only performed for BTBPE and HBB.

For calculating the chronic dietary exposure to the three emerging BFRs, food consumption and body weight data at the individual level were retrieved from the Comprehensive Database. Occurrence data and consumption data were linked at the relevant FoodEx2 level.

Chronic dietary exposures were calculated by combining mean occurrence values of the chemicals for food samples collected in different countries (pooled European occurrence data) with the average daily consumption for each food at the individual level in each dietary survey and age class. Consequently, individual average exposures per day and body weight were obtained for all individuals. The following formula describes the calculation made:

where:

e¯i is the average exposure of individual i

x¯f is the mean chemical concentration in each food or food group f (belonging to set of foods F i for individual i)

cf,d,i is the consumed amount of food f by individual i on day d

bw i is individual body weight of individual i

d is the survey day (belonging to the set of survey days D i for individual i)

|D i| represents the number of survey days of individual i

The distributions of individual exposures were then used to calculate the mean and high (95th percentile) exposure per survey and per age class. These exposure estimates were obtained using the LB and UB mean concentration of BTBPE and HBB.

All analyses were run using the SAS Statistical Software (SAS enterprise guide 8.5).

Risk characterisation

The general principles of the risk characterisation for chemicals in food as described by WHO/IPCS (2009), updated in 2020) were applied as well as the different EFSA guidance documents relevant to this step of the risk assessment (see Annex A).

Uncertainty analysis

The risk assessments in this Opinion followed the normal approach of the CONTAM Panel, including elements to address standard sources of uncertainty. Uncertainty analysis was therefore performed using the approach for a standardised assessment, described in Section 3 of EFSA's Guidance on uncertainty analysis (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2018). Sources of uncertainty affecting each assessment were tabulated and their individual impacts on the assessment were evaluated qualitatively, except where the available data were clearly insufficient to perform an assessment. The overall (combined) impacts of the non‐standard uncertainties on the conclusions of each assessment regarding genotoxicity, hazard characterisation and risk 24 were then evaluated quantitatively, except where no conclusion was reached. The quantitative evaluations were performed by expert group discussion rather than formal or semi‐formal expert knowledge elicitation (section 12.6 of the Guidance) since this was found to be sufficient in each case and was practical, considering the number of substances to be assessed. The resulting probabilities are reported as % certainty for the more probable outcome for each conclusion, following EFSA's guidance on communication of uncertainty (EFSA, 2019a).

ASSESSMENT

Occurrence data in food

Occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA

The occurrence data in food that were used for the human dietary exposure assessment in the current Opinion are described in this section.

In the previous Opinion, two countries, Ireland and the UK, submitted data on three emerging BFRs, i.e. BTBPE, DBDPE and HBB, covering the years 2006–2008 (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). For BTBPE, only 11% of the analytical results were reported as > LOQ. For DBDPE and HBB, all reported analytical values were < LOQ. The CONTAM Panel concluded at that time, that based on these data a meaningful exposure assessment was not possible.

Since then, and by the 8th of September 2025, a total of 2833 analytical results were submitted to EFSA (see Table 4) for HBB (n = 2004), BTBPE (n = 630), DBDPE (n = 76), BEH‐TEBP (n = 21), EH‐TBB (n = 21), PBEB (n = 34), PBT (n = 34), TBX (n = 13) sampled between year 2010 and 2025 (Annex E).

TABLE 4Number of analytical results and percentage of left‐censored (LC) data submitted to EFSA by compound.Compound n % LCCovered by Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU BEH‐TEBP 21100 EH‐TBB 21100Not covered by Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU BTBPE 63078 DBDPE 7696 HBB 200482 PBEB 34100 PBT 34100 TBX 13100 Total 2833 82

Number of analytical results and percentage of left‐censored (LC) data submitted to EFSA by compound.

Only the data availability for HBB and BTBPE was considered appropriate to perform a dietary exposure assessment. The limited, and left‐censored, data available for the other six BFRs were not further considered.

66 samples on ‘Fish and seafood’ were available for DBDPE. However, the only three positive samples were from 2010, thus not representative of the recent situation. In addition, insufficient data were available on other food categories. Thus, the CONTAM Panel deemed it not appropriate to perform an exposure assessment for DBDPE.

A list of validation steps was applied to the available data for HBB and BTBPE before using them to estimate dietary exposure. Data providers were contacted to clarify inconsistencies identified during the data validation. The following paragraphs describe modifications that were made to the initial data set based on the feedback received and/or expert judgement.

A number of analytical results were reported as not corrected for recovery (n = 370) and no recovery factor was provided. In this case the recovery was assumed to be 100%. When no indication was provided on the application of a correction factor (n = 1794), it was assumed that the result was reported as corrected.

Analytical results reported to be related to ‘suspect sampling’ (n = 6, all for HBB) were excluded based on the fact that sampling strategy cannot be considered randomised.

For the majority of the results (n = 1408), the reported analytical method was gas chromatography–tandem mass spectrometry (GC–MS/MS), followed by gas chromatography– high resolution mass spectrometry (GC–HRMS) (n = 187), gas chromatography (GC) coupled to triple‐quadrupole–tandem massv spectrometry (GC‐QqQ‐MS–MS) (n = 185), GC–MS (n = 90). For 758 analytical results (30%), no information on the analytical method was available. All analytical results were retained.

After the removal of the above‐mentioned six ‘suspect’ samples, 2628 analytical results were included in the final dataset for HBB (n = 1998) and BTBPE (n = 630).

The number of analytical results per year and country in this final dataset is presented in Table 5. Most results for HBB were provided by Portugal (68%) and France (28%) between 2010 and 2017. France was the main data provider for BTBPE (90%) between 2014 and 2017.
