Quantified results were only reported in food commodities of animal origin. More specifically:

−For HBB quantified results were found in various food of animal origin: for the ‘Fish and seafood’ category (n = 161), the quantified samples referred to various types of ‘Fish meat’ (marine, diadromous, freshwater and unspecified), and to ‘Crustaceans’ and ‘Molluscs’ samples submitted by Norway and France. For these samples, concentrations ranged from 0.00005 μg/kg in ‘Fish (meat)’ to 0.041 μg/kg in mussels, with an overall 52% left censorship. For the category of ‘Meat and meat products’, 113 samples had positive results, with HBB levels quantified in fat tissue, meat, liver and liver and marinated meat, and concentrations ranging from 0.00008 μg/kg in rabbit meat to 0.147 μg/kg in sheep fat tissue, and 33% of left‐censored data. For the ‘Milk and dairy products’ and ‘Eggs and egg products’ categories, the number of positive results was 52 and 40, respectively, with values between 0.00003 and 0.101 μg/kg in milk and from 0.0002 to 0.016 μg/kg in eggs. For these latter categories, the proportion of left‐censored data was 32%.

For HBB quantified results were found in various food of animal origin: for the ‘Fish and seafood’ category (n = 161), the quantified samples referred to various types of ‘Fish meat’ (marine, diadromous, freshwater and unspecified), and to ‘Crustaceans’ and ‘Molluscs’ samples submitted by Norway and France. For these samples, concentrations ranged from 0.00005 μg/kg in ‘Fish (meat)’ to 0.041 μg/kg in mussels, with an overall 52% left censorship. For the category of ‘Meat and meat products’, 113 samples had positive results, with HBB levels quantified in fat tissue, meat, liver and liver and marinated meat, and concentrations ranging from 0.00008 μg/kg in rabbit meat to 0.147 μg/kg in sheep fat tissue, and 33% of left‐censored data. For the ‘Milk and dairy products’ and ‘Eggs and egg products’ categories, the number of positive results was 52 and 40, respectively, with values between 0.00003 and 0.101 μg/kg in milk and from 0.0002 to 0.016 μg/kg in eggs. For these latter categories, the proportion of left‐censored data was 32%.

−For BTBPE quantified results were found for 103 samples of ‘Fish and seafood’ (0.0001–0.020 μg/kg, with highest value measured in oysters), 19 samples of ‘Meat and meat products’ (0.00005–0.015 μg/kg in sheep fat tissue), 9 samples of ‘Eggs and egg products’ (0.0004–0.013 μg/kg in whole eggs), 10 samples of ‘Milk and dairy products’ (0.0001–0.0008 μg/kg, with highest value in goat milk). The amount of left‐censored results ranged between 69% and 88%, with the highest percentage in ‘Meat and meat products’.

For BTBPE quantified results were found for 103 samples of ‘Fish and seafood’ (0.0001–0.020 μg/kg, with highest value measured in oysters), 19 samples of ‘Meat and meat products’ (0.00005–0.015 μg/kg in sheep fat tissue), 9 samples of ‘Eggs and egg products’ (0.0004–0.013 μg/kg in whole eggs), 10 samples of ‘Milk and dairy products’ (0.0001–0.0008 μg/kg, with highest value in goat milk). The amount of left‐censored results ranged between 69% and 88%, with the highest percentage in ‘Meat and meat products’.

For all the other food categories reported in Table 6, analytical results were 100% left‐censored.

To be noted that most samples in food of plant of origin (n = 1319) for HBB (‘Grains and grain‐based products’, ‘Vegetables and vegetable products’, ‘Starchy roots or tubers and products thereof, sugar plants’, ‘Legumes, nuts, oilseeds and spices’, ‘Fruit and fruit products’, ‘Animal and vegetable fats and oils and primary derivatives thereof’ – Olive oils, ‘Alcoholic beverages’) and the samples for ‘Food products for young population' (n = 34) were analysed with an LOQ of 10 μg/kg. Analytical results for these samples were all left‐censored and were not included in the dietary exposure assessment Uncertainty introduced by the high LOQs used for the analysis of these food categories and their subsequent exclusion from the dietary exposure assessment might have caused an underestimation of the exposure.

Annex C (Table C.2) contains the mean LB and UB occurrence values used in the dietary exposure assessment for each of the FoodEx2 food category for which a mean concentration could be estimated.

Occurrence data in food and dietary exposure assessments in the scientific literature since the previous Opinion

This section provides an overview of the occurrence in food samples of the BFRs covered in the current Opinion from studies reported in the scientific literature for European countries since the publication of the previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). The overview is not intended to be exhaustive.

Details of the studies are provided in Appendix B, including reported levels of the BFRs analysed (expressed as means or ranges), type of commodity, detection frequency, country and year of sampling.

No studies on occurrence in food from European countries were found for EBTEBPI, HBCYD, DBNPG, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, TBNPA, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP or TTBP‐TAZ.

For TDBPP, DBHCTD, HCTBPH and OBTMPI two studies were identified reporting levels below the LOD (Fernandes et al., 2016; Castro et al., 2020, see Appendix B).

The studies identified concerned BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE, BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, PBB‐Acr and TBX. Many of the studies failed to measure the BFRs since they were present at concentrations below the method LOD. The following paragraphs summarise those studies where compounds were measured above LOD and reported; more details including the LODs for the individual studies can be found in Appendix B.

Data from the scientific literature from European countries for BTBPE and HBB was broadly in line with the data submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1).

BEH‐TEBP

For BEH‐TEBP, no studies in the scientific literature were reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

Xu et al. (2017) reported analysis of flame retardants, including BEH‐TEBP, in 24 h duplicate diet samples collected 2013–2014 from a Norwegian cohort (n = 61), samples. BEH‐TEBP was detected in 44% of the samples with a mean concentration of 0.012 ± 0.023 ng/g ww (range from < 0.03 (LOQ) to 0.14 ng/g ww). Several studies reported measurements of BEH‐TEBP in individual food samples:

Xu et al. (2017) reported analysis of flame retardants, including BEH‐TEBP, in 24 h duplicate diet samples collected 2013–2014 from a Norwegian cohort (n = 61), samples. BEH‐TEBP was detected in 44% of the samples with a mean concentration of 0.012 ± 0.023 ng/g ww (range from < 0.03 (LOQ) to 0.14 ng/g ww). Several studies reported measurements of BEH‐TEBP in individual food samples:

In Germany, BEH‐TEBP was measured in blue mussels and eelpout filet composite samples, collected in 2015 from three coastal sites in the North and Baltic Seas (Fliedner et al., 2020). BEH‐TEBP ranged between < 0.0384–0.0559 ng/g ww in blue mussels and < 0.0404–0.0728 ng/g ww in eelpout filet.

In Germany, BEH‐TEBP was measured in blue mussels and eelpout filet composite samples, collected in 2015 from three coastal sites in the North and Baltic Seas (Fliedner et al., 2020). BEH‐TEBP ranged between < 0.0384–0.0559 ng/g ww in blue mussels and < 0.0404–0.0728 ng/g ww in eelpout filet.

In Belgium, Poma et al. (2018) reported concentrations of BFRs in individual food items (1289 samples) collected during 2015–2016. BEH‐TEBP was only found in two samples from the vegetable oil category (at 0.295 ng/g ww in mixed oils and 0.350 ng/g ww in olive oil), and in one sample from the Meat and meat products category (duck foie gras; 0.202 ng/g ww, LOD: 0.200 ng/g ww), however, the authors report possible analytical false positive results.

In the UK, Tao et al. (2017) studied the levels of BEH‐TEBP in samples of 14 different food groups collected in 2015. BEH‐TEBP was detected in 63% of the samples with mean concentrations ranging between 0.20 and 0.57 ng/g lipid in meat samples (beef, lamb, pork and chicken), < 0.10–1.1 ng/g lipid in fish samples (salmon, mackerel, tuna and trout), while mean concentration was reported as 1.8 ng/g lipid in eggs and 0.22 ng/g lipid in cheese samples.

Ma et al. (2023) also studied the levels in 108 individual food samples (covering 15 food categories) collected in the UK between 2020 and 2021. BEH‐TEBP was reported as one of the most abundant ‘emerging’ BFRs with 61% detection frequency and a mean concentration of 0.360 ng/g ww (or 11 ng/g lipid) (range: < 0.037–4.700 ng/g ww or < 0.33–65 ng/g lipid). The authors reported an increase in the levels of BEH‐TEBP between 2015 (Tao et al., 2017) and 2020–2021 in the different food categories, which was calculated at 1100%, 3000%, 140% and 11% in meat, fish, cheese and chicken eggs, respectively.

BEH‐TEBP concentrations reported in the Ma et al. (2023) study were found comparable to the concentrations reported in foodstuffs collected as part of the 6th total diet study (TDS) in China (from 24 provinces) < 0.015–3.009 ng/g ww (Niu et al., 2021) and from several e‐waste recycling sites in China (Wenling and Luqiao districts located within Taizhou City, one of the largest e‐waste recycling areas in Eastern China), i.e. 0.81–16.3 ng/g lw as reported by Labunska et al. (2015) and < 0.05–0.96 ng/g ww in home‐produced eggs reported by Zheng et al. (2016). Moreover, the concentration of BEH‐TEBP in this study were generally one order of magnitude higher than the concentrations reported in foodstuffs collected from Belgium (Poma et al., 2018), Tanzania (range: < LOD–9.8 ng/g lipid in eggs of free‐ranging chicken; Polder et al., 2016) and China, non‐e‐waste recycling areas (range: 1.11–15 ng/g lipid; Shi et al., 2016).

EH‐TBB

For EH‐TBB, no studies in the scientific literature were reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

In France, Vénisseau et al. (2018) reported concentrations of BFRs in food samples in 577 food samples comprising six different food categories (fish, crustacea and molluscs, milk, sheep livers, meat and eggs). EH‐TBB was measured in the samples and low detection frequencies and concentrations were found (in 10% of fish samples: range < LOD–10.0 pg/g ww; in 18% of crustacea and molluscs: < LOD–8.0 pg/g ww, in 4% of milk < LOD–2.92 pg/g ww, in 0% of sheep livers, in 9% of meat samples: < LOD–11.79 pg/g ww and in 5% of eggs: < LOD–1.26 pg/g ww).

In France, Vénisseau et al. (2018) reported concentrations of BFRs in food samples in 577 food samples comprising six different food categories (fish, crustacea and molluscs, milk, sheep livers, meat and eggs). EH‐TBB was measured in the samples and low detection frequencies and concentrations were found (in 10% of fish samples: range < LOD–10.0 pg/g ww; in 18% of crustacea and molluscs: < LOD–8.0 pg/g ww, in 4% of milk < LOD–2.92 pg/g ww, in 0% of sheep livers, in 9% of meat samples: < LOD–11.79 pg/g ww and in 5% of eggs: < LOD–1.26 pg/g ww).

Two studies retrieved from the UK reported high concentrations of EH‐TBB. Tao et al. (2017) reported concentrations of EH‐TBB in samples of 14 different food groups collected in 2015. EH‐TBB was detected in 77% of the samples analysed and mean concentrations reported ranged between 0.19 and 1.4 ng/g lipid in meat samples (beef, lamb, pork and chicken), 0.22–0.43 ng/g lipid in fish samples (salmon, mackerel, tuna and trout), while mean concentration was reported as 0.10 ng/g lipid in eggs and 0.11 ng/g lipid in cheese samples. Ma et al. (2023) reported concentrations of BFRs in UK food items collected between December 2020 and October 2021. A total of 108 individual food samples were analysed (covering 15 food categories). EH‐TBB was detected in 11% of the samples analysed with mean concentration reported at 21 pg/g ww (1.5 ng/g lipid) and range of < 4.4–290 pg/g ww (0.051–35 ng/g lipid). Overall, concentrations of EH‐TBB were found similar between 2015 (Tao et al., 2017) and 2020–2021 in the different food categories in the UK, however looking at the food categories separately, a 26% increase was observed in meat samples and a 93%, 88% and 22% decrease in cheese, eggs and fish, respectively.

Two studies retrieved from the UK reported high concentrations of EH‐TBB. Tao et al. (2017) reported concentrations of EH‐TBB in samples of 14 different food groups collected in 2015. EH‐TBB was detected in 77% of the samples analysed and mean concentrations reported ranged between 0.19 and 1.4 ng/g lipid in meat samples (beef, lamb, pork and chicken), 0.22–0.43 ng/g lipid in fish samples (salmon, mackerel, tuna and trout), while mean concentration was reported as 0.10 ng/g lipid in eggs and 0.11 ng/g lipid in cheese samples. Ma et al. (2023) reported concentrations of BFRs in UK food items collected between December 2020 and October 2021. A total of 108 individual food samples were analysed (covering 15 food categories). EH‐TBB was detected in 11% of the samples analysed with mean concentration reported at 21 pg/g ww (1.5 ng/g lipid) and range of < 4.4–290 pg/g ww (0.051–35 ng/g lipid). Overall, concentrations of EH‐TBB were found similar between 2015 (Tao et al., 2017) and 2020–2021 in the different food categories in the UK, however looking at the food categories separately, a 26% increase was observed in meat samples and a 93%, 88% and 22% decrease in cheese, eggs and fish, respectively.

Concentrations of EH‐TBB measured in UK foodstuffs were higher in comparison to concentrations reported in France (Vénisseau et al., 2018). In contrast to BEH‐TEBP, concentrations of EH‐TBB measured in UK foodstuffs were comparable to those reported in Chinese food samples (< 30–222 pg/g ww; Niu et al., 2021), and 1–2 orders of magnitude lower than the concentrations reported (range of 3.09–62.2 ng/g lipid) in foodstuffs collected from an e‐waste recycling site in China (Labunska et al., 2015).

Concentrations of EH‐TBB measured in UK foodstuffs were higher in comparison to concentrations reported in France (Vénisseau et al., 2018). In contrast to BEH‐TEBP, concentrations of EH‐TBB measured in UK foodstuffs were comparable to those reported in Chinese food samples (< 30–222 pg/g ww; Niu et al., 2021), and 1–2 orders of magnitude lower than the concentrations reported (range of 3.09–62.2 ng/g lipid) in foodstuffs collected from an e‐waste recycling site in China (Labunska et al., 2015).

DBDPE

For DBDPE, one study in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion (Tlustos et al., 2010). Since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

Santín et al. (2013) reported concentrations of up to 130 ng/kg lipid in various species of river fish from Spain.

Santín et al. (2013) reported concentrations of up to 130 ng/kg lipid in various species of river fish from Spain.

Ma et al. (2023) found mean concentrations of DBDPE of 18 ng/kg ww in meat, and 45 ng/kg ww in fish and prawns from the UK. An average dietary exposure estimate of 0.066–0.24 ng/kg bw per day, and a high‐end estimate of 0.39–1.5 ng/kg bw per day was made by the authors (see Section 3.3.1.2).

Ma et al. (2023) found mean concentrations of DBDPE of 18 ng/kg ww in meat, and 45 ng/kg ww in fish and prawns from the UK. An average dietary exposure estimate of 0.066–0.24 ng/kg bw per day, and a high‐end estimate of 0.39–1.5 ng/kg bw per day was made by the authors (see Section 3.3.1.2).

Munschy et al. (2015) found DBDPE in samples of mussels and oysters at concentrations up to 20.1 ng/kg ww in France.

BTBPE

For BTBPE, only one study in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion (Tlustos et al., 2010). Since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) measured BFRs, including BTBPE, in market basket samples comprising five different food categories (fish, meat, vegetable oils, dairy products and eggs, n = 4 homogenates for each food category) collected in 2010. BTBPE was detected at low concentrations in the range 1.1–3.6 pg/g ww in fish and dairy products, and in eggs with an average of 3.9 pg/g ww.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. Ma et al. (2023) found BTBPE in samples from the UK at mean concentrations of 190 ng/kg ww in meat, 200 ng/kg ww in fish and prawns, 97 ng/kg ww in cheese and 2300 ng/kg ww in chicken eggs. An average dietary exposure estimate of 1.2–4.4 ng/kg bw per day, and a high‐end estimate of 7.1–27 ng/kg bw per day was made by the authors (see Section 3.3.2.2).

HBB

For HBB, one study in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion (Tlustos et al., 2010). Since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

HBB was found by Zacs et al. (2021) where concentrations up to 9.82 ng/kg ww were found in meat, up to 5.71 ng/kg ww in eggs, up to 21.8 ng/kg ww in milk and dairy products, up to 17.8 ng/kg ww in fish and fish products and up to 19.5 ng/kg ww in products of plant origin from Latvia.

HBB was found by Zacs et al. (2021) where concentrations up to 9.82 ng/kg ww were found in meat, up to 5.71 ng/kg ww in eggs, up to 21.8 ng/kg ww in milk and dairy products, up to 17.8 ng/kg ww in fish and fish products and up to 19.5 ng/kg ww in products of plant origin from Latvia.

Vénisseau et al. (2018) reported mean concentrations of 0.363 ng/kg ww in fish (detected in 65% of samples), 0.257 ng/kg ww in Crustaceous/molluscs (57% samples), 0.321 ng/kg ww in milk (72% samples), 0.617 ng/kg ww in eggs (68% samples), 0.633 ng/kg ww in sheep liver (57% samples) and 0.701 ng/kg ww in meat (85% samples) from France.

Trabalón et al. (2017) found up to 0.2 ng/g ww in a variety of 10 different fish species.

DBE‐DBCH

For DBE‐DBCH no information on occurrence data in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, several studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified:

DBE‐DBCH was included in a study by Zacs et al. (2021) where it was found in samples of meat at concentrations up to 34.6 ng/kg ww, 25.0 ng/kg ww in eggs, 60.9 ng/kg ww in milk and dairy products, 132 ng/kg ww in fish and fish products and up to 28.6 ng/kg ww in food of plant origin from Latvia.

DBE‐DBCH was included in a study by Zacs et al. (2021) where it was found in samples of meat at concentrations up to 34.6 ng/kg ww, 25.0 ng/kg ww in eggs, 60.9 ng/kg ww in milk and dairy products, 132 ng/kg ww in fish and fish products and up to 28.6 ng/kg ww in food of plant origin from Latvia.

Tao et al. (2017) measured α‐DBE‐DBCH and β‐DBE‐DBCH separately in a variety of food products sampled in the UK. α‐DBE‐DBCH was found at concentrations of up to 0.95 ng/g lipid in meat, 1.6 ng/g lipid in liver samples of beef, lamb, chicken and pork, 1.3 ng/g lipid in fish, 0.42 ng/g lipid in eggs and 0.44 ng/g lipid in cheese. β‐DBE‐DBCH was found at concentrations of up to 7.6 ng/g lipid in meat, 85 ng/g lipid in liver, 39 ng/g lipid in fish, 3.0 ng/g lipid in eggs and 0.99 ng/g lipid in cheese.

For β‐DBE‐DBCH the intake estimate was 0.6 ng/day for mothers and 0.3 ng/day for toddlers again based on fish consumption only.

TBX

For TBX no information on occurrence data in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, TBX was reported to be found in Crustaceous/molluscs from France (Vénisseau et al., 2018) at mean concentrations of 0.123 ng/kg ww (73% of samples) and in fish samples in Sweden (Sahlström et al., 2015) with a mean concentration of 20 ng/kg ww (100% samples).

OBTMPI

For OBTMPI no information on occurrence data in the scientific literature was reported in the previous Opinion. This compound was included in the study by Fernandes et al. (2016) but nothing was measured above the LOD.

Food processing and food contact materials

No data were identified in the scientific literature about the effects of cooking and processing on emerging and novel BFRs in food. However, the CONTAM Panel considers it reasonable to assume that lipophilic BFRs will behave in a similar way to other lipophilic POPs, i.e. that (i) they are stable to heat at normal cooking temperatures and (ii) will associate with lipids, which means that amounts in food may be reduced if fats released from the food during cooking or processing are discarded. On the other hand, if moisture is lost during the heating of foods but no fat is discarded, then the total amount will remain unchanged but the concentrations may increase due to weight loss.

Food contact materials such as black plastic kitchen utensils made using recycled plastics have been shown to be a source of BFRs. For example, Liu et al. (2024) investigated consumer products purchased in the USA, including food serving items and kitchen utensils from recycled plastics. Approximately 15% of the tested food contact materials contained BFRs. The authors hypothesised that contamination of the recycled plastics with plastics from electronic devices etc. may be responsible for their findings, and therefore migration from such items may be a source of additional exposure to BFRs. There are only limited specific references on the presence of the BFRs assessed in this Opinion in food contact materials including kitchen utensils, such as for BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4.3). The potential additional exposure from kitchen utensils might have caused an underestimation of the dietary exposure. However, this contribution is likely to be minor and within the range of the large uncertainty due to the small amount of available occurrence data in food.

BFRs included in the 2012 Opinion and covered in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU

Tris(2,3‐dibromopropyl) phosphate (TDBPP) [CAS number 126‐72‐7]

Production, use, analysis and environmental fate

Production and use

WHO/IPCS (1995) reported that TDBPP was first produced in 1950. In 1975, the commercial production of TDBPP in the USA was reported to be 4100–5400 tonnes (US‐EPA, 1976). Prior to 1977, 4500 tonnes were produced annually in the US, but by 1986, production had ceased in the US. In Japan, production in 1976 and 1977 was estimated to have been 100 and 300 tonnes per year, respectively, but had ceased by 1995 (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

In addition to its use as a flame retardant for cellulose and triacetate and polyester fabrics, TDBPP has also been used in urethane foam and acrylic carpets and sheets, polyvinyl‐ and phenolic resins, polystyrene foam, paints, lacquers, paper, latexes and cured unsaturated polyesters products. Rigid foams containing TDBPP were used in insulation, furniture, automobile interior parts and water flotation devices. Around two thirds of the TDBPP produced in the USA in the 1970s was used in fabrics used for children's clothing which could contain about 5–10% by weight of the flame retardant (US‐EPA, 1976; Kirk‐Othmer, 1978–1984, as cited in EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Use and exposure has greatly decreased after a ruling by the US Consumer Product Safety Commission in April 1977 when TDBPP was removed from the market due to concerns about its toxicity, but there is evidence that TDBPP was used well after that decade. 25

Analysis

Analytical methods for TDBPP usually consist of some kind of extraction followed by clean‐up using techniques such as gel permeation chromatography (GPC) or sold phase extraction (SPE), followed by instrumental analysis using typically either GC–MS or liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC–MS).

Most of the scientific literature on TDBPP is focused on textiles or environmental samples (water, sediments, etc), but examples of the methods that were reported over the last decade that were used for food or wildlife are summarised below.

Castro et al. (2020) used matrix solid‐phase dispersion (MSPD), which integrated sample extraction and clean‐up in a single step, followed by liquid chromatography–tandem mass spectrometry (LC–MS/MS) for the simultaneous determination of 18 organophosphorus flame retardants and/or plasticisers including TDBPP in marine mussel (Mytilus edulis and Mytilus galloprovincialis) samples. For TDBPP the instrumental LOD (iLOD) was 16 ng/mL which was not as good as most of the other compounds because ionisation was less efficient. This equated to a LOD of 4 ng/g dry weight in the mussel samples.

In a study on glaucous gull from the Canadian Arctic, Verreault et al. (2018) measured a range of BFRs including TDBPP. Samples were homogenised with diatomaceous earth, extracted with dichloromethane:n‐hexane (50:50) using an accelerated solvent extractor followed by SPE using cartridges containing Isolute NH2 aminopropyl silica gel. Instrumental analysis was by LC–MS/MS, and the method had a LOD of 0.06 ng/g dry weight.

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

TDBPP has a reported log Koc value of 3.29 which accounts for its strong potential for adsorption to soil. This, along with a measured water solubility of around 8.0 mg/L, means that TDBPP is expected to leach only slowly into groundwater (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). No information on the environmental fate of TDBPP could be found, but there were several reports in the literature on concentrations in soil, sediment, water and dust.

There is very little reported on concentrations of TDBPP in environmental samples, especially from European Countries. Where studies have included TDBPP, it is often reported at very low levels, typically < LOD.

Occurrence in the environment

Soil

Zhang et al. (2021) reported on the spatial occurrence and composition profile of organophosphate esters in farmland soils from different regions of China. Whilst concentrations in soil were measured for 11 out of 12 compounds measured, TDBPP, with a LOD of 0.020 ng/g, was not found. This was consistent with earlier studies, in which TDBPP had a low detection frequency (< 20%) in the dust from rural areas of Nanjing, China.

Sediment

TDBPP was not detected (LOD = 0.03 ng/g) in a study on the optimisation and development of analytical methods for the determination of new BFRs in sediments and suspended particulate matter that included analysis of samples from the Western Scheldt estuary in the Netherlands (Brandsma et al., 2015).

TDBPP was detected in about one third of sediment samples tested from the Jiulong River Estuary and adjacent Western Taiwan Strait, at concentrations up to 0.926 ng/g in a study by Cai et al. (2022).

Dust

There are no studies reporting concentrations of TDBPP in samples of dust from Europe.

In a study on flame retardants in house dust samples from California, TDBPP was found (LOD = 20 ng/g) in 62% of samples taken in 2006 ranging from less than 20 ng/g to 8900 ng/g with a median of 35 ng/g. In samples dating from 2011, TDBPP was found in 38% ranging from less than 20 to 310 ng/g with the median being < LOD (Dodson et al., 2012).

Concentrations were much lower in a study by Li et al. (2020) reporting concentrations in dust from urban and rural domestic houses and college dormitories in Nanjing, China, where the highest concentration was just over 2 ng/g.

Water

There are a few reports of TDBPP in water, including a global systematic review by Vasseghian et al. (2022). It reported concentrations (μg/L) of different types of organophosphates in surface waters of China including TDBPP (4 samples) from 1.7 × 10−6 to 0.0016 with an average of 0.000477 and standard deviation of 0.000484.

Wildlife

Verreault et al. (2018) found TDBPP in glaucous gull from the Canadian Arctic in 14% of samples at concentrations up to 0.57 ng/g (w/w).

Xie et al. (2024) found TDBPP in liver samples of Indo‐Pacific finless porpoises from the northern South China Sea, China, taken between 2007 and 2020 at a mean concentration of 9.9 ng/g ww ranging from 1.6 to 25.8 ng/g with an LOD of 0.5 ng/g and a detection frequence of 70%.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), there were no data on the absorption of TDBPP.

Regarding distribution, and based on the study by Nomeir and Matthews (1983), the CONTAM Panel noted that after oral administration of 1.38 mg/kg bw (14C‐TDBPP) to male Sprague–Dawley rats, radioactivity was first detected in lung, liver and kidney. Radioactivity was also measured in other tissues (blood, muscle, skin and fat). The in vitro and in vivo data on metabolism showed transformation of TDBPP into BBPP (bis(2,3‐dibromopropyl) phosphate, a major metabolite). Five other metabolites were identified in urine and bile. The clearance half‐life of radioactivity ranged from 2.5 days (blood, muscle, skin, lung and fat) to 3.8 days (liver and kidney). 24 h after exposure, the radioactivity was excreted in the urine (24% of the radioactivity) and faeces (11.5%).

In a study by Minegishi et al. (1988) 26 , the authors performed studies on the absorption, distribution and excretion of flame retardants, including TDBPP, with a single administration by gavage of 14C‐labelled (50 μmol/kg) in male Wistar rats. The authors found that for TDBPP, the recovered radioactivity on day 7 was 50% in urine, 33% in faeces, 13% in expired air (as 14CO2) and 3.7% in the body. The measured biological half‐life in blood and different tissues was between 36 and 72 h.

Since the previous Opinion, no new studies have been identified.

In summary, these data show that TDBPP is highly absorbed (> 50%), highly metabolised, distributed and accumulated in different tissues, and is eliminated mainly via urine.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

The previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported that nephrotoxicity (pyknosis of the renal tubular epithelial cells on day 1, necrosis on day 2, regeneration from day 3 and large nuclei formation from day 4) was observed in male Wistar rats exposed to a single oral dose of TDBPP (reported purity 98%) at 200 mg/kg bw 27 and observed till day 10 (Fukuoka et al., 1988).

In a 4‐week dietary study, male weanling rats received 0, 100 or 1000 mg/kg TDBPP in the diet, equivalent to 0, 10 and 100 mg/kg bw per day, respectively (Kerst, 1974, as cited in NICNAS, 2005).

The CONTAM Panel identified a NOAEL of 10 mg/kg bw per day, based on significant decrease in body weight gain and food consumption and decreases in the weight of heart, liver, spleen, kidney and testes observed at 100 mg/kg bw per day. However, no histopathological changes were noted.

In a developmental toxicity study, pregnant Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to TDBPP (reported purity 99.7%) at 0, 5, 25 or 125 mg/kg bw per day on GD6–15 and sacrificed on GD20 (Seabaugh et al., 1981, as cited in NICNAS, 2005). The CONTAM Panel identified a NOAEL for maternal effects of 25 mg/kg bw per day based on decreased body weight gain at 125 mg/kg bw per day and the NOAEL for developmental effects was 125 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that TDBPP is a bacterial mutagen both in the presence and absence of metabolic activation (with a higher activity in the presence of metabolic activation), induced gene mutations and chromosomal aberrations in mammalian test systems in vitro and micronuclei (after ip exposure) and chromosomal aberrations (after iv exposure) in animals in vivo. It induced also DNA damage in the liver, kidneys and small intestine and to a lesser extent in the brain, lungs, testes, spleen and large intestine of rats exposed to a single ip injection of TDBPP. In transgenic rodent mutation assays, exposure by gavage of TDBPP was shown to induce mutations in the kidney and liver, but not in the stomach in lacI transgenic male B6C3F1 mice (Big Blue®) and increases in lacI mutations was also observed in the cortex and outer and inner medullas of the kidney in lacI (Big Blue) transgenic F344 rats (see EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Carcinogenic and toxic effects of TDBPP (administered as the technical product Firemaster LV‐T23P, purity: not reported (NR)) to the kidney have been investigated in rats (Reznik et al., 1979, as cited in NICNAS, 2005). F344 rats were fed diet containing 50 or 100 mg/kg TDBPP (corresponding to 2 and 4 mg/kg bw per day in males and 2.5 and 5 mg/kg bw per day in females) for 103 weeks followed by 1–2 weeks observation period prior to sacrifice. Tubular‐cell adenoma of the kidney was noted in TDBPP treated males and females. Tubular‐cell carcinoma was observed at the top dose in males. The only significant non‐neoplastic findings in kidneys were small foci of dilated and hyperplastic or dysplastic proximal convoluted tubules. These changes were seen in both males and females at the lowest dose.

The carcinogenic potential of TDBPP (administered as the technical product Firemaster LV‐T23P, purity: NR) has been tested in mice (Reznik et al., 1979, as cited in NICNAS, 2005; US‐NCI, 1978, as cited in WHO/IPCS, 1995). B6C3F1 mice were fed diet containing 500 or 1000 mg/kg TDBPP (corresponding to 60 and 120 mg/kg bw per day in males and 65 and 130 mg/kg bw per day in females) for 103 weeks followed by 1–2 weeks observation period prior to sacrifice. Tubular‐cell adenomas of the kidney were seen in TDBPP treated males and females. Tubular‐cell carcinomas were observed at the top dose in males. The only significant non‐neoplastic findings in kidneys were small foci of dilated and hyperplastic or dysplastic proximal convoluted tubules. These changes were seen in both males and females at the lowest dose. TDBPP increased the incidence of squamous‐cell papilloma and carcinoma of the forestomach and of adenoma and carcinoma of the lungs in both males and females. Moreover, in treated females there was an increased incidence of liver cell adenoma and carcinoma.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

No new studies were identified since the previous Opinion. However, more details on the carcinogenicity studies of TDBPP were provided in the NTP Report (2014) (see Appendix C, Table C.1). Previously the results were only available from secondary sources.

In rats, there was no significant effect on survival. Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma was observed in males and females (see Table 7). For males there was a significant positive association between the incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma and TDBPP at the highest dose. Non‐neoplastic lesions were seen in renal tubules in high‐dose males and females, a few tubular cells were slightly enlarged and showed nuclear dysplasia consisting of nuclear enlargement, chromatin clumping and parachromatin clearing. A clear effect was observed at the lowest dose tested.

Summary of tumour and non‐neoplastic lesions in rats in the NTP (2014) study.

In mice, there was no significant effect on survival. Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma in males and females was observed as well as renal tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma in males but not in females (see Table 8). Variable amounts of haemorrhage, necrosis and calcification were associated with renal tubular‐cell carcinoma. Renal tubular dysplasia was also observed in males and in females. Increased incidence of squamous‐cell papilloma of the forestomach was observed in males and females. In addition, carcinoma of the forestomach was observed in high‐dose males and both doses in females. Chronic inflammation was frequently found in the submucosa below the papillary lesion. The squamous‐cell carcinoma of the forestomach also often showed superficial hyperkeratinisation. A dose‐related increase in hepatocellular adenoma or carcinoma was noted in females. In males, no such dose‐related increase in hepatocellular neoplasms was apparent. Finally, bronchiolar/alveolar adenoma and carcinoma were seen in males and in females. A clear effect was observed at the lowest dose tested.

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

TDBPP is nephrotoxic. It is genotoxic, and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, forestomach, liver and lung).

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on TDBPP as retrieved by the previous Opinion (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results for TDBPP with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this positive prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

The QSAR Toolbox profilers provided the following information on the structural alerts and predicted mode of toxicity: according to the ‘DNA binding by OECD’ and ‘DNA alerts for Ames, CA and MNT by OASIS’ profilers, the alert ‘Vicinal Dihaloalkanes’ is triggered in TDBPP because of the two Br atoms in the vicinal position (see Figure 1). In the QSAR Toolbox, this alert is associated with the production of DNA adducts. In addition, the ‘in vitro mutagenicity (Ames test) by ISS’ profiler flags the presence of an alert associated to an alkyl ester of phosphonic acid, as of concern for genotoxicity due to the presence of electrophilic sites. The QSAR Toolbox profiling results are provided in Annex B, while additional information on the alerts triggered can be consulted within the software.

Chemical structures of the six BFRs included in the 2012 Opinion and covered by Recommendation 2014/118/EU: Tris(2,3‐dibromopropyl) phosphate (TDBPP), N,N′‐ethylenebis(tetrabromophthalimide) (EBTEBPI), Dibromoneopentyl glycol (DBNPG), Bis(2‐ethylhexyl) tetrabromophthalate (BEH‐TEBP), 2‐Ethylhexyl 2,3,4,5‐tetrabromobenzoate (EH‐TBB), Hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD).

The CONTAM Panel noted that the computational analysis supports the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Some in vitro studies on the mode of action of TDBPP were identified on metabolic disturbances, mitochondrial toxicity and mineralocorticoid effects (Gu et al., 2019; Ma et al., 2021; Zhang et al., 2017). However, none of them were related to the observed effects in experimental animals.

No in vivo studies in mammalian species were available on neurobehaviour and reproductive effects, which have been identified as critical effects for other BFR families. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel evaluated one study in a neuronal cell line and a study in zebrafish identified as possible indicators of such hazards.

In vitro, the effects of 50 μM TDBPP on cell number, cell growth and differentiation into dopaminergic or cholinergic phenotypes were studied in differentiated PC12 cells after 6 days of exposure (Dishaw et al., 2011). Results showed a significant reduction in cell number without any impairment of cell growth and a significant increase in the emergence of both dopaminergic and cholinergic neurophenotypes.

In zebrafish (Dishaw et al., 2014), larvae exposure to TDBPP from 0 to 5 days postfertilisation showed the greatest toxicity (expressed as the percentage of mortality) at a concentration of 1 μM and higher. Neurotoxicity has been studied through the measurement of the larvae swimming activity on the 6th day post‐fertilisation. TDBPP (0.56 and 1 μM) significantly altered the locomotor activity level whereas lower levels of exposure (0.1, 0.18 or 0.31 μM) did not. In addition, hypoactivity was observed with TDBPP only during the light phase of the cycle. In zebrafish larvae, TDBPP was reported to be more potent than the known neurotoxicant chlorpyrifos, which was included in the same study for comparison.

Taken together, these two studies provide some evidence of neurotoxicity for TDBPP.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel concluded that TDBPP is nephrotoxic. It is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats and mice. The critical studies are the carcinogenicity studies in rats and mice (see Section 3.2.2.3). No studies in humans were identified.

The Panel performed benchmark dose (BMD) modelling according to the 2022 EFSA Guidance on the use of the BMD approach in risk assessment (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022). The results of the BMD modelling for the critical studies in rats and mice (NTP, 2014) are summarised in Table 9. Details of the BMD analyses, including the individual reports of the modelling are shown in Annex D. For quantal data the default benchmark response (BMR) of 10% was applied as recommended in the EFSA BMD guidance (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022).

TABLE 9Benchmark dose (BMD) modelling for the critical studies of TDBPP (NTP, 2014; for details of the BMD analyses see Annex D).StudyObserved effect, (sex)BMDL10 BMD10 BMDU10 (mg/kg bw per day)RatsKidney tubular‐cell adenoma, F2.263.736.66Kidney tubular‐cell adenoma or adenocarcinoma, M 0.073 0.4110.859MiceKidney adenoma or adenocarcinoma, M42.469.997.7Kidney adenoma, F142255375Stomach papilloma or carcinoma, M13.540.866.3Stomach papilloma or carcinoma, F14.833.355.5Lung adenoma or carcinoma, M18.857.5256Lung adenoma or carcinoma, F35.973.1137Liver adenoma or carcinoma, F13.431.760.5

Benchmark dose (BMD) modelling for the critical studies of TDBPP (NTP, 2014; for details of the BMD analyses see Annex D).

The lowest BMDL10 was 0.073 mg/kg bw per day, based on combined incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenoma or carcinoma in male rats. Combining the incidence of these tumours was considered appropriate because renal adenomas and adenocarcinomas are part of the same neoplastic progression pathway. The CONTAM Panel noted that the dose‐spacing in this study was not optimal since there was only a two‐fold difference between the two dose levels tested, and both showed a similar tumour incidence. However, modelling of the data on the sum of kidney tubular‐cell adenoma and carcinoma in male rats gave an acceptable fit. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel selected this lowest BMDL10 as the Reference Point.

No Reference Point could be established for the non‐neoplastic effects that were identified as critical endpoints for other BFRs evaluated, e.g. neurotoxicity and reproductive toxicity.

Since TDBPP is both genotoxic and carcinogenic, the CONTAM Panel considered that no health‐based guidance value (HBGV) can be established as any level of exposure could theoretically lead to cancer (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2005). Instead, the margin of exposure (MOE) approach would normally be used for risk characterisation. For genotoxic and carcinogenic compounds, and according to EFSA Scientific Committee (2005); EFSA Scientific Committee (2025), a MOE of 10,000 or higher, if it is based on the BMDL10 for carcinogenicity in an animal study, would be of low concern from a public health point of view and might be considered as a low priority for risk management actions.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TDBPP in food were submitted to EFSA, and only one study in the scientific literature was identified reporting levels below the LOD (Castro et al., 2020, see Section 3.1.2). No estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

The uncertainty analysis was performed as described in Section 2.8. Due to the lack of data on occurrence and dietary exposure for TDBPP, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the hazard assessment. All the identified sources of uncertainty affecting the hazard assessment were assessed as having negligible or low impact on the conclusions (Table 10).
