This ordinal scale provides a relative assessment of the impact of the individual uncertainties as an aid to quantifying their combined impact by expert judgement, as described in Section 11 of EFSA Scientific Committee (2018).

Uncertainty analysis: Genotoxicity and carcinogenicity

Uncertainties affecting the evidence on the genotoxic potential of TDBPP (see Section 3.2.1.2.3.) were assessed to have negligible impact on the assessment (Table 10). TDBPP is genotoxic in vitro and in vivo, and carcinogenic in both mice and rats. Based on the overall evidence, and following an expert group discussion, the CONTAM Panel judged it was almost certain (99%–100% certainty) that TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic.

Uncertainty analysis: Reference Point for carcinogenicity

The Reference Point for carcinogenicity was affected by uncertainties arising from the number and spacing of doses in the critical study (NTP, 2014), which were assessed as having medium impact on the hazard characterisation (Table 10). Only two dose levels were used, at 2 and 4 mg/kg bw per day, resulting in similar responses (48% and 54% incidence of tumours) far above the BMR.

EFSA's Guidance on BMD analysis states that, in such cases, the available information may not be sufficient for estimating the BMD (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022) and, with only two active doses and a control, informative prior distributions would be needed for some of the parameters in the model to make the model identifiable (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022).

Nevertheless, results of the BMD analysis for the present case met the acceptability criteria contained in the EFSA BMD Guidance. In the advanced output available from the software (see Annex D, section D.2.1 and Figures therein) comparison between prior and posterior distributions for each parameter in the model showed peaked posteriors for all parameters, and less difference between prior and posterior for the technical parameter d, which influences the shape and slope of the fitted dose–response models. This indicates that the resulting model fits were driven mainly by the prior for this technical parameter d, which is therefore informative for this dataset. Recent simulation studies that include similar datasets have demonstrated that the default prior used for the technical parameter d produces BMD estimates which meet the acceptability criteria and BMDLs with appropriate coverage, i.e. close to the nominal 95% probability that BMDL < true BMD (Aerts et al., 2022).

The construction of the default prior used for the technical parameter d was inspired by the priors used in the US‐EPA Bayesian implementation of BMD analysis. It is biologically appropriate because it closely resembles the distribution of median d estimates obtained for data from more than 3000 NTP datasets analysed by Kremer et al. (2026). Furthermore, the mass of the prior for values below 1 is restricted to around 20%, which avoids estimation of the BMDL being implausibly low or on the boundary set by the optimisation algorithm as occurs when using a frequentist analysis, where prior distributions are not involved (Kremer et al., 2026).

Based on these considerations, and following an expert group discussion, the CONTAM Panel judged with at least 90% certainty (i.e. probability) that the Reference Point for TDBPP of 0.073 mg/kg bw per day would not be lower if the non‐standard uncertainties were resolved.

N,N'‐Ethylenebis(tetrabromophthalimide) (EBTEBPI) [CAS number 32588‐76‐4]

Production, use, analysis and environmental fate

Production and use

The previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported that EBTEBPI was used as an additive in high‐impact polystyrene (HIPS) (Weil & Levchik, 2009) and that it was stated to be stable against light and heat. It may be used in polyethylene, polypropylene, thermoplastic polyesters, polyamide, ethylene propylene‐diene terpolymers (EPDM rubbers) and other synthetic rubbers, polycarbonate, ethylene copolymers, ionomer resins, epoxies and textile treatments (WHO/IPCS, 1997).

ECHA, in its investigation report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024), stated that EBTEBPI is notified in 7% of notifications received for aromatic BFRs following a call for evidence to gather information on the uses. Most commonly reported sectors for the use of EBTEBPI were automotive followed by electrical and electronic equipment (EEE), while aerospace and defence, buildings and construction and textile sectors were reported sporadically.

Analysis

At the time of the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), no information could be identified on methods of analysis of EBTEBPI in environmental or food samples. No new information has been identified.

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

It was stated in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) that Hardy (2004) performed experimental studies on the bioconcentration of a number of BFRs in carp (Cyprinus carpio). The results on EBTEBPI were not fully conclusive, but a bioconcentration factor of < 0.3 or < 33 was indicated depending on the concentration tested. The experimental set‐up did not include exposure via feed. No new information on environmental fate or on occurrence in the environment was identified.

ECHA, in its investigation report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024), performed release estimation to identify the key substances, sectors and lifecycle steps for the overall release of aromatic BFRs to the environment. Among the aromatic BFRs investigated, EBTEBPI was found to contribute to 1.1% to the releases.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), the Panel identified a study where female rats were treated with EBTEBPI (14C‐labelled) by gavage at 0.67 mg/kg bw, daily for 14 days. The Panel noted that the main route of excretion was via the faeces (65% of the dose), whereas 15% of the dose was recovered in the urine. No accumulation was found in tissues.

No new studies were identified since the previous Opinion.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

The previous Opinion reported that in 28‐day and 90‐day dietary studies with EBTEBPI in Sprague–Dawley rats, the NOAELs identified by the CONTAM Panel were the highest dose tested, which were equivalent to about 1000 mg/kg bw per day (US‐EPA HPVIS, 2012; HSBD, 2005, as cited in EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

In a developmental toxicity study, pregnant female Sprague–Dawley rats were administered EBTEBPI by gavage at doses of up to 1000 mg/kg bw daily from GD6 to GD15 (Rodwell, 1988a, as cited in US‐EPA HPVIS, 2012; HSBD, 2005). In another study, pregnant New Zealand White rabbits were administered EBTEBPI by gavage at a dose of 1000 mg/kg bw daily from GD7 to GD19 (Rodwell, 1988b, as cited in US‐EPA HPVIS, 2012; HSBD, 2005). In both studies, the NOAEL for maternal and developmental toxicity identified by the CONTAM Panel were the highest dose tested, 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that the mutagenicity of EBTEBPI had been investigated in a number of in vitro systems. It was not mutagenic in several tests in S. Typhimurium and E. coli or in Saccharomyces cerevisiae (D4) (see EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

EBTEBPI had no porphyrinogenic activity in vitro in cultured chick embryo liver cells.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

No new studies have been identified except information on genotoxicity in the REACH dossier 28 as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

− EBTEBPI (purity NR) was not mutagenic to five strains of S. Typhimurium (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA1538) and E. coli, WP2 uvrA when tested with or without S9 metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver S9 (1982).

EBTEBPI (purity NR) was not mutagenic to five strains of S. Typhimurium (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA1538) and E. coli, WP2 uvrA when tested with or without S9 metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver S9 (1982).

− EBTEBPI (purity NR) was reported to be negative in an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in CHO cells in the presence or absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat hepatic S9) (2008).

EBTEBPI (purity NR) was reported to be negative in an in vitro chromosomal aberration assay in CHO cells in the presence or absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat hepatic S9) (2008).

− EBTEBPI (purity NR) was reported to be negative in an in vitro gene mutation test in Chinese hamster V79 cells using the hprt gene in the presence or in the absence of metabolic activation (phenobarbitone/beta‐naphthoflavone rat liver S9‐mix) (2016).

EBTEBPI (purity NR) was reported to be negative in an in vitro gene mutation test in Chinese hamster V79 cells using the hprt gene in the presence or in the absence of metabolic activation (phenobarbitone/beta‐naphthoflavone rat liver S9‐mix) (2016).

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

Based on the limited data available, EBTEBPI did not show effects in short‐term (28‐ and 90‐day) toxicity studies in rats or in developmental toxicity studies in rats and rabbits, up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested. No chronic toxicity/carcinogenicity, reproductive toxicity or developmental neurotoxicity studies were identified.

According to the data reported in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI was not mutagenic in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and E. coli WP2uvrA with or without metabolic activation. It was also negative in a gene mutation assay at the hprt locus in V79 cells and in a chromosomal aberration test in CHO cells in presence or absence of metabolic activation. The aneugenic potential of EBTEBPI has not been investigated. No in vivo genotoxicity data were identified, but would not be required considering the negative in vitro data (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2011). Based on this information, the CONTAM Panel concluded that EBTEBPI is not genotoxic.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on EBTEBPI as retrieved by the previous Opinion (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for EBTEBPI with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

In the previous Opinion, two studies were reported, neither of them providing useful information for the hazard assessment. No new studies were identified.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Based on the information reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel concluded that EBTEBPI is not genotoxic.

Because of the limited data available, the CONTAM Panel concluded that it was not possible to identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on EBTEBPI in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update (see Section 3.1.2). Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the lack of data on occurrence and dietary exposure for EBTEBPI, and the insufficiency of the available toxicological data for establishing a Reference Point, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the assessment of genotoxic potential. In view of the negative in vitro data in bacteria and mammalian cells (gene mutation and chromosomal damage), with no identified sources of uncertainty, the CONTAM Panel judged with > 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that EBTEBPI is not genotoxic.

Hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD) [CAS number 25495‐98‐1]

Production, use, analysis and environmental fate

Production and use

There is very little information in the scientific literature about HBCYD. It is unfortunate that there is great similarity in both name and abbreviations often used for hexabromocyclododecane (HBCDDs; HBCD) and hexabromocyclodecane (HBCYD, HBCD). This leads to confusion sometimes even within the same published article (see Section 1.2).

The previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) stated that HBCYD together with tetrakis(hydroxymethyl)phosphonium salts may be used as an alternative to cyclic methylphosphonates in cotton‐polyester blends (Weil & Levchik, 2009).

Analysis

No new literature has been identified that could be unambiguously associated with HBCYD (and not confused with HBCDDs).

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

No new information has been identified in the literature since the previous Opinion that could be unambiguously associated with HBCYD (and not confused with HBCDDs).

Hazard identification and characterisation

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on HBCYD. Regarding the mode of action, in the previous Opinion one study was reported, but it did not provide useful information for the hazard assessment.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for HBCYD, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on HBCYD (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for HBCYD with ‘LOW reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability (see Section 2.2).

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on HBCYD in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Bis(2‐ethylhexyl) tetrabromophthalate (BEH‐TEBP) [CAS number 26040‐51‐7]

Production, use, analysis and environmental fate

Production and use

The previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported that BEH‐TEBP is used as an additive flame retardant replacing PentaBDE in many applications, e.g. in polyurethane foam, PVC and neoprene rubber and in wire and cable insulation. It is also used as a plasticiser (Andersson et al., 2006; de Wit et al., 2011). BEH‐TEBP may also be used in combination with EH‐TBB (see Section 3.1.5), in differing proportions resulting in commercial mixtures with different trade names.

Annual production in the United States of America (US) was 450–4500 tonnes during 1990–2006 (US‐EPA, 2006, 2010, as cited by de Wit et al., 2011).

Analysis

Little has changed since the previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) where it was reported that BEH‐TEBP can be analysed using multi‐analyte methods (i.e. methods that analyse BEH‐TEBP together with other BFRs) (e.g. Covaci et al., 2011; Klif, 2010). Extraction of BEH‐TEBP is usually done with solvent mixtures of medium polarity such as cyclohexane:acetone or dichloromethane:n‐hexane. Clean‐up can be performed using gel permeation chromatography (GPC) for lipid removal, florisil (Klif, 2010) or alternatively it can be isolated using pressurised liquid extraction (PLE) followed by clean‐up using acid silica and subsequent carbon column clean‐up (Zhou et al., 2010). Instrumental analysis is usually performed using either GC–MS or LC–MS based methods.

Since the previous Opinion was published, there have been many publications on BEH‐TEBP in dust and environmental samples but very few of food. Bichon et al. (2018) reported on ‘simultaneous analysis of historical, emerging and novel BFRs in food and feed’ (including BEH‐TEBP) using a common extraction and purification method. The method used falls into the description above and was based on clean‐up using acidified silica, florisil and carbon columns, followed by analysis using gas and liquid chromatography coupled to high resolution and tandem mass spectrometry.

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

There is very little information about the environmental fate of BEH‐TEBP but given the similar chemical characteristics it can be assumed to behave in a broadly similar way to the other BFRs. There is one study reported by de Jourdan et al. (2013) on the environmental fate and persistence of three novel brominated flame retardants in aquatic mesocosms, where Firemaster BZ‐54, a commercial mixture containing BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB in a ratio of 1:4 was one of the compounds investigated. It was found that BEH‐TEBP (and other compounds) was environmentally persistent in both the particulate and the sediment compartments. The median dissipation times (DT50) differed in each compartment, with more rapid disappearance in the particulate (9–30 days) compared with the sediment compartment (> 100 days). The degradation products were more concentrated in the particulate compartment and corresponded to known photodegradation products; the proposed degradation pathway being through the hydrolysis of the ester groups to form an anhydride. The ratio of EH‐TBB to BEH‐TEBP differed in the mesocosm compartments compared with the technical product used for treatment, indicating increased degradation of EH‐TBB relative to BEH‐TEBP (de Jourdan et al., 2013).

Occurrence in the environment

Air

Drage et al. (2016) reported on concentrations of flame retardants (including BEH‐TEBP) in air and soil on a transect in the West Midlands (UK). BEH‐TEBP was found in a third of the air samples tested, but could not be quantified due to the low recovery of the relevant internal standards.

In studies by Salamova et al. (2014) and Hao et al. (2020) where BFRs were measured in air from the arctic region, BEH‐TEBP was included but not detected.

BEH‐TEBP has also been investigated in air in studies that were conducted outside Europe and concentrations were always very low (e.g. an average of 3.61 pg/m3 was reported for BEH‐TEBP in air samples collected in a test home in Harbin, China, between January 2017 and June 2018).

ECHA, in its investigation report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024) conducted a review and found several reports of BEH‐TEBP in air. Concentrations were generally between < LOD and 60 pg/m3 except where there were known or suspected sources of contamination, e.g. near e‐waste dismantling sites. Higher concentrations were also found in air samples taken from Arctic locations as is common for persistent organic pollutants in general (Carlsson et al., 2018).

Soil

Drage et al. (2016) reported on concentrations of flame retardants (including BEH‐TEBP) in air and soil on a transect in the West Midlands (UK). BEH‐TEBP was not found in soil. The ECHA (2024) review identified a study on BFRs in indoor and outdoor media in Stockholm, Sweden but BEH‐TEBP was not detected in soil (Newton et al., 2015). No studies with quantified data for BEH‐TEBP in soil could be found for European countries or elsewhere globally.

Sediment

No data were identified for BEH‐TEBP in sediment from European countries, but there were some studies from outside Europe (Chokwe et al., 2019; Olukunle & Okonkwo, 2015; Xu et al., 2012). Concentrations were < 60 ng/g dry weight, where detected.

Dust

For many BFRs dust can be an important source of exposure. This is especially the case for toddlers and infants due to low body weight, high crawling activity and high frequency of hand‐to‐mouth contact. A summary of reported occurrence of BEH‐TEBP in dust samples from European countries, and estimates of exposure via dust when reported by the authors is given in Appendix D (Table D.1). Concentrations reported are typically < 2 ng/g to 5000 ng/g but values as high as 25,000 were reported for dust from UK offices (Tao et al., 2016). Exposure estimates are typically a few 10s of ng/kg bw per day with a high value reported of 47 ng/kg bw per day for children in a study conducted in Spain (Esplugas et al., 2022). These values are comparable to the exposure estimates reported in the literature for BEH‐TEBP from the diet, e.g. 0.91 to 3.3 ng/kg bw per day across population groups (Ma et al., 2023) and 1.2 ng/kg bw per day for the 95th percentile (Xu et al., 2017) (see Section 3.2.4.3).

Water

No reports on BEH‐TEBP in inland water were identified for Europe. Guo et al. (2017) measured the concentrations of 65 flame retardants in water samples from five Lake Michigan tributaries. The mean concentrations of BEH‐TEBP from five different sites investigated ranged from 83 to 690 pg/L.

The ECHA investigation report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024) identified a study (Carlsson et al., 2018) that reported concentrations of up to 7100 ng/L (7,100,000 pg/L) in marine water from the Arctic. Other studies identified in their search reported concentration that were < 3 ng/L.

Wildlife

Most of the studies on BEH‐TEBP in wildlife that are reported are on either fish or birds. There is one study from Europe (Sühring et al., 2013) on BFRs in eels from German rivers. Where detected, concentrations were < 7.4 ng/g. Other studies identified were from outside Europe and concentrations, when detected in fish, birds or bird eggs, were of the same order of magnitude.

The ECHA investigation report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024) identified reports of BEH‐TEBP in whale, seal, bear and gull of up to 6090 pg/g lipid weight taken from the Norwegian Arctic, and concentrations of around 400 pg/g dry weight in tree leaves and shoots (Fagus sylvatica or Populus nigra italica) from Germany. Marine specimens from the North Sea had concentrations of < 75 pg/g.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information relating to toxicokinetics in mammals was described in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). Since then, several studies have been identified.

Because of the similar structure between DEHP and BEH‐TEBP, Springer et al. (2012) hypothesised that the cleavage by spontaneous‐ or esterase‐mediated hydrolysis of BEH‐TEBP could lead to a monoester metabolite mono‐(2‐ethyhexyl) tetrabromophthalate (TBMEHP), as was observed for DEHP with the formation of MEHP. The authors studied the in vitro metabolism of BEH‐TEBP using commercial hepatic porcine esterase, and confirmed the formation of TBMEHP.

Roberts et al. (2012) investigated the metabolism of BEH‐TEBP in human and rat liver microsomes. The authors did not detect metabolites of BEH‐TEBP in human or rat subcellular fractions.

Bearr et al. (2012) studied the in vitro metabolism of the flame‐retardant mixture Firemaster BZ‐54, containing BEH‐TEBP in mice (and other non‐mammalian species) hepatic subcellular fractions. The authors found that BEH‐TEBP was poorly metabolised (metabolites had not been characterised).

Phillips et al. (2016) studied the maternal transfer of Firemaster 550 components containing BEH‐TEBP in Wistar rat. Rats were orally exposed to 300 or 1000 mg of Firemaster 550 for 10 consecutive days during gestation (GD9–18) or lactation (PND3–12). The authors concluded that low levels of BEH‐TEBP were transferred to the fetus during both lactation and gestation.

Silva et al. (2016) orally administered a single dose of 500 mg/kg bw of the technical product Uniplex FRP‐45, reported to contain > 95% BEH‐TEBP plus < 5% of unknown impurities (the authors reported the identification of EH‐TBB < 5%) to female Sprague–Dawley rats. The authors identified tetrabromophthalic acid (TBPA) and tetrabromobenzoic acid (TBBA) in the urine and serum of rat (faeces were not collected).

Knudsen, Sanders, and Birnbaum (2017) investigated the toxicokinetics of BEH‐TEBP in female Sprague–Dawley rats and male B6C3F1 mice by administering a single dose and repeated exposure of [14C]‐BEH‐TEBP by gavage and iv route at 0.07 mg/kg or 7 mg/kg. The authors showed that in rats, after oral administration, less than 0.3% of BEH‐TEBP dose was excreted in urine, whereas 98% of the radioactivity was eliminated in faeces by 72 h. The authors noted that less than 0.1% of the oral dose (0.07 mg/kg) was eliminated in the bile over the first 24 h. After iv injection (0.07 mg/kg), BEH‐TEBP was metabolised to TBMEHP (% conversion not reported); both the parent compound and metabolite were eliminated via biliary excretion. The authors observed that male B6C3F1 mice appeared to excrete a similar proportion of the dose, but eliminated BEH‐TEBP more rapidly via the faecal route than female rats.

After repeated administration, the same authors assessed accumulation potential of BEH‐TEBP in female Sprague–Dawley rats by measuring radioactivity recoveries in excreta and tissues collected 24 h after 10 daily oral administrations of BEH‐TEBP at 0.07 mg/kg (Knudsen, Sanders, & Birnbaum, 2017). The authors stated that BEH‐TEBP could accumulate in some tissues (mainly in liver and adrenal tissues); specifically, the study results showed that concentrations in adrenal and liver tissue increased after 10 exposures compared to a single administration but the % of radioactivity in adrenal and liver tissue was 0.01% and 0.4%, respectively.

Chen et al. (2020) administered BEH‐TEBP to female Balb/c mice by gavage (8 μg/mL, approximately 200 μg/kg bw) and analysed for metabolites after 4 and 8 h post exposure. The authors did not detect metabolites in blood.

In summary, after oral administration in rodents BEH‐TEBP was poorly absorbed and eliminated mainly by the faecal route as a parent compound. After repeated exposure to BEH‐TEBP accumulation in some tissues has been reported (mainly in liver and adrenal tissues).

Levels in humans

No information on levels of BEH‐TEBP in human samples was reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, several studies have been identified reporting concentrations of BEH‐TEBP as well as other BFRs covered in this assessment in samples from European countries. Details of these studies are reported in Appendix E (Table E.1), and a brief summary of the results related to BEH‐TEBP are reported below.

Regarding human milk samples,

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), BEH‐TEBP was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) along with 10 PBDEs and 17 other alternative halogenated flame retardants. BEH‐TEBP was reported to have a detection frequency in the human milk samples analysed of < 29% (LOD = 0.023 ng/g lipid). Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.613 to 1.34 ng/g lipid and from 1.432 to 2.750 ng/g lipid, respectively, and BEH‐TEBP concentrations were reported to be the highest among the analysed halogenated flame retardants across all countries.

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), BEH‐TEBP was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) along with 10 PBDEs and 17 other alternative halogenated flame retardants. BEH‐TEBP was reported to have a detection frequency in the human milk samples analysed of < 29% (LOD = 0.023 ng/g lipid). Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.613 to 1.34 ng/g lipid and from 1.432 to 2.750 ng/g lipid, respectively, and BEH‐TEBP concentrations were reported to be the highest among the analysed halogenated flame retardants across all countries.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs. In 2010, the levels of BEH‐TEBP ranged from < 0.1 to 4.6 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 36%), while in 2014–2015 a higher detection frequency (50%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.1 to 0.73 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.25 ng/g lipid.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs. In 2010, the levels of BEH‐TEBP ranged from < 0.1 to 4.6 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 36%), while in 2014–2015 a higher detection frequency (50%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.1 to 0.73 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.25 ng/g lipid.

Sahlström et al. (2015) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOD (< 26 pg/g ww) in two pooled human milk samples from Sweden collected in 2009–2010.

Sahlström et al. (2015) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOD (< 26 pg/g ww) in two pooled human milk samples from Sweden collected in 2009–2010.

Regarding levels in blood/serum

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOD (30 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOD (30 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

BEH‐TEBP was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 7.0 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

BEH‐TEBP was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 7.0 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Van der Schyff et al. (2023) reported on flame retardants in children from nine European countries from the HBM4EU aligned studies. BEH‐TEBP was investigated in blood from France only collected between 2014 and 2016, and was reported to be below the LOQ (0.7 μg/L) in all samples analysed.

Van der Schyff et al. (2023) reported on flame retardants in children from nine European countries from the HBM4EU aligned studies. BEH‐TEBP was investigated in blood from France only collected between 2014 and 2016, and was reported to be below the LOQ (0.7 μg/L) in all samples analysed.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOQ (5 pg/g serum) in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect BEH‐TEBP above the LOQ (5 pg/g serum) in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No studies in experimental animals were identified in the previous Opinion.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, several new studies have been identified.

BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) was administered to groups of 8 male Balb/c mice by gavage at 0 or 200 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days and then the livers were examined for histopathological findings and for biochemical parameters (Yin & Zhang, 2021). The authors reported that BEH‐TEBP resulted in hepatocyte oedema, narrowing of the sinusoids and dilation of the central vein.

BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) was administered to groups of 8 male Balb/c mice by gavage at 0 or 200 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days and then the livers were examined for histopathological findings and for biochemical parameters (Yin & Zhang, 2021). The authors reported that BEH‐TEBP resulted in hepatocyte oedema, narrowing of the sinusoids and dilation of the central vein.

Three groups of Balb/c male mice (5 weeks of age, n = 8/group) were daily exposed by gavage to saline or BEH‐TEBP at 0, 20 or 200 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days and examined for their learning and memory performances in a Morris water maze (Bao & Jing, 2021). Behavioural results did not show learning impairments during the 5‐day training period of the test. The memory retention of the location of the platform was tested at the 7th day of testing with results showing non‐significant dose‐related reductions in the time spent in the platform quadrant and the number of crossings of the platform area.

Three groups of Balb/c male mice (5 weeks of age, n = 8/group) were daily exposed by gavage to saline or BEH‐TEBP at 0, 20 or 200 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days and examined for their learning and memory performances in a Morris water maze (Bao & Jing, 2021). Behavioural results did not show learning impairments during the 5‐day training period of the test. The memory retention of the location of the platform was tested at the 7th day of testing with results showing non‐significant dose‐related reductions in the time spent in the platform quadrant and the number of crossings of the platform area.

Two groups of Balb/c male mice (8–9 weeks of age, n = 10/group) were daily exposed to saline added with DMSO or BEH‐TEBP at a dose of 20 mg/kg bw per day for 60 days and explored for their learning and memory performances in a step‐down passive avoidance test and a Morris water maze (Xiong et al., 2024). The results showed learning and memory disturbances in both tests reflected by a significant increase in the number of errors in the 1 and 24 h‐ step‐down test, and a significant reduction in the latency and number of the platform crossed in the Morris water maze. The CONTAM Panel noted that this study provided limited information due to the single dose level and unclear information on the route of administration of BEH‐TEBP, and did not consider it further.

Two groups of Balb/c male mice (8–9 weeks of age, n = 10/group) were daily exposed to saline added with DMSO or BEH‐TEBP at a dose of 20 mg/kg bw per day for 60 days and explored for their learning and memory performances in a step‐down passive avoidance test and a Morris water maze (Xiong et al., 2024). The results showed learning and memory disturbances in both tests reflected by a significant increase in the number of errors in the 1 and 24 h‐ step‐down test, and a significant reduction in the latency and number of the platform crossed in the Morris water maze. The CONTAM Panel noted that this study provided limited information due to the single dose level and unclear information on the route of administration of BEH‐TEBP, and did not consider it further.

A 90‐day repeated dose oral toxicity study has been conducted in groups of 10 male and 10 female Wistar Han rats with BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) administered by gavage doses of 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Fulcher, 2018; REACH dossier, 29 as submitted by the registrant(s)). The study was performed in compliance with GLP and OECD TG408. There were no treatment‐related findings in the wide range of observations, including mortality, body and organ weights, functional performance tests of motor activity and grip strength, sensory reactivity, ophthalmology, haematology, blood chemistry and histopathology. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

A 90‐day repeated dose oral toxicity study has been conducted in groups of 10 male and 10 female Wistar Han rats with BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) administered by gavage doses of 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Fulcher, 2018; REACH dossier, 29 as submitted by the registrant(s)). The study was performed in compliance with GLP and OECD TG408. There were no treatment‐related findings in the wide range of observations, including mortality, body and organ weights, functional performance tests of motor activity and grip strength, sensory reactivity, ophthalmology, haematology, blood chemistry and histopathology. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

In a comparative study on effects of a number of BFRs on liver and thyroid toxicity, conducted by the NTP, BEH‐TEBP was administered for 5 days via oral gavage to groups of 6 male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats at doses of 0, 0.07, 0.71, 7.1, 71 and 706 mg/kg bw per day (NTP, 2019a; Shockley et al., 2020a). After the 5‐day exposure, there were no dose‐related effects on survival, body weight, gross pathology, organ weights or histopathology of the liver or thyroid gland or levels of thyroid hormones (NTP, 2019a; Shockley et al., 2020a). Shockley et al., (2020a) in an investigation of metabolic oxidative stress and liver disease pathways found no changes in the transcriptome related to these parameters, indicating that liver toxicity would not be anticipated also after longer exposure.

In a comparative study on effects of a number of BFRs on liver and thyroid toxicity, conducted by the NTP, BEH‐TEBP was administered for 5 days via oral gavage to groups of 6 male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats at doses of 0, 0.07, 0.71, 7.1, 71 and 706 mg/kg bw per day (NTP, 2019a; Shockley et al., 2020a). After the 5‐day exposure, there were no dose‐related effects on survival, body weight, gross pathology, organ weights or histopathology of the liver or thyroid gland or levels of thyroid hormones (NTP, 2019a; Shockley et al., 2020a). Shockley et al., (2020a) in an investigation of metabolic oxidative stress and liver disease pathways found no changes in the transcriptome related to these parameters, indicating that liver toxicity would not be anticipated also after longer exposure.

Yuan et al. (2024a) dosed 6‐weeks old Sprague–Dawley male rats with BEH‐TEBP (purity 95%) at 0, 5, 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days by gavage. BEH‐TEBP exposure resulted in significant dose‐related increases in serum TT4 and FT4 levels at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, with corresponding decreases in TSH and relative thyroid weight, which were significant at the highest dose. There was no effect on TT3 or FT3. The authors reported that histopathological analysis of the thyroid showed that the thyroid follicular epithelium in the high‐dose group was flatter and thinner, and the follicular cavity was markedly dilated, compared with the control group.

Yuan et al. (2024a) dosed 6‐weeks old Sprague–Dawley male rats with BEH‐TEBP (purity 95%) at 0, 5, 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days by gavage. BEH‐TEBP exposure resulted in significant dose‐related increases in serum TT4 and FT4 levels at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, with corresponding decreases in TSH and relative thyroid weight, which were significant at the highest dose. There was no effect on TT3 or FT3. The authors reported that histopathological analysis of the thyroid showed that the thyroid follicular epithelium in the high‐dose group was flatter and thinner, and the follicular cavity was markedly dilated, compared with the control group.

Fetal testosterone production was unaltered on GD18 in rats where the pregnant dams were exposed to BEH‐TEBP at a single dose of 750 mg/kg bw per day from GD14 to GD18, which is a critical period for sexual differentiation of the reproductive tract (Furr et al., 2014).

Fetal testosterone production was unaltered on GD18 in rats where the pregnant dams were exposed to BEH‐TEBP at a single dose of 750 mg/kg bw per day from GD14 to GD18, which is a critical period for sexual differentiation of the reproductive tract (Furr et al., 2014).

The same daily dose of 750 mg/kg per day given by daily oral gavage to pregnant rats from GD17 to GD21 did not induce formation of multinucleated germ cells in the testes of fetuses at GD21 (Spade et al., 2018).

The same daily dose of 750 mg/kg per day given by daily oral gavage to pregnant rats from GD17 to GD21 did not induce formation of multinucleated germ cells in the testes of fetuses at GD21 (Spade et al., 2018).

Information on repeated dose toxicity studies was available in the REACH dossier on BEH‐TEBP, as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

−In a 28‐day feeding study (OECD TG 407, OECD, 2008), CD rats were exposed to 0, 22, 223 or 2331 mg/kg bw per day to BEH‐TEBP (purity > 95%). The authors stated that only minor changes were observed and only at the highest dose (slightly lower bodyweight gain in females, marginally low alanine aminotransferase (ALT) serum levels compared to concurrent controls in females, and marginally low phosphorus concentrations in animals of both sexes).

In a 28‐day feeding study (OECD TG 407, OECD, 2008), CD rats were exposed to 0, 22, 223 or 2331 mg/kg bw per day to BEH‐TEBP (purity > 95%). The authors stated that only minor changes were observed and only at the highest dose (slightly lower bodyweight gain in females, marginally low alanine aminotransferase (ALT) serum levels compared to concurrent controls in females, and marginally low phosphorus concentrations in animals of both sexes).

−In a developmental toxicity study in Sprague–Dawley rats (OECD TG414, OECD, 2018), pregnant female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage from GD5 to GD19 to 0, 250, 500 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day BEH‐TEBP (purity 90.6%). No treatment‐related effects in dams were observed. Some changes in skeletal ossification were recorded in fetuses/offspring at the highest dose. However, the authors considered these to be normally occurring variations. The authors reported a NOAEL for both maternal and developmental toxicity of 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

In a developmental toxicity study in Sprague–Dawley rats (OECD TG414, OECD, 2018), pregnant female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage from GD5 to GD19 to 0, 250, 500 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day BEH‐TEBP (purity 90.6%). No treatment‐related effects in dams were observed. Some changes in skeletal ossification were recorded in fetuses/offspring at the highest dose. However, the authors considered these to be normally occurring variations. The authors reported a NOAEL for both maternal and developmental toxicity of 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

Some studies considered binary mixtures of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB (Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023) or the technical product Firemaster BZ‐54 which contains only BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB (Hays & Kirman, 2017). Considering the limited knowledge of effects with experiments with single chemicals, the CONTAM Panel found it useful to consider evidence from these mixture studies with known composition and limited impurities. These studies are briefly described below.

Witchey et al. (2020) exposed pregnant Wistar rats orally to a number of flame retardant mixtures including a binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB in the relative proportions present in the technical product Firemaster 550 (68.1% EH‐TBB and 31.9% BEH‐TEBP). The binary mixture was administered on a wafer cookie starting at 72 h after mating and continued until PND21 at a single dose level of approximately 3.3 mg/kg bw per day (equivalent to 1.05 mg/kg bw per day BEH‐TEBP and 2.25 mg/kg bw per day EH‐TBB), based on the initial weight of the dams. There was a decrease in body weight gain in the offspring throughout the observation period. From PND65, the pups were randomly selected for behavioural testing. Changes in socioemotional behaviour were reported with deficits greater in female offspring than in males. PND1 pups from this study were subjected to transcriptomic and lipidomic analyses (Witchey et al., 2023) (see Section 3.2.4.2.5 on mode of action).

Subsequently, Schkoda et al. (2023) analysed tissues collected from the animals reported in Witchey et al. (2020) for skeletal effects. Bone health and parameters such as cortical and trabecular density were evaluated in offspring at 8 months using X‐ray micro‐computed tomography. Serum was collected from the trunk blood at the time of killing and used for analysis of 5‐hydroxytryptophan and osteocalcin. BFR exposure of dams resulted in reduced bone mineral density of the femur in female offspring, with no effects observed in males. The average marrow area of the femur was also decreased in female offspring but not in males. There were no changes in 5‐hydroxytryptophan or osteocalcin concentrations in the serum of BFR exposed offspring.

Hays and Kirman (2017) summarised data from three unpublished studies carried out on effects of Firemaster BZ‐54 in rodents and used these for risk assessment of this technical product. Firemaster BZ‐54 contained 70% EH‐TBB and 30% BEH‐TEBP. For two of these studies, Hays and Kirman (2017) did not report any data other than NO(A)ELs/LO(A)ELs. For the third study, dose–response data were reported for changes in body weight and spleen weight. The three studies were conducted in the same strain of Sprague–Dawley rats [Crl:CD(SD)]. The studies are briefly summarised below:

−Study 1 (WIL, 1997, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): In a 28‐day study, groups of six rats of each sex were given oral (gavage) doses of 0, 160, 400 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 dissolved in corn oil. Males in the highest dose group exhibited reduced food consumption and body weight, while females showed these effects across all dose groups. Clinical observations revealed relaxed vaginal opening in all treated female groups and salivation in both sexes at the highest dose. Renal tubule epithelial regeneration was observed in a dose‐dependent manner in exposed males from all treatment groups and in 100% of the exposed female animals. The authors conducting the study identified a lowest observed effect level (LOEL) of 160 mg/kg bw per day for BZ‐54. In the absence of access to the data to support this conclusion, the CONTAM Panel was not able to use this study for hazard characterisation.

Study 1 (WIL, 1997, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): In a 28‐day study, groups of six rats of each sex were given oral (gavage) doses of 0, 160, 400 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 dissolved in corn oil. Males in the highest dose group exhibited reduced food consumption and body weight, while females showed these effects across all dose groups. Clinical observations revealed relaxed vaginal opening in all treated female groups and salivation in both sexes at the highest dose. Renal tubule epithelial regeneration was observed in a dose‐dependent manner in exposed males from all treatment groups and in 100% of the exposed female animals. The authors conducting the study identified a lowest observed effect level (LOEL) of 160 mg/kg bw per day for BZ‐54. In the absence of access to the data to support this conclusion, the CONTAM Panel was not able to use this study for hazard characterisation.

−Study 2 (MPI, 2008a, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): In a 2008 developmental toxicity study, groups of 25 female rats were administered oral (gavage) doses of 0, 50, 100 or 300 mg/kg per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 in peanut oil from gestation days 6 through 19. Maternal toxicity, evidenced by decreased food consumption, body weight gains and sparse abdominal hair, was noted in the mid and high‐dose groups. Fetal body weights were reduced in these dose groups, and skeletal malformations (fused cervical neural arches) and variations were observed in a small number of fetuses at the highest dose. The authors conducting the study identified a NOAEL of 50 mg/kg bw per day for Firemaster BZ‐54. In the absence of access to the data to support this conclusion, the CONTAM Panel was not able to use this study for hazard characterisation.

Study 2 (MPI, 2008a, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): In a 2008 developmental toxicity study, groups of 25 female rats were administered oral (gavage) doses of 0, 50, 100 or 300 mg/kg per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 in peanut oil from gestation days 6 through 19. Maternal toxicity, evidenced by decreased food consumption, body weight gains and sparse abdominal hair, was noted in the mid and high‐dose groups. Fetal body weights were reduced in these dose groups, and skeletal malformations (fused cervical neural arches) and variations were observed in a small number of fetuses at the highest dose. The authors conducting the study identified a NOAEL of 50 mg/kg bw per day for Firemaster BZ‐54. In the absence of access to the data to support this conclusion, the CONTAM Panel was not able to use this study for hazard characterisation.

−Study 3 (MPI, 2008b, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): A two‐generation reproductive toxicity study was conducted with Firemaster BZ‐54. Groups of 19–23 female rats were administered oral (gavage) doses of 15, 50 or 165 mg/kg bw per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 in peanut oil for at least 10 weeks prior to mating, continuing through mating, gestation and up to lactation day 21. Parental and F1 females showed lower body weights and body weight gains, with F1 males being slightly affected. Pups from both generations exhibited reduced body and spleen weights. No other treatment‐related effects were observed. Based on the observed effects, the authors conducting the study identified a NOAEL of 50 mg/kg bw per day for parental and neonatal toxicity, while the NOAEL for reproductive performance and fertility was identified to be 165 mg/kg bw per day.

Study 3 (MPI, 2008b, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017): A two‐generation reproductive toxicity study was conducted with Firemaster BZ‐54. Groups of 19–23 female rats were administered oral (gavage) doses of 15, 50 or 165 mg/kg bw per day of Firemaster BZ‐54 in peanut oil for at least 10 weeks prior to mating, continuing through mating, gestation and up to lactation day 21. Parental and F1 females showed lower body weights and body weight gains, with F1 males being slightly affected. Pups from both generations exhibited reduced body and spleen weights. No other treatment‐related effects were observed. Based on the observed effects, the authors conducting the study identified a NOAEL of 50 mg/kg bw per day for parental and neonatal toxicity, while the NOAEL for reproductive performance and fertility was identified to be 165 mg/kg bw per day.

New data were also available for a technical product containing BEH‐TEBP, i.e. Firemaster 550 (Baldwin et al., 2017; Gillera et al., 2020; Krentzel et al., 2021; Marinello et al., 2023; Patisaul et al., 2013; Phillips et al., 2016; Rock et al., 2018; Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023). The CONTAM Panel concluded that data on this technical product added little to the body of evidence for the effects of BEH‐TEBP (and EH‐TBB) and were not suitable for identifying a Reference Point for use in risk characterisation. This is because the studies provide limited information on composition and impurities, and Firemaster 550 included organophosphate flame retardants.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, genotoxicity studies were available in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s), and the registrant concluded that BEH‐TEBP is not genotoxic. ECHA did not make any conclusions on mutagenicity of BEH‐TEBP as it does not have a harmonised classification for mutagenicity under the Classification, Labelling and Packaging (CLP) Regulation (EC) No 1272/2008. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) was reported to be negative in a reverse mutation assay (both plate incorporation and pre‐incubation methods) in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and E. coli WP2uvrA in the presence and absence of S9 mix from Aroclor‐induced rat liver. It was also reported that BEH‐TEBP (RC9927) to be negative in pour‐plate assays in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA1538. BEH‐TEBP (purity 90.6%) was also negative in a V79/hprt gene mutation assay with and without metabolic activation (tested up to 5000 μg/mL; 4 and 24 h treatment).

BEH‐TEBP (purity NR) was reported to be negative in a reverse mutation assay (both plate incorporation and pre‐incubation methods) in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and E. coli WP2uvrA in the presence and absence of S9 mix from Aroclor‐induced rat liver. It was also reported that BEH‐TEBP (RC9927) to be negative in pour‐plate assays in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA1538. BEH‐TEBP (purity 90.6%) was also negative in a V79/hprt gene mutation assay with and without metabolic activation (tested up to 5000 μg/mL; 4 and 24 h treatment).

In an in vitro chromosome aberration test in human lymphocytes with and without metabolic activation, BEH‐TEBP (RC9927) showed a weak clastogenic activity only at the highest tested concentration (1000 μg/mL) in the absence of any cytotoxicity.

In an in vitro chromosome aberration test in human lymphocytes with and without metabolic activation, BEH‐TEBP (RC9927) showed a weak clastogenic activity only at the highest tested concentration (1000 μg/mL) in the absence of any cytotoxicity.

Two in vivo micronucleus tests in bone marrow of CD‐1 mice after single ip or sub‐acute (5 days) dermal administration of BEH‐TEBP (purity > 95%) at doses up to 2000 mg/kg bw were reported to be negative (Substance Evaluation Conclusion document, KEMI, 2020).

Two in vivo micronucleus tests in bone marrow of CD‐1 mice after single ip or sub‐acute (5 days) dermal administration of BEH‐TEBP (purity > 95%) at doses up to 2000 mg/kg bw were reported to be negative (Substance Evaluation Conclusion document, KEMI, 2020).

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

Toxicity studies on BEH‐TEBP have indicated effects on the liver (hepatocyte oedema, narrowing of the sinusoids and dilation of the central vein) in mice following oral administration for 28 days at 200 mg/kg bw per day (only dose tested). No significant effects on learning or memory retention were reported in mice exposed to BEH‐TEBP for 28 days (0, 20 or 200 mg/kg bw per day), whereas learning and memory performance was impaired in mice exposed daily to 20 mg/kg bw per day for 60 days. In rats, BEH‐TEBP exposure for 28 days by oral gavage resulted in significant increases in serum TT4 and FT4 levels at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, with a corresponding decrease in TSH and thyroid weight, which were significant at the highest dose, but no effect on TT3 or FT3. No effects were reported in a 90‐day study in rats at doses up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day, or in a 5‐day study at BEH‐TEBP doses up to 706 mg/kg bw per day, including on the thyroid.

According to information provided in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s), BEH‐TEBP was not mutagenic in bacteria or in mammalian cells in vitro, but was a weak clastogen in human lymphocytes in vitro. In vivo, it was negative in micronucleus tests in mice after ip or dermal exposure.

In rats, exposure of a binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB (BEH‐TEBP: 1.05 mg/kg bw per day; EH‐TBB: 2.25 mg/kg bw per day) via dams during gestation and lactation resulted in changes in socioemotional behaviour with deficits greater in female offspring than in males (Witchey et al., 2020). Effects of exposure of pregnant rats to this binary mixture resulted in reduced bone mineral density and average marrow area of the femur in female offspring, with no effects observed in males (Schkoda et al., 2023).

Three unpublished studies on Firemaster BZ‐54 containing 70% EH‐TBB and 30% BEH‐TEBP were summarised in the publication of (Hays & Kirman, 2017), only one of which (MPI, 2008b) was described with sufficient detail to allow evaluation by the CONTAM Panel. In this two‐generation reproduction toxicity study, decreased bodyweights and spleen weights were reported, particularly in the F1 pups, with a NOAEL of 50 mg/kg bw per day of the technical product, corresponding to 15 mg BEH‐TEBP and 35 mg EH‐TBB /kg bw per day.

From the available studies, the lowest LOAEL for the binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB is 3.3 mg/kg bw per day (equivalent to 2.25 mg EH‐TBB and 1.05 mg BEH‐TEBP/kg bw per day), identified based on changes in socioemotional behaviour deficits and skeletal effects in female offspring of rat dams exposed to the mixture during gestation and lactation.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on BEH‐TEBP (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for BEH‐TEBP with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

In the previous Opinion, no human studies on BEH‐TEBP were reported. Since then, eight publications representing six epidemiological studies were identified. In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion is shown, including those on BEH‐TEBP.

The studies on BEH‐TEBP pertained to populations in China (four studies) or the USA (two studies). Various endpoints were assessed including thyroid hormone profile (three studies), thyroid cancer (one study), type 2 diabetes (one study), BMI (one study) and respiratory disease (including lung function assessment) (one study). One study assessed DNA methylation. Exposure was assessed in ambient air and wristbands (one study), in dust (one study) or in serum (four studies).

Guo et al. (2018) in a cross‐sectional study collected blood samples from 174 school students (age 10 years) living near a petrochemical complex in South China. PBDE congeners and eight other flame retardants were measured, of which six are assessed in the current Opinion (see Appendix F). Associations with thyroid hormone levels were evaluated for the single chemicals and for sums of the PBDEs and NFRs. BEH‐TEBP was associated with lower FT4 levels (β −0.21, 95% CI –0.42, −0.009), but no associations were found for T3, T4, FT3 and TSH.

Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) assessed the association between 10 BFRs (of which six are assessed in the current Opinion, see Appendix F) and thyroid hormone profile (FT3, FT4 and TSH) in rural Central Appalachia (USA) (n = 101). Exposure was assessed in ambient air and in wristbands. In wristbands, BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB were the most abundant BFRs, with median concentrations of 31 and 87 ng/g wristband, respectively. The combined contribution of these two chemicals to the sum of 10 BFRs was 94%. Among the multiple analyses performed, no statistically significant associations with the thyroid hormone profiles were reported for BEH‐TEBP.

Liu, Li, et al. (2022) in a case–control study with a cross‐sectional exposure assessment collected 481 serum samples from patients with thyroid cancer (n = 242) and healthy controls (n = 239) in Shandong Province, eastern China. The levels of 9 BFRs (of which seven are assessed in the current Opinion, see Appendix F) and 9 organophosphate esters, thyroid hormones and serum lipid parameters, were measured in all the participants at the time of diagnosis and the authors assessed the association between the levels of BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk. BEH‐TEBP was detected in less than 20% of samples and was not further analysed.

Mendy et al. (2023, 2024) assessed the association between the levels of BFRs in dust and respiratory disease (wheezing, respiratory infections, hay fever/allergies, lung function) in children at age 5 in the USA using a birth cohort (HOME study). Exposure was assessed prenatally (Mendy et al., 2024; n = 342) or at age 1 (Mendy et al., 2023; n = 234) via house dust concentrations (μg/g dust) and house dust loadings (μg/m2). As regards the prenatal exposure adjusted analyses, both prenatal BEH‐TEBP log10 dust concentration (RR, 1.57; 95% CI, 1.28, 1.94) and prenatal BEH‐TEBP log10 dust loading (RR, 1.28; 95% CI, 1.08, 1.52) were statistically significantly associated with wheeze. In the adjusted analyses, BEH‐TEBP log10 loading at age 1 was statistically marginally significantly associated with hay fever/allergies (RR, 1.12; 95% CI, 1.00, 1.24). BEH‐TEBP log10 loading at age 1 was also statistically significantly associated with peak expiratory flow (mL/min, −9.05; 95% CI, −17.67, −0.43) but not with forced expiratory volume. The same study was also used to evaluate associations between prenatal levels of BFRs and cord blood DNA methylation in an epigenome‐wide association study (Mendy et al., 2025; n = 252). This study found house dust concentrations of BEH‐TEBP to be associated with higher ATXN10 (cg07527826) methylation and with lower FAM159B (cg12662072) methylation. In the discussion of these results, the authors stated that the FAM159B gene is involved in cell signalling and regulation and was associated with psoriasis, body size and body impedance. They also stated that ATXN10 is a gene involved in neuron function and bone mineral density and height.

Zheng, Chen, et al. (2025) assessed the association between mixed serum exposure to dechlorane plus and novel brominated flame retardants and type 2 diabetes risk in a cross‐sectional case–control study in China, including 60 type 2 diabetes patients and 57 healthy controls. Serum levels of seven BFRs were measured (of which five are assessed in the current Opinion, see Appendix F). After adjusting for age, sex and high‐density lipoprotein, all mixture models consistently showed a significant positive association between mixed dechlorane plus/BFRs exposure and diabetes risk. Concentrations of BEH‐TEBP were not statistically significantly different between cases and controls in unadjusted comparisons (adjusted associations for single exposures were not reported) and BEH‐TEBP was not a main contributor to the mixture effects.

Zheng, Sun, et al. (2025) studied the association between BFRs and BMI in a cross‐sectional study set in Inner Mongolia, China (n = 77). Serum levels of seven BFRs were measured (of which five are assessed in the current Opinion, see Appendix F). Unadjusted correlations and linear regressions did not show an association between BEH‐TEBP and BMI. Adjusted models are not presented.

In summary, the available epidemiological evidence on BEH‐TEBP pertained to populations in China or the USA and it was very limited in terms of the total number of available studies (n = 7) and the number of available studies per endpoint (n = 1–2). Moreover, the exposure assessed in three out the seven studies was done in environmental samples without additional dietary/food data and studies were mostly cross‐sectional in design (apart from Mendy et al., 2023, 2024, 2025). No statistically significant associations were observed for thyroid function, apart from a lower FT4 in one cross‐sectional study. A statistically significant signal was observed for the association between BEH‐TEBP levels measured in dust and wheeze, hay fever/allergies and peak expiratory flow. One study reported alterations in DNA methylation.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

In the previous Opinion, the only identified study with BEH‐TEBP was performed at concentrations up to 30 μM in chicken embryonic hepatocytes in vitro (Egloff et al., 2011). No effects on RNA expression levels or cell viability were observed. The previous Opinion also reported a study in which groups of fathead minnows (Pimephales promelas) were exposed via the diet to either of the technical products Firemaster 550 (BEH‐TEBP: 745 μg/g feed, EH‐TBB: 1658 μg/g feed) or Firemaster BZ‐54 (BEH‐TEBP: 907 μg/g feed, EH‐TBB: 2087 μg/g feed). The fish were fed 6% of their body weight per day resulting in daily intake of 45 mg/kg bw for BEH‐TEBP and 99 mg/kg bw for EH‐TBB in the FM 550 feed, and 54 mg/kg bw for BEH‐TEBP and 125 mg/kg bw for EH‐TBB in the BZ‐54 feed. The fish were dosed for 56 days followed by a 22‐day recovery period (Bearr et al., 2010). There were no effects on fish weight or length. Blood and liver samples were investigated for DNA strand breaks using the comet assay. A significant increase in percent tail DNA was reported in liver cells but not in blood cells (Bearr et al., 2010).

Since then, several studies have been identified (see Appendix G, Table G.1), and a brief summary is provided below.

Dose‐related increased serum TT4 and FT4 levels in rats following exposure to 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days (Yuan et al., 2024a) suggest interference with thyroid hormone regulation. The consensus of several in vitro studies examining effects on the thyroid hormone system is that BEH‐TEBP has low anti‐thyroid hormone and thyroid agonistic activities. However, BEH‐TEBP at a concentration of 3.75 μM inhibited DIO 3,3′‐T2 and T3 formation (Smythe et al., 2017). BEH‐TBPH treatment of human thyroid follicular epithelial Nthy ori3–1 cells resulted in increased mRNA and protein levels of TG, TPO and IL‐10 (at 100 nM) (Yuan et al., 2024a). Knock down of IL‐10 diminished the effects of BEH‐TBPH on these thyroid proteins, suggesting that IL‐10 is a target through which BEH‐TBPH influences thyroid function.

BEH‐TEBP and its carboxylic acid metabolites exhibited anti‐glucocorticoid activity by direct competition with the glucocorticoid receptor in mammalian cell models (Klopčič et al., 2016). No glucocorticoid agonistic activities were observed. BEH‐TEBP Activated PXR with EC50 values of 2.0 μM and showed PXR antagonist activity (IC50 = 13.9 μM). BEH‐TEBP also upregulated CYP3A4 mRNA expression which is PXR dependent. Zebrafish exposed to BEH‐TEBP via the water (0.2–200 nM) for up to 2 months showed dose‐dependent increase of visceral fat accumulation, which was significant at 2 nM exposure (Fu, Zhu, et al., 2024). Increased blood glucose, triglycerides and liver fat accumulation were observed and signs of NAFLD in males in which the liver transcriptome indicated upregulation of glycolysis and insulin‐signalling pathways. The CONTAM Panel noted that PPARγ dysregulation is a plausible mechanism for the metabolic effects of BEH‐TEBP. The phenotypes observed (visceral fat, hyperglycaemia, triglyceride elevation and NAFLD‐like liver) mirror classic PPARγ overactivation, with compensatory glycolysis and insulin‐signalling gene upregulation reinforcing metabolic stress (Gavrilova et al., 2003; Panasyuk et al., 2012).

Several neurotoxicity studies have been identified showing the potency of BEH‐TEBP to impair some mechanistical endpoints. In mice, BEH‐TEBP (20 or 200 mg/kg per day, 28 days, p.o.) impaired the hippocampal expression of proteins related to synaptic plasticity (BDNF, p‐CREB, PSD‐95) and cell signalling (GRP78, PERK and C/EBP homologous protein CHOP), and altered levels of oxidative stress markers (MDA, SOD and GSH) (Bao & Jing, 2021). The daily exposure of mice to BEH‐TEBP (20 mg/kg per day, 60 days, route of administration unclear) activated the extracellular signal‐regulated kinase 1/2 (ERK1/2) signalling pathway in hippocampus associated with oxidative stress responses and apoptosis (Xiong et al., 2024). Alternative in vivo models were also used to assess the neurotoxicity of BEH‐TEBP. In planarians (flatworms), BEH‐TEBP (0.01–100 μM) was tested in adult and regenerating animals and showed no effects in this model (Zhang et al., 2019). A study performed in wild‐type zebrafish larvae (Liu, Song, et al., 2022) showed behavioural alterations limited to a reduction of phototactic response and concomitant decreases in several opsin genes expression. The same study reported impairments in blood–brain barrier permeability in vivo using a transgenic zebrafish model exposed to BEH‐TEBP (0–20 μM per day, 5 days post‐fertilisation), and in vitro in hCMEC/D3 cells. BEH‐TEBP or its metabolite mono‐(2‐ethyhexyl) tetrabromophthalate were tested for their toxicity in human umbilical vein endothelial cells (Chen et al., 2020; Xiang et al., 2017). Results related to the cell cycle, apoptosis, cell viability and specific gene expression (eNOS, VEGF1, PECAM1) showed the ability of the metabolite to cause higher toxicity and to affect gene expression to a greater extent than the parent compound BEH‐TEBP. In SH‐SY5Y neuroblastoma cells, no effects on cell viability, mitochondrial function, neural plasticity and development, promotion of oxidative stress and inflammation of BEH‐TEBP have been reported after 2–24 h of exposure at doses ranging 2.5–20 μM, suggesting absence of toxicity in this neural cell line at the low concentrations tested (Esplugas et al., 2023).

Experiments in fish indicate an effect of BEH‐TEBP on reproduction. Feeding medaka a diet containing Firemaster 550 (containing BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) resulted in downregulation of the vast majority of genes along the hypothalamus‐pituitary‐gonadal‐liver axis. There was an associated reduction in cumulative egg production. Exposure of zebrafish with BEH‐TEBP (4.00, 15.95 and 63.80 μg/g of food) for 21 days induced a dose‐related reduction of fertility in F0 females (Ma et al., 2018). Mating behaviour was reduced in male zebrafish exposed to low nanomolar concentrations of BEH‐TEBP in the water (Fu, Zhu, et al., 2024). Experiments on zebrafish sperm showed reduced cell viability, total mobility and swimming speed measurements at 0.01 μM BEH‐TEBP and higher concentrations. In silico docking of BEH‐TEBP to the human androgen receptor (AR) indicated strong binding (−119 kcal/mol) similar to that of the known AR agonist, flutamide (−122 kcal/mol). Whilst BEH‐TEBP showed no induction of AR‐mediated reporter gene expression in the hormone‐induced two‐hybrid androgen receptor yeast AR‐GRIP1 assay, it inhibited dehydroxytestosterone‐induced expression by 9%, 7%, 11% and 22% in the 0.125, 0.25, 0.5 and 1 μM exposure groups, respectively. BEH‐TEBP reduced mouse TM3 Leydig cell testosterone production at an exposure concentration of 200 μM with no effects at lower (0.2–20 μM) concentrations. Furthermore, BEH‐TEBP inhibited the proliferation of mouse Leydig cells by 1%, 3%, 7% and 22% at concentrations of 0.2, 2, 20 and 200 μM, respectively. These results suggest that BEH‐TEBP interferes with reproduction in zebrafish. Possible relevance to mammalian reproduction is indicated by in vitro experiments in mouse Leydig cells, which showed effects on androgen receptor (AR) signalling, transactivation experiments with the human AR in yeast and by in silico docking experiments with the human AR (Fu, Hua, et al., 2024, Appendix G).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The toxicological properties of BEH‐TEBP are not well‐characterised. No long‐term studies were identified.

In the absence of access to the original data reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel could not make an independent evaluation of the possible genotoxicity of BEH‐TEBP, but noted that this compound does not have a harmonised classification for mutagenicity under the CLP Regulation (EC) No 1272/2008 based on the available data. The CONTAM Panel concluded that BEH‐TEBP does not induce gene mutations in bacteria and mammalian cells. There was evidence of clastogenicity in vitro at a concentration of 1000 μg/mL, although this was not confirmed in non‐oral in vivo in the erythrocyte micronucleus tests. However, it was not possible to verify whether the compound reached the bone marrow in the in vivo tests. Following the EFSA Opinion on genotoxicity (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2011), the Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP.

Studies conducted with BEH‐TEBP alone indicated possible effects on the liver in mice. Neurotoxicity studies pointed to induction of oxidative stress in the hippocampus, but produced contradictory neurobehavioural results possibly related to methodological differences (e.g. study design, route of administration, doses used and behavioural endpoints assessed) (Bao & Jing, 2021; Fulcher, 2018; Xiong et al., 2024).

Yuan et al. (2024a) reported dose‐related increases in serum TT4 and FT4 at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, together with decreased TSH and decreased relative thyroid weight, and changes in thyroid histopathology in rats exposed to BEH‐TEBP for 28 days. However, the CONTAM Panel noted that no changes in histopathology were seen in a 90‐day OECD guideline study at doses up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Fulcher, 2018).

Administration of a binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, at a combined dose level of approximately 3.3 mg/kg bw per day, to rats during pregnancy and lactation resulted in effects on socioemotional behaviour and bone. In view of the differences between the design and endpoints assessed in the studies with BEH‐TEBP alone and those with binary mixtures of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, it is not possible to attribute the effects to one or both of the components.

Three unpublished studies on a technical product containing 30% BEH‐TEBP and 70% EH‐TBB were summarised in a publication by Hays and Kirman (2017). According to Hays and Kirman (2017), two of these studies reported developmental and/or renal effects, but the data were not available to the CONTAM Panel and therefore could not be assessed. In the third study (MPI, 2008b), reduced body and spleen weight were reported in a two‐generational reproductive study, and the data were presented in the publication of Hays and Kirman (2017).

The available epidemiological studies did not provide sufficient evidence to characterise the hazards.

The only data suitable for dose–response modelling were the continuous data sets on spleen and body weights from the MPI (2008b), as cited by Hays & Kirman, 2017). The CONTAM Panel applied benchmark dose (BMD) modelling on these data, following the EFSA guidance on BMD modelling (EFSA SC, 2022), with a Benchmark Response (BMR) of 10% for body weight and a BMR of 15% for spleen weight based on the consideration in the JMPR guidance document that increases of less than 10% or 15% of body weight or spleen weight might be non‐adverse (JMPR, 2015). The outcome is shown in Table 11). The lowest calculated BMDLs calculated on the combined dose of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB were for the female F1 generation, at 31 mg/kg bw per day for bodyweight, corresponding to 10 mg/kg bw per day BEH‐TEBP.
