The CONTAM Panel noted the lack of consistency in the findings of different studies, possibly related to different study designs and endpoints examined.

The findings reported at the lowest dose levels were on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in the study of a binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB in rats (Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023), which provided a BEH‐TEBP dose of 1.05 mg/kg bw per day. The CONTAM Panel noted that the greater solubility and oral absorption of EH‐TBB compared to BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4.2.1 and 3.2.5.2.1) indicates that the effects are more likely to be attributable to EH‐TBB. However, in the absence of information on relative potency, the CONTAM Panel does not know what the relative contributions of the two substances are, and therefore to be conservative made a worst‐case assumption for each substance. Accordingly, in order to identify a Reference Point specifically for BEH‐TEBP, the Panel attributed all of the findings to BEH‐TEBP and adopted the LOAEL value of 1 mg/kg bw per day (rounded) as the Reference Point, noting that this approach is highly conservative.

The Panel concluded that, due to the limitations and uncertainties in the current database, the establishment of a health‐based guidance value for BEH‐TEBP was not appropriate. Instead, the MOE approach was used for risk characterisation (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2025).

Usually, an MOE of 100, covering variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans (factor 4 × 2.5 = 10) and within the human population (factor 3.2 × 3.2 = 10), is considered sufficient to conclude that exposure does not raise a health concern.

To allow for the absence of a NOAEL and the necessity of using a LOAEL, the EFSA Guidance on selected default values indicates that an additional uncertainty factor will be needed, the size of which should be determined on a case‐by‐case basis (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). ECHA, in its Guidance on information requirements and chemical safety assessment (ECHA, 2012) proposed the application of ‘an assessment factor ranging from 3 (as minimum/majority of cases) to 10 (as maximum/exceptional cases)’ with the following considerations: ‘An AF of 3 may be more appropriate for instance in situations, where the effects at the LOAEL are mild, or the LOAEL represents the lower boundary of the exposure range in which the effect is observed. Higher numerical values should be considered in situations where the effects at the LOAEL are severe and irreversible, or the shape of the dose–response curve is shallow or the quality of the study (e.g. group sizes, statistical methods, study design, exposure data) gives rise to uncertainties about the reliability of the identified LOAEL’ (ECHA, 2012). In this instance, since the effects at the LOAEL for BEH‐TEBP are mild, the CONTAM Panel considered that a factor of 3 is sufficient to extrapolate from a LOAEL to NOAEL.

According to the EFSA Scientific Committee Guidance on selected default values, an additional factor can be considered in case of deficiencies in the database on a case‐by‐case basis. A default value has not been proposed, as it will be directly dependent on the data set available (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). The WHO/IPCS (1994, 1999) has recommended a factor of 3 or 5 if there are minor deficiencies in the database and a factor of 10 if there are major deficiencies in the database. There are limitations in the database for BEH‐TEBP, such as inconsistencies between studies of non‐standard design, most studies being on mixtures rather than on the pure substance, and some studies only being available in summary form, so that they could not be fully assessed by the Panel. Taking into account also the sensitivity of the endpoints examined in the critical study, and the worst‐case approach of attributing the effects solely to BEH‐TEBP, the CONTAM Panel considered an additional factor of 5 to be appropriate for deficiencies in the database.

Overall, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 1500 (100 for inter and intra‐species variability, 3 for LOAEL to NOAEL extrapolation and 5 for the gaps in the toxicological database) do not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

The CONTAM Panel could not set a Reference Point for neoplastic effects, since no carcinogenicity data are available. In addition, it was not possible to conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Limited occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and no estimation of the dietary exposure based on these data was possible.

In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of BEH‐TEBP and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2 and Appendix B.

Two studies were identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP and these are summarised below:

Exposure estimates for BEH‐TEBP in the UK population were calculated based on daily consumption data for UK citizens from the National Diet and Nutrition Survey (2008–2019) and occurrence data from samples collected in 2020–2021 (Ma et al., 2023). Average dietary exposures to BEH‐TEBP ranged from 0.91 to 3.3 ng/kg bw per day across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old), while high‐end exposure estimates, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate + 2 standard deviations, ranged from 5.3 to 20 ng/kg bw per day (Ma et al., 2023).

Exposure estimates for BEH‐TEBP in the UK population were calculated based on daily consumption data for UK citizens from the National Diet and Nutrition Survey (2008–2019) and occurrence data from samples collected in 2020–2021 (Ma et al., 2023). Average dietary exposures to BEH‐TEBP ranged from 0.91 to 3.3 ng/kg bw per day across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old), while high‐end exposure estimates, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate + 2 standard deviations, ranged from 5.3 to 20 ng/kg bw per day (Ma et al., 2023).

Exposure estimates were reported in the Norwegian adult population for BEH‐TEBP based on 24‐h duplicate diet method from samples collected in 2013–2014. Dietary intake of BEH‐TEBP was 1.2 ng/kg bw per day, in the 95th percentile. Intake of red meat, poultry products and fruits was weakly correlated to dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP. The authors suggested that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP from fruit intake might be related to the usage of this compound as a plasticiser in wrapping film and sheeting used as food packaging (Xu et al., 2017).

Exposure estimates were reported in the Norwegian adult population for BEH‐TEBP based on 24‐h duplicate diet method from samples collected in 2013–2014. Dietary intake of BEH‐TEBP was 1.2 ng/kg bw per day, in the 95th percentile. Intake of red meat, poultry products and fruits was weakly correlated to dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP. The authors suggested that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP from fruit intake might be related to the usage of this compound as a plasticiser in wrapping film and sheeting used as food packaging (Xu et al., 2017).

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature (Čechová et al., 2017; Tao et al., 2017, see Section 3.2.4.2.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 1.34 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 6.15 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported a lower mean concentration in samples from the UK of 0.25 ng/g lipid that would result in an average exposure of 1.15 ng/kg bw per day.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 2.75 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 18.9 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported only maximum concentrations that were not used for these estimations.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from four European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Non‐dietary exposure

Several studies were identified on the non‐dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP. Whilst exposure estimates were not made, child car seats were identified as a source of exposure to BEH‐TEBP with concentrations up to 39,000 ng/g (Harrad et al., 2023).

A range of BFRs including BEH‐TEBP were measured in a variety of black plastic kitchen utensils by Kuang et al. (2018). Concentrations up to 30,000 ng/g were found and it was estimated that exposure via cooking in median and high exposure scenarios could be as high as 1651 ng per day.

Estimated daily dermal exposure to BEH‐TEBP for adults from hand wipe and settled dust data (direct and indirect methods used to assess dermal exposure) were 61 pg/kg bw per day and 23 pg/kg bw per day at the 95th percentile high exposure scenario (Tay et al., 2018).

Silicone (polydimethylsiloxane or PDMS) wristbands and cotton T‐shirts were used to assess the exposure of e‐waste recyclers in Dhaka, Bangladesh to a variety of BFRs by Wang, Peris, et al. (2020). The median daily exposure doses via dermal transfer from the front of the T‐shirt to the front body trunk was in the range 0.005–0.12 ng/kg bw per day with a median value of 0.02 ng/kg bw per day for BEH‐TEBP.

Some estimates of exposure to BEH‐TEBP from dust have been made and these are collated in Appendix D. They are typically < 1 ng/kg bw per day. These non‐dietary sources of exposure to BEH‐TEBP are several orders of magnitude smaller than estimates of dietary exposure (Section 3.2.4.4 above). Exposure to BEH‐TEBP from dust is also similarly lower than exposure from the diet (Appendix D).

In a study by Sjöström et al. (2024), to study children's exposure to halogenated flame retardants and organophosphate esters through dermal absorption and hand‐to‐mouth ingestion in Swedish preschools, it was found that BEH‐TEBP was one of the highest contributors to exposure in terms of mass. Exposure was estimated using handwipes and 2.8 ng BEH‐TEBP per handwipe was found.

In summary, non‐dietary sources of exposure to BEH‐TEBP were lower than estimates made for dietary exposure reported in the literature (ranging from 0.91 to 3.3 ng/kg bw per day).

Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data in food on BEH‐TEBP were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the EU population to be made. Limited data obtained from the scientific literature indicated that dietary exposure from food in two European countries (UK and Norway, see Section 3.2.4.3) was up to 20 ng/kg bw per day at P95 exposure. The Panel noted this would result in MOEs in the region of 106, which does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects. The indicative exposure estimates obtained for breastfed infants (see Section 3.2.4.3) would result in similar MOEs.

Regarding potential neoplastic effects, the EFSA guidance on the threshold of toxicological concern (TTC) specifies that, for substances raising a concern about potential DNA‐reactive mutagenicity and/or carcinogenicity, if exposure is below 2.5 ng/kg bw per day, the substance is considered of low probability of adverse health effects (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2019). Although the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP, the Panel noted that the dietary exposure from two studies from Europe obtained from the literature are above the TTC for genotoxic compounds, and therefore further genotoxicity information is needed.

Uncertainty analysis

The uncertainty analysis was performed as described in Section 2.8. Uncertainties affecting the hazard assessment for BEH‐TEBP are listed in Table 12.

Inconsistencies between studies of non‐standard design, most studies being on mixtures rather than on the pure substance, and some studies only being available in summary form, so that they could not be fully assessed by the Panel. These deficiencies in the database were addressed by including an additional uncertainty factor of 5 in the MOE that would not raise a health concern (see below). Description of the uncertainty Impact on the risk characterisation a Margin of exposure (MOE) – 1 – Low impact. The usual factor of 100 (to cover for variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans, and within the human population) is considered sufficient. The additional factors of 3 (to account for the absence of a NOAEL) and 5 (for deficiencies in the database, as mentioned above), are considered sufficient. Risk characterisation (non‐neoplastic effects) – 1 – Low impact. Not possible for EFSA to perform an exposure assessment since insufficient occurrence data were submitted to EFSA. Based on dietary exposure estimates reported in the literature for two European countries (UK and Norway), the resulting MOEs did not raise a health concern. It is uncertain whether this is representative of the situation in other European countries. The uncertainty affecting the exposure estimates from the literature is of large magnitude, but its impact on the risk characterisation is low due to the very large size of the calculated MOE.Abbreviations: ADME, absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion; BMD, benchmark dose; BMDL, benchmark dose lower credible limit; BMR, benchmark response; MOA, mode of action; TK, toxicokinetic. a 0 – Uncertainty with negligible impact; 1 – Uncertainty with low impact; 2 – Uncertainty with medium impact; 3 – Uncertainty with high impact. This ordinal scale provides a relative assessment of the impact of the individual uncertainties as an aid to quantifying their combined impact by expert judgement, as described in section 11 of EFSA Scientific Committee (2018).

Uncertainties identified and their impact on the outcome of the hazard identification/characterisation and risk characterisation of BEH‐TEBP.

Abbreviations: ADME, absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion; BMD, benchmark dose; BMDL, benchmark dose lower credible limit; BMR, benchmark response; MOA, mode of action; TK, toxicokinetic.

0 – Uncertainty with negligible impact; 1 – Uncertainty with low impact; 2 – Uncertainty with medium impact; 3 – Uncertainty with high impact. This ordinal scale provides a relative assessment of the impact of the individual uncertainties as an aid to quantifying their combined impact by expert judgement, as described in section 11 of EFSA Scientific Committee (2018).

Uncertainty analysis: Genotoxicity

The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data and could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4.2), so no further analysis of the uncertainties was performed.

Uncertainty analysis: Hazard characterisation

The CONTAM Panel identified substantial uncertainties affecting the hazard assessment (Table 12) that did not allow the establishment of a HBGV. Instead, the MOE approach was used, addressing the hazard uncertainties by applying UFs of 100 (to cover for variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans, and within the human population), 3 (to account for the absence of a NOAEL) and 5 (for deficiencies in the database).

Considering the combined impact of the uncertainties and the extent to which they are addressed by the applied UFs, the CONTAM Panel judged with 90%–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that, when using the Reference Point of 1 mg/kg bw per day, MOEs ≥ 1500 would not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

Uncertainty analysis: Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data in food on BEH‐TEBP were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the European population to be made. Based on the dietary exposure data from food from the scientific literature from two European countries (UK and Norway, see Section 3.2.4.3), the CONTAM Panel noted this would result in MOEs in the region of 106.

Considering these very large MOEs, and the uncertainties affecting the exposure estimates reported from two European countries, the CONTAM Panel judged with 95–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that current exposure of the European population to BEH‐TEBP does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

2‐Ethylhexyl 2,3,4,5‐tetrabromobenzoate (EH‐TBB) [CAS number 183658‐27‐7]

Production, use, analysis and environmental fate

Production and use

The previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported that EH‐TBB was found in several additive flame‐retardant products used as replacements for PBDE mixtures. It is one of the two major components (35%) of Firemaster 550, which is used as a replacement for PentaBDE in polyurethane foam applications (de Wit et al., 2011) and can also be found in Firemaster BZ‐54 (70%) (Ma et al., 2011). In both commercial products BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4) is also present (de Wit et al., 2011; Ma et al., 2011). Stapleton et al. (2008) reported that the EH‐TBB/BEH‐TEBP ratio observed in Firemaster 550 commercial mixture is generally 4:1 by mass, and studied the combined occurrence of both compounds in foam samples taken from different baby products, e.g. car seats, changing table pads sleep positioners, mattresses and pillows (Stapleton et al., 2011). It was also found that all samples containing EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP also contained triphenylpolyphosphate, which is another ingredient in Firemaster 550.

Analysis

It was reported in the previous Opinion that EH‐TBB can be analysed using the same methods as for PBDEs, BEH‐TEBP and DBDPE (Covaci et al., 2011; Papachlimitzou et al., 2012). It can be extracted using toluene, dichloromethane or mixtures of solvents (e.g. hexane:acetone or MTBE:acetone). Clean‐up is usually done by GPC, sometimes combined with silica column fractionation, silica gel or florisil where a more polar solvent is required for elution. Carbon columns can also be used to separate EH‐TBB. Instrumental determination is usually done by some type of GC–MS or LC–MS.

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

There is evidence that like many other BFRs, EH‐TBB is persistent and bioaccumulates in wildlife and at higher trophic levels (e.g. Lee et al., 2019; Lee & Kwon, 2016). In a study reported by de Jourdan et al. (2013) described earlier under BEH‐TEBH, on the environmental fate and persistence of three novel BFRs in aquatic mesocosms, EH‐TBB was the other main component of the commercial mixture Firemaster BZ‐54 (see Section 3.2.4).

Occurrence in the environment

Soil

No data were identified from the EFSA literature search for EH‐TBB in European soils. A review of soil contamination by PBDEs and other BFRs by McGrath et al. (2017) showed that EH‐TBB was generally not found, but had been reported in soil close to a waste disposal site in Australia at concentrations ranging from < LOD (0.02 ng/g dry weight) to 1.79 ng/g (mean 0.3 ng/g) (McGrath et al., 2017). It had also been reported in soils from a forested area of China by Zheng et al. (2015) in concentrations ranging from < LOD (0.0024 ng/g) to 1.6 ng/g with a mean of 0.184 ng/g, and in soil from around an e‐waste processing workshop in Viet Nam (Someya et al., 2016) at concentrations between < LOD and 0.32 ng/g. EH‐TBB was not found in soils at the other sites investigated.

EH‐TBB was measured in soil from Stockholm, Sweden, in a study by Newton et al. (2015) identified by a literature search conducted by ECHA (2024). EH‐TBB was below the LOD in all samples; the LOD was stated as 3.2 pg per sample and samples of 1.5 g were initially taken although some variation can occur due to the amount of moisture and organic content.

Sediment

As for soils, data on EH‐TBB could only be found in the search conducted by EFSA for sediment samples taken from outside of Europe. Chokwe et al. (2019) reported levels of 0.5–17 ng/g with a mean of 3.25 ng/g for sediment from the Vaal River in South Africa.

La Guardia et al. (2013) investigated inland and coastal sediments in the Thekwini Metropolitan Municipality, South Africa and found EH‐TBB at concentrations up to 13,900 ng/g with a mean of 545 ng/g.

Olukunle and Okonkwo (2015) measured BFRs in leachates and sediments from selected municipal solid waste landfill sites in Gauteng Province, South Africa. In sediment, concentrations of EH‐TBB ranged from < LOD to 56 ng/g with a mean of 10 ng/g; in leachate concentrations were 8.7 pg/L to 142 pg/L with a mean of 60 pg/L.

The ECHA literature search for the report on aromatic BFRs (ECHA, 2024) identified reports of EH‐TBB in sediment from the North Sea and River Elbe in Germany at concentrations up to 0.258 ng/g dry weight.

Dust

As mentioned earlier (Section 3.2.4.1.3), for many BFRs dust can be an important source of exposure, and this is especially the case for toddlers and infants due to low body weight, high crawling activity and high frequency of hand‐to‐mouth contact. A summary of reported occurrence of EH‐TBB in dust samples from European countries and estimates of exposure via dust when reported by the authors, is given in Appendix D (Table D.2). Concentrations reported are typically a few tens of ng/g but a value as high as 690 ng/g was reported by Larsson et al. (2018) for dust from pre‐school environments. Fromme et al. (2014) gave the highest exposure estimate for EH‐TBB from dust that could be identified which was 63 ng/kg bw per day for toddlers from Germany.

Given that reported estimates of exposure from diet are all less than 2 ng/kg bw per day (see Section 3.2.5.4 below), dust may be an important source of exposure for EH‐TBB at least for some individuals.

Water

No studies reporting values for concentrations of EH‐TBB in European water were identified by the EFSA literature search. Outside of Europe, Shanmuganathan et al. (2018) reported levels of up to around 500 ng/L in influent water at Canadian water plants.

Wang et al. (2017) reported on novel brominated flame retardants in seawater near a coastal mariculture area of the Bohai Sea, China. The concentration of EH‐TBB was found to be in the range of 0.81–6.69 ng/L in seawater. This was compared to a value of 1.3–7.9 pg/L in water from the Great Lakes (Venier et al., 2014).

The ECHA investigation report on aromatic BFRs identified three studies from Europe (ECHA, 2024). Carlsson et al. (2018) measured new brominated flame retardants including EH‐TBB in water from the Arctic but concentrations were below the LOD which was not stated (LOQ for water was stated to be 0.2 pg/L). Valls‐Cantenys et al. (2013) reported on the application of polydimethylsiloxane rod extraction to the determination of 16 halogenated flame retardants in water, including river water and Ria and Sea water from coastal areas. EH‐TBB was not detected in any sample using the method which had a LOD of 1.3 ng/L.

Wildlife

There are only limited data for EH‐TBB in wildlife worldwide. Berger et al. (2023) reported concentrations of 0.34–37 ng/g lipid in the blubber of harbour seal pups and 1.8–41 ng/kg lipid in liver of harbour seal pups from the NorthWest Atlantic region.

Lewis et al. (2020) measured BFRs including EH‐TBB in Adelie penguin colonies in East Antarctica. Most samples were < LOD but concentrations of up to 0.08 ng/g dry weight were found.

Gentes et al. (2012) found EH‐TBB in liver of ring‐billed gulls nesting in the St Lawrence River, downstream from Montreal, Canada at concentrations ranging from < LOD–1.55 ng/g ww. All samples of plasma were < LOD.

EH‐TBB was found in fewer than half of glaucous gulls from Cape Dorset in NU (Eastern Canadian Arctic) at concentrations up to 0.92 ng/g ww.

Vorkamp et al. (2018) measured halogenated flame retardants in peregrine falcon eggs from Greenland. EH‐TBB was found in 54% samples at concentrations between < 0.620 and 54.3 ng/g lipid with a mean concentration of 4.61 ng/g lipid.

The ECHA literature search for the report on aromatic BFRs also identified reports of EH‐TBB in whale, seal, bear, gull from northern Norway at concentrations up to 1050 pg/g lipid and in eelpout and blue mussels from the North Sea, Germany at concentrations of up to 14.3 pg/g (ECHA, 2024).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information relative to toxicokinetic in mammals had been described in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). Since then, several studies have been identified.

Roberts et al. (2012) investigated the metabolism of EH‐TBB in human and rat liver microsomes. The authors found that EH‐TBB was metabolised to 2,3,4,5‐tetrabromobenzoic acid (TBBA).

Hoffman et al. (2014) collected in adult volunteers (n = 64), urine, hand wipe samples and dust samples from their homes where FM550 was detected in the home environment. The authors indicated that urinary TBBA could be a useful biomarker of Firemaster 550 exposure.

Hoffman et al. (2024) analysed the urine from 180 children (mean age: 4.5 years, ranging from 38 to 73 months) for TBBA and the serum for 11 BFRs. The authors collected dust, handwipe, wristband and foam in the home environment and analysed them for the same BFRs. The authors found a significant association between concentrations of EH‐TBB in dust, handwipe and wristband with the detection of TBBA in urine.

Bearr et al. (2012) studied the in vitro metabolism of the flame retardant mixture, Firemaster BZ‐54 containing EH‐TBB (as well BEH‐TEBP), with hepatic subcellular fractions from mice and fathead minnow, common carp and snapping turtle. The authors found that BEH‐TEBP was metabolised to greater extent in mice compared to other species (fathead minnow and carp) (metabolites have not been characterised).

Phillips et al. (2016) studied the maternal transfer of Firemaster 550 components containing EH‐TBB (as well as BEH‐TEBP) in Wistar rat. Rats were orally exposed to approximately 1 or 3.3 mg/kg bw per day of Firemaster 550 for 10 consecutive days during gestation (GD9–18) or lactation (PND3–12). The authors concluded that EH‐TBB accumulates in tissue largely via lactational transfer than during gestational transfer.

Knudsen et al. (2016) investigated the toxicokinetic of EH‐TBB in female Sprague–Dawley rats and male B6C3F1 mice by administering a single dose and repeated exposure of [14C]‐EH‐TBB by gavage and iv route at 0.1, 1, 10 or 100 mmol/kg (0.05–55 mg/kg). In female rats following oral administration, the authors showed that EH‐TBB was well absorbed (85%) in comparison to iv‐injected rats. No accumulation of EH‐TBB or its metabolites was observed in tissues after single administration of up to 100 mmol/kg or repeated administration of 0.1 mmol/kg to rats. EH‐TBB was metabolised into TBBA, TBBA‐glycine and TBBA‐sulfate that were eliminated primarily in urine. In faeces, only EH‐TBB and TBBA were found, and TBBA‐sulfate was detected. More than 90% of administered [14C]‐radioactivity at all dose levels was eliminated in urine and faeces within 48 h, and less than 1% remained in the tissues after 72 h. At 0.1 mmol/kg, based on radioactivity recovery in excreta, the excretion in urine and faeces were 51% and 47% of the dose after 72 h, respectively. Similar excretion pathways were observed in male B6C3F1/Tac mice at 0.1 mmol/kg. From the data collected, the authors performed on a one‐compartment model, and calculated an absorption half‐life of 4 h, a distribution/elimination half‐life of 5 h with a clearance rate of 35 ± 10 mL/h.

Chen et al. (2020) administered to female Balb/c mice by gavage (8 μg/mL) and searched the metabolites after 4 and 8 h post exposure. Compared to BEH‐TEBP, the authors indicated that EH‐TBB was metabolised into TBBA.

In summary, following oral administration in rodents, EH‐TBB is well absorbed, readily metabolised and eliminated by both urinary and faecal routes.

Levels in humans

No information on levels of EH‐TBB in human samples was reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, several studies have been identified reporting concentrations of EH‐TBB as well as other BFRs covered in this assessment in samples from European countries. Details of these studies are reported in Appendix E (Table E.1), and a summary of the results related to EH‐TBB are reported below.

Regarding human milk samples,

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) described above, EH‐TBB was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 47% and 69%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.037 to 0.240 ng/g lipid and from 0.091 to 0.683 ng/g lipid, respectively.

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) described above, EH‐TBB was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 47% and 69%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.037 to 0.240 ng/g lipid and from 0.091 to 0.683 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs. In 2010, the levels of EH‐TBB ranged from < 0.01 to 2.1 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 44%), while in 2014–2015 a higher detection frequency (90%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.01 to 0.48 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.21 ng/g lipid.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs. In 2010, the levels of EH‐TBB ranged from < 0.01 to 2.1 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 44%), while in 2014–2015 a higher detection frequency (90%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.01 to 0.48 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.21 ng/g lipid.

Sahlström et al. (2015) did not detect EH‐TBB above the LOD (< 13 pg/g ww) in two pooled human milk samples from Sweden collected in 2009–2010.

Sahlström et al. (2015) did not detect EH‐TBB above the LOD (< 13 pg/g ww) in two pooled human milk samples from Sweden collected in 2009–2010.

Regarding levels in blood/serum,

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect EH‐TBB above the LOD (10 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect EH‐TBB above the LOD (10 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

In the study by Tay et al. (2019) on 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort, EH‐TBB was also reported below the LOD in all samples (LOD = 36 pg/serum sample, volume not reported).

In the study by Tay et al. (2019) on 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort, EH‐TBB was also reported below the LOD in all samples (LOD = 36 pg/serum sample, volume not reported).

Sales et al. (2017) detected EH‐TBB at 50 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Sales et al. (2017) detected EH‐TBB at 50 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), no experimental data were identified on EH‐TBB tested as a single chemical.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, the only study to consider EH‐TBB alone, was the NTP comparative study reported above for BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4.2) (NTP 30 ; Shockley et al., 2020a). In this case, male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats were administered EH‐TBB for 5 days via oral gavage in corn oil at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.55, 5.5, 55 and 550 mg/kg bw per day (n = 6). There were no dose‐related effects on survival, body weight, gross pathology or histopathology. The relative heart weight was decreased at doses of 0.55 mg/kg bw per day and higher, with a very flat dose–response relationship (less than 10% decrease at all doses). In view of the very small change over a wide range of doses (three orders of magnitude), the CONTAM Panel considered this effect not to be adverse and possibly incidental. The relative kidney weight was also reduced, but at the highest dose only. A few significant changes in haematology (reticulocytes, monocytes, large unstained cells) and clinical chemistry (bile salts, sodium, chloride, albumin) were found but these seem to be unrelated to the treatment. The liver and thyroid gland of the control and highest dose group were examined for neoplastic and non‐neoplastic lesions and none were found. Both T3 and FT4 concentrations in serum were reduced at the highest EH‐TBB dose of 550 mg/kg bw per day. However, the observed numerical decrease in TT4 was not statistically significant.

As mentioned in Section 3.2.4.2.3, new data were also available for studies that considered binary mixtures of EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP (Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023), or the technical product Firemaster BZ‐54 which contains EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP (Hays & Kirman, 2017) and these are described in Section 3.2.4.2.3.

Studies on a technical product containing EH‐TBB, i.e. Firemaster 550, were also identified (see Section 3.2.4.2.3). As for BEH‐TEBP, the CONTAM Panel concluded that data on this technical product added little to the body of evidence for the effects of EH‐TBB and were not suitable for identifying a Reference Point for use in risk characterisation.

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

From the single study with exposure to EH‐TBB alone, few effects were reported, i.e. reduced T3 and free T4 at 550 mg/kg bw per day, with a NOAEL of 55 mg/kg bw per day (Shockley et al., 2020a).

A LOAEL of 3.3 mg/kg bw per day has been reported for developmental behavioural and skeletal effects of the binary mixture of EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP, in which EH‐TBB contributed 2.25 mg/kg bw per day (Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023). From a study on a technical product containing 70% EH‐TBB and 30% BEH‐TEBP, the CONTAM Panel calculated a BMDL10 of approximately 30 mg/kg bw per day for decreased bodyweight and spleen weight in a two‐generation study (MPI, 2008b, as reported in Hays & Kirman, 2017), corresponding to 20 mg/kg bw per day EH‐TBB.

No long‐term studies, reproductive toxicity studies or experimental data on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB were identified.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on EH‐TBB (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for EH‐TBB with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability (see Section 2.2).

Read‐across

In the absence of experimental data on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel explored the possibility to perform read‐across from BEH‐TEBP, PBB‐Acr and HEEHP‐TEBP (for details on these compounds see Section 3.2.4, 3.3.11 and 3.3.17, respectively) regarding their structural similarity obtained from the Similarity Matrix reported by ‘istSimilarity’ (tool available in VEGA HUB platform;19 Floris et al., 2014): profiling results from the OECD QSAR Toolbox indicated endpoint specific similarity for the four substances, which do not trigger any DNA binding alerts. (Q)SAR predictions from VEGA software indicated that all four of these substances would be non‐mutagenic in the Ames test, noting the ‘MODERATE reliability’ of the predictions for EH‐TBB. See Annex B (Table B.4) for details.

However, the CONTAM Panel noted that the uncertainty in the read‐across conclusion would be too high considering: (i) the doubts on in vitro clastogenicity for BEH‐TEBP (see Section 3.2.4) and PBB‐Acr (see Section 3.3.11) (contrasting results) and (ii) the major differences in kinetics (absorption) between BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, which do not enable to extrapolate the results of the in vivo clastogenicity/aneugenicity studies of BEH‐TEBP to the target compound EH‐TBB. Moreover, the Panel was unable to conclude on the genotoxic potential of BEH‐TEBP.

Observations in humans

In the previous Opinion, no human studies were identified. Since then, six publications representing four epidemiological studies were identified. In Appendix F, an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on EH‐TBB, is shown.

Various endpoints were assessed including thyroid hormone profile (three studies), thyroid cancer (one study) and respiratory disease (one study). Exposure was assessed either in ambient air and wristbands (one study), in dust (one study) or in serum (two studies).

In the study by Guo et al. (2018) on the association with thyroid hormone levels in 10‐year‐old students living near a petrochemical complex in South China (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), EH‐ TBB levels were associated with lower FT4 levels (β −0.48, 95%CI ‐0.80, −0.15), but no associations were found for T3, T4, FT3 and TSH.

In the study by Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) that assessed the association between several BFRs and thyroid hormone profile (FT3, FT4 and TSH) in rural Central Appalachia (USA) (see details in Section 3.2.4.2), EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP were the most abundant novel BFRs in wristbands, with median concentrations of 87 and 31 ng/g wristband, respectively. The combined contribution of these two chemicals to the sum of 10 BFR concentrations was 94%. Among the multiple analyses performed, no statistically significant associations with the thyroid hormone profiles were reported for EH‐TBB.

In the study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), EH‐TBB was detected in 21% of the control population (geometric mean, 0.1 ± 3.0 ng/g lipid weight). Among the multiple analyses performed, no statistically significant associations were reported for EH‐TBB.

In the studies by Mendy et al. (2023, 2024) that assessed the association between the levels of BFRs in dust and respiratory disease in the USA HOME study (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), as regards the prenatal exposure adjusted analyses, both EH‐TBB log10 dust concentration (RR, 1.51; 95% CI, 1.23, 1.85) and EH‐TBB log10 dust loading (RR, 1.26; 95% CI, 1.08, 1.47) were associated with wheeze. In the adjusted analyses, EH‐TBB log10 loading at age 1 was statistically significantly associated with respiratory infections (RR, 1.34; 95% CI, 1.01, 1.79). The same study was also used to evaluate associations between prenatal levels of BFRs and cord blood DNA methylation in an epigenome‐wide association study (Mendy et al., 2025). EH‐TBB dust concentrations were not statistically significantly associated with single CpG sites in the study, but when differentially methylated regions (DMRs) were considered, EH‐TBB concentrations were associated with DMRs overlapping with genes involved in cell division and DNA repair, inflammation and immunity, and gene regulation, as stated by the authors.

In summary, the available epidemiological evidence on EH‐TBB pertained to populations in China or the USA and it was very limited in terms of the total number of available studies (six studies) and the number of available studies per endpoint (1–2 endpoints). Moreover, the exposure assessed in three out of the six studies was done in environmental samples without additional dietary/food data. No statistically significant associations were observed for thyroid function or thyroid cancer, apart from a decrease in FT4 level associated with EH‐TBB levels in one cross‐sectional study. A statistically significant signal was observed for the association between EH‐TBB levels measured in dust and wheeze and respiratory infections.

Other model systems and mode of action

The previous Opinion reported the study in fathead minnows describe above for BEH‐TEBP, in which fish were exposed via the diet to either of the technical products Firemaster 550 (BEH‐TEBP: 745 μg/g feed, EH‐TBB: 1658 μg/g feed) or Firemaster BZ‐54 (BEH‐TEBP: 907 μg/g feed, EH‐TBB: 2087 μg/g feed) (Bearr et al., 2010).

Since then, several studies have been published and an overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.1) and a brief summary is provided below.

Reduced serum T3 and FT4 levels in rats suggest interference with thyroid hormone regulation. The consensus of several in vitro studies examining effects on the thyroid hormone system is that EH‐TBB has low anti‐thyroid hormone and thyroid agonistic activities.

Experiments in fish indicate a possible effect of EH‐TBB on reproduction. Exposure of zebrafish with EH‐TBB (3.00, 13.63 and 54.53 μg/g of food) for 21 days induced a dose‐related reduction of fertility in F0 females (Ma et al., 2018). Feeding medaka a diet containing Firemaster 550 (containing BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) for 21 days resulted in downregulation of the vast majority of genes along the hypothalamus‐pituitary‐gonadal‐liver axis. This was associated with a reduction in cumulative egg production. There is only limited evidence from a number of in vitro studies that EH‐TBB (or BEH‐TEBP) directly interfere with sex steroid hormones. A maximal anti‐oestrogenic effect of 62% was measured at 0.5 mg L−1 of EH‐TBB in the YES assay (Saunders et al., 2013).

EH‐TBB and its carboxylic acid metabolites exhibited competitive inhibition of glucocorticoids for the glucocorticoid receptor in mammalian cell models (Klopčič et al., 2016). No glucocorticoid agonistic activities were observed. EH‐TBB (10 μM) and its metabolite, 2,3,4,5‐tetrabromobenzoic acid (10 μM), activated PXR and showed PXR antagonist activity. EH‐TBB also upregulated CYP3A4 mRNA expression which is PXR dependent.

Other in vitro effects of EH‐TBB included increased hepatocyte lipid accumulation and reduced expression of genes involved in lipid metabolism, such as PPARγ and PGC (Maia et al., 2022). However, EH‐TBB did not affect PPARα and PPARγ activities in transactivation assays in Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells or human liver (HepG2) cells (Belcher et al., 2014; Skledar et al., 2016).

There is some in vitro evidence that EH‐TBB has impact on vitamin D signalling pathway. Namely, EH‐TBB 250 nM significantly reduced expression of proteins involved in vitamin D metabolism and pathways (CYP2R1, CYP24A1, RxRa) in hepatic cells but not in MCF‐7 cells, and impaired cell cycle response to vitamin D (Sousa et al., 2022).

EH‐TBB did not show AHR activity in reporter gene assays (Saunders et al., 2013). Whereas dietary exposure of rats to EH‐TBB resulted in upregulation of AHR target genes, with Cypa1a showing up to 162‐fold induction (at 550 mg/kg bw per day), there was no corresponding increase in CYP1A1 activity (Shockley et al., 2020a). Activity of 7‐ethoxyresorufin deethylase (EROD) was increased in double‐breasted cormorant hepatocytes exposed to EH‐TBB, indicative of AHR activity (Pagé‐Larivière et al., 2018). Considering the absence of activity in an AHR reporter gene assay, any direct effect of EH‐TBB on AHR activation requires verification.

EH‐TBB and its metabolite 2,3,4,5 tetrabromobenzoic acid decreased cell viability and increased LDH release from the cells in human umbilical vein endothelial cells exposed for 24h. The metabolite of EH‐TBB increased specific gene expression (VEGFR1, eNOS, PECAM1) and reduced NO release in cells, and increased platelet number of mouse serum whereas the parent compound EH‐TBB did not. All these results suggest a higher level of toxicity for the metabolite compared to the parent compound (Chen et al., 2020).

Exposure of neuroblastoma cells SH‐SY5Y with EH‐TBB (2.5–20 μM) for 24 h showed no effects on cell viability, mitochondrial function, neural plasticity and development, promotion of oxidative stress and inflammation, suggesting the absence of toxicity in this neural cell line at the low μM concentrations tested (Esplugas et al., 2023).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel noted the lack of consistency in the findings of different studies, possibly related to different study designs and endpoints examined. No data on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB were identified, and read‐across was considered inappropriate (see above).

The findings reported at the lowest dose levels were neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in the study of a binary mixture of EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP (Schkoda et al., 2023; Witchey et al., 2020, 2023), which provided an EH‐TBB dose of 2.25 mg/kg bw per day. However, in the absence of information on relative potency, the CONTAM Panel does not know what the relative contributions of the two substances are, and therefore to be conservative made a worst‐case assumption for each substance. Accordingly, in order to identify a Reference Point specifically for EH‐TBB the CONTAM Panel attributed all findings to EH‐TBB, and adopted the LOAEL value of 2 mg/kg bw per day (rounded) as the Reference Point.

The Panel concluded that, due to the limitations and uncertainties in the current database, the establishment of a health‐based guidance value for EH‐TBB was not appropriate. Instead, the MOE approach should be used for risk characterisation (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2025).

Usually, an MOE of 100, covering variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans (factor 4 × 2.5 = 10) and within the human population (factor 3.2 × 3.2 = 10), is considered sufficient to conclude that exposure does not raise a health concern.

To allow for the absence of a NOAEL and the necessity of using a LOAEL, the EFSA Guidance on default values indicates that an additional uncertainty factor will be needed, the size of which should be determined on a case‐by‐case basis (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). ECHA, in its Guidance on information requirements and chemical safety assessment (ECHA, 2012) proposed the application of ‘an assessment factor ranging from 3 (as minimum/majority of cases) to 10 (as maximum/exceptional cases)’ with the following considerations: ‘An AF of 3 may be more appropriate for instance in situations, where the effects at the LOAEL are mild, or the LOAEL represents the lower boundary of the exposure range in which the effect is observed. Higher numerical values should be considered in situations where the effects at the LOAEL are severe and irreversible, or the shape of the dose–response curve is shallow or the quality of the study (e.g. group sizes, statistical methods, study design, exposure data) gives rise to uncertainties about the reliability of the identified LOAEL’ (ECHA, 2012). In this instance, the CONTAM Panel considered that since the effects at the LOAEL for EH‐TBB are mild, the factor of 3 was sufficient to extrapolate from a LOAEL to a NOAEL.

According to the EFSA Scientific Committee Guidance on selected default values, an additional factor can be considered in case of deficiencies in the database on a case‐by‐case basis. A default value has not been proposed, as it will be directly dependent on the data set available (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). The WHO/IPCS (1994, 1999) recommended a factor of 3 or 5 if there are minor deficiencies in the database and a factor of 10 if there are major deficiencies in the database. There are limitations in the database for EH‐TBB, such as inconsistencies between studies of non‐standard design, all but one of the studies being on mixtures rather than on the pure substance, no experimental data on genotoxicity and some studies only being available in summary form, so that they could not be fully assessed by the Panel. Taking into account also the sensitivity of the endpoints examined in the critical study, and the approach of attributing the effects solely to EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel considered an additional factor of 5 to be appropriate for deficiencies in the database.

As a result, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 1500 (100 for inter and intra‐species variability, 3 for LOAEL to NOAEL extrapolation and 5 for the gaps in the toxicological database) do not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

The CONTAM Panel could not set a Reference Point for neoplastic effects, since no carcinogenicity data are available. In addition, it was not possible to conclude on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Limited occurrence data in food (all left‐censored) were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

In the scientific literature, no information was identified for EH‐TBB in the previous EFSA Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), but since then, a few studies published in peer‐reviewed journals have been identified reporting on occurrence data on EH‐TBB; an overview is given in Section 3.1.2.

Two studies were identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to EH‐TBB, and these are summarised below:

Exposure estimates for EH‐TBB in the UK population were calculated based on daily consumption data for UK citizens from the national diet and nutrition survey (2008–2019) and occurrence data from samples collected in 2020–2021. Average dietary intakes of EH‐TBB ranged from 0.053 to 0.19 ng/kg bw per day, while high‐end estimates, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate + 2 standard deviations, ranged from 0.31 to 1.2 ng/kg bw per day, across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old) (Ma et al., 2023).

Exposure estimates for EH‐TBB in the UK population were calculated based on daily consumption data for UK citizens from the national diet and nutrition survey (2008–2019) and occurrence data from samples collected in 2020–2021. Average dietary intakes of EH‐TBB ranged from 0.053 to 0.19 ng/kg bw per day, while high‐end estimates, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate + 2 standard deviations, ranged from 0.31 to 1.2 ng/kg bw per day, across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old) (Ma et al., 2023).

The average daily intake of EH‐TBB was reported for the Chinese population at 1.33 and 0.97 ng/kg bw per day for the 5th (2009−2012) and 6th (2015−2018) TDSs, respectively. Interestingly, contribution of vegetables to the EH‐TBB exposure in the Chinese population was the greatest in both the 5th and 6th TDSs (48.5 and 39.2%, respectively), followed by meat (18.2 and 14.9%, respectively) and cereals (13.7 and 28.6%, respectively).

The average daily intake of EH‐TBB was reported for the Chinese population at 1.33 and 0.97 ng/kg bw per day for the 5th (2009−2012) and 6th (2015−2018) TDSs, respectively. Interestingly, contribution of vegetables to the EH‐TBB exposure in the Chinese population was the greatest in both the 5th and 6th TDSs (48.5 and 39.2%, respectively), followed by meat (18.2 and 14.9%, respectively) and cereals (13.7 and 28.6%, respectively).

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. As for BEH‐TEBP, to provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature (Čechová et al., 2017; Tao et al., 2017, see Section 3.2.5.2.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 0.24 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 1.10 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported a lower mean concentration in samples from the UK of 0.21 ng/g lipid that would result in an average exposure of 0.96 ng/kg bw per day.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 0.683 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 4.7 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported only maximum concentrations that were not used for these estimations.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from four European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data in food on EH‐TBB were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the EU population to be made. Limited data obtained from the scientific literature indicated that average dietary exposure from food in the UK was < 0.2 ng/kg bw per day and high‐end estimates up to 1.2 ng/kg bw per day (see Section 3.2.5.3). The Panel noted this exposure in the UK population would result in MOEs in the region of 106–7, which does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects. The indicative exposure estimates obtained for breastfed infants (see Section 3.2.5.3) would result in similar MOEs.

Regarding potential neoplastic effects, the EFSA guidance on the threshold of toxicological concern (TTC) specified that for substances raising a concern about potential DNA‐reactive mutagenicity and/or carcinogenicity, if exposure is below 2.5 ng/kg bw per day, the substance is considered of low probability of adverse health effects (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2019). Although the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB, the Panel noted that dietary exposure from one study in Europe obtained from the literature is below the TTC for genotoxic compounds, indicating a low probability of adverse health effects.

Uncertainty analysis

The uncertainty analysis was performed as described in Section 2.8. The identified sources of uncertainty affecting the hazard assessment are listed in Table 13.

Uncertainty analysis: Genotoxicity

No experimental data were identified for EH‐TBB and the CONTAM Panel did not identify any suitable source substances with adequate data to support assessment by read‐across. Consequently, the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of EH‐TBB (see Section 3.2.5.2), and no further analysis of the uncertainties was performed.

Uncertainty analysis: Hazard characterisation

The CONTAM Panel identified substantial uncertainties affecting the hazard assessment (Table 13) that did not allow the establishment of a HBGV. Instead the MOE approach was used, addressing the hazard uncertainties by applying UFs of 100 (to cover for variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans, and within the human population), 3 (to account for the absence of a NOAEL) and 5 (for deficiencies in the database).

Considering the combined impact of the uncertainties and the extent to which they are addressed by the applied UFs, the CONTAM Panel judged with 90–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that, when using the Reference Point for EH‐TBB of 2 mg/kg bw per day, MOEs ≥ 1500 would not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

Uncertainty analysis: Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data in food on EH‐TBB were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the EU population to be made. Based on dietary exposure data from food from the scientific literature from one European country (UK, see Section 3.2.5.3), the Panel noted this would result in MOEs in the region of 106–7.

Considering these very large MOEs, and the uncertainties affecting the exposure estimates, the CONTAM Panel judged with 90%–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that current exposure of the European population to EH‐TBB does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects. Compared to the situation with BEH‐TEBP, it was noted that the certainty is reduced due to exposure data (one European country only), and less conservativism in the hazard characterisation (regarding the assumption that effects are solely due to EH‐TBB).

Dibromoneopentyl glycol (DBNPG) [CAS number 3296‐900]

Production, use and environmental fate

Production and use

The previous Opinion reported that there were more than 50 suppliers of DBNPG in the world. It is supplied as FR‐522 to make flame retardant unsaturated polyester resins (Weil & Levchik, 2009). NTP (1996), described DBNPG as a reactive flame retardant that is used primarily in unsaturated polyester resins for moulded products and in rigid polyurethane foams. No new information about the production and use of DBNPG was identified.

Analysis

No information was identified on methods for environmental or food samples for DBNPG.

Environmental fate and occurrence in the environment

No information could be found about the environmental fate of DBNPG or on levels in wildlife or the environment.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

For the current update the CONTAM Panel identified two studies published before the previous Opinion that are reported here for completeness. These two studies referred to 2,2‐Bis(bromomethyl)‐1,3‐propanediol (called BBMP or BMP), which corresponds to DBNPG.

Hoehle et al. (2009) investigated the ADME of DBNPG in rats. Male F344 rats (n = 4 animals per dose) were administered by gavage with a single dose of 10, 100, 150, 300 and 600 mg/kg of 14C‐labelled DBNPG, and 10 and 15 mg/kg by iv. In parallel, the authors performed repeated dose studies with 100 mg/kg administered daily by oral gavage for 1, 5 or 10 days. DBNPG was rapidly absorbed (Cmax reached at 40 min) and efficiently metabolised to a glucuronide metabolite. The authors found that less than 1% of the dose (100 mg/kg bw) was retained in tissues at 72 h: adipose tissues, liver, kidneys, muscle and skin contained 0.2%, 0.7%, 0.1%, 0.3% and 0.3% of the initial dose, respectively. Within 24 h, 89% of the administered radioactivity had been recovered in the urine. The elimination in faeces accounted for less than 10% of the dose at 72 h.

In the repeated daily oral dose study, this route and rate of elimination was similar to the single administration. After oral administration, the majority of the radioactivity detected in plasma extracts was a glucuronide metabolite. DBNPG glucuronide was excreted in the bile, and eliminated predominantly in the urine. Because of enterohepatic recirculation, a low percentage of the dose was eliminated in the faeces.

Rad et al. (2010) investigated the in vitro glucuronidation of DBNPG using both hepatic microsomes from human and male Fisher rat, human intestinal microsomes and also human hepatic recombinant hepatics UDP‐glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes. The authors reported that DPNPG was converted to a monoglucuronide metabolite in human intestinal microsomes, and to a lesser extent in human hepatic microsomes. The rate of DBNPG glucuronidation in rat hepatocyte was 90‐fold higher than in human (hepatocyte). From also human hepatic recombinant UGT enzymes, the authors reported that only UGT2B7 was involved in the glucuronidation of DBNPG with a similar rate to that of human microsomes.

In a satellite experiment with hepatic microsomes from different species, the authors compared the glucuronidation activity between rats (male and female F344), mice (male B6C3F1), male Syrian hamster, male rhesus monkey and humans. The order of hepatic microsomal glucuronidation activity of DBNPG was rats, mice > hamsters > monkeys > humans.

In summary, DBNPG is rapidly absorbed in rats after oral exposure and metabolised in the liver to a monoglucuronide conjugate. The elimination of DBNPG glucuronide in bile and enterohepatic recycling results in a low systemic bioavailability of DBNPG. The vast majority (89%) of radiolabelled DBNPG was recovered in the urine 24 h following administration by oral gavage. Similar metabolism has been described in humans in vitro.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

The previous Opinion reported data on four oral studies in rodents administered the technical product FR‐1138, containing 78.6% DBNPG, 6.9% 2,2‐bis(bromomethyl)‐1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (TBNPA, see Section 3.3.12), 6.6% 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 0.2% pentaerythritol and 7.7% dimers and structural isomers. FR‐1138 was administered for 13 weeks at doses up to 1700 or 1600 mg/kg bw per day in male and female rats, respectively and 3000 or 2900 mg/kg bw per day in male and female mice, respectively. The most sensitive effects were papillary degeneration and necrosis of the kidney and hyperplasia of the transitional‐cell epithelium of the urinary bladder (Elwell et al., 1989; NTP, 1996). The lowest NOAEL reported in these studies was 100 mg/kg bw per day for males of both rats and mice.

Decrease of male reproductive organ weights was only observed with concurrent reduction of body weight in 13 weeks feeding studies with the technical product FR‐1138 (76.8% DBNPG) in mice at 1300 and 3000 mg/kg bw per day, and in rats at 1700 mg/kg bw per day. Oestrous cyclicity and spermatozoal parameters were not affected (NTP, 1996).

The previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported also a two‐generation reproduction toxicity study, CD‐1 mice were fed a diet containing 0, 141, 274 and 589 mg/kg bw per day of DBNPG (purity 87%) (the following impurities were reported: 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (6.7%), 1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (5.5%) and 3,3‐bis(bromomethyl)‐oxetane (0.5%), 0.3% water) (Treinen et al., 1989). Reduction of postpartum body weights and weight gain in males and females was noted at ≥ 274 mg/kg bw per day as well as a dose‐dependent decrease in live pup weight. The number of litters per pair and the number of live pups born per litter were significantly reduced at 589 mg/kg bw per day. In the F1 generation, weight gain was significantly reduced at the two highest dose levels in males and at the highest dose level in females. The number of live pups born per litter and adjusted live pup weight was decreased at 589 mg/kg bw per day. Cross‐breeding of F0 exposed males and females of the high‐dose group with F0 controls after 11 weeks of dosing resulted in a reduction of the number of live pups per litter and of pup weight only when exposed females were mated with control males. Histological examination revealed kidney lesions in treated male and female rats. The number of follicles was reduced in the ovaries of cross‐bred F0 females in the high‐dose group. The same effect was found in F1 females of the two high‐dose groups. There was no effect on reproductive organ weights and oestrual cyclicity.

Fischer 344/N rats were fed a diet containing the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG), at concentrations of 0, 2500, 5000 or 10,000 mg/kg feed for 2 years. Average daily doses of FR‐1138 were 0, 100, 200 or 430 mg/kg bw for males and 0, 115, 230 or 460 mg/kg bw for females (NTP, 1996). Survival at 2 years of male and female rats in the two highest dose groups was significantly lower than controls. In both males and females, significantly increased incidences of neoplasms in various organs were observed, with males exhibiting a wider range of affected organs than females. Details of the tumour incidence reported by NTP (1996) were not included in the previous Opinion, but are now provided below (see Table 14).

In addition, non‐neoplastic effects were observed: in males at several doses, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration, transitional epithelial hyperplasia (pelvis) and papillary epithelial hyperplasia), lung (alveolar/bronchiolar hyperplasia), thyroid gland (follicular cell hyperplasia), seminal vesicle (hyperplasia), pancreas (hyperplasia), urinary bladder (hyperplasia) and forestomach (mucosal hyperplasia) and in females, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration and papillary epithelial hyperplasia) at the highest dose only.

B6C3F1 mice were fed a diet containing 0, 312, 625 or 1250 mg/kg of the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG) for 2 years. Average daily doses of FR‐1138 were 0, 35, 70 or 149 mg/kg bw for males and 0, 40, 80 or 170 mg/kg bw for females (NTP, 1996). Survival in both males and females of the highest dose group was significantly lower than that of controls. Details of the tumour incidence reported by NTP (1996) were not included in the previous Opinion, but are now provided below (see Table 15).

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that DBNPG (purity 84% or 96.3%) was mutagenic in S. Typhimurium strain TA100 in the presence of liver S9 fraction from Aroclor‐induced Syrian hamsters. A dose‐related increase in chromosomal aberrations was found in the presence of metabolic activation (rat liver) in Chinese hamster ovary cells (NTP, 1996; purity NR). In vivo, the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG) induced significant increases in the frequencies of micronucleated normochromatic erythrocytes in peripheral blood of male and female mice exposed for 13 weeks via feed. Inconsistent results (one positive and one negative) were noted in bone marrow micronucleus tests in male mice, where DBNPG (purity NR) was administered by gavage three times at 24 h intervals. A bone marrow micronucleus test in mice, where DBNPG (purity NR) was administered ip, was positive only in females (NTP, 1996). Additional data from reproductive reports led to the conclusion that DBNPG reaches the germ cells (see EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Three genotoxicity studies were identified.

The effects of time (1–24 h) and concentration (5–100 μM) on induction of DNA strand breaks by DBNPG (purity of 98%, vehicle 0.5% EtOH) were assessed via the alkaline comet assay in SV‐40 immortalised human urothelial cells (UROtsa). DNA strand breaks (measured by an increase in % DNA in tail) were observed at 1 and 3 h following incubation of cells with non‐cytotoxic concentrations of DBNPG (25 μM). Results indicated also that 1 h exposure (5–100 μM) induced a concentration‐dependent increase in the % DNA in the comet tails. Strand breaks were not present after 6 h of incubation. There was evidence for DBNPG oxidative stress: an elevation of intracellular ROS formation as well as induction of Nrf2 and HSP70 protein levels. Moreover, DNA strand breaks were attenuated when cells were pre‐treated with N‐acetyl‐l‐cysteine (NAC). In addition, oxidative base modifications were revealed when a lesion specific endonuclease, human 8‐hydroxyguanine DNA glycosylase 1 (hOGG1) was introduced into the comet assay (Kong et al., 2011).

An in vitro study investigated the susceptibility of target (UROtsa cells) and non‐target cells (primary rat hepatocytes) to DBNPG‐induced genotoxicity. No increase in DNA strand breaks was detected after 1 h exposure of rat hepatocytes to 25–100 μM DBNPG. In contrast at the same concentrations and exposure time a concentration‐dependent increase in DNA strand breaks was observed in UROtsa cells. To assess the ability of DBNPG to covalently bind to DNA, UROtsa cells and rat hepatocytes were incubated with [14C]‐DBNPG (10–250 μM) for 1 or 24 h (Kong et al., 2013). Following 1 h incubation, the amount of [14C]‐DBNPG equivalents bound to DNA was similar for hepatocytes and UROtsa cells. However, after 24 h incubation with [14C]‐DBNPG, the extent of binding measured in rat hepatocytes was significantly lower than that measured in UROtsa cells (13–111 and 48–1041 pmol/mg DNA, respectively) (Kong et al., 2013). In addition, HPLC analysis indicated that DBNPG was not metabolised and/or eliminated in UROtsa cells at any of the concentrations tested but was extensively converted to a monoglucuronide in hepatocytes. These results demonstrate that a target cell line (UROtsa cells) is more susceptible to DBNPG‐induced DNA damage in comparison to non‐target cells (hepatocytes). This increased susceptibility may relate to the deficiency of metabolic and/or antioxidant capacity in target cells.

The increased susceptibility of target cells to DBNPG‐induced genotoxicity was confirmed in an in vivo mammalian alkaline Comet assay performed on urinary bladder and liver cells (not validated) from Sprague–Dawley male rats exposed by gavage to 0, 300, 600 mg DBNPG/kg bw per day (purity 98%), twice at 21 h apart. Animals were sacrificed 3 h after the second administration. Increased DNA damage was observed in the urinary bladder but not in the liver. In these experimental conditions, no decrease in body weight and no abnormal necropsy or histopathological findings were observed (Wada et al., 2014; REACH dossier, 31 as submitted by the registrant(s)).

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

In sub‐chronic toxicity studies, the technical product FR‐1138 (78.6% DBNPG) induced papillary degeneration (from 400 mg/kg bw per day in male rats and at 1600 mg/kg bw per day in female rats) and necrosis (from 500 mg/kg bw per day in male mice and at 2900 mg/kg bw per day in female mice) of the kidney and hyperplasia of the transitional‐cell epithelium of the urinary bladder in rats and mice (at 1600 mg/kg bw per day in male rats, and 1300 and 1200 mg/kg bw per day in male and female mice, respectively). In a two‐generation reproductive toxicity in mice with DBNPG (87% purity), fetotoxicity was observed in presence of maternal toxicity as well as a decrease in fertility. Crossover mating indicated a specific effect on the female reproductive capacity.

DBNPG (purity 84% or 96.3%, depending on the study) was genotoxic in vitro. The technical product FR‐1138 (78.6% DBNPG) is genotoxic and carcinogenic in a variety of organs in rats (e.g. skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland,) and mice (e.g. Harderian gland, lung, forestomach, subcutaneous tissue).

No in vivo studies in mammalian species were available on neurobehaviour effects, which has been identified as critical effect for other BFR families.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on DBNPG as retrieved by the previous Opinion (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results for DBNPG with ‘GOOD reliability’ (four models). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the predictions for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this positive prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

The QSAR Toolbox profilers provided the following information on the structural alerts and predicted mode of toxicity: DBNPG triggers the ‘Aliphatic halides’ alert according to the ‘DNA binding by OECD’ profiler, and the ‘Aliphatic halogens’ alert according to the ‘in vitro mutagenicity (Ames test) by ISS’ profiler because of the aliphatic Br atom (see Figure 1). In the QSAR Toolbox, this alert is associated with DNA alkylation by nucleophilic substitution of second order (SN2) mechanism. The QSAR Toolbox profiling results are provided in Annex B, while additional information on the alerts triggered can be consulted within the software.

The CONTAM Panel noted that the computational analysis supports the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor the current update.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Two new studies on mode of action have been identified.

Induction of DNA strand breaks by DBNPG was shown in alkaline comet assays in immortalised urothelial (UROtsa) cells after 1 and 3 h of exposure to non‐cytotoxic concentrations, but not at later time points up to 24 h (Kong et al., 2011, 2013). There was evidence for DBNPG related oxidative stress: an elevation of intracellular ROS formation, which was statistically significant after 20 min of exposure, induction of oxidised DNA bases (analysed after 1 h exposure only), as well as induction of Nrf2 (significant after 3, 6 and 24 h of exposure) and HSP70 protein levels (significant after 6 and 18 h of exposure). Even if the induced damage seems rapidly repaired (see above), these early genotoxic events may, in part, contribute to the DBNPG‐induced carcinogenesis observed in rodents.

The susceptibility of target (UROtsa cells) and non‐target cells (primary rat hepatocytes) to DBNPG‐induced genotoxicity was investigated (see above). In contrast to hepatocytes, DBNPG exhibited genotoxic potential in UROtsa cells as evidenced by the concentration‐dependent increase in DNA strand breaks and DNA binding. Total content of intracellular GSH quantified in UROtsa cells was 4‐fold lower than that in hepatocytes. HPLC analysis indicated DBNPG was not metabolised in UROtsa cells at any of the concentrations tested (10–250 μM, up to 24 h incubation) in the absence or presence of metabolic co‐factors (UDPGA and NADPH). In hepatocytes, it was extensively converted to a monoglucuronide. This is a less DNA‐reactive metabolite which produced less DNA strand breaks than DBNPG, with no evidence of other metabolites being formed. The extent of glucuronidation by rat hepatocytes was dependent on the substrate ([14C]‐DBNPG) concentration and duration of exposure. There was extensive metabolism at low substrate concentrations (10–50 μM) and incomplete conjugation of DBNPG and persistence of free parent DBNPG at high concentrations. This is most likely due to the concentration‐dependent saturation of UDP‐glucuronosyltransferases. The degree of covalent binding of [14C]‐DBNPG equivalents to cellular DNA was similar in both cell types at 1 h but clearly different at 24 h. The authors of the study proposed that increased susceptibility of DBNPG to induce DNA damage in UROtsa cells may be related to the deficiency of antioxidant and/or metabolic capabilities of these cells (Kong et al., 2013).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel concluded that DBNPG (purity 84% or 96.3%, depending on the study) is genotoxic in vitro, and that the technical product FR‐1138 (containing DBNPG with a purity of 78.6%) is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats and mice.

The Panel noted that one of the impurities of the technical product FR‐1138 is TBNPA, covered in this Opinion in Section 3.3.12. TBNPA was shown to be mutagenic and clastogenic in vitro. In vivo, it was negative in a micronucleus test in mice exposed by the oral route; however, exposure of bone marrow was not demonstrated.

TBNPA has been classified by the ECHA's RAC as a ‘Germ cell mutagen Category 2, H341: Suspected of causing genetic defects’ (ECHA RAC, 2020). No carcinogenicity study on TBNPA has been identified but based on read‐across from DBNPG, ECHA's RAC supports the classification of TBNPA as ‘Carc 1B, H350: May cause cancer’.

No information was identified on the other main impurity of the technical product FR‐1138. In the absence of knowledge of the relative potency of these compounds, and for the purpose of hazard characterisation of DBNPG, the carcinogenic effects observed in animal studies with FR‐1138 were attributed to DBNPG.

Papillary degeneration and necrosis of the kidney and hyperplasia of the transitional‐cell epithelium of the urinary bladder were observed in rats and mice exposed by gavage or by feed to the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG) for 13 weeks. Depression of reproductive organ weight was only observed with concurrent reduction of body weight in male and female mice, and in male and female rats (in mice at 1300 and 3000 mg/kg bw per day, and in rats at 1700 mg/kg bw per day).

In a two‐generation reproductive toxicity, mice were fed with a test substance containing 87% DBNPG, fetotoxicity was observed in presence of maternal toxicity at doses ≥ 274 mg/kg bw per day as well as decrease in fertility was observed at 589 mg/kg bw per day. Crossover mating between F0 mice exposed to 589 mg/kg bw per day and control F0 mice indicated a specific effect on the female reproductive capacity. No in vivo studies in mammalian species were available on neurobehaviour effects, which has been identified as critical effect for other BFR families.

The critical studies are the carcinogenicity studies in rats and mice since other effects were observed only at higher dose levels (see Section 3.2.6.2). No studies in humans were identified.

The CONTAM Panel performed benchmark dose (BMD) modelling according to the 2022 EFSA Guidance on the use of the BMD approach in risk assessment (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022). The results of the BMD modelling for the critical studies in rats and mice (NTP, 1996) are summarised in Tables 16 and 17, respectively. Details of the BMD analyses, including the individual reports of the modelling are shown in Annex D.
