There is insufficient evidence that there is a substantial dose‐effect.

For quantal data the default benchmark response (BMR) of 10% was applied as recommended in the EFSA BMD guidance (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022).

The lowest BMDL10 for the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG) was 1.16 mg/kg bw per day, based on increased incidence of mammary gland fibroadenoma, adenoma or carcinoma in female rats. The CONTAM Panel noted that the dose‐spacing in this study was not optimal since the dose levels only differed by two‐fold and the incidence was in the region of 90% at all tested doses. However, DBNPG is clearly a multi‐site carcinogen, and modelling of the data for mammary gland fibroadenoma, adenoma or carcinoma in female rats gave an acceptable fit. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel selected this lowest BMDL10 as the Reference Point.

Although the tumour incidences in the treated groups do not increase with dose, the statistically significant increase in fibroadenoma incidence across all three DBNPG treated groups compared to controls is a concern, as the effect is consistent and biologically plausible. This plausibility is partially supported by mechanistic evidence, including genotoxicity and potential hormonal effects, given that fibroadenomas in rats are often associated with hormonal imbalance. Therefore, a Reference Point could be established based on the observed increase in rat mammary fibroadenoma incidence. Assuming the observed effects were due entirely to DBNPG, and correcting for the purity of the test material, the CONTAM Panel established a Reference Point of 0.9 mg/kg bw per day for DBNPG.

Since the technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG) is both carcinogenic and genotoxic, the CONTAM Panel considered that no HBGV can be established as any level of exposure could theoretically lead to cancer (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2005). Instead, the MOE approach would normally be used for risk characterisation. For genotoxic and carcinogenic compounds, and according to EFSA Scientific Committee (2005); EFSA Scientific Committee (2025), an MOE of 10,000 or higher, if it is based on the BMDL10 from an animal carcinogenicity study, would be of low concern from a public health point of view and might be considered as a low priority for risk management actions.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on DBNPG in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the lack of data on occurrence and exposure for DBNPG, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the hazard assessment, using the methods described in Section 2.8. All but one of the identified sources of uncertainty affecting the hazard assessment were assessed as having negligible or low impact on the conclusions (Table 18).

Uncertainty analysis: Genotoxicity and carcinogenicity

Uncertainties affecting the evidence on the genotoxic potential of DBNPG (see Section 3.2.6.2.3) were assessed to have low impact on the assessment (Table 18). DBNPG (purity 84% or 96.3%, depending on the study) is genotoxic in vitro, and the technical product FR‐1138 (containing DBNPG with a purity of 78.6%) is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats and mice. There is evidence of DNA strand breaks and covalent binding to DNA with DBNPG (purity 98%).

Based on the overall evidence, and taking into account of the associated uncertainties (Table 18), and following an expert group discussion, the CONTAM Panel judged with 95%–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic.

Uncertainty analysis: Reference Point

The Reference Point for carcinogenicity was affected by uncertainties arising from the number and spacing of doses in the critical study (0, 115, 230, 460 mg/kg bw per day), which were assessed as having low impact on the hazard characterisation (Table 18). The critical study was carried out with a technical product in which DBNPG represents 78.6%, and included impurities that could contribute to the toxicity/carcinogenicity. Since the remaining non‐standard uncertainties were all considered to have a negligible or low impact, the Reference Point of 0.9 mg/kg bw per day, which assumed that the effects were entirely attributed to DBNPG, should be conservative.

Considering this, and the general considerations around the prior distributions used in the Bayesian BMD analysis (see Section 3.2.1.5 ), the CONTAM Panel judged with at least 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that the Reference Point of DBNPG of 0.9 mg/kg bw per day would not be lower if the non‐standard uncertainties were resolved.

Decabromodiphenyl ethane (DBDPE) [CAS number 84852‐53‐9]

DBDPE is a non‐polymeric additive flame retardant, considered by many stakeholders as an alternative to the PBDE technical mixture DecaBDE. It is widely used in the automotive industry, for electrical and electronic equipment. For textiles it can be used at concentrations making up to 40% of the material produced.

The physicochemical properties of DBDPE are reported in Table 1. DBDPE is registered in REACH (10,000–100,000 tonnes per annum) and it has been found to meet the vPvB criteria (see Table 2).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012a, 2012b), it was reported that after a single oral dose of 1000 mg/kg bw in Sprague–Dawley rats, DBDPE was poorly absorbed by the gastrointestinal tract (Environment Agency, England and Wales, 2007).

Since the previous Opinion, two studies have been identified (Knudsen, Sanders, Hughes, et al., 2017; Zheng et al., 2022):

Knudsen, Sanders, Hughes, et al. (2017) investigated the disposition of DBDPE in female Sprague–Dawley rats and male B6C3F1/Tac mice. A single dose of 0.02 mg/kg bw of 14C‐DBDPE was administered via oral and iv route in rats and mice. A second group of rats was administered for 10 days with 0.02 mg/kg bw per day of 14C‐DBDPE via oral route.

Knudsen, Sanders, Hughes, et al. (2017) investigated the disposition of DBDPE in female Sprague–Dawley rats and male B6C3F1/Tac mice. A single dose of 0.02 mg/kg bw of 14C‐DBDPE was administered via oral and iv route in rats and mice. A second group of rats was administered for 10 days with 0.02 mg/kg bw per day of 14C‐DBDPE via oral route.

After a single oral administration to Sprague–Dawley female rats, 75% of the dose was retrieved in faeces after 24h. In a pilot study with bile‐duct cannulated rat, the authors found that 0.1% of the dose was recovered in bile which suggests that radioactivity recovered in faeces represents unabsorbed dose. Similar results were obtained in male B6C3F1 mice. The authors conducted an i.v. study in rats, but did not calculate oral bioavailability. Less than 1% of the radioactivity was retained in tissues collected at 72 h in rat.

After a single oral administration to Sprague–Dawley female rats, 75% of the dose was retrieved in faeces after 24h. In a pilot study with bile‐duct cannulated rat, the authors found that 0.1% of the dose was recovered in bile which suggests that radioactivity recovered in faeces represents unabsorbed dose. Similar results were obtained in male B6C3F1 mice. The authors conducted an i.v. study in rats, but did not calculate oral bioavailability. Less than 1% of the radioactivity was retained in tissues collected at 72 h in rat.

The results showed that DBDPE was poorly absorbed, and mainly excreted (98%) by the faecal route (unchanged form) within 72 h after a single oral administration to rats and mice.

The results showed that DBDPE was poorly absorbed, and mainly excreted (98%) by the faecal route (unchanged form) within 72 h after a single oral administration to rats and mice.

After 10 daily oral administrations to Sprague–Dawley female rats, the authors measured 2% ± 0.4% and 94% ± 8% of the radioactivity in urine and faeces, respectively. The concentrations in adrenal and liver tissue increased after 10 days exposure. However, the radioactivity retained in these tissues collected 24 h after the final dose was low (0.06% and 0.008% of the total dose in liver and adrenal tissues, respectively).

After 10 daily oral administrations to Sprague–Dawley female rats, the authors measured 2% ± 0.4% and 94% ± 8% of the radioactivity in urine and faeces, respectively. The concentrations in adrenal and liver tissue increased after 10 days exposure. However, the radioactivity retained in these tissues collected 24 h after the final dose was low (0.06% and 0.008% of the total dose in liver and adrenal tissues, respectively).

Zheng et al. (2022) studied the biotransformation of several BFRs including DBDPE, to evaluate whether this was responsible for the presence of 2,4,6‐TBP found in humans using human and rat liver microsomes. No information was reported by the authors on the biotransformation of DBDPE except that it did not result in the formation of 2,4,6‐TBP.

Zheng et al. (2022) studied the biotransformation of several BFRs including DBDPE, to evaluate whether this was responsible for the presence of 2,4,6‐TBP found in humans using human and rat liver microsomes. No information was reported by the authors on the biotransformation of DBDPE except that it did not result in the formation of 2,4,6‐TBP.

Furthermore, data reported in the REACH dossier, 32 as submitted by the registrant(s), support a very low oral bioavailability of DBDPE in rodents following oral administration of radiolabelled DBDPE. It should be noted that the CONTAM Panel could not independently assess these data as it did not have access to the original study reports.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion reported a study on five serum samples from Sweden in which DBDPE could not be identified above the LOD (1.03 ng/g lipid) (Karlsson et al., 2007).

Since then, several studies have been identified reporting on the levels of DBDPE in human samples from European countries, including human milk and blood/serum. These studies are briefly described below, and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

DBDPE was reported to be below the LOD (2.2 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013). In a later study on 16 pools of human milk collected between 2016 and 2018 still in Ireland, DBDPE was detected in 3 out of the 16 pools analysed, with concentrations ranging from < LOD to 4.6 ng/g lipid (LOD 2.5 ng/g lipid) and a mean value of 0.74 ng/g lipid (Wemken et al., 2020).

DBDPE was reported to be below the LOD (2.2 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013). In a later study on 16 pools of human milk collected between 2016 and 2018 still in Ireland, DBDPE was detected in 3 out of the 16 pools analysed, with concentrations ranging from < LOD to 4.6 ng/g lipid (LOD 2.5 ng/g lipid) and a mean value of 0.74 ng/g lipid (Wemken et al., 2020).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. DBDPE was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (< 11 pg/g ww).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. DBDPE was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (< 11 pg/g ww).

Tao et al. (2017) analysed DBDPE in milk samples from UK mothers collected in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10). In 2010, the levels of DBDPE ranged from < 0.78 to 250 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 4%), while in 2014–2015 a slightly higher detection frequency (10%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.78 to 58 ng/g lipid.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed DBDPE in milk samples from UK mothers collected in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10). In 2010, the levels of DBDPE ranged from < 0.78 to 250 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 4%), while in 2014–2015 a slightly higher detection frequency (10%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.78 to 58 ng/g lipid.

Regarding blood/serum samples,

Cequier et al. (2013) developed an analytical method for the determination of PBDEs and other BFRs in serum samples. The method was applied to the analysis of 10 serum samples from Norway and DBDPE was not detected in any of the samples (LOD = 20 pg/mL). In a later study, Cequier et al. (2015) also reported levels of DBDPE below the LOD in serum samples from 46 women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012.

Cequier et al. (2013) developed an analytical method for the determination of PBDEs and other BFRs in serum samples. The method was applied to the analysis of 10 serum samples from Norway and DBDPE was not detected in any of the samples (LOD = 20 pg/mL). In a later study, Cequier et al. (2015) also reported levels of DBDPE below the LOD in serum samples from 46 women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012.

DBDPE was also measured in 61 serum samples from a Norwegian cohort collected in 2013 with a reported geometric mean of 3.4 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 2%, LOD 42 pg/serum sample (Tay et al., 2019).

DBDPE was also measured in 61 serum samples from a Norwegian cohort collected in 2013 with a reported geometric mean of 3.4 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 2%, LOD 42 pg/serum sample (Tay et al., 2019).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect DBDPE above the LOD (15 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected 2009–2010.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect DBDPE above the LOD (15 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected 2009–2010.

Van der Schyff et al. (2023) reported on flame retardants in children from nine European countries from the HBM4EU aligned studies. DBDPE was investigated in blood from France, Greece and Slovenia, with a detection frequency of 13–18% (LOD = 0.013 μg/L). No further details were provided as compounds with detection frequencies < 40% were excluded by the authors for statistical analysis.

No further details were provided as compounds with detection frequencies < 40% were excluded by the authors for statistical analysis.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect DBDPE above the LOD of 13 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect DBDPE above the LOD of 13 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

In the previous Opinion several studies were reported. No clinical signs, changes in clinical chemistry or haematology were observed in male and female Sprague–Dawley rats exposed by gavage to DBDPE (purity 96.3%) for 90 days up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Hardy et al., 2002). Statistically significant differences were only found between control and female rats exposed to 1000 mg/kg bw per day in absolute and relative liver weights.

No significant changes in body, liver and kidney weights (absolute or relative) were observed in male rats orally exposed for 90 days to 100 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Wang et al., 2010). A significant increase in the T3 level was found in the treated group suggesting that DBDPE can alter thyroid hormone homeostasis. However, there was no effect on T4 level, and TSH was not measured.

No evidence of maternal toxicity, developmental toxicity or teratogenicity was noted in rats and rabbits exposed by gavage up to 1250 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day from GD6 to GD15 for rats and GD6 to GD18 for rabbits (as cited in Hardy et al., 2010).

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that DBDPE was not genotoxic in reverse mutation assays in S. Typhimurium and E. coli WP2 and did not induce chromosomal aberrations in V79 Chinese hamster lung cells (San and Wagner, 1991; Stankowski, 1988, as cited in Hardy et al., 2010) (see EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

A number of studies were identified since the previous Opinion. Some studies were identified in the scientific literature while others were found in the REACH dossier32, as submitted by the registrant(s). For the latter, the CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original study reports.

An overview of these studies can be found in Appendix C (Table C.1).

DBDPE was also included in the same comparative study of a number of BFRs conducted by the NTP and described in Section 3.2.4.2.3 (Shockley et al., 2020a). Male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats exposed to DBDPE (purity 94%) by gavage for 5 days to 0 (corn oil), 0.1, 0.97, 9.71, 97.1 and 970 mg/kg bw per day showed no effects on serum TT4, TT3 or TSH nor on absolute liver weight. The clinical chemistry endpoints and the haematology endpoints measured were unchanged compared to concurrent controls. There was no effect on UDP GT1a1 liver enzyme level. No histological changes were present in the liver or thyroid gland (Shockley et al., 2020a). The NOAEL was 970 mg/kg bw per day.

A number of publications reported on a 28‐day study in male Sprague–Dawley rats exposed by gavage to DBDPE (purity > 98%) at 0 (corn oil), 5, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day conducted by the same research group (Gao et al., 2022; Jing et al., 2019; Li et al., 2021; Sun et al., 2020; Wang, Chen, et al., 2019; Xue et al., 2022; Zheng et al., 2021). The results are described below:

−At the highest dose group, DBDPE was reported to cause morphological changes in the heart and abdominal aorta (Jing et al., 2019). It also induced ultrastructural damage in the heart: slight mitochondrial swelling and cristae disorder at 50 mg/kg bw per day, and additionally, mitochondrial swelling, membrane breach and more cristae disorder, or vacuolation at 500 mg/kg bw per day. Ultrastructural damage (endothelial cell nucleus swelling, subendothelial space expansion, endothelial cell shedding and internal elastic lamina thinning) in the aorta was also observed at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, and ultrastructural changes of smooth muscle cells at 500 mg/kg bw per day. There was no alteration in serum creatine kinase (CK) activity, whereas lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) activity showed a significant increase at 500 mg/kg bw per day. Additionally, DBDPE led to endothelial dysfunction, as evidenced by the Endothelin‐1 (ET‐1) and intercellular adhesion molecule‐1 (ICAM‐1) elevation at the highest dose group. DBDPE‐induced inflammation was characterised by the upregulation of key inflammatory mediators detected in the serum, including interleukin‐1β (IL‐1β), IL‐6 and tumour necrosis factor alpha (TNFα) at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day. In the heart, superoxide dismutase (SOD) and glutathione peroxidase (GSH‐Px) activities were significantly increased at all doses and malondialdehyde (MDA) content was significantly increased at the two highest doses. The authors concluded that DBDPE could induce oxidative stress, inflammation and eventually lead to endothelial dysfunction and cardiovascular injury (Jing et al., 2019). The NOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day. Zheng et al. (2021) reported also morphological damage in the abdominal aortas and ultrastructural changes at the two DBDPE highest doses.

At the highest dose group, DBDPE was reported to cause morphological changes in the heart and abdominal aorta (Jing et al., 2019). It also induced ultrastructural damage in the heart: slight mitochondrial swelling and cristae disorder at 50 mg/kg bw per day, and additionally, mitochondrial swelling, membrane breach and more cristae disorder, or vacuolation at 500 mg/kg bw per day. There was no alteration in serum creatine kinase (CK) activity, whereas lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) activity showed a significant increase at 500 mg/kg bw per day. Additionally, DBDPE led to endothelial dysfunction, as evidenced by the Endothelin‐1 (ET‐1) and intercellular adhesion molecule‐1 (ICAM‐1) elevation at the highest dose group. DBDPE‐induced inflammation was characterised by the upregulation of key inflammatory mediators detected in the serum, including interleukin‐1β (IL‐1β), IL‐6 and tumour necrosis factor alpha (TNFα) at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day. In the heart, superoxide dismutase (SOD) and glutathione peroxidase (GSH‐Px) activities were significantly increased at all doses and malondialdehyde (MDA) content was significantly increased at the two highest doses. The authors concluded that DBDPE could induce oxidative stress, inflammation and eventually lead to endothelial dysfunction and cardiovascular injury (Jing et al., 2019). The NOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day. Zheng et al. (2021) reported also morphological damage in the abdominal aortas and ultrastructural changes at the two DBDPE highest doses.

−The two highest DBDPE doses induced cardiomyocyte injury and fibrosis. Cellular apoptosis increased also in these groups. Ultrastructural observation of cardiomyocytes revealed that DBDPE could induce mitochondria damage and dissolution (Gao et al., 2022).

The two highest DBDPE doses induced cardiomyocyte injury and fibrosis. Cellular apoptosis increased also in these groups. Ultrastructural observation of cardiomyocytes revealed that DBDPE could induce mitochondria damage and dissolution (Gao et al., 2022).

−Decreased serum FT3 at ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day and increased TSH and thyrotropin‐releasing hormone (TRH) were observed at 500 mg/kg bw per day; however, there was no effect on TT4, TT3 and FT4 (Wang, Chen, et al., 2019). DBDPE exposure caused significant changes in histological structure of the thyroid gland: smaller size of follicular cavities and disorder of the array of thyroid follicular epithelial cells. Other dose‐related changes were also observed: increased height of the thyroid follicular epithelial cells, swollen and vacuolated follicular cells, oedema in follicular epithelial cells, infiltration of mast cells in the follicular stroma, exfoliated epithelial cells and decreased average colloid area. In addition, there were changes in ultrastructure of the thyroid gland (e.g. atrophy of the rough endoplasmic reticulum and vacuolation of the mitochondria). No morphological changes were observed in the pituitary gland and hypothalamus. Oxidative damages of the thyroid gland (dose‐related decreased SOD and increased MDA content) were also observed. The authors concluded that DBDPE exposure could disrupt thyroid function and the underlying mechanism was oxidative stress and perturbations of the hypothalamic–pituitary–thyroid (HPT) axis (Wang, Chen, et al., 2019). The LOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day based on dilatation of the rough endoplasmic reticulum, swelling of mitochondria and disappearance of mitochondrial crests.

Decreased serum FT3 at ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day and increased TSH and thyrotropin‐releasing hormone (TRH) were observed at 500 mg/kg bw per day; however, there was no effect on TT4, TT3 and FT4 (Wang, Chen, et al., 2019). DBDPE exposure caused significant changes in histological structure of the thyroid gland: smaller size of follicular cavities and disorder of the array of thyroid follicular epithelial cells. Other dose‐related changes were also observed: increased height of the thyroid follicular epithelial cells, swollen and vacuolated follicular cells, oedema in follicular epithelial cells, infiltration of mast cells in the follicular stroma, exfoliated epithelial cells and decreased average colloid area. In addition, there were changes in ultrastructure of the thyroid gland (e.g. atrophy of the rough endoplasmic reticulum and vacuolation of the mitochondria). The LOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day based on dilatation of the rough endoplasmic reticulum, swelling of mitochondria and disappearance of mitochondrial crests.

− DBDPE had no effect on liver (absolute or relative) weight but caused liver morphological changes (feathery necrosis at ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day and irregular arrangement of hepatic cords, and inflammatory cell infiltration at the two highest doses) (Sun et al., 2020). It induced oxidative stress (increases in MDA content and decreases in SOD activity at high dose), increased γ‐glutamyl transferase (50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) and glucose levels (500 mg/kg bw per day) in serum (Sun et al., 2020). The LOAEL was 50 mg/kg bw per day.

DBDPE had no effect on liver (absolute or relative) weight but caused liver morphological changes (feathery necrosis at ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day and irregular arrangement of hepatic cords, and inflammatory cell infiltration at the two highest doses) (Sun et al., 2020). It induced oxidative stress (increases in MDA content and decreases in SOD activity at high dose), increased γ‐glutamyl transferase (50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) and glucose levels (500 mg/kg bw per day) in serum (Sun et al., 2020). The LOAEL was 50 mg/kg bw per day.

−Exposure to DBDPE resulted in histopathological changes in the testis (significant seminiferous epithelium deletion) and decreased sperm number at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, decreased motility at 5, 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, and increased sperm malformation rates at 500 mg/kg bw per day (Li et al., 2021). Oxidative stress was observed in the testis with significant increases in MDA content and significant decreases in T‐SOD activity at 500 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE exposure led to significant increase of TUNEL‐positive cells in the 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day groups indicating testicular apoptosis (Li et al., 2021).

Exposure to DBDPE resulted in histopathological changes in the testis (significant seminiferous epithelium deletion) and decreased sperm number at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, decreased motility at 5, 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day, and increased sperm malformation rates at 500 mg/kg bw per day (Li et al., 2021). Oxidative stress was observed in the testis with significant increases in MDA content and significant decreases in T‐SOD activity at 500 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE exposure led to significant increase of TUNEL‐positive cells in the 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day groups indicating testicular apoptosis (Li et al., 2021).

− DBDPE caused histopathological changes in the testis (morphological changes and expanded and vacuolised mitochondria and fractured or vanished mitochondrial cristae at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) (Xue et al., 2022). It reduced also sperm quantity (at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) and motility (at all doses), and raised the malformation rate (at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day). DBDPE had an effect on the clinical chemistry parameters in rats: increased glucose, LDL‐C and TC levels. Glucose levels were significantly negatively correlated with sperm motility and sperm concentration, and were significantly positively correlated with sperm malformation rate. Furthermore, LDL‐C levels showed a significant negative correlation with sperm motility and sperm concentration and a significant positive correlation with sperm malformation rate. Immunofluorescence analysis showed that the DNA damage marker γ‐H2AX increased significantly in rat testicular cells after exposure for 28 days to 500 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Xue et al., 2022). The NOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day.

DBDPE caused histopathological changes in the testis (morphological changes and expanded and vacuolised mitochondria and fractured or vanished mitochondrial cristae at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) (Xue et al., 2022). It reduced also sperm quantity (at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day) and motility (at all doses), and raised the malformation rate (at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day). DBDPE had an effect on the clinical chemistry parameters in rats: increased glucose, LDL‐C and TC levels. Glucose levels were significantly negatively correlated with sperm motility and sperm concentration, and were significantly positively correlated with sperm malformation rate. Furthermore, LDL‐C levels showed a significant negative correlation with sperm motility and sperm concentration and a significant positive correlation with sperm malformation rate. Immunofluorescence analysis showed that the DNA damage marker γ‐H2AX increased significantly in rat testicular cells after exposure for 28 days to 500 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Xue et al., 2022). The NOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day.

Male and female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE (purity 96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) by gavage for 28 days (OECD TG 407) to 0 (corn oil), 125, 400 and 1250 mg/kg bw per day. Exposure was followed by a 14‐day withdrawal in subsets of the control and high‐dose groups only. The NOAEL was 1250 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)).

Male and female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE (purity 96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% Dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) by gavage for 90 days (OECD TG 408) to 0 (corn oil), 100, 320 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. At the end of the 90‐day treatment period all animals in the low and mid dose groups and 10 randomly selected rats/sex in the control and high‐dose groups were sacrificed. The remaining rats in the control and high‐dose groups were continued untreated for a 28‐day recovery period prior to sacrifice. Mean liver weights were increased at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Low‐grade microscopic liver changes were observed in male rats. The liver changes were characterised by minimal to slight hepatocellular vacuolation (high‐dose males) and minimal to slight centrilobular hepatocytomegaly (high, and possibly mid dose, males), and were considered an adaptive response. No changes were found in the livers of female rats. The liver changes resolved after a 28‐day recovery period. No effects were observed on the reproductive organs. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg bw per day (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)).

Male C57BL/6J mice were exposed by gavage to 0, 5 or 50 mg DBDPE (purity NR)/kg bw per day for 7 weeks (Qi et al., 2025). The bodyweights of the mice in the 50 mg/kg bw per day group were slightly lower. At both doses, there were statically significant increases in relative testicular weight. DBDPE decreased the sex hormone levels (serum and intratesticular testosterone et both doses, LH and FSH at 50 mg/kg bw per day) and sperm count. The steroidogenic acute regulatory protein (StAR) decreased in a dose‐dependent manner. DBDPE impaired testicular morphology (testicular damage characterised most notably by a reduction in cell layers, disorganisation of the germinal epithelium and pronounced loss of germ cells, especially at 50 mg/kg bw per day). DBDPE dose‐dependently increased the sperm malformation rate. Oxidative stress (increase of MDA content and decrease of SOD levels in testicular homogenates) and apoptosis maybe potential target pathways of DBDPE (Qi et al., 2025).

Male ApoE−/− mice were exposed by gavage to 0 (corn oil) or 100 mg DBDPE (98% purity)/kg bw per day for 6 weeks. DBDPE promoted atherosclerosis in ApoE−/− mice. The number of lesions in the aortic roots increased; the plaques formed in the lumen increased, and the plaque area increased significantly. Exposure significantly increased the lipid content in the aortic root. No significant change was observed in the area of aortic fibrosis in the DBDPE group. ROS levels in plaques in the aortic roots of ApoE−/− mice exposed to DBDPE significantly increased, suggesting that DBDPE caused oxidative stress in the aorta (Li et al., 2025).

Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE (purity NR) by gavage at 0, 5, 50 or 500 mg/kg bw per day for 8 weeks (Cui et al., 2025). It was shown that DBDPE exposure led to a dose‐related statistically significant increase in relative liver weight from the lowest dose. Five mg/kg bw per day caused mild tissue injury (mild loosening of hepatocyte arrangement was observed in certain regions) and metabolic disturbances. At 50 mg/kg bw per day, a substantial accumulation of lipid vacuoles was detected in the liver, indicative of steatosis. Exposure at 500 mg/kg bw per day resulted in prominent lipid vacuole formation within hepatocytes, also consistent with steatosis, accompanied by marked alterations in cellular morphology. In addition, inflammatory infiltration and ballooning degeneration were observed in some areas of the liver, suggesting significant metabolic injury. DBDPE exposure resulted in significant increases in total cholesterol (TC), triglycerides (TG) and LDL levels but significant decreases in HDL levels only at low dose levels. The LOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day.

Male and female Balb/C mice were exposed to DBDPE (purity 98.5%), by gavage for 30 days to 0 (corn oil), 5, 20, 100 and 200 mg/kg bw per day (Sun et al., 2018). There were no signs of toxicity and no significant treatment effect on body weight, or liver‐to‐body weight ratios. The levels of ALT and aspartate aminotransferase (AST) increased markedly in a dose‐dependent manner. Blood glucose levels were increased according to the dose and time of exposure. There was also an increase in TSH, T3 and fT3 at 200 mg/kg bw per day. UDPGT, PROD and EROD activities were increased significantly at ≥ 100 mg/kg bw per day. Histopathologic liver changes were characterised by hepatocyte hypertrophy and cytoplasmic vacuolisation (Sun et al., 2018). The LOAEL was 5 mg/kg bw per day based on the increased ALT serum level.

Reproductive/developmental toxicity

During the perinatal period (GD6‐PND21), pregnant ICR mice were exposed to DBDPE (purity 96%) by gavage to 0 and 100 μg/kg bw (Yan et al., 2018; REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)). After weaning, male offspring were fed on either a low‐fat diet or a high‐fat diet for 12 weeks. DBDPE increased the risk of obesity in mouse offspring and affected triglyceride synthesis, bile secretion, purine synthesis, mitochondrial function and glucose metabolism. Furthermore, the use of high‐fat diet feeding may further exacerbate these effects. The authors claimed that their results show that early‐life exposure to low doses of DBDPE may promote the development of metabolic dysfunction, which in turn can induce obesity (Yan et al., 2018; REACH dossier).

Pregnant ICR mice were exposed to DBDPE (purity NR) by gavage at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.5 and 5 mg/kg bw per day from GD1 to GD17 (Xie et al., 2025). Fetal size was measured on GD18. Exposure caused placental damage, resulting in fetal growth restriction (FGR). Fetal weight was decreased in the 0.5 and 5 mg/kg bw groups. Crown‐rump length decreased and the rate of FGR was markedly increased at the highest dose but not significantly at lower doses. A dose‐dependent reduction in placental weight following DBDPE exposure was observed (at the two highest doses). No significant difference was found in placental diameter, however, there was a notable decrease in the fetal‐to‐placental weight ratio (at 0.5 and 5 mg/kg bw per day). A significant reduction in the total placental area, as well as a decrease in both the placental spongiotrophoblast and labyrinth areas were observed at all doses. In humans, a reduced fetus‐to‐placental weight is a marker for a variety of adverse outcomes in the infants as well as of prognostic value predictive of later life health outcomes (Beaumont et al., 2023; Kinjo et al., 2024; Rivera‐Núñez et al., 2025). Additionally, the spongiotrophoblast‐to‐labyrinth area ratio was increased in exposed groups (statistically significant at 0.5 mg/kg bw per day). There was also a decrease in placental microvascular density in exposed mice (Xie et al., 2025). The LOAEL was 0.05 mg/kg bw per day.

Female Kunming mice were orally exposed to DBDPE (purity NR) for 30 days at 50 μg/kg bw per day (Shi, Xu, et al., 2022). DBDPE‐exposed female mice were mated with untreated male mice, and pre‐ and post‐implantation embryos were isolated. A loss of embryos (statistically significant decrease in the rate of 2‐cell and 8‐cell embryos and blastocysts formation) and a statistically significant lower litter size were noted in DBDPE‐exposed mice. Moreover, from GD14.5 onwards, the embryo length of DBDPE‐exposed mice was significantly reduced, indicating delayed fetal development.

In another study in the same mouse strain by the same authors, female Kunming mice were exposed to DBDPE (purity NR) by gavage for 14 days to 0, 5, 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day. After treatment, female mice were intraperitoneally injected with PMSG and 48 h later with HCG and then mated with sexually mature males (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022). DBDPE inhibited preimplantation embryo development in a dose‐dependent manner by affecting the circadian rhythm (from 50 μg/kg bw per day) and endogenous melatonin levels (decrease serum melatonin in serum from 5 μg/kg bw per day in 2‐cell embryo and 8‐cell embryo). The highest dose significantly reduced the rate of 2‐cell embryos. The ratio of the 4‐cell embryo, 8‐cell embryo and morula dramatically decreased at 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day and even the lowest dose resulted in a significant decrease in the rate of blastocyst formation. However, DBDPE exposure did not affect the size of the blastocyst. It was shown that exogenous melatonin restored DBDPE‐induced developmental inhibition and apoptosis of preimplantation embryos. It mitigated DBDPE‐induced mitochondrial dysfunction and mechanical changes in blastocysts and consequently ameliorated the adverse reproductive outcomes of DBDPE‐exposed blastocysts. The LOAEL was 5 μg/kg bw per day. The results related to developmental neurotoxicity outcomes in these two studies are reported below.

DBDPE (purity NR) was administered by gavage at doses of 0, 100, 320 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day in corn oil to female Crl:CD (SD) rats (F0 dams) from GD6 through lactation day 21. There were no test substance‐related effects observed in this study consistent with systemic toxicity. There was no evidence of maternal toxicity at any dose level. There were no effects on reproductive performance or on litter or offspring developmental parameters. There were no effects on mean male and female pup body weight gains, on postweaning body weight or the onset of sexual maturation as measured by the age of attainment of vaginal opening or preputial separation. The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was 1000 mg/kg bw per day (the highest dose tested) (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)).

DBDPE (purity 96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was administered by gavage at doses of 0, 125, 400 and 1250 mg/kg bw per day in corn oil to female Sprague–Dawley rats from GD6 through lactation day 21 (OECD TG 414). No evidence of maternal or developmental toxicity or teratogenicity was observed in treated rats. The NOAEL was 1250 mg/kg bw per day (the highest dose tested) (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)).

DBDPE (purity 96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was administered by gavage at doses of 0, 125, 400 and 1250 mg/kg bw per day in methylcellulose to female rabbits from GD6 through GD18 (OECD TG414). No evidence of maternal toxicity, developmental toxicity or teratogenicity was observed in treated rabbits. The NOAEL was 1250 mg/kg bw per day (the highest dose tested) (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)).

DBDPE (purity NR) was tested in an extended one‐generation reproductive toxicity study with developmental neurotoxicity cohort (OECD TG443) (REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s)). Male and female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Parental males were exposed for 10 weeks before mating up to necropsy after litters were weaned. Parental females were exposed for 10 weeks before mating, up to 2 weeks during mating, during resulting pregnancies, up to necropsy on day 23 of lactation. F1 males and females were exposed from weaning until termination of respective cohort.

−Cohort 1A: Reproductive/developmental toxicity testing ‐ treated from weaning to approximately 13 weeks of age.

Cohort 1A: Reproductive/developmental toxicity testing ‐ treated from weaning to approximately 13 weeks of age.

−Cohort 1B: Spare cohort ‐ treated from weaning to approximately 14 weeks of age.

Cohort 1B: Spare cohort ‐ treated from weaning to approximately 14 weeks of age.

−Cohort 1C: Additional cohort for assessment of sexual maturation ‐ treated from weaning to completion of sexual maturation (approximately seven to 8 weeks of age).

Cohort 1C: Additional cohort for assessment of sexual maturation ‐ treated from weaning to completion of sexual maturation (approximately seven to 8 weeks of age).

Two additional cohorts were included to assess the developmental neurotoxicity of DBDPE due to the uncertainties of the first neurotoxicity study reported in the developmental toxicity/teratogenicity section of the REACH dossier: Cohort 2A: Developmental neurotoxicity testing (neurobehavioural testing followed by neuro‐histopathology assessment as adults) – treated from weaning up to approximately day 75 of age (see below). Cohort 2B: Developmental neurotoxicity testing – no direct treatment, assigned to neuro‐histopathology assessment at weaning (see below). Administration of DBDPE at dose levels up to and including 1000 mg/kg bw per day was well tolerated with no treatment‐related mortality, no adverse effects on general condition, body weight, food consumption, thyroid hormones, sperm parameters or macropathology in either the F0 or F1 generation. Oestrous cycle, mating performance, fertility, gestation length and gestation index were unaffected by treatment. For F1 litters, the general condition of offspring, litter size, offspring survival, sex ratio and development were unaffected by parental treatment at dose levels up to and including 1000 mg/kg bw per day. In addition, sexual maturation (selected F1), spleen immunophenotyping (F1 Cohort 1A) and ovarian follicle counts (F1 Cohort 1A) were unaffected at all dose levels. There was no adverse effect on anogenital distance. Minor non‐adverse effects were restricted to F0 and F1 male animals that showed a higher incidence of minimal or slight macrovesicular/microvesicular fatty change in the liver at all dose levels; this pathology correlated with the increased body weight relative liver weight for F0 males that received 1000 mg/kg bw per day. The authors concluded that the NOAEL for both systemic toxicity and reproductive/developmental toxicity is 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

Developmental neurotoxicity

Several studies were identified and details are reported in Appendix C (Table C.2).

In the same study by Shi, Xu, et al. (2022) reported above in which female Kunming mice were orally exposed to DBDPE (purity NR) at 50 μg/kg per day (gavage) for 30 days, a set of females were given hormones (Pregnant mare's serum gonadotropin (PMSG) and Human chorionic gonadotropin (HCG)) before being mated with untreated males and 1‐, 2‐ and 8‐cell embryos collected not later than 56 h after HCG injection. Blastocytes were collected 94 h after HCG and fetuses taken on GD11.5, GD14.5 and GD18.5. The other set of females was maintained to have pups that were tested for their behavioural performances at 6 weeks of age. At 6 weeks of age, DBDPE‐exposed mice exhibited a higher level of activity in the open‐field whereas anxiety‐related behaviours measured in the open‐field and the light–dark box were unaffected. Results obtained from the two objects recognition test showed that DBDPE exposure resulted in memory and cognitive impairments in both male and female mice. Social deficits were observed only in DBDPE‐exposed females assessed for their social abilities (social memory and novelty) in the three‐chamber test. All these results suggest the developmental toxicity and the neurotoxicity of a preconception exposure to DBDPE.

In the same study of Shi, Qiu, et al. (2022), female Kunming mice were exposed by gavage to DBDPE (purity NR) at 0, 5, 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day for 14 days and were then tested for their circadian rhythm using a voluntary wheel running test. This test consisted in keeping mice individually in cages within light‐tight chambers. The animals were first exposed to 12:12‐h light/dark cycle for 4 days and exposed to a complete 24 h dark period for the 4 following days to measure their own endogenous circadian rhythm after 14 days of exposure (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022). Clock gene expression was studied in the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the same animals before and during the test. Results suggested that DBDPE exposure led to impaired circadian rhythm disorders at doses of 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day.

In the study reported in the developmental toxicity/teratogenicity section of the REACH dossier,39 DBDPE (purity NR) was administered by gavage at doses of 0, 100, 320 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day to female Crl:CD (SD) rats from GD6 to PND21. The animals were divided into four subsets with two of them used for the behavioural testing of the offspring. Detailed clinical observations, open‐field observations and hindlimb and forelimb grip strength were conducted on subset A at PND21, 35, 45 and 60. Motor activity of one pup/sex/litter of the same subset was evaluated on PND13, 17 21 and 61 using a SmartFrame Cage Rack system with two‐dimensional infrared photobeam grid during a session of 60 min.

One pup/sex/litter of the same subset was tested on PND24 and PND63 for their auditory startle response (ASR). Prepulse inhibition and ASR habituation were tested at both timepoints. This test is a conditioning paradigm that allows to test the reflexive contraction of skeletal muscles in response to an abrupt acoustic stimulus, named ASR. The startle stimulus used for each trial was 115 ± 5 dB mixed‐frequency noise burst stimulus approximately 40 milliseconds (ms) in duration. ASR magnitude is reduced with the repetition of the same stimulus, or the presentation of a weaker acoustic or non‐acoustic stimulus before the ASR‐inducing stimulus, a phenomenon known as prepulse inhibition. Trials including prepulse stimuli 4, 8 and 12 dB above the background noise (70 dB) were applied in a sequence of 50 trials ending with the 115 dB stimulus alone.

Learning and memory performances were assessed using a passive avoidance paradigm. One pup/sex/litter from subset B was tested at PND23 and 30, and from subset A at PND64 and 71. The passive avoidance test is designed to evaluate the conditioning learning and memory abilities of the animals. It consists of a two‐chamber apparatus with one being a dark compartment in which they may receive an electric shock. The test assesses the ability of the animal to learn to avoid a painful stimulus with the amount of time that it stays in the lighted chamber being indicative of its learning performances.

No effects of DBDPE on detailed clinical observations (DCOs), motor activity, auditory startle response, and learning and memory performances were reported in both sexes at any dose level at any age.

Subsets A and C were used for necropsy, including brain morphometric measurements. The results showed some test substance‐related morphometric changes in brains of male rats on PND22 and PND72 at 100, 320 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. There were lower group mean morphometric brain measurements in the cortex (Level 1), hippocampus (Level 3) and cerebellum (Level 5) on PND22 and PND72. The morphometric changes were not associated with statistically significant changes in brain weight or macroscopic brain measurements, although there was a slight non‐significant decrement of group mean brain weight in the highest dose group of males at PND22. There were no microscopic changes in brain, spinal cord, nerve roots or ganglia. There were no test substance‐related changes in female rats.

Peer review by two independent pathologists suggested that these were considered ambiguous findings and an artefact of the brain section and measuring method could not be excluded. Thus, the authors of the study concluded that a NOAEL for developmental neurotoxicity could not be determined with sufficient certainty for males (REACH dossier).

Two additional cohorts were added in the extended one‐generation reproductive toxicity study described above (REACH dossier39) and studied for developmental neurotoxicity of DBDPE. Behaviour of the animals of the cohort 2A was assessed using a functional observational battery (FOB) testing at PND70. The FOB is a non‐invasive procedure investigating various behavioural and activity‐related parameters with the objective to detect gross functional deficits in rodents. Auditory startle response habituation, reactivity investigations and motor activity (ambulatory and rearing) were unaffected by treatment. There were no in cage or in hand observations that could be related to treatment, and arena observations were limited to a slight increase in the incidence of females with elevated/slightly abnormal gait in the treated groups but with no evidence for a dose response. All differences in brain morphometry parameters were considered by the study authors to be incidental as they were inconsistent between sexes, inconsistent between cohorts, the magnitude was considered small, had no histopathological correlates and/or had no significant mean brain weight differences.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, three studies have been reported in the REACH dossier32, as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel had no access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

DBDPE (96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was negative in a reverse mutation assay in S. Typhimurium TA 1538, TA 1535, TA 1537, TA 98, TA 100 and E. coli WP2 uvrA (OECD TG471) in presence and absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver microsomes).

DBDPE (96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was negative in a reverse mutation assay in S. Typhimurium TA 1538, TA 1535, TA 1537, TA 98, TA 100 and E. coli WP2 uvrA (OECD TG471) in presence and absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver microsomes).

DBDPE (96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was negative in an in vitro chromosomal aberration test in Chinese hamster lung fibroblasts (V79) (OECD TG473) in presence and absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver microsomes).

DBDPE (96.3% DBDPE, 3.6% dodecabromodiphenyl ethane) was negative in an in vitro chromosomal aberration test in Chinese hamster lung fibroblasts (V79) (OECD TG473) in presence and absence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐induced rat liver microsomes).

DBDPE (purity 94%–99%; contains nonabromodiphenyl oxide and octabromodiphenyl oxide as impurities) was negative in an in vitro gene mutation assay in L5178Y/TK+/‐ mouse lymphoma cells with and without metabolic activation (S9‐mix of Aroclor 1254‐induced male F344/N rats).

DBDPE (purity 94%–99%; contains nonabromodiphenyl oxide and octabromodiphenyl oxide as impurities) was negative in an in vitro gene mutation assay in L5178Y/TK+/‐ mouse lymphoma cells with and without metabolic activation (S9‐mix of Aroclor 1254‐induced male F344/N rats).

Summary of toxicity in experimental animals

Morphological and ultrastructural changes were observed in the heart and abdominal aorta of rats exposed by gavage for 28 days to doses ≥ 50 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Gao et al., 2022; Jing et al., 2019; Zheng et al., 2021). Cardiomyocyte injury and fibrosis were also observed as well as apoptosis and mitochondria damage.

DBDPE promoted atherosclerosis in ApoE−/− mice exposed by gavage to 100 mg/kg bw per day for 6 weeks. It was shown that DBDPE caused oxidative stress in the aorta (Li et al., 2025).

DBDPE induced hepatic lipid metabolism disorders in rats exposed by gavage. Steatosis, accompanied by marked alterations in cellular morphology were observed. In addition, there were inflammatory infiltration and ballooning degeneration in some areas of the liver, suggesting significant metabolic injury. DBDPE exposure resulted in significant increases in TC, TG and LDL levels (Cui et al., 2025).

Liver toxicity (histopathological and hepatic enzymes changes) was observed in mice exposed for 30 days to ≥ 5 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Sun et al., 2018) or in rats exposed for 28 days to 50 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Sun et al., 2020). In mice, DBDPE may induce drug‐metabolising enzymes, interfere with thyroid hormone levels and could also affect the glucose metabolism homeostasis.

Changes in histological structure and ultrastructure of the thyroid gland and in thyroid hormone levels were noted in rats exposed to ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day DBDPE by gavage for 28 days (Wang, Chen, et al., 2019). DBDPE exposure could disrupt thyroid function through oxidative stress and perturbations of HPT axis.

Twenty‐eight‐day gavage exposure of male rats to DBDPE result in testicular damage in physiological structure (≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day), decreased sperm number (≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day) and motility (≥ 5 mg/kg bw per day) and increased sperm malformation rates (500 mg/kg bw per day) (Li et al., 2021; Xue et al., 2022).

Exposure of male mice to DBDPE by gavage at 5 or 50 mg/kg bw per day for 7 weeks increased the relative testicular weight. DBDPE decreased the sex hormone levels and sperm count. DBDPE impaired testicular morphology (testicular damage characterised most notably by a reduction in cell layers, disorganisation of the germinal epithelium and pronounced loss of germ cells, especially at 50 mg/kg bw per day) and dose‐dependently increased the sperm malformation rate. Oxidative stress and apoptosis are potential target pathways of DBDPE (Qi et al., 2025).

Exposure of ICR mice to DBDPE (from 0.05 mg/kg bw per day) during gestation caused placental damage, resulting in fetal growth restriction (FGR from 0.5 mg/kg bw per day) (Xie et al., 2025).

DBDPE oral exposure affected oocyte development in mice (Shi & Feng, 2021; Shi, Qiu, et al., 2021; Yan et al., 2022). Asymmetric division of oocytes was markedly impaired (Shi & Feng, 2021). DBDPE exposure in utero caused also low birth weight in cubs and then the cubs grow rapidly during lactation. A loss of embryos and a significant lower litter size were noted in female Kunming mice exposed to 50 μg DBDPE/kg bw per day for 30 days before mating. The embryo length of DBDPE‐exposed mice was also significantly reduced, indicating delayed fetal development (Shi, Xu, et al., 2022). In addition, DBDPE (5–500 μg/kg bw per day) inhibited preimplantation embryo development in a dose‐dependent manner by affecting the circadian rhythm and endogenous melatonin levels (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022) (see Section 3.3.1.1.6 on mode of action).

Brain and behavioural toxicity of DBDPE related to learning and memory, spontaneous activity and social behaviour were reported in Kunming mice gestationally exposed at 50 μg/kg bw per day for 30 days (Shi, Xu, et al., 2022) whereas impaired circadian rhythm disorders were observed at doses of 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day for 14 days (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022). Two developmental neurotoxicity studies reported in the REACH dossier did not show any effects of DBDPE on learning memory, motor activity, auditory startle and general behaviour assessed through a functional observational battery in rats early exposed to DBDPE (GD6‐PND21) at doses of 100, 320 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

These contradictory results obtained at very different doses and with different rodent species from one study to another raise the question of the neurotoxicity of DBDPE, which has to be investigated in further studies.

No evidence of maternal toxicity, developmental toxicity or teratogenicity was noted in rats and rabbits exposed during gestation by gavage up to 1250 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Hardy et al., 2010; REACH dossier).

In an extended one‐generation reproductive toxicity in rats reported in the REACH dossier, administration of DBDPE at dose levels up to and including 1000 mg/kg bw per day did not induce adverse effects in the parental generation nor in the offspring. The NOAEL for both systemic toxicity and reproductive/developmental toxicity is 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

No long‐term or carcinogenicity study was identified.

According to the data reported in the REACH dossier, DBDPE was not mutagenic in bacteria or in mammalian cells and did not induce chromosomal aberrations in Chinese hamster lung cells. Based on this information, the CONTAM Panel concluded that DBDPE is not genotoxic.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on DBDPE as retrieved by the previous Opinion (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBDPE with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability (see Section 2.2).

Observations in humans

In the previous Opinion, no human studies on DBDPE were reported. Since then, six publications were identified. In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on DBDPE, is shown.

Five of the included publications pertained to populations in China (two were from the same study population) and one was located in the USA. Exposure was assessed in serum in four studies; the fifth study assessed exposure from indoor air and wristbands. Various endpoints were assessed including thyroid hormone profile (five studies), thyroid cancer (one study), serum lipids (one study), liver injury markers (one study) and changes in mitochondrial DNA copy number (one study).

In a study by Zhao et al. (2021), five of the BFRs assessed in the current Opinion (see Appendix F) were determined in 172 serum samples (118 males and 54 females) collected from non‐occupational residents of a major BFR‐producing region in North China. The age of the participants varied from 21 to 58 with an average of 36 years. All the five BFRs presented high detection frequencies (> 90%), and DBDPE was the most abundant one. Using a cross‐sectional study design, the relationship between the serum levels of the BFRs and a series of thyroid hormones, serum lipids and liver injury biomarkers, was evaluated. Results showed that a 10‐fold increment in the serum DBDPE level was associated with lower TT3 level after adjustment for confounders (−0.037 nmol/L) [95% CI: −0.070, −0.003]. DBDPE was not associated with any of the other biomarkers.

In another cross‐sectional study from the same region by Chen et al. (2019), a total of 302 blood samples were collected from two populations in the largest DBDPE manufacturing area located in North China: 133 occupationally exposed workers from a DBDPE manufacturing plant and 169 non‐ occupationally exposed residents from a nearby food processing plant. The levels of DBDPE and thyroid function parameters (TT4, FT4, TT3, FT3, TSH, TG‐Ab and TPO‐Ab) were measured in serum samples. Serum concentrations of DBDPE ranged from 3.148 to 54,360 ng/g lipid, with a geometric mean of 332.6 ng/g lipid. A 10‐fold higher DBDPE concentration was associated with 4.73 nmol/L higher TT4 [95% confidence interval (CI): 2.75, 6.71] and 0.046 nmol/L [95% CI: 0.012, 0.081] higher TT3, corresponding to approximately 4.73% [95% CI: 2.75%–6.71%] and 2.38% [95% CI: 0.62%–4.20%] higher concentrations, respectively. DBDPE in serum was also significantly and positively associated with the concentrations of TG‐Ab and TPO‐Ab.

In the same study population, Chen et al. (2022) also assessed the association between DBDPE and changes in mitochondrial DNA copy number in whole blood (in 131 workers and 161 non‐occupationally exposed residents). A 10‐fold increase in the serum BDBPE concentration was associated with a reduced DNA copy number [β = −0.063, 95% CI: −0.088 to −0.038], corresponding to a decrease of 6.91% [95% CI: −9.65% to −4.17%].

In the study by Guo et al. (2018) on the association with thyroid hormone levels in 10‐year‐old students living near a petrochemical complex in South China (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), DBDPE levels were not associated with the serum thyroid hormone levels. The sum of the 13 PBDE congeners and the sum of all eight new flame retardants were associated with higher TT3 levels. The authors concluded that this positive association was mainly due to the dominant contribution of BDE‐209.

In the study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), overall, and in sex‐stratified logistic regression models, DBDPE exhibited significantly higher ORs in the third and fourth quartiles in both male and female participants but confidence intervals were wide indicating imprecise effect estimates (OR males: 3rd quartile: 33.35 [95%CI: 4.45, 250.02], 4th quartile: 13.56 [95%CI: 2.30, 79.85]; OR females: 3rd quartile: 1.93 [95%CI 0.92, 4.03], 4th quartile: 4.13 [95%CI: 2.04, 8.34]). DBDPE was not associated with thyroid hormone levels in the control participants.

In the USA study by Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), DBDPE concentrations, measured in wristbands of 101 adult study participants, were not statistically significantly associated with thyroid hormone profiles.

In summary, the available epidemiological evidence on DBDPE pertained to a limited number of cross‐sectional studies in populations in China (n = 5) and the USA (n = 1), five of which were studies in adults and one was in children. Results for thyroid hormone levels are inconsistent across studies, with positive, inverse and null associations reported for TT3 in relation to serum DBDPE levels, as well as one study reporting increased TT4 levels. Further, one study reported association with higher odds of thyroid cancer, and one reported changes in mitochondrial DNA copy number. No statistically significant associations were observed for lipid levels, liver injury markers or other thyroid function markers.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

A number of studies to inform the MOA of DBDPE were identified in the scientific literature since the previous Opinion. An overview of those studies is given in Appendix G (Table G.2).

In summary, morphological and ultrastructural changes were observed in the heart and abdominal aorta after 28‐day gavage exposure of rats to DBDPE (Jing et al., 2019; Gao et al., 2022; Zheng et al., 2021, see above). These effects can be induced by oxidative stress and/or inflammation that can lead to endothelial dysfunction and cardiovascular injury. Cardiomyocyte injury and fibrosis were also observed as well as apoptosis and mitochondria damage.

Li et al. (2025) showed that exposure to DBDPE promotes the formation of atherosclerotic plaque in aortic roots of ApoE−/− mice by increasing macrophage recruitment and foam cell formation.

Liver toxicity (histopathological and hepatic enzyme changes) was observed in mice (Sun et al., 2018) or rats exposed for 30 or 28 days to DBDPE (Sun et al., 2020). DBDPE may induce drug‐metabolising enzymes including CYP450s and UDPGT, interfere with thyroid hormone levels mediated by AHR and CAR signalling pathways and could also affect the glucose metabolism homeostasis.

The impact of DBDPE on the liver in rats and L02 cells (immortalised hepatocyte line) was investigated. DBDPE exposure could induce lipid metabolism disorders, up‐regulate the expressions of Notch pathway genes and promote Arrestin domain containing protein 1‐ mediated microvesicles (ARMMs)‐derivation (Cui et al., 2025).

Changes in histological structure and ultrastructure of the thyroid gland and in thyroid hormone levels were noted in rats exposed by gavage to DBDPE for 28 days (Wang, Chen, et al., 2019). The authors noted that DBDPE exposure disrupted thyroid function associated with oxidative stress and perturbations of HPT axis.

Twenty‐eight‐day gavage exposure of male rats to DBDPE resulted in testicular damage in physiological structure, decreased sperm number and motility and increased sperm malformation rates (Li et al., 2021; Xue et al., 2022). By shortening telomere length and reducing telomerase activity DBDPE caused also cell senescence and apoptosis in testis of rat (Li et al., 2021). There was a dose‐related significant increase in apoptotic index in testis at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day. Consistently, immunofluorescence analysis showed that the DNA damage marker γ‐H2AX increased significantly in rat testicular cells after exposure for 28 days to 500 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day (Xue et al., 2022). Oxidative stress was demonstrated in the testis (Li et al., 2021). DBDPE‐induced glycolipid metabolism disorder and promoted apoptosis of spermatogenic cells through the mitochondrial‐mediated apoptosis pathway, leading to reproductive toxicity by decreasing sperm quantity and quality (Xue et al., 2022). No 1‐ or 2‐generation reproductive toxicity studies are available.

DBDPE oral exposure affected oocyte development in mice (Shi & Feng, 2021; Shi, Qiu, et al., 2021; Yan et al., 2022). Asymmetric division of oocytes was markedly impaired (Shi & Feng, 2021). DBDPE exposure in utero caused also low birth weight in pups and then the pups grow rapidly during lactation. In utero exposure to DBDPE affects the whole‐body metabolic profile of newborns; the pups exhibited accelerated energy utilisation and lipid accumulation for rapid growth and ultimately obesity (Yan et al., 2022). DBDPE exposure impaired mitochondrial function, causing oxidative damage, autophagy and apoptosis in oocytes (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2021).

DBDPE did not affect spindle rotation in oocytes after fertilisation, but led to a decrease of pronuclei (PN) in zygotes from Kunming mice (see above for description of the study). DBDPE was found to interfere with the self‐assembly of F‐actin in pronuclei of embryo from Kunming mice resulting in their size reduction in zygotes. In addition, DBDPE reduced the expression of the Zscan4c transcription factor involved in maintaining genome stability during embryogenesis. This early developmental instability may lead to metabolic and structural defects observed in later life. The authors noted that these data indicated that DBDPE exposure might affect embryonic development and result later in behavioural impairments (learning and memory, spontaneous activity, social behaviour) (Shi, Xu, et al., 2022).

DBDPE (5, 50, 500 μg/kg bw per day) exposure‐induced circadian rhythm disorder in adult female Kunming mice exposed by gavage for 14 days. It decreased also endogenous melatonin levels during pregnancy, which impaired preimplantation embryo development. DBDPE exposure impaired embryos by inducing oxidative stress, abnormal DNA methylation, mitochondrial dysfunction and mechanical property changes. Finally, DBDPE‐induced embryo damage led to adverse reproductive outcomes (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022).

To investigate the mechanisms of reproductive toxicity of DBDPE, experiments using the mouse premeiotic germ cell line GC‐2 were performed. Results of transcriptomics from GC‐2 cells indicated that DBDPE lead to changes in oxidative stress, apoptosis, inflammation, proliferation and differentiation. Furthermore, DBDPE exposure inhibited the Kelch‐like ECH‐associated protein1‐NF‐E2‐related factor2 (Keap1‐Nrf2) signalling pathway (Qi et al., 2025).

Exposure of ICR mice to DBDPE during gestation caused placental damage, resulting in fetal growth restriction (FGR) (Xie et al., 2025). Mechanistically, the mitochondrial dysfunction induced by gestational DBDPE exposure initiates oxeiptosis (a cell death mechanism, which involves reactive oxygen species (ROS)‐dependent activation of Keap1‐Phosphoglycerate mutase family member 5 (PGAM5)‐Apoptosis‐inducing factor mitochondria associated 1 (AIFM1), referred to as the (Keap1‐PGAM5‐AIFM1)) in placental trophoblasts, exacerbating placental injury and ultimately leading to FGR.

Neurotoxicity of DBDPE has been suggested in vitro through the exposure of human neuroblastoma SK‐N‐SH cells (Dong, Wang, Wang, et al., 2023; Dong, Wang, Zhang, et al., 2023). Results showed the ability of DBDPE to induce cell apoptosis through the initiation of key cellular events such as mitochondrial dysfunction, ROS production, DNA damage through caspase‐dependent and ‐independent pathways. Neurotoxicity of DBDPE was assessed also in zebrafish showing a dose‐related increase in the locomotor activity of larvae exposed to DBDPE (0, 3, 30, 300 μg/L) during the first 5 days after fertilisation (Li et al., 2024). The exposure of zebrafish embryos over the 120 h postfertilisation showed significant increases in the locomotor activity during the dark–light transition period, and the whole‐body contents of various neurotransmitters like glutamate, epinephrine, norepinephrine and different metabolic changes suggesting a mitochondrial dysfunction as a potential mechanism for DBDPE neurotoxicity (Yang, Zhu, et al., 2023). The hyperactivity observed has been related also to the disruption of the cholinergic neurotransmission system with ChAT as a potential target for neurotoxicity of DBDPE (Hua et al., 2022). A transgenerational study was conducted to assess the brain (protein and neurotransmitter contents, global DNA methylation and specific gene expression) and behaviour (sociability and recognition) toxicity of DBDPE in three successive generations (Sun et al., 2023). The results showed some specific brain and behaviour alterations in the female offspring whereas the males remained quite unaffected, suggesting the ability of DBDPE exposure at environmentally relevant doses to induce transgenerational neurotoxicity in zebrafish.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Based on the information reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel concluded that DBDPE is not genotoxic. No long‐term or carcinogenicity studies were identified.

Toxicological effects (histopathological, enzymatic or hormonal effects) were observed in the heart, abdominal aorta, liver or thyroid in some sub‐acute toxicity studies in rats at doses ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day. In most of the sub‐acute and sub‐chronic studies performed according to the OECD test guidelines and reported in the REACH dossier, no effect was observed up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day (the highest dose tested).

No evidence of maternal toxicity, developmental toxicity or teratogenicity was noted in rats and rabbits exposed during gestation by gavage up to 1250 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day and DBDPE up to and including 1000 mg/kg bw per day did not induce adverse effects in the parental generation nor in the offspring in an extended one‐generation reproductive toxicity in rats. However, testicular damage was observed in rats exposed for 28 days to ≥ 50 mg/kg bw per day and exposure of ICR mice to DBDPE (from 0.05 mg/kg bw per day) during gestation caused placental damage, resulting in fetal growth restriction (FGR from 0.5 mg/kg bw per day).

Two studies were performed in female Kunming mice exposed to low doses of DBDPE (5–500 μg/kg bw per day and showed effects on embryos (loss of embryos, decrease in litter size and reduction in embryo length) indicating delayed fetal development (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022; Shi, Xu, et al., 2022). DBDPE inhibited also preimplantation embryo development in a dose‐dependent manner by affecting the circadian rhythm and endogenous melatonin levels (see Appendix C, Table C.1).

Brain and behavioural toxicity of DBDPE related to learning and memory, spontaneous activity and social behaviour were reported in Kunming mice gestationally exposed at 50 μg/kg bw per day for 30 days (Shi, Xu, et al., 2022).

It is to be noted that two developmental neurotoxicity studies and an EOGRTS (REACH dossier) did not show any effect of DBDPE on learning memory, motor activity, auditory startle and general behaviour assessed through a functional observational battery in rats early exposed to DBDPE at doses up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

The CONTAM Panel noted the different effect levels reported among these studies, that could be related to different rodent species, different study designs, and using different tests which explored different aspects of neurobehaviour (see details in Appendix C, Table C.2). Therefore, it can be concluded that the results of the studies are not contradictory, and all evidence was included for hazard characterisation.

The CONTAM Panel selected the study by Shi, Qiu, et al. (2022) as the critical study. Critical effects were the decrease in the rate of blastocyst formation and the increase in total wheel running activity in Kunming mice exposed during gestation to DBDPE. The decrease in the rate of blastocyst formation was the most sensitive effect. It was dose‐related and observed from the lowest dose; while the effects on the other embryo stages appeared only at the two highest doses. Likewise, the effect in wheel running activity was only significant at the two highest doses.

The CONTAM Panel performed BMD modelling according to the 2022 EFSA Guidance on the use of the BMD approach in risk assessment (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022). The Panel selected a benchmark response (BMR) of 10% for the neurodevelopmental effects, considering the high variability typically observed in neurobehavioural endpoints. For the rate of blastocyst formation, the Panel selected a BMR of 10%. This approach is consistent with that applied in the EFSA CONTAM Panel PBDEs Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2024a). The results of the BMD modelling for the critical study in Kunming mice (Shi, Qiu, et al., 2022) are summarised in Table 20. Details of the BMD analyses, including the individual reports of the modelling are shown in Annex D.

The lowest BMDL10 for DBDPE was 1.07 μg/kg bw per day, based on the increase of total wheel running activity (LD). The CONTAM Panel selected this lowest BMDL10 as the Reference Point. Although the decrease in the rate of blastocyst formation yielded a lower BMDL, the BMD models did not provide an adequate fit to the data, resulting in a credible interval too wide to be considered reliable (see Table 20 and BMD reports in Annex D).

The CONTAM Panel concluded that, due to the limitations and uncertainties in the current database, the establishment of a health‐based guidance value for DBDPE was not appropriate. Instead, the MOE approach was used for risk characterisation (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2025).

Usually, an MOE of 100, covering variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans (factor 4 × 2.5 = 10) and within the human population (factor 3.2 × 3.2 = 10), is considered sufficient to conclude that exposure does not raise a health concern.

According to the EFSA Scientific Committee Guidance on selected default values, an additional factor can be considered in case of deficiencies in the database on a case‐by‐case basis. A default value has not been proposed, as it will be directly dependent on the data set available (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). The WHO/IPCS (1994, 1999) recommended a factor of 3 or 5 if there are minor deficiencies in the database and a factor of 10 if there are major deficiencies in the database. The CONTAM Panel noted that the limitations in the database for DBDPE are the lack of long‐term toxicity studies, while there are several studies on repeated dose toxicity, developmental and reproductive toxicity, and neurodevelopmental toxicity. It was noted that DBDPE is not genotoxic, and the Reference Point comes from a study showing mild effects on a sensitive endpoint, at much lower doses than those at which effects were seen in other studies. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel considered an additional factor of 3 to be sufficient for deficiencies in the database.

Overall, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 300 (100 for inter and intra‐species variability, and 3 for the gaps in the toxicological database) do not raise a health concern.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Occurrence data of DBDPE in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1). Due to the limited number of quantified results (n = 3), the CONTAM Panel considered these data insufficient to perform any exposure estimation.

In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of DBDPE and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2.

One study was identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to DBDPE. In UK, Ma et al. (2023) estimated the average and high‐end daily dietary intake for a number of BFRs, including DBDPE, for all population groups. Concentrations of BFRs in UK food items were obtained from a total of 108 samples collected between 2020 and 2021 from three supermarkets. Consumption data for UK citizens of all age groups were obtained from the National Diet and Nutrition Survey (2008–2019). Average dietary exposure to DBDPE was calculated in the range of 0.066–0.24 ng/kg bw per day, while the high‐end estimations, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate +2 standard deviations, were estimated between 0.39 and 1.5 ng/kg bw per day across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old).

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature (Tao et al., 2017; Wemken et al., 2020, see Section 3.3.1.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the only reported mean concentration in human milk samples from Ireland of 0.74 ng/g lipid (Wemken et al., 2020), resulted in an estimate of 3.4 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) did not report on mean levels.

For the exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption none of the studies identified reported P95 concentrations, only maximum concentrations. Thus no estimation of exposure was performed.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from one European country collected about a decade ago, and with low detection frequencies. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data on DBDPE in food were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the EU population to be made. Limited data obtained from the scientific literature indicated that average dietary exposure from food in the UK was up to 0.24 ng/kg bw per day and high‐end estimates were up to 1.5 ng/kg bw per day. The selected Reference Point for DBDPE, based on the increase of total wheel running activity, is 1.07 μg/kg bw per day, i.e. 1070 ng/kg bw per day. This MOE of about 700 does not raise a health concern, recognising the limitations in the exposure data, but also the low Reference Point derived from a study in which effects were observed at much lower doses than in other studies (μg/kg bw per day compared to mg/kg bw per day).

Uncertainty analysis

The uncertainty analysis was performed using the methods described in Section 2.8. Identified sources of uncertainty affecting the assessment are documented in Table 21.
