Uncertainty analysis: Genotoxicity

Uncertainties affecting the evidence on the genotoxic potential of DBDPE (see Section 3.3.1.1) were assessed to have low impact on the assessment (Table 21). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data (REACH dossier), but the in vitro tests were all negative and covered every genetic endpoint.

Based on the overall evidence, and taking into account the associated uncertainties (Table 21), and following an expert group discussion, the CONTAM Panel judged with > 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that DBDPE is not genotoxic.

Uncertainty analysis: Reference Point

The CONTAM Panel identified substantial uncertainties affecting the hazard assessment for DBDPE (Table 21) that did not allow the establishment of a HBGV. Instead, the MOE approach was used, addressing the hazard uncertainties by applying UFs of 100 and 3.

The CONTAM Panel noted that the limitations in the database for DBDPE are the lack of long‐term toxicity studies, while there are several studies on repeated dose toxicity, developmental and reproductive toxicity, and neurodevelopmental toxicity. It was noted that DBDPE is not genotoxic, and the Reference Point comes from a study showing mild effects on a sensitive endpoint, at much lower doses than those at which effects were seen in other studies. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel considered an additional factor of 3 to be sufficient for deficiencies in the database.

Considering the combined impact of the uncertainties and the extent to which they are addressed by the applied UFs, the CONTAM Panel judged with 90%–95% certainty (i.e. probability) that, when using the Reference Point of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day, MOEs ≥ 300 would not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

Uncertainty analysis: Risk characterisation

Insufficient occurrence data in food on DBDPE were submitted to EFSA to allow robust estimates of dietary exposure for the EU population to be made. Based on data from the scientific literature for one European country (UK, see Section 3.3.1.2), the CONTAM Panel noted this would result in an MOE of 700.

The CONTAM Panel noted that the reported high exposure estimates were based on mean concentrations plus 2 SDs and referred only to the UK.

Considering the MOE of 700 for the UK exposure estimates, the uncertainties affecting extrapolation to exposure in other European countries and uncertainties affecting the Reference Point (see above), the CONTAM Panel judged with 66–90% certainty (i.e. probability) that current P95 exposure of the European population to DBDPE does not raise a health concern.

1,2‐Bis(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy)ethane (BTBPE) [CAS number 37853‐59‐1]

BTBPE is a non‐polymeric additive flame retardant that has been used as a replacement for the PBDE technical mixture OctaBDE in polystyrene, thermoplastics and resins (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of BTBPE are reported in Table 1. It has been identified as a substance of very high concern (SVHC) due to its vPvB properties (see Table 2). BTBEP is currently not registered under REACH, and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year. Nevertheless, the measured concentrations during service life (indoor dust) and end‐of‐life (indoor air at WEEE recycling facility) are comparable to those of DBDPE (see Section 3.3.1), which is the aromatic BFR with the highest registered aggregated volume at ~26,700 tonnes/year. As there are currently no active registration dossiers under REACH for this substance, the quantities present in imported articles into the EU do not give rise to registration obligations under REACH.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012a, 2012b), two studies in rats were identified (Hakk et al., 2004; Nomeir et al., 1993). The results of these studies showed that BTBPE was poorly absorbed; after oral administration of 14C‐BTBPE, less than 1% of radioactivity was retrieved in urine whereas more than 80% was excreted in faeces (Hakk et al., 2004; Nomeir et al., 1993).

Since the previous Opinion, no relevant studies were identified.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion reported a study on five serum samples from Sweden in which BTBPE could not be identified above the LOD (1.31 ng/g lipid) (Karlsson et al., 2007).

Since then, several studies have been identified in the scientific literature reporting on the levels of BTBPE in human samples from European countries, including human milk and blood/serum. These studies are briefly described below and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

BTBPE was reported to be below the LOD (0.2–0.3 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013).

BTBPE was reported to be below the LOD (0.2–0.3 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013).

In Sweden, BTBPE was detected in one out of two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010 in Sweden at the LOD (1.0 pg/g ww) (Sahlström et al., 2015).

In Sweden, BTBPE was detected in one out of two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010 in Sweden at the LOD (1.0 pg/g ww) (Sahlström et al., 2015).

Čechová et al. (2017) analysed BTBPE in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 22 and 26%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.023 to 1.103 ng/g lipid and from 0.078 to 2.380 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.023 to 1.103 ng/g lipid and from 0.078 to 2.380 ng/g lipid, respectively.

In the study by Tao et al. (2017) that analysed several BFRs in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10), the levels of BTBPE in the samples from 2010 ranged from < 0.1 to 56 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 28%), while in 2014–2015 a higher detection frequency (40%) was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.1 to 0.71 ng/g lipid.

Regarding blood/serum samples,

Cequier et al. (2013) detected BTBPE in 2 out of 10 serum samples from Norway (sampling year not reported) at concentrations of 0.35 and 0.48 ng/g lipid (LOD = 2.3 pg/mL). In a later study, Cequier et al. (2015) also analysed BTBPE in serum samples from 46 women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012 with median (mean) levels of 0.19 (0.21) ng/g lipid (detection frequency 9%).

Sales et al. (2017) detected BTBPE just below the LOQ of 0.1 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

The previous EFSA Opinion cited three studies on toxicity of BTBPE in experimental animals (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). Only one of these concerned toxicity in mammalian species. Acute toxicity studies of BTBPE indicated an oral LD50 > 10,000 mg/kg bw in rats and dogs, with no obvious effects observed in rats exposed to 35 mg/kg bw per day via the diet for 14 days (Matthews, 1984, as cited by Nomeir et al., 1993). Additionally, BTBPE impacted hepatic gene expression related to xenobiotic biotransformation, lipid metabolism and thyroid hormone regulation, increasing cytochrome Cyp1a4 and Cyp1A5 mRNA levels and altering deiodinase expression with decrease in type III iodothyronine 5′‐deiodinase and increase in type I iodothyronine deiodinase mRNA levels (Egloff et al., 2011).

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that mutagenicity assays with yeast (Saccharomyces cerevisiae) and in Salmonella strains TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA1538, both in the presence and absence of rat liver S9 fraction, were negative (WHO/IPCS, 2005) (see EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, a few additional experimental animal studies informing on toxicity of BTBPE have been published.

Cui, Xiao, Wang, et al. (2024) investigated the effects of BTBPE on the intestinal mucosa. Sprague–Dawley male rats (6–7 weeks old) were treated with BTBPE at 0, 2.5, 25, 250 mg/kg bw by oral gavage once daily for 30 days. Histological changes of the rat jejunum and ileum were observed through H&E staining and qualitatively reported. A small amount of inflammatory cell infiltration was observed in the 2.5 mg/kg bw per day group and this was further increased in the 25 and 250 mg/kg bw per day group. Goblet cells were slightly reduced, and epithelial cells were slightly damaged in both the 2.5 and 25 mg/kg bw per day groups. In the group treated with 250 mg/kg bw per day, the number of goblet cells was much decreased and epithelial cells were severely damaged. In a complementary study in Sprague–Dawley male rats subjected to an identical treatment regime there was a dose‐dependent increase in the relative proportion of necrotic cells in the liver, which was statistically significant at 25 and 250 mg/kg bw per day (Cui, Xiao, Zhang, et al., 2024). This was associated with dose‐related changes in expression of genes related to antioxidant defence and inflammation. Cui et al. (2026) reported further data on liver effects from the same or an identical experiment. Dose‐dependent effects on liver fibrosis markers were noted, with most markers being significantly elevated at 25 mg/kg bw per day and one (Masson stain) at 2.5 mg/kg bw per day.

Male Sprague–Dawley rats (180–220 g) were treated with 2.5, 25 or 250 mg/kg bw BTBPE daily by gavage for 30 days (Zhang et al., 2023). There was a small but statistically significant increase in body weight of the animals in the 250 mg/kg bw group. There was no change in relative or absolute thymus or liver weights. Serum FT4 concentrations were significantly lower in all treatment groups, compared with the control, but no dose–response relationship was observed. TT4 concentration was reduced in the 25 mg/kg bw group only. FT3 and TT3 concentrations were unaltered.

In a later study by the same laboratory, male Sprague–Dawley rats (6–7‐week‐old, 180–220 g) were given BTBPE (0 or 250 mg/kg bw per day) by daily oral gavage for 28 days followed by 12 h fasting before they were killed (Yuan et al., 2024b). TT4 and FT4 concentrations were numerically reduced by average in the treated group, but this was not statistically significant.

Shockley et al. (2020a) exposed 7‐week‐old male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats to BTBPE given by daily oral gavage at doses of 0, 0.07, 0.69, 6.88, 68, 680 mg/kg bw per day for 5 days. No treatment‐related liver histopathologic alterations were reported, and there were no changes in expression of genes in the liver.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data on BTBPE for the Ames test (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for BTBPE with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, three new studies were identified all pertaining to populations in China. The endpoints evaluated were thyroid hormone profile (three studies), liver injury biomarkers (one study) and thyroid cancer (one study). In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on BTBPE, is shown.

In the study by Guo et al. (2018) on the association with thyroid hormone levels in 10‐year‐old students living near a petrochemical complex in South China (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), the authors did not find an association between BTBPE levels and serum thyroid hormone levels in the samples from the 174 school students.

In the study by Zhao et al. (2021) that assessed the cross‐sectional relationship between the serum levels of five BFRs and a series of thyroid/liver injury biomarkers in residents of a major BFR‐producing region in China (for details see description in Section 3.3.1.1), a 10‐fold increment in the serum level of BTBPE was associated with higher bilirubin levels: total bilirubin level (0.869 μmol/L) [95% CI: 0.175, 1.564], direct bilirubin (DBIL) level (0.231 μmol/L) [95% CI: 0.075, 0.388] and indirect bilirubin level (0.638 μmol/L) [95% CI: 0.091, 1.185]. There were no statistically significant associations with thyroid hormone levels.

In the study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), BTBPE was not associated with thyroid cancer risk and was not associated with thyroid hormone levels in the control participants.

In summary, the available epidemiological evidence on BTBPE is limited to three cross‐sectional studies in populations in China, two of which were studies in adults and one was in children. None of these three studies observed associations between BTBPE and thyroid hormone levels. One study observed an increase in total, direct and indirect bilirubin levels. The one study that studied thyroid cancer risk, reported no association.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Within the literature retrieved on BTBPE, several modes of action have been proposed to explain BTBPE toxicity. An overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.3) and a brief summary is provided below.

Mitochondrial dysfunction may be a predominant mechanism, with evidence of impaired mitochondrial functions in zebrafish testes, including ultrastructural damage, increased apoptosis‐inducing factor expression and disordered respiratory chain function (Zheng et al., 2024). Studies in human endothelial cells (HUVECs) have shown reduced mitochondrial membrane potential and decreased ATP production at low micromolar concentrations (Shi, Wang, et al., 2021). Mitochondrial dysfunction might be associated with oxidative stress which has been reported as another pathway, with studies demonstrating increased ROS production and MDA content alongside decreased antioxidant defences such as reduced GSH content and SOD activity (Shi, Wang, et al., 2021). Both apoptotic and necroptotic cell death pathways are activated by BTBPE, as evidenced by increased expression of apoptotic markers including CASP3, CYTC, BAX and BCL2 in endothelial cells (Shi, Wang, et al., 2021), and necroptosis via RIP1, RIP3 and MLKL activation in intestinal tissue (Cui, Xiao, Wang, et al., 2024). In rat liver cells, BTBPE necrosis was reported to be mediated by GAS5 and NUAK1, through binding to miR‐743a‐5p (Cui, Xiao, Zhang, et al., 2024). Liver fibrosis following BTBPE exposure was found to involve activation of hepatic stellate cells by macrophage extracellular traps (Cui et al., 2026). Hepatic stellate cells that came in contact with macrophage extracellular showed PPAR‐mediated excessive collagen and αSMA production, leading to hepatic fibrosis. Endocrine disruption mechanisms include anti‐oestrogenic activity at concentrations of 12.5 μM and above (Ezechiáš et al., 2012), predicted AR antagonistic activity from QSAR models (Bajard et al., 2021), and in vivo disruption of sex hormone regulation (Choi et al., 2021). Inflammatory signalling through the IRX3/NOS2 pathway has been identified as promoting inflammation and necroptosis in intestinal tissue (Cui, Xiao, Zhang, et al., 2024), whilst widespread effects on cellular protein phosphorylation levels across multiple compartments have also been documented in zebrafish (Zheng et al., 2024).

Notably, some studies have reported negative findings that help define the toxicological profile of BTBPE. No effects on hepatic gene expression were observed in rats at doses up to 680 mg/kg body weight per day for 5 days (Shockley et al., 2020a, 2020b), and no inhibition of thyroid hormone metabolism through DIO and SULT activity was detected in human liver preparations, possibly attributable to the absence of hydroxyl groups in the BTBPE chemical structure (Smythe et al., 2017).

Overall, the data indicate that reproductive toxicity mediated through mitochondrial dysfunction and endocrine disruption are of concern for BTBPE, with effects occurring at relatively low concentrations in zebrafish. Male reproductive toxicity has been shown in zebrafish at concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 10 μg/L in water, with observed impairments in mating behaviour, reduced sperm quality and decreased reproductive success (Zheng et al., 2024). These reproductive effects are accompanied by disrupted sex hormone regulation, including elevated E2 levels, increased E2/11‐KT ratio and altered oestrogen receptor expression (Choi et al., 2021). Vascular endothelial toxicity has also been observed, with cytotoxic effects appearing at 5 μM and above in human umbilical vein endothelial cells, and inhibition of cell migration and tube formation at 10 μM (Shi, Wang, et al., 2021). Additionally, intestinal damage characterised by compromised gut barrier integrity, loss of tight junction proteins, inflammatory gene activation and necroptosis has been documented in Sprague–Dawley (SD) rats following oral exposure to BTBPE or in vitro in rat intestinal ICE6 cells (Cui, Xiao, Wang, et al., 2024, see above).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel noted that the database for BTBPE is limited and characterised by significant gaps. A small number of experimental animal studies identified intestinal effects, and thyroid hormone effects without effects on the thyroid gland. No sub‐chronic or long‐term studies, or studies addressing possible developmental and reproductive toxicity or developmental neurotoxicity, were identified.

BTBPE was negative in Ames test, but no data on clastogenicity or aneugenicity were identified. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of BTBPE.

The available evidence includes limited observations in humans (three cross‐sectional studies in Chinese populations).

Due to major limitations in the database, the CONTAM Panel could not identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Occurrence data of BTBPE in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and the CONTAM Panel considered these data sufficient to perform an exposure estimation (see below). In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of BTBPE and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2.

Regarding the data submitted to EFSA, Table 22 shows the LB and UB summary statistics of the estimated chronic dietary exposure to BTBPE, across surveys for each age group.

The mean UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.006 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.104 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’, across surveys.

The P95 LB dietary exposure ranged from 0.002 ng/kg bw per day in the ‘Elderly’ and ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.014 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Other children', across surveys. The P95 UB dietary exposure ranged from 0.011 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Very Elderly’ to 0.328 ng/kg bw per day in ‘Elderly’, across surveys.

Dietary exposure estimates to BTBPE in the surveys of pregnant women and lactating women (one survey each), were within the range of exposure estimates in the adult population and will not be further discussed.

Detailed summary statistics on the exposure estimates to BTBPE, for each dietary survey are presented in Annex C (Table 3).

The range of percentage contribution to the mean exposure across surveys is shown in parenthesis. The contribution to the total mean LB dietary exposure to BTBPE of the raw primary commodities under each food category at Level 1 and Level 3 of the FoodEx2 classification in each survey is available in Annex C (Table C.4 and Table C.5, respectively).

In the literature, Ma et al. (2023) found in samples from the UK collected in 2020–2021, BTBPE at mean concentrations of 190 ng/kg in meat, 200 ng/kg in fish and prawns, 97 ng/kg in cheese and 2300 ng/kg in chicken eggs. Exposure estimates for BTBPE in the UK population were calculated based on daily consumption data for UK citizens from the national diet and nutrition survey (2008–2019). Average dietary intakes of BTBPE ranged from 1.2 to 4.4 ng/kg bw per day, while high‐end estimates, assuming food contaminated at the average concentrations consumed at the mean rate +2 standard deviations, ranged from 7.1 to 27 ng/kg bw per day, across population groups (from 0 up to > 75 years old).

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature (Čechová et al., 2017; Tao et al., 2017, see Section 3.3.2.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 1.103 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 5.06 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) did not report mean concentrations.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 2.38 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 16.4 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported only maximum concentrations in samples from the UK, that were not used for these estimations.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from three European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

In the absence of a Reference Point for BTBPE, it is not possible to perform an MOE approach to risk characterisation. However, the CONTAM Panel concluded that, because occurrence data for BTBPE in food were submitted to EFSA and exposure could be estimated, an indication should be given as to whether dietary exposure indicates a health concern.

The EFSA guidance on the TTC specifies that, for substances raising a concern about potential DNA‐reactive mutagenicity and/or carcinogenicity, if exposure is below 2.5 ng/kg bw per day, the substance is considered of low probability of adverse health effects (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2019). The next lowest TTC value is 300 ng/kg bw per day. Although the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxic potential of BTBPE, the Panel noted that the highest dietary exposure estimates obtained from the occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA are below all of the TTC values, indicating a low probability of adverse health effects. However, this is based on a dietary exposure assessment with some significant sources of uncertainty (see Section 3.3.2.4).

Uncertainty analysis

The assessment of genotoxicity was inconclusive, and it was not possible to establish a Reference Point or conclude on risk characterisation for BTBPE. Identified sources of uncertainty affecting the exposure assessment are documented in Table 24.

Hexabromobenzene (HBB) [CAS number 87‐82‐1]

HBB is a non‐polymeric additive BFR. The physicochemical properties of HBB are reported in Table 1. HBB is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year. However, it has been identified in the environment at concentrations comparable to DBDPE (ECHA, 2024).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

In the previous Opinion two studies were cited. Yamaguchi et al. (1986) investigated the distribution and elimination of HBB in male Wistar rats after a single oral dose of 0.2 mg HBB per animal. The authors measured HBB in brain, liver, kidney, adipose tissue and blood until 40 days after exposure. The maximum concentrations were observed in adipose tissue (after 1 day). Compared to the other tissues, the concentration in liver decreased rapidly, indicating a rapid metabolism of HBB. The authors found a biphasic elimination, with half‐lives of 0.7 (initial phase or alpha) and 48 days (terminal phase or beta) for HBB. Koss et al. (1982) administered HBB at 16.6 mg/kg bw every other day by gavage in female Wistar rats during 2 weeks. The authors identified the formation of pentabromobenzene in faeces, probably due to a reductive debromination of HBB.

For the current update, the CONTAM Panel identified three additional studies; a second study by Yamaguchi et al. (1988), Sapota et al. (1997) 33 and Shin et al. (2023).

Yamaguchi et al. (1988) administered a single oral dose of 0.2 mg HBB to male Wistar rats. Rats were sacrificed at 1, 2, 4 and 8 h, and 1, 3, 10, 20 and 40 days after exposure, respectively. The authors measured the highest HBB concentration in adipose tissues, liver, brain and kidneys. HBB was rapidly metabolised into debrominated metabolites, pentabromobenzene (PeBB), tetrabromobenzene (TeBB) and tribromobenzene (TrBB). Among the three measured metabolites, TeBB accumulated in adipose tissues, liver, brain and kidneys. The half‐life of total HBB and its metabolite was 16 days.

The authors reported that 10% of the administered dose was excreted as HBB and its debrominated metabolites in faeces. After 10 days, the remaining HBB and its debrominated metabolites were 20%. The authors suggested that 70% of the HBB could be metabolised to other compounds such as hydroxylated compounds (Yamaguchi et al., 1988).

Sapota et al. (1997) administered a single oral dose of 600 mg/kg and 4500 mg/kg (14C‐HBB) in female Wistar rats. The highest measured radioactivity was: fat tissue > adrenals > sciatic nerve > liver > lung > brain > kidney > muscle. The maximum radioactivity concentration was observed between 2 and 8 h in the different tissues, except for fat tissues where the maximum concentration was observed at 48 h after the administration.

Within 72 h after administration, 1.2% and 75% of the administered dose were excreted in urine and faeces, respectively. The authors reported a terminal half‐life of 440 h (18 days). The percentage of radioactivity remaining in rats 48 h after exposure was between 24 and 30%.

Shin et al. (2023) performed a metabolomics study using hepatic cells treated by various concentrations of HBB during 24 or 48 h and with a period of observation of 21 days. The authors found 35 metabolites with a decreasing concentration within 15–17 days.

In summary, the available data showed that HBB and its metabolites accumulated in lipid‐rich tissues.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not identify any study on levels of HBB in human samples from European countries, but reported on studies in samples from Japan and China (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Since then, studies have been identified reporting on the levels of HBB in human samples from European countries, including human milk and blood/serum. These studies are briefly described below and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

HBB was reported to be below the LOD (0.2–0.3 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013).

HBB was reported to be below the LOD (0.2–0.3 ng/g lipid) in 11 pooled human milk samples collected in 2010 from 109 first‐time mothers in Ireland (Pratt et al., 2013).

Similarly, HBB was not detected (< 0.9 pg/g ww) in either of two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010 in Sweden (Sahlström et al., 2015) nor in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) (LOD not reported) (Tao et al., 2017).

Similarly, HBB was not detected (< 0.9 pg/g ww) in either of two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010 in Sweden (Sahlström et al., 2015) nor in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) (LOD not reported) (Tao et al., 2017).

Čechová et al. (2017) analysed HBB in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 116), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 44 and 88%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.036 to 0.048 ng/g lipid and from 0.099 to 0.170 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Čechová et al. (2017) analysed HBB in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 116), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 44 and 88%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.036 to 0.048 ng/g lipid and from 0.099 to 0.170 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Regarding blood/serum,

Cequier et al. (2013) detected HBB in 3 out of the 10 serum samples from Norway analysed at concentrations of 0.04, 0.07 and 0.08 ng/g lipid (LOD = 0.30 pg/mL). In a later study, Cequier et al. (2015) also analysed HBB in serum samples from 46 women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012 with median (mean) levels of 0.03 (0.07) ng/g lipid (detection frequency 48%).

Cequier et al. (2013) detected HBB in 3 out of the 10 serum samples from Norway analysed at concentrations of 0.04, 0.07 and 0.08 ng/g lipid (LOD = 0.30 pg/mL). In a later study, Cequier et al. (2015) also analysed HBB in serum samples from 46 women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012 with median (mean) levels of 0.03 (0.07) ng/g lipid (detection frequency 48%).

HBB was also measured in 61 serum samples from a Norwegian cohort collected in 2013 with a detection frequency of 5% and a reported geometric mean of 5.9 ng/g lipid (Tay et al., 2019).

HBB was also measured in 61 serum samples from a Norwegian cohort collected in 2013 with a detection frequency of 5% and a reported geometric mean of 5.9 ng/g lipid (Tay et al., 2019).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect HBB above the LOD (1 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected between 2009 and 2010.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect HBB above the LOD (1 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected between 2009 and 2010.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect HBB above the LOD of 5 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Sales et al. (2017) did not detect HBB above the LOD of 5 pg/g serum in one pool of human cord blood from Belgium.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

Several toxicological studies on HBB were described in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). Only those with oral administration and in vitro studies are considered here. A number of studies demonstrated effects on liver microsomal enzymes at various doses from 15 to 3024 mg/kg bw per day. Excretion of porphyrins in urine was reported following ‘chronic’ intragastric exposure to HBB at 15, 75 or 375 mg/kg bw per day, providing further evidence of hepatotoxicity. HBB was also reported to increase liver weight of pups of dams fed with 80 mg/kg feed from before mating until PND21 but was not teratogenic. It did not activate the AHR in a CALUX assay or the human nuclear androgen receptor in HepG2 cells at the concentrations tested.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that HBB was negative in a Salmonella mutation assay with and without metabolic activation (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

No new studies were identified.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on HBB (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for HBB with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, five publications were identified, four pertaining to populations in China and one in the USA. Exposure was assessed in serum in all studies in China, and in indoor air and wristbands in the USA study. The endpoints assessed were thyroid hormone profile (three studies), thyroid cancer (one study), type 2 diabetes (one study), BMI (one study) and liver injury markers (one study). In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on HBB, is shown.

In the study by Zhao et al. (2021) that assessed the cross‐sectional relationship between the serum levels of five BFRs and a series of thyroid/liver injury biomarkers in residents of a major BFR‐producing region in China (for details se description in Section 3.3.1.1), no associations were observed between HBB serum levels and any of the thyroid or liver biomarkers.

The study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), did not observe an association between HBB serum levels and thyroid cancer risk in either males or females. Further, HBB was not associated with thyroid hormone levels in the control participants.

In the USA study by Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), HBB concentrations, measured in wristbands of 101 adult study participants, were not statistically significantly associated with thyroid hormone profiles.

In the study by Zheng, Chen, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum HBB concentrations were higher in type 2 diabetes cases (n = 60) than in healthy controls (n = 57) in unadjusted comparisons. Mixture models, including seven BFRs and two dechlorane plus, consistently showed a significant positive association between mixed dechlorane plus/BFRs exposure and diabetes risk, and HBB was identified as a major contributor to the mixture effect.

In the study by Zheng, Sun, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum HBB concentrations did not show statistically significant associations with BMI. Adjusted models are not presented.

In summary, the five epidemiological studies that assessed health outcomes related to HBB are cross‐sectional studies, four in Chinese populations and one in a USA population. In none of these studies statistically significant associations were observed between HBB serum levels and thyroid hormone profiles, liver injury biomarkers or thyroid cancer risk. In a small case–control study, HBB appeared to be a major contributor to the association between mixture exposure and type 2 diabetes risk.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Few studies are available, and an overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.4). There is some evidence of effects on vitamin D metabolism (Sousa et al., 2022) and oxidative stress (Usenko et al., 2016). Bajard et al. (2019, 2021) have reported agonistic activity towards the progesterone and androgen receptors, but the evidence supporting this is unclear.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Very few toxicological data are available for HBB. There is some evidence for hepatotoxicity. Three epidemiological studies did not show statistically significant associations between HBB serum levels and thyroid hormone profiles, liver injury biomarkers or thyroid cancer risk.

HBB did not induce mutations in S. Typhimurium with and without metabolic activation. However, there is no information on clastogenicity or aneugenicity therefore an overall conclusion on genotoxicity cannot be reached.

Overall, the data were not sufficient to identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Occurrence data of HBB in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and the CONTAM Panel considered these data sufficient to perform an exposure estimation (see below). In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of HBB and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2.

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. Only one study reported levels of HBB above the LOD (Čechová et al., 2017, see Section 3.3.3.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 0.048 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.22 ng/kg bw per day.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 0.17 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 1.17 ng/kg bw per day.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from three European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

In the absence of a Reference Point for HBB, it is not possible to perform an MOE approach to risk characterisation. Given the bioaccumulation potential of HBB, the CONTAM Panel did not consider it appropriate to apply a TTC approach (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2019).

Uncertainty analysis

The assessment of genotoxicity was inconclusive and it was not possible to establish a Reference Point or conclude on risk characterisation for HBB. Identified sources of uncertainty affecting the exposure assessment and genotoxicity are documented in Table 26.

4‐(1,2‐Dibromoethyl)‐1,2‐dibromocyclohexane (DBE‐DBCH) [CAS number 3322‐93‐8]

DBE‐DBCH is used as an additive flame retardant mainly in polystyrene and polyurethane products and in electrical cable coating and some construction materials (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). There are four DBE‐DBCH stereoisomers (α‐, β‐, γ‐ and δ‐DBE‐DBCH) consisting of two enantiomeric pairs. Technical grade DBE‐DBCH consists of near equimolar amounts of α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH, whereas γ‐ and δ‐DBE‐DBCH appear in the technical mixture when heated at temperatures above 120°C.

The physicochemical properties of DBE‐DBCH are reported in Table 1. DBE‐DBCH is currently not registered under REACH, and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year (see Table 2).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No toxicokinetic studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, three studies have been identified.

Chu et al. (2012) investigated the metabolism of DBE‐DBCH (α‐ and β‐isomers) in male Wistar Han rat liver microsomes. The authors found that both α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH can be debrominated and metabolised into quantifiable metabolites (OH‐DBE‐DBCH and (OH)2‐DBE‐DBCH metabolites).

Chu et al. (2012) investigated the metabolism of DBE‐DBCH (α‐ and β‐isomers) in male Wistar Han rat liver microsomes. The authors found that both α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH can be debrominated and metabolised into quantifiable metabolites (OH‐DBE‐DBCH and (OH)2‐DBE‐DBCH metabolites).

Nguyen et al. (2017) studied the metabolism of DBE‐DBCH (β‐DBE‐DBCH isomer) in human Liver Microsomes. The authors showed that DBE‐DBCH was metabolised via cytochrome P450 enzyme into hydroxylated and debrominated metabolites (OH‐DBE‐DBCH, (OH)2‐DBE‐DBCH, dihydroxy tribromoethyl cyclohexane or (OH)2‐TriBECH, and bromo‐(1,2‐dibromocyclohexyl) acetic acid or DBCBA).

Nguyen et al. (2017) studied the metabolism of DBE‐DBCH (β‐DBE‐DBCH isomer) in human Liver Microsomes. The authors showed that DBE‐DBCH was metabolised via cytochrome P450 enzyme into hydroxylated and debrominated metabolites (OH‐DBE‐DBCH, (OH)2‐DBE‐DBCH, dihydroxy tribromoethyl cyclohexane or (OH)2‐TriBECH, and bromo‐(1,2‐dibromocyclohexyl) acetic acid or DBCBA).

In a computational toxicokinetic study, Ma et al. (2020) measured the affinities of β‐DBE‐DBCH in human CYPs 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1 and 3A4 by using molecular docking, MD simulations and density functional theory. The authors found that β‐DBE‐DBCH binding affinities may follow the order of 2A6 > 2C9 > 2B6 > 2E1 > 3A4 ≈ 2C19 ≈ 1A2 > 2D6. Among all CYPs, 2A6, 2C19, 2E1 and 3A4 isoforms have been reported as the specific CYP isoforms responsible for the metabolism of β‐DBE‐DBCH.

In a computational toxicokinetic study, Ma et al. (2020) measured the affinities of β‐DBE‐DBCH in human CYPs 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1 and 3A4 by using molecular docking, MD simulations and density functional theory. The authors found that β‐DBE‐DBCH binding affinities may follow the order of 2A6 > 2C9 > 2B6 > 2E1 > 3A4 ≈ 2C19 ≈ 1A2 > 2D6. Among all CYPs, 2A6, 2C19, 2E1 and 3A4 isoforms have been reported as the specific CYP isoforms responsible for the metabolism of β‐DBE‐DBCH.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report any study on levels of DBE‐DBCH in human samples. Since then, several studies have been identified reporting on the levels of DBE‐DBCH in human milk and blood/serum from European countries. These studies are briefly described below, and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed 2 pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010: α‐DBE‐DBCH was detected in both pooled samples at a mean concentration of 4 pg/g ww, while β‐DBE‐DBCH was detected in one of the pooled samples at the LOD of 0.5 pg/g ww.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed 2 pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010: α‐DBE‐DBCH was detected in both pooled samples at a mean concentration of 4 pg/g ww, while β‐DBE‐DBCH was detected in one of the pooled samples at the LOD of 0.5 pg/g ww.

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), DBE‐DBCH isomers were analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 25% and 32%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.017 to 0.038 ng/g lipid and from 0.035 to 0.083 ng/g lipid, respectively.

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), DBE‐DBCH isomers were analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) with a detection frequency between 25% and 32%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.017 to 0.038 ng/g lipid and from 0.035 to 0.083 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs and noted that the most abundant compound was DBE‐DBCH (α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH). Regarding α‐DBE‐DBCH, in 2010 the levels of ranged from < 0.13 to 1.7 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 20%), while in 2014–2015 a detection frequency of 100% was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.3 to 1.1 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.67 ng/g lipid. Regarding β‐DBE‐DBCH, in 2010 the levels of ranged from < 0.13 to 38 ng/g lipid with a mean value of 6.8 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 76%), while in 2014–2015 a detection frequency of 100% was reported and concentrations ranged from 0.43 to 10 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 2.5 ng/g lipid. The authors reported that this mean concentration was comparable to that of BDE‐47 or α‐HBCDD (2.8 or 2.1 ng/g lipid, respectively).

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) for several BFRs and noted that the most abundant compound was DBE‐DBCH (α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH). Regarding α‐DBE‐DBCH, in 2010 the levels of ranged from < 0.13 to 1.7 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 20%), while in 2014–2015 a detection frequency of 100% was reported and concentrations ranged from < 0.3 to 1.1 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 0.67 ng/g lipid. Regarding β‐DBE‐DBCH, in 2010 the levels of ranged from < 0.13 to 38 ng/g lipid with a mean value of 6.8 ng/g lipid (detection frequency 76%), while in 2014–2015 a detection frequency of 100% was reported and concentrations ranged from 0.43 to 10 ng/g lipid, with a mean value of 2.5 ng/g lipid. The authors reported that this mean concentration was comparable to that of BDE‐47 or α‐HBCDD (2.8 or 2.1 ng/g lipid, respectively).

Regarding levels in blood/serum,

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) analysed 24 matched serum samples form mothers and their toddlers collected between 2009 and 2010, and detected α‐DBE‐DBCH above the LOQ in two toddler serum samples (at 0.7 and 1.8 ng/g lipid), and β‐DBE‐DBCH in one of these toddler's samples at 1.3 ng/g lipid.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2014) analysed 24 matched serum samples form mothers and their toddlers collected between 2009 and 2010, and detected α‐DBE‐DBCH above the LOQ in two toddler serum samples (at 0.7 and 1.8 ng/g lipid), and β‐DBE‐DBCH in one of these toddler's samples at 1.3 ng/g lipid.

DBE‐DBCH was also measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort, and it was reported below the LOD for α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH (LOD = 3.9 and 4.8 pg/serum sample, respectively, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

DBE‐DBCH was also measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort, and it was reported below the LOD for α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH (LOD = 3.9 and 4.8 pg/serum sample, respectively, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, one study in rodents has been published. Rats were fed technical grade DBE‐DBCH in the diet at concentrations of 0, 10, 50, 250, 1250 or 5000 mg/kg (corresponding to 0, 0.9, 4.2, 21, 98 and 329 mg/kg bw per day in males and 0, 0.8, 3.9, 19 and 321 mg/kg bw per day in females for 28 days (Curran et al., 2017). Top dose animals were euthanised due to rapid weight loss. There were dose‐dependent clinical and histopathological changes consistent with mild hepatic and renal inflammation and increased serum testosterone in males. The authors identified NOAELs of about 4 mg/kg bw per day and also performed BMD modelling.

Genotoxicity

Three studies were identified informing on the genotoxicity of DBE‐DBCH 34 :

DBE‐DBCH (supplied by the NTP chemical repository [Radian Corporation, Austin, TX]) was negative in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA97 strains using a pre‐incubation assay, both in the absence and presence of metabolic activation provided by liver S‐9 from Aroclor‐induced male Sprague–Dawley rats and Syrian hamsters (Zeiger et al., 1987).

DBE‐DBCH (supplied by the NTP chemical repository [Radian Corporation, Austin, TX]) was negative in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537 and TA97 strains using a pre‐incubation assay, both in the absence and presence of metabolic activation provided by liver S‐9 from Aroclor‐induced male Sprague–Dawley rats and Syrian hamsters (Zeiger et al., 1987).

DBE‐DBCH was tested for its ability to induce sister chromatid exchanges (SCEs) and chromosomal aberrations in CHO cells with and without a metabolic activation system (rat liver S9 fraction). It was negative for induction of chromosomal aberrations under both experimental conditions, while increased SCE frequency was reported at cytotoxic concentrations (independent of metabolic activation) (Loveday et al., 1989).

DBE‐DBCH was tested for its ability to induce sister chromatid exchanges (SCEs) and chromosomal aberrations in CHO cells with and without a metabolic activation system (rat liver S9 fraction). It was negative for induction of chromosomal aberrations under both experimental conditions, while increased SCE frequency was reported at cytotoxic concentrations (independent of metabolic activation) (Loveday et al., 1989).

DBE‐DBCH also tested positive for the induction of mutations in the L5178Y tk+/tk− mouse lymphoma cell forward mutation assay in the absence of metabolic activation (McGregor et al., 1991). These study results were reported in NTP (2007). 35

DBE‐DBCH also tested positive for the induction of mutations in the L5178Y tk+/tk− mouse lymphoma cell forward mutation assay in the absence of metabolic activation (McGregor et al., 1991). These study results were reported in NTP (2007). 35

In summary, in vitro, DBE‐DBCH is not mutagenic in bacteria and did not induce chromosomal aberrations in CHO cells (with and without metabolic activation). Positive results were reported; however, for induction of SCEs in the same cell line (with S9 from rat liver) and of gene mutations in L5178Y mouse lymphoma cells (in the absence of metabolic activation). Based on the available data the CONTAM Panel concluded that DBE‐DBCH is mutagenic in mammalian cells in vitro. No in vivo data are available. The aneugenic potential was not investigated.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on DBE‐DBCH (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results for DBE‐DBCH with ‘LOW reliability’ (three models) and negative Ames test results with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, one publication was identified, pertaining to a population in China. In Appendix F, an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion is shown, including the one on DBE‐DBCH.

In the study by Guo et al. (2018) on the association with thyroid hormone levels in 10‐year‐old students living near a pretrochemical complex in South China (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), DBE‐DBCH levels (no distinction between the diastereomers) were not associated with the serum thyroid hormone levels.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

An overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.5) and a brief summary is provided below.

The 2012 Opinion reported two toxicity studies on juvenile brown trout exposed to β‐DBE‐DBCH in the diet at concentrations of 2.02, 14.7 and 118.4 pmol/g fat for 56 days. There were no effects on liver and gonad somatic indices or serum levels of steroid hormones (Gemmill et al., 2011). A subsequent study at the same doses reported reduced plasma tT4 at the top dose and a significant increase in mean thyroid epithelial cell height in all dose groups (Park et al., 2011). No new studies in fish were identified.

With respect to mode of action, previously reported studies identified activation of the androgen receptor (AR) in HepG2 cells with the γ‐ and δ‐ isomers more potent than α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH (Khalaf et al., 2009; Larsson et al., 2006). More recent studies with varying model systems have provided further evidence for this association (Bereketoglu et al., 2021; Kharlyngdoh et al., 2016; Kharlyngdoh et al., 2018; Wong et al., 2016).

Caspase‐3 and measures of oxidative stress were elevated only at cytotoxic concentrations in a macrophage cell line (Wang, Wei, et al., 2020) and therefore the relevance is unclear.

In zebrafish larvae, there are indications of developmental and locomotor behavioural toxicity, immunotoxicity, oxidative stress and proapoptotic effects (Wang, Wei, et al., 2019) and of delayed hatching and toxicity (Pradhan et al., 2013). Studies in cultured Purkinje neurons suggested potential neurotoxicity of β‐DBE‐DBCH (Stojak et al., 2019).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

DBE‐DBCH has shown evidence of effects on liver and kidneys in a short term (28‐day) study in rats. Studies in model systems indicate potential for neurotoxicity, effects on development and the thyroid, and activation of the AR.

DBE‐DBCH is not mutagenic in bacteria, but it induced gene mutations in mammalian cells in vitro. It did not induce chromosomal aberrations in CHO cells. No in vivo data are available. The CONTAM Panel considered that DBE‐DBCH is mutagenic in vitro in mammalian cells.

Taking into account the in vitro mutagenicity and lack of long‐term studies, the CONTAM Panel was not able to identify a Reference Point for DBE‐DBCH.

The CONTAM Panel noted that there are four stereoisomers of DBE‐DBCH: α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH which are present in the technical product, and γ‐ and δ‐DBE‐DBCH, which are formed when the technical product is heated at temperatures above 120°C. MOA studies indicate that γ‐ and δ‐DBE‐DBCH are more potent than α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH in activation of the AR. In principle, it is possible that γ‐ and δ‐DBE‐DBCH could enter the environment as a result of combustion of materials containing DBE‐DBCH as a flame retardant. However, most studies do not distinguish between the isomers. Therefore, the relative potencies could not be addressed.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data of DBE‐DBCH in food were submitted to EFSA, but some studies in the scientific literature were identified (see Section 3.1.2 for an overview).

One study was identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to DBE‐DBCH. Sahlström et al. (2015) reported occurrence levels and exposure of α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH, among other BFRs from analysis in Swedish market basket food samples collected in 2010, food baskets comprising of fish, meat, vegetable oils, dairy products and eggs (n = 4 food baskets). Both α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH were detected only in fish samples, at 97 and 17 pg/g ww respectively, while concentration in other food categories were below the LOD (See Section 3.1.2 and Appendix B). The dietary exposure was based on measured concentrations and intake assumptions from questionnaire data about dietary habits of the participants. The estimated median dietary daily intake for both mothers and toddlers (n = 20) based on fish intake only, was 3.5 and 1.7 ng per day for α‐DBE‐DBCH, respectively and 0.6 and 0.3 ng per day for β‐DBE‐DBCH, respectively.

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature (Čechová et al., 2017; Tao et al., 2017, see Section 3.3.4.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk for DBE‐DBCH isomers across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 0.038 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.17 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) analysed α‐ and β‐DBE‐DBCH in samples from the UK: for α‐DBE‐DBCH the reported mean concentration of 0.67 ng/g lipid would result in an average exposure of 3.1 ng/kg bw per day, while for β‐DBE‐DBCH, the reported mean concentrations of 6.8 and 2.5 ng/g lipid for samples collected in 2010 and 2014–2015, respectively, would result in average exposures of 31.2 and 11.5 ng/kg bw per day, respectively.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration for DBE‐DBCH isomers of 0.083 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.57 ng/kg bw per day. The study by Tao et al. (2017) reported only maximum concentrations that were not used for these estimations.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from four European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible, due to the lack of a Reference Point and the limited data on dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the lack of data on occurrence and exposure for DBE‐DBCH, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the conclusion on genotoxicity of DBE‐DBCH using the methods described in Section 2.8.

Based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells of DBE‐DBCH in the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity data, the CONTAM Panel concluded that DBE‐DBCH is genotoxic. Based on the overall evidence, and taking into account the associated uncertainties, the CONTAM Panel judged with > 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that DBE‐DBCH is genotoxic.

Pentabromoethylbenzene (PBEB) [CAS number 85‐22‐3]

PBEB is a non‐polymeric additive flame retardant. PBEB is a compound of high persistency in the environment, although it may undergo reductive and photolytical debromination. It has been reported to be used as an additive flame retardant in unsaturated polyesters, styrene butadiene copolymers and in other textiles and also in circuit boards, adhesives, wire and cable coatings, and in polyurethane foam (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of PBEB are reported in Table 1. PBEB is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year. It has been predicted by QSAR to be ‘(very)persistent’, ‘very bioaccumulative’ and ‘toxic’ (ECHA, 2024).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report on any study in European countries, but identified one study from China in which PBEB was not detected in serum samples from office cleaners, university students or policemen (Zhu et al., 2009).

Since then, some studies in human samples from European countries, including human milk and blood/serum, have been identified. These studies are briefly described below and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) did not detect PBEB in 2 pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010 (< 0.6 pg/g ww).

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), PBEB was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37). A low detection frequency of 2% and 3% was reported in samples from Norway and Slovakia, while it was not detected in samples from the Netherlands.

PBEB was also not detected in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) (LOD not reported) (Tao et al., 2017).

Regarding blood/serum,

PBEB was neither detected above the LOD in 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010 (LOD = 0.9 pg/25 μL, Sahlström et al. (2014), nor in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 14 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

In the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b), the information given on hazard identification of PBEB was that the LD50 in rabbits following dermal exposure exceeded 8000 mg/kg bw.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that PBEB was negative in the Ames test in S. Typhimurium strains TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 with and without metabolic activation.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since then, some studies on the effects of PBEB on rodents have been published.

Pregnant CD‐1 mice were orally administered PBEB at 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day in olive oil from gestation until the day before delivery, with dosing resumed on PND3 until weaning (Wang et al., 2024). Offspring were then fed a high‐fat ‘Western diet’. The perinatally exposed male (but not female) offspring exhibited liver injury, which was significant at the highest dose only (20 mg/kg bw per day), but without clear dose–response. Inflammation with elevated plasma ALT and AST markers and histological liver damage was observed, which is consistent with the observed hepatotoxicity. PBEB exposure led to overexpression of TWEAK in liver macrophages, promoting M1 macrophage polarisation and activating NF‐κB and NLRP3 inflammasome pathways, triggering pyroptosis. These cellular and molecular effects were predominantly observed at 20 mg/kg bw per day and in male mice. The NOAEL was 2 mg/kg bw per day based on liver inflammation scores in males.

Pregnant CD‐1 mice were orally administered PBEB at 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day in olive oil from gestation until the day before delivery, with dosing resumed on PND3 until weaning (Wang et al., 2024). Offspring were then fed a high‐fat ‘Western diet’. The perinatally exposed male (but not female) offspring exhibited liver injury, which was significant at the highest dose only (20 mg/kg bw per day), but without clear dose–response. Inflammation with elevated plasma ALT and AST markers and histological liver damage was observed, which is consistent with the observed hepatotoxicity. PBEB exposure led to overexpression of TWEAK in liver macrophages, promoting M1 macrophage polarisation and activating NF‐κB and NLRP3 inflammasome pathways, triggering pyroptosis. These cellular and molecular effects were predominantly observed at 20 mg/kg bw per day and in male mice. The NOAEL was 2 mg/kg bw per day based on liver inflammation scores in males.

In a study designed to study mechanisms of obesogenic effects of PBEB, pregnant CD‐1 mice were orally administered PBEB at 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day in olive oil from gestation until the day before delivery, with dosing resumed on PND3 until weaning with offspring maintained until 12 weeks of age (Xu et al., 2023). Perinatal exposure to 20 mg/kg bw per day (but not lower doses) caused increased absolute weight and hypertrophy of epididymal white adipose tissue in male offspring, without a clear dose–response. The NOAEL was 2 mg/kg bw per day based on increased weight gain and epididymal white adipose tissue hypertrophy in male offspring.

In a study designed to study mechanisms of obesogenic effects of PBEB, pregnant CD‐1 mice were orally administered PBEB at 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day in olive oil from gestation until the day before delivery, with dosing resumed on PND3 until weaning with offspring maintained until 12 weeks of age (Xu et al., 2023). Perinatal exposure to 20 mg/kg bw per day (but not lower doses) caused increased absolute weight and hypertrophy of epididymal white adipose tissue in male offspring, without a clear dose–response. The NOAEL was 2 mg/kg bw per day based on increased weight gain and epididymal white adipose tissue hypertrophy in male offspring.

Five‐week‐old Sprague–Dawley rats were administered PBEB by gavage at 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days to investigate its effect on thyroid function (Lu et al., 2020). In females, serum TT3 and relative thyroid weight decreased at all doses. Serum TT4 and TSH increased in both sexes. Thyroid follicles became depleted and epithelial cell layers thickened at ≥ 2 mg/kg bw per day but it was unclear if this refers to males or females or both. PBEB altered thyroid transcriptome profiles with 17, 42 and 119 differentially expressed genes at the three doses, including downregulation of Tg, Tpo, Slc5a5, Ttr and Dio1, and upregulation of Tshb. The LOAEL was 0.2 mg/kg bw per day based on increased serum TSH and reduced thyroid weight.

In females, serum TT3 and relative thyroid weight decreased at all doses. Serum TT4 and TSH increased in both sexes.
