The LOAEL was 0.2 mg/kg bw per day based on increased serum TSH and reduced thyroid weight.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on PBEB (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for PBEB with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, five publications were identified, four studies pertaining to populations in China, and one study in the USA. Exposure was assessed in serum in all Chinese studies, and in indoor air and wristbands in the USA study. Various endpoints were assessed including thyroid hormone profile (three studies), thyroid cancer (one study), type 2 diabetes (one study), BMI (one study) and liver injury markers (one study). In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on PBEB, is shown.

In the study by Zhao et al. (2021) that assessed the cross‐sectional relationship between the serum levels of five BFRs and a series of thyroid/liver injury biomarkers in residents of a major BFR‐producing region in China (for details se description in Section 3.3.1.1), serum PBEB levels were associated with a higher serum TT3 levels, but not with the other thyroid hormones and liver biomarkers measured.

In the study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), PBEB was detected in less than 20% of samples and was not further analysed.

In the USA study by Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), PBEB concentrations, measured in wristbands of 101 adult study participants, were not statistically significant associated with thyroid hormone profiles.

In the type 2 diabetes case–control study in China by Zheng, Chen, et al. (2025) (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum PBEB concentrations were not statistically significantly different between cases and controls in unadjusted comparisons (adjusted associations for single exposures were not reported) and PBEB was not a main contributor to the mixture effects.

In the study by Zheng, Sun, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum PBEB concentrations showed a statistically significant positive correlation with BMI. Adjusted models were not presented.

In summary, there are five cross‐sectional studies that assessed PBEB and four of these assessed associated health outcomes; in the fifth study PBEB was not further analysed due to low detection levels. Statistically significant associations were observed between PBEB serum levels and TT3 levels in one study, and PBEB and BMI in another (unadjusted correlation) but no other associations were reported.

Mode of action

An overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.6).

In mouse 3T3‐L1 cells, PBEB selectively activated retinoid X receptor α (RXRα) but not the peroxisome proliferator‐activated receptor γ (PPARγ), disrupting AMPK and PI3K/AKT signalling pathways involved in lipid metabolism (Xu et al., 2023). Inhibiting TWEAK/Fn14 signalling reduced inflammatory markers and inflammasome activation in vitro (Wang et al., 2024).

In cell‐based dual‐luciferase assays using CV‐1 cells, PBEB acted as a thyroid receptor β (TRβ) antagonist with an IC₅₀ of 9.82 × 10−7 M, showing no agonist activity between 10−10 and 10−6 M (Lu et al., 2020). Molecular modelling indicated that PBEB binds within the TRβ ligand‐binding pocket, inducing conformational changes in helices H11 and H12.

Yeast reporter gene assays showed PBEB exhibited no oestrogenic or androgenic or anti‐oestrogenic and anti‐androgenic activities up to 4.1 μM (Bajard et al., 2021).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

PBEB was negative in an Ames test, but no data were found on clastogenicity or aneugenicity making it impossible to conclude on possible genotoxicity.

Two developmental studies in mice were available. Both of these studies focus on specific effects (liver inflammation or obesogenic effects) and were mechanistic in nature. One involved administration of a high‐fat diet and reported liver injury in male offspring only. The second reported increase in absolute weight and hypertrophy of epididymal white adipose tissue in male offspring. The NOAEL in both studies was 2 mg/kg bw per day, but a clear dose–response was lacking in either study. A third animal study focused on effects of PBEB on the thyroid system. They found a dose‐dependent increase in TSH and reduced thyroid weight in a 28‐day study in rats, with a LOAEL of 0.2 mg/kg bw per day. The mode of action for this effect could be that PBEB acts as an antagonist of TRβ. Observations in humans indicate an association between serum PBEB levels and an increase in serum TT3 levels (Zhao et al., 2021).

Due to the limited scope of the three available animal studies – particularly the narrow range of endpoints investigated and the use of a high‐fat diet in one study – as well as the absence of sub‐chronic or long‐term studies and the lack of studies addressing developmental toxicity, reproductive toxicity or developmental neurotoxicity, the CONTAM Panel was unable to identify a suitable Reference Point for PBEB.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Limited occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and no estimation of the dietary exposure based on these data was possible.

In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of PBEB and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2 and Appendix B.

One study was identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to PBEB. Sahlström et al. (2015) reported occurrence levels and exposure of PBEB, among other BFRs from analysis in Swedish market basket food samples collected in 2010, food baskets comprising of fish, meat, vegetable oils, dairy products and eggs (n = 4 food baskets). PBEB was detected in fish (100% detection frequency) and meat (50% detection frequency) samples, at 8.7 and 0.60–2.0 pg/g ww respectively, while the concentration in other food categories was below the LOD. The dietary exposure was based on measured concentrations and intake assumptions from questionnaire data about dietary habits of the participants. The estimated median dietary daily intake for both mothers and toddlers (n = 20) based on fish intake only, was 0.3 and 0.2 ng per day, respectively.

No estimation of the exposure of breastfed infants was possible as the studies from European countries identified in the literature reported values < LOD or with low detection frequency (< 3%).

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible, due to the lack of a Reference Point and the limited data on dietary exposure.

Pentabromotoluene (PBT) [CAS number 87‐83‐2]

PBT is a reactive flame retardant primarily used in textiles, and can be released from oligomeric BFRs, such as BC‐58 (a TBBPA carbonate oligomer) and FR‐1025 (a pentabromobenzyl acrylate oligomer) (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of PBT are reported in Table 1. PBT is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report any study on levels of PBT in human samples. Since then, several studies have been identified in human milk and blood/serum from European countries. These studies are briefly described below, and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk samples,

Čechová et al. (2017) analysed PBT in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006), the Netherlands (2011–2014) and Slovakia (2011–2012) with a detection frequency between 85% and 100%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.028 to 0.049 ng/g lipid and from 0.063 to 0.089 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Čechová et al. (2017) analysed PBT in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006), the Netherlands (2011–2014) and Slovakia (2011–2012) with a detection frequency between 85% and 100%. Mean and P95 concentrations across countries ranged from 0.028 to 0.049 ng/g lipid and from 0.063 to 0.089 ng/g lipid, respectively.

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. PBT was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (< 0.4 pg/g ww).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed two pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. PBT was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (< 0.4 pg/g ww).

PBT was below the LOD in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) (LOD not reported) (Tao et al., 2017).

PBT was below the LOD in human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) (LOD not reported) (Tao et al., 2017).

Regarding levels in blood/serum,

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect PBT above the LOD (0.9 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect PBT above the LOD (0.9 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

PBT was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 14 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

PBT was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 14 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

The previous Opinion reported on some studies on PBT. In a sub‐chronic study during 91 days in male and female Sprague–Dawley rats fed a diet containing PBT (purity 98%) at 0, 0.05, 0.5, 5.0, 50 or 500 mg/kg (about 0, 0.003–0.004, 0.03–0.04, 0.35–0.4, 3.5–4.0 or 34–40 mg/kg bw per day, covering both males and females), clinical observations revealed no adverse effects (Chu et al., 1987; Danish EPA, 2000). Mild histopathological changes were observed in the liver (e.g. anisokaryosis, accentuated zonation), kidney (e.g. eosinophilic inclusions in the proximal tubules) and thyroid gland (e.g. reduced follicular size, increased epithelial heights). A NOAEL of 5 mg PBT/kg feed (equivalent to 0.35 mg/kg bw per day) was identified by the authors (Chu et al., 1987; Danish EPA, 2000). In another study no adverse fetal effects were observed when doses up to 600 mg/kg bw were given orally to rat dams during organogenesis (Danish EPA, 2000, as cited in SFT, 2009).

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion also reported that according to Zeiger et al. (1987) (as reported by Danish EPA, 2000), PBT was not mutagenic in an Ames test (pre‐incubation assay) with S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 with and without metabolic activation (hepatic S9 fraction from Aroclor 1254‐treated rats and hamsters). No data are available on chromosomal aberrations or other genotoxic effects.

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, no new studies have been identified.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on PBT (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for PBT with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction is consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, four publications were identified, pertaining to populations in China. Exposure was assessed in serum in all studies, and various endpoints were assessed including thyroid hormone profile (two studies), thyroid cancer (one study), liver injury markers (one study), type 2 diabetes (one study) and BMI (one study). In Appendix F, an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion is shown, including those on PBT.

In the study by Zhao et al. (2021) that assessed the cross‐sectional relationship between the serum levels of five BFRs and a series of thyroid/liver injury biomarkers in residents of a major BFR‐producing region in China (for details see description in Section 3.3.1.1), PBT serum levels were associated with a lower ratio of liver biomarkers AST/ALT. No associations were observed for any of the other thyroid hormones or liver injury biomarkers.

In the study by Liu, Li, et al. (2022) that assessed the association between the levels of several BFRs in serum and thyroid cancer risk in Shandong Province (China) (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2), serum PBT levels were not associated with thyroid cancer risk. In control participants, PBT was associated with lower total T3 levels (−3.14%; 95% CI: −6.42, −0.30).

In the study by Zheng, Chen, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum PBT concentrations were not statistically significantly different between type 2 diabetes cases and controls in unadjusted comparisons (adjusted associations for single exposures were not reported) and PBT was not a main contributor to the mixture effects.

In the study by Zheng, Sun, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum PBT concentrations did not show statistically significant associations with BMI. Adjusted models are not presented.

Mode of action

The previous Opinion reported that PBT was found to be a very weak agonist (more than six orders of magnitude less potent than 2,3,7,8‐TCDD) of the AHR in the CALUX bioassay (Brown et al., 2004).

An overview of the studies identified since the previous Opinion is given in Appendix G (Table G.7).

Preadipocyte (3T3‐L1) cell line and a hepatocyte (HepG2) cell line were used to investigate the possible lipid metabolism disruption caused by PBT. PBT did not significantly affect preadipocytes proliferation, but increased lipid accumulation in hepatocytes and also display a significant increase of PPARγ gene expression (Maia et al., 2022).

MCF‐7 cells were treated with PBT using short and long‐term (4 weeks) exposure protocols. Cell viability was not altered; however, cell proliferation was reduced after 24 h or 4 weeks exposure (Sousa et al., 2022).

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified PBT as having ‘scarce toxicological data indicating lower toxicological concern'.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Mild histopathological changes were observed in the liver, kidney and thyroid gland in rats exposed to PBT via diet for 91 days. The NOAEL was ≈ 0.35 mg/kg bw per day. No adverse fetal effects were observed in rats exposed by gavage up to 600 mg/kg bw per day during organogenesis.

PBT was negative in an Ames test in the presence or absence of metabolic activation. No data on clastogenicity or aneugenicity were identified. Therefore, the CONTAM Panel cannot conclude on the genotoxic potential of PBT.

No long‐term studies, reproductive toxicity studies or neurodevelopmental toxicity studies have been identified.

The CONTAM Panel concluded that the database on PBT is too weak to identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Limited occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and no estimation of the dietary exposure based on these data was possible.

In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of PBT and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2 and Appendix B.

One study was identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to PBT. Sahlström et al. (2015) reported occurrence levels and exposure of PBT, among other BFRs from analysis in Swedish market basket food samples collected in 2010, food baskets comprising of fish, meat, vegetable oils, dairy products and eggs (n = 4 food baskets). PBT was detected only in fish (100% detection frequency) samples, at 1.8 pg/g ww, while concentration in other food categories were below the LOD. The dietary exposure was based on measured concentrations and intake assumptions from questionnaire data about dietary habits of the participants. The estimated median dietary daily intake for both mothers and toddlers (n = 20) based on fish intake only, was 0.07 and 0.03 ng per day, respectively.

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature. Only one study reported levels above the LOD (Čechová et al., 2017, see Section 3.3.6.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands and Slovakia) of 0.049 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.22 ng/kg bw per day.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 0.089 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.61 ng/kg bw per day.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from three European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible, due to the lack of a Reference Point and the limited data on dietary exposure.

1,3,5‐Tris(2,3‐dibromopropyl)‐1,3,5‐triazine‐2,4,6‐trione (TDBP‐TAZTO) [CAS number 52434‐90‐9]

TDBP‐TAZTO is used as an additive flame retardant as a mixture of two congeners, one substituted with one and the other with two 2,3‐dibromopropyl groups (DBP‐TAZTO and BDBP‐TAZTO), respectively (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). This compound is the subject of a review by Bar and Szychowski (2022).

The physicochemical properties of TDBP‐TAZTO are reported in Table 1. TDBP‐TAZTO is registered in REACH (100–1000 tonnes per annum) (see Table 2).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, two studies have been identified.

Tong et al. (2021) developed an innovative method (matrix‐assisted laser desorption/ionisation imaging mass spectrometry (MALDI–IMS) using 1,5‐diaminonaphthalene hydrochloride and silver trifluoromethanesulfonate (NDA/AgOTf) as the matrix for the in‐situ localisation) for TDBP‐TAZTO quantification and distribution analyses in mice organs. ICR mice (18 males and 18 females) were treated by gavage to 80 or 160 mg/kg bw and sacrificed at day 15 or 30. The authors found that TDBP‐TAZTO accumulates in mice organs in the following order: kidney > liver > heart > brain.

Ma, Ma, et al. (2024) studied the in vitro metabolism of TDBP‐TAZTO using human liver microsome. The major CYP450 isoforms involved in the metabolism of TDBP‐TAZTO were the CYP2E1 and 3A4 followed by the CYP2C9. The authors detected four metabolites: a hydroxylated debrominated metabolite (C12H16Br5N3O4), a dihydroxylated metabolite (C12H15Br6N3O5), a desaturated debrominated metabolite (C12H14Br5N3O3) and an N‐dealkylated metabolite (C9H11Br4N3O3).

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were reported in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion several studies have been identified in the scientific literature addressing the toxicity of TDBP‐TAZTO (Han et al., 2022; Li et al., 2015; Zhang et al., 2024; Zhou et al., 2018) and two studies focused on the neurotoxic effects (Dong et al., 2015; Ye et al., 2015).

Li et al. (2015) studied the effects of TDBP‐TAZTO on growth, blood biochemical parameters organs and related gene expression in both male and female Balb/c mice daily administered by gavage for 28 days at 0, 2, 10 or 50 mg/kg bw per day. The results indicated that TDBP‐TAZTO exposure had no effect on body weight gain and food intake. The serum ALT was increased in male mice at all 3 dose levels, although not in a dose‐dependent manner, while the relative liver weight was significantly increased only in males exposed to 50 mg/kg bw per day. A higher relative kidney weight was observed in males exposed to 10 mg/kg bw per day whereas the index was decreased in females treated with 50 mg/kg bw per day. Histopathological analyses showed both liver and lung lesions (liver: cell necrosis and ballooning degeneration; lung: epithelium hyperplasia, mitochondrial swelling and inflammatory cell infiltration) with increasing severity according to the level of dose.

Male Wistar rats (4‐week‐old) were administered daily with TDBP‐TAZTO for 4 weeks by gavage at doses of 0, 2 or 50 mg/kg bw per day (Zhou et al., 2018). No variations of body weight nor relative organ weights were observed at the end of the period of exposure between the three groups, except for a significant increase of the relative brain weight in the two TDBP‐TAZTO‐exposed groups compared to controls. Free T4 and estradiol blood levels were decreased in the 2 and the 50 mg/kg bw per day‐treated groups, respectively. AST and ALT activities were significantly reduced in 50 mg/kg per day‐exposed animals compared to control rats. Slight histopathological lesions were reported in lung (diffuse thickened epithelial walls, partial fibrosis and vasodilatation), spleen (enlargement in the marginal zone) and kidney (tissular congestion and inflammation) according to the dose of exposure and the organ.

TDBP‐TAZTO liver toxicity was assessed in vivo (rats) and in vitro (HepG2 cells) (Han et al., 2022). The in vivo part of the study consisted of four groups of 20 Wistar rats each (4‐week‐old, half male and half female in each group) daily administered with TDBP‐TAZTO by gavage for 28 days. The dose levels were 0, 313, 625 and 1250 mg/kg bw per day. Body and organ weights, blood chemistry and histopathological parameters were analysed at the end of the exposure period. The body weight and the relative organ weights revealed insignificant differences between the four groups except for the liver that showed a significant increase in the relative organ/body weight with the dose of TDBP‐TAZTO. The blood chemistry results including liver AST and ALT activities, total protein and albumin contents, lipid (CT and TG) concentrations and haematological parameters showed higher values in TDBP‐TAZTO‐exposed rats compared to controls which confirmed the liver to be a target organ for this BFR.

Zhang et al. (2024) studied the respiratory effects of TDBP‐TAZTO. Male C57BL/6J mice (5–6 weeks old) were daily administered by gavage for 14 days at a dose of 20 mg/kg bw per day. Parameters including lung function, body weight, organ indices, pathological alterations and the regulatory expression of lung inflammatory signalling were assessed in TDBP‐TAZTO‐treated male mice. Mice were also co‐treated with estradiol (E2) to assess its potential protective role against TDBP‐TAZTO‐induced damages. Results showed the ability of TDBP‐TAZTO to induce significant damages in the lung tissue, including haemorrhage, alveolar wall thickening, infiltration of inflammatory cells into the alveolar space and interstitium, and collagen deposition. The lung injury scores, as indicator of the severity of lung injury, were significantly higher in TDBP‐TAZTO‐exposed mice than in controls. Lung function was impaired as reflected by the increase in physiological variables as the accumulated volume and the airway pressure–time index in TDBP‐TAZTO‐treated animals compared to controls. Immunohistochemical analysis revealed that TDBP‐TAZTO exposure reduced ERα expression and increased nuclear factor kappa‐light‐chain‐enhancer of activated B cells (NF‐κB) expression. Co‐exposure of estradiol with TDBP‐TAZTO significantly mitigated the COPD‐like phenotypes. The authors concluded that TDBP‐TAZTO induced chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD)‐like phenotypes in lungs of mice orally exposed, with a role for the ERα‐mediated inflammatory pathway in the physiopathology of COPD induced by this compound.

Dong et al. (2015) administered TDBP‐TAZTO (purity > 97%) at 0, 5 or 50 mg/kg bw per day to male Balb/c mice daily by gavage for 30 days and studied for brain and behaviour toxicity. Depressive‐related behaviour was measured in the forced swim test and the tail suspension test, locomotor activity in the open‐field and apoptosis‐related protein expression (Bax and Bcl‐2) in the hippocampus of the same animals. TDBP‐TAZTO induced a significant dose‐dependent increase in depressive‐related behaviour in both tests whereas locomotor activity remained unchanged.

Ye et al. (2015) administered male Sprague–Dawley rats daily by gavage with TDBP‐TAZTO (purity > 97%) at 0, 5 or 50 mg/kg bw per day for 6 months and studied for brain and behaviour toxicity during the last 2 weeks of the 6th month. Depressive‐related behaviour was measured in the forced swim test, locomotor activity in the open‐field, and spatial learning and memory performances in the Morris water maze. Brains and adrenal glands were removed and studied for histopathological and immunohistochemical examination and measurements of oxidative stress markers, pro‐inflammatory cytokines and apoptosis‐related proteins. TDBP‐TAZTO dose‐dependently induced significant increases in depressive‐related behaviour and in plasma ACTH and serum corticosterone levels. TDBP‐TAZTO also impaired the learning and memory performances of the animal at both doses whereas locomotor activity remained unchanged.

Additional studies were available in the REACH dossier, 36 as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

The technical product AP 729 (purity NR) was tested in a Combined Repeated Dose Toxicity Study with the Reproduction/Developmental Toxicity Screening Test (OECD TG 422) in Wistar rats by oral gavage at doses of 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. AP 729 was administered for 2 weeks prior to mating, during mating and post‐mating periods for males, during pregnancy and up to Lactation Day 13 for females. There were no treatment‐related effects on systemic, reproduction and fertility parameters and on F1 generation pups up to and including the highest dose tested of 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Moreover, no treatment‐related neurological abnormalities were observed at any of the doses tested. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested (REACH dossier).

The technical product AP 729 (purity NR) was tested in a Combined Repeated Dose Toxicity Study with the Reproduction/Developmental Toxicity Screening Test (OECD TG 422) in Wistar rats by oral gavage at doses of 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. AP 729 was administered for 2 weeks prior to mating, during mating and post‐mating periods for males, during pregnancy and up to Lactation Day 13 for females. There were no treatment‐related effects on systemic, reproduction and fertility parameters and on F1 generation pups up to and including the highest dose tested of 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Moreover, no treatment‐related neurological abnormalities were observed at any of the doses tested. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested (REACH dossier).

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, two studies have been cited in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s): an in vitro mammalian cell micronucleus test (OECD Guideline 487), and an in vivo mammalian alkaline comet assay (OECD Guideline 489). However, no information on the protocol or results was available in the publicly available REACH dossier due to confidentiality restrictions.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on TDBP‐TAZTO, that referred to the REACH dossier (see above and Annex B). In the absence of further details, the CONTAM Panel could not interpret this information.

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results for TDBP‐TAZTO with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (three models) and negative Ames test results with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The CONTAM Panel assessed the validity of the positive predictions with moderate reliability according to VEGA, including the relevance of the analogues with experimental data identified by the software and the accuracy and consistency of their predictions. The Panel noted that the analogues were not sufficiently similar to the target compound to be confident in the reliability of the predictions, and thus these predictions were not further considered.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Studies in other model systems and on mode of action

The previous Opinion reported two studies in zebrafish and one study in cultured cells (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012a, 2012b). Exposure of adult zebrafish to 0, 0.25, 1 or 4 mg/L TDBP‐TAZTO in water for 28 days had no effect on survival or growth. Significant damage to the liver and gill was observed at 4 mg/L. Thyroid hormones, testosterone, estradiol, liver superoxide dismutase activity, malondialdehyde content and brain cholinesterase activity were not affected (Zhang et al., 2011). Zebrafish eggs and embryos exposed to TDBP‐TAZTO at 0, 0.5, 1, 2.5, 5 or 10 μg/mL of water showed higher mortality at longer exposure periods in the three highest dose groups, and hatching rate was reduced in the four highest dose groups. Dose‐dependent defects in inflation of the gas bladder were observed in the three highest dose groups. Electron microscopy examination of the gas bladder revealed changes in the electron density of the cytoplasmic vesicles and disruption of the mitochondrial cristae (Li et al., 2011). The one in vitro study reported, showed that TDBP‐TAZTO was cytotoxic for primary cultured cerebellar granule neurons at concentrations in the range of 5–10 μM (Qu et al., 2011).

New publications related to brain effects and mode of action of TDBP‐TAZTO have been published since the previous Opinion, most of them related to impacts on steroidogenesis and oestrogenic and androgenic activities. Several in vitro studies also explored the neurotoxicity of TDBP‐TAZTO. An overview of these studies is given in Appendix G (Table G.8) and a brief summary is provided below.

In vitro, TDBP‐TAZTO impacted cell viability and triggered apoptosis and oxidative stress in SH‐SY5Y neuronal cell line (Dong et al., 2015; Szychowski et al., 2021) whereas neuronal differentiation remains unaffected (Szychowski et al., 2021). Dose‐dependent increases in apoptosis‐related and protein expressions and pro‐inflammatory cytokine levels were observed in hippocampus of adult rats exposed to TDBP‐TAZTO for 6 months (Ye et al., 2015). However, such effects on apoptosis and neuroinflammation were not observed in primary mouse astrocytes exposed for 48 h to TDBP‐TAZTO (Szychowski & Skóra, 2023a).

In the same model of primary mouse astrocyte cell culture, TDBP‐TAZTO disturbed the production of progesterone, testosterone and oestradiol, and the expression of key‐related enzymes involved in the hormonal production (Szychowski & Skóra, 2023b), suggesting the ability of this compound to dysregulate sex steroid hormone production. The effects of TDBP‐TAZTO on steroidogenesis, production of steroid hormones, enzyme‐related expression and interactions with hormonal receptors were studied in vitro in various cell lines, including H295R adrenocortical cells (Li et al., 2016), MCF‐7 human breast adenocarcinoma cells (Cao et al., 2018; Krivoshiev et al., 2016), MDA kb‐2 epithelial cells (Bajard et al., 2023) and mouse‐derived spermatogonic GC‐1 spg cells (Tabecka‐Lonczynska et al., 2023,b, 2024).

TDBP‐TAZTO showed a significant anti‐oestrogenic activity in MCF‐7 cells co‐exposed to 17β‐estradiol (Krivoshiev et al., 2016) by affecting the ERα‐mediated signalling pathway using an in vitro ER‐dependent luciferase reporter gene assay (Cao et al., 2018). TDBP‐TAZTO exhibited anti‐androgenic properties in MDA kb‐2 cells alone or in combination with other anti‐androgenic FRs using the same type of luciferase reporter gene assay (Bajard et al., 2023); this finding confirmed the predicted androgen receptor antagonist activity of this compound from two QSAR models (Bajard et al., 2021).

In mouse‐derived spermatogonic GC‐1 spg cells, TDBP‐TAZTO impaired the process of steroidogenesis by reducing the expression of proteins and enzymes involved in the synthesis of gonadal hormones, including progesterone and slightly, estradiol and testosterone (Tabecka‐Lonczynska, Kaczka, & Kaleniuk, 2023). The ability of TDBP‐TAZTO to inhibit sex hormone production was also demonstrated in H295R adrenocortical cells (Li et al., 2016) due to the interference of this compound with steroidogenic gene expression when activated with the co‐exposure to the cAMP inducer forskolin. The impact of TDBP‐TAZTO on steroidogenesis and hormone production was related to its ability to disrupt androgenic‐signalling pathways, to modulate autophagy processes, and to affect cell proliferation and survival in GC‐1 spg cells (Tabecka‐Lonczynska et al., 2024). The ability of TDBP‐TAZTO to affect AhR, PPARγ and ER‐related gene expression and then to dysregulate the steroid‐related signalling pathway in the same model of mouse‐derived spermatogonic GC‐1 spg cells (Tabecka‐Lonczynska, Skóra, et al., 2023) suggests that TDBP‐TAZTO may affect sex steroid hormone production.

Li et al. (2015) reported the modifications in the expression of apoptosis‐related genes (p53 and bcl‐2) in liver and lung of both male and female Balb/c mice daily administered by gavage for 28 days at 0, 2, 10 or 50 mg/kg bw per day, suggesting that mitochondria is one of the major targets for TDBP‐TAZTO and one of the pathways that led to toxic effects of TDBP‐TAZTO in such organs.

ERα gene expression was significantly decreased in male Wistar rats (4‐week‐old) daily administered daily with TDBP‐TAZTO for 4 weeks by gavage at doses of 0, 2 or 50 mg/kg bw per day whereas CYP19A1 mRNA expression increased with the dose, but it was not significant. Concomitant histopathological lesions were reported in lung, spleen and kidney according to the dose of exposure and the organ but it was not significant (Zhou et al., 2018).

TDBP‐TAZTO exhibited cell damages and apoptosis in A549 adenocarcinoma human alveolar basal epithelial cells and triggered oxidative stress at concentrations lower than the ones inducing cytotoxicity and apoptosis (Szychowski & Skóra, 2023c). TDBP‐TAZTO also interfered with mTOR‐PPARγ signalling pathway and p62 autophagy pathway, disrupting the cell functioning given the major role of both signalling pathways in cell metabolism and differentiation.

In HepG2 cells, TDBP‐TAZTO (0, 12.5, 25 and 50 μM, cell exposure for 72 h) impaired the cell growth and induced apoptosis via mitochondrial pathway and death receptor signalling pathway (Han et al., 2022).

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified TDBP‐TAZTO as having ‘scarce toxicological data indicating toxicological concern'.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Although two studies on genotoxicity were cited in the REACH dossier, there was no information on the protocol or results reported. Thus, the CONTAM Panel was unable to conclude on the genotoxic potential of TDBP‐TAZTO.

Experimental studies of TDBP‐TAZTO in animals include 28‐day studies and a combined repeated dose toxicity study with the reproduction/developmental toxicity screening test, plus several neurotoxicity studies from the literature. Based on the available data, neurotoxicity appears to be the critical effect, with effects on depressive‐related behaviour in the forced swim test and the tail suspension test at the lowest tested dose of 5 mg/kg bw per day (Dong et al., 2015; Ye et al., 2015).

Estimated BMDLs were obtained (see Annex D) but did not meet the acceptability criteria specified in the Guidance on BMD analysis (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2022).

There were no long‐term/carcinogenicity studies identified.

The CONTAM Panel identified the LOAEL of 5 mg/kg bw per day as the Reference Point for TDBP‐TAZTO.

The CONTAM Panel concluded that due to the limitations and uncertainties in the current database, the establishment of a health‐based guidance value for TDBP‐TAZTO was not appropriate. Instead, the MOE approach was used for risk characterisation (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2025).

Usually, an MOE of 100, covering variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans (factor 4 × 2.5 = 10) and within the human population (factor 3.2 × 3.2 = 10), is considered sufficient to conclude that exposure does not raise a health concern.

To allow for the absence of a NOAEL and the necessity of using a LOAEL, the EFSA Guidance on selected default values indicates that an additional uncertainty factor will be needed, the size of which should be determined on a case‐by‐case basis (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). ECHA, in its Guidance on information requirements and chemical safety assessment (ECHA, 2012) proposed the application of ‘an assessment factor ranging from 3 (as minimum/majority of cases) to 10 (as maximum/exceptional cases)' with the following considerations: ‘An AF of 3 may be more appropriate for instance in situations, where the effects at the LOAEL are mild, or the LOAEL represents the lower boundary of the exposure range in which the effect is observed. Higher numerical values should be considered in situations where the effects at the LOAEL are severe and irreversible, or the shape of the dose–response curve is shallow or the quality of the study (e.g. group sizes, statistical methods, study design, exposure data) gives rise to uncertainties about the reliability of the identified LOAEL’ (ECHA, 2012). In this instance, since the effects at the LOAEL for TDBP‐TAZTO are mild, the CONTAM Panel considered that a factor of 3 is sufficient to extrapolate from a LOAEL to NOAEL.

According to the EFSA Scientific Committee Guidance on selected default values, an additional factor can be considered in case of deficiencies in the database on a case‐by‐case basis. A default value has not been proposed, as it will be directly dependent on the data set available (EFSA Scientific Committee, 2012). The WHO/IPCS (1994, 1999) recommended a factor of 3 or 5 if there are minor deficiencies in the database and a factor of 10 if there are major deficiencies in the database. There are limitations in the database for TDBP‐TAZTO, such as absence of long‐term/carcinogenicity studies. Taking into account also the sensitivity of the endpoints examined in the critical study, the CONTAM Panel considered an additional factor of 3 to be appropriate for deficiencies in the database.

Overall, the CONTAM Panel considered that MOEs ≥ 900 (100 for inter and intra‐species variability, 3 for LOAEL to NOAEL extrapolation and 3 for the gaps in the toxicological database) do not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

The CONTAM Panel could not set a Reference Point for neoplastic effects, since no carcinogenicity data are available. In addition, two studies on genotoxicity were cited in the REACH dossier but there was no information on the protocol or results reported. Thus, it was not possible to conclude on the genotoxic potential of TDBP‐TAZTO.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TDBP‐TAZTO in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible, due to the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis: Hazard characterisation

The CONTAM Panel identified substantial uncertainties affecting the hazard assessment (Table 27) that did not allow the establishment of a HBGV. Instead, the MOE approach was used, addressing the hazard uncertainties by applying UFs of 100 (to cover for variability with respect to kinetic and dynamic differences between animal species and humans, and within the human population), 3 (to account for the absence of a NOAEL) and 3 (for deficiencies in the database).

Considering the combined impact of the uncertainties and the extent to which they are addressed by the applied UFs, the CONTAM Panel judged with at least 90% certainty (i.e. probability) that, when using the Reference Point of 5 mg/kg bw per day, MOEs ≥ 900 would not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

1,2,5,6‐Tetrabromocyclooctane (TBCO) [CAS number 3194‐57‐8]

TBCO is an additive flame retardant. Technical TBCO consists of 34% α‐HBCO and 66% β‐TBCO, and interconversion of these isomers has been proposed as a possibility (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of TBCO are reported in Table 1. TBCO is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report any study on levels of TBCO in human samples. Since then, one study in human milk and one study in blood/serum from European countries have been identified, both reporting TBCO below the LOD (Appendix E, Table E.1):

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) in which human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) were analysed, TBCO was not detected in any sample (LOD = 0.002 ng/g lipid).

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) in which human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) were analysed, TBCO was not detected in any sample (LOD = 0.002 ng/g lipid).

TBCO was measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort and it was reported below the LOD in all cases (LOD = 7.3 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

TBCO was measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort and it was reported below the LOD in all cases (LOD = 7.3 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

The previous Opinion contained no information on hazard identification for TCBO but it highlighted TBCO had potential for bioaccumulation (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Since then, the CONTAM Panel has not identified any experimental animal studies in mammalians on effects of TBCO. All available animal research on TBCO was conducted using fish models, specifically Japanese medaka and zebrafish exposed through waterborne or oral exposure, or exposure via maternal transfer to the oocytes (see Section 3.3.8.1.6).

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on TBCO (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for TBCO with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (three models) and positive Ames test results with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Studies on TBCO have revealed reproductive, developmental, and cytotoxic effects across multiple biological systems using various exposure pathways. An overview of those studies is given in Appendix G (Table G.9) and a brief summary is provided below.

In fish models, reproductive and endocrine effects are those most studied, with reduced fecundity ranging from 18% to 56.8% observed following oral exposure with dietary levels between 58 and 1034 μg/g feed (Devoy, Raza, Jones, et al., 2023; Devoy, Raza, Kleiner, et al., 2023; Saunders et al., 2015). Impaired oocyte maturation represents another effect, with significant reductions in maturation stimulated by 17α,20β‐dihydroxy‐4‐pregnen‐3‐one, used as maturation inducing hormone (MIH), occurring through both in vitro waterborne exposure at concentrations of 20–200 μg/L and in vivo oral exposure at 100–1000 μg/g food for 21 days (Raza et al., 2023). The reproductive effects extend across generations through maternal transfer, with F1 fecundity reduced by 33–34% at both low (40.6 μg/g) and high (1034 μg/g) oral doses in the F0 generation (Devoy, Raza, Kleiner, et al., 2023). These effects persisted in the F2 generation with a 29.8% reduction, although F3 fish showed complete recovery (Devoy, Raza, Kleiner, et al., 2023).

Developmental effects have been studied in fish. Exposure of Japanese medaka embryos to TBCO via water at 100–1000 μg/L from 2 h post‐fertilisation until 1 day post‐hatch significantly impaired hatching, causing both delayed hatching time and reduced success rates (p < 0.001) (Sun et al., 2016). A background‐colour preference assay was devised to assess vision of the fish larvae. The visual performance by larvae was compromised at relatively low waterborne concentrations, with significant impairment observed in larvae exposed from 2 h post‐fertilisation until 14 days post‐hatch at 10 and 100 μg/L (Sun et al., 2016). Cardiac function was also affected through waterborne exposure from 2 to 5 h post‐fertilisation, with decreased heart rates documented at 100 μg/L and 1000 μg/L (Sun et al., 2016). In zebrafish, waterborne exposure from 1 to 120 h post‐fertilisation at concentrations of 2, 20 or 200 μg/L produced dose‐dependent mortality, spine curvature and uninflated swim bladders, with significant effects at 20 μg/L (Van Essen, Devoy, et al., 2021). Maternal transfer is an exposure pathway in fish. F0 Japanese medaka fed oral diets containing low (74.7 μg/g feed) or high (663 μg/g feed) TBCO doses produced F1 females which showed 26.0% and 56.8% reduced fecundity, respectively, associated with dose‐dependent transfer of TBCO to oocytes (Devoy, Raza, Jones, et al., 2023). Both doses also impaired MIH‐stimulated oocyte maturation with 23.4% and 20.0% reductions (Devoy, Raza, Jones, et al., 2023). This demonstrates that in fish TBCO can be transferred from parent to offspring and that this could cause reproductive impairment in the next generation without direct oral exposure.

Cytotoxicity studies in human neuroblastoma cells using direct in vitro exposure revealed that TBCO reduced cell viability at concentrations of 0.5 μM and above (Shi et al., 2020). This cytotoxicity was accompanied by increased membrane permeability, apoptosis, elevated intracellular calcium concentrations and increased reactive oxygen species production (Shi et al., 2020). In vitro endocrine activity assays indicated multiple modes of action. In porcine testicular cells, TBCO elevated both testosterone and oestradiol at concentrations between 0.03 and 3.0 mg/L (Mankidy et al., 2014). Anti‐androgenic activity reached a maximum of 59% at 1500 mg/L in the yeast androgen screen (YAS) assay, while exposure to 15 mg/L in H295R cell assays produced a 3.3‐fold increase in oestradiol (Saunders et al., 2013). Additionally, a review of the literature and the ToxCast database identified presence of anti‐oestrogenic activity of TBCO (Bajard et al., 2021).

The mechanisms underlying TBCO toxicity appear to be multifaceted and operate at various levels of biological organisation. Disruption of steroidogenesis represents a primary mechanism, evidenced by altered expression of key steroidogenic enzymes including CYP11a, CYP19a, CYP17a and CYP21a (Devoy, Raza, Kleiner, et al., 2023; Mankidy et al., 2014; Saunders et al., 2015). Studies using oral exposure documented downregulation of cholesterol metabolism genes and changes in luteinizing hormone receptor expression (Devoy, Raza, Kleiner, et al., 2023; Saunders et al., 2015). Interestingly, some studies found no consistent changes in plasma oestradiol or vitellogenin levels despite significant transcript alterations (Raza et al., 2023), suggesting post‐transcriptional regulation or compensatory mechanisms.

Impaired oocyte maturation in fish may have involved disruption of insulin‐like growth factor signalling pathways and altered microRNA expression that regulates these pathways, as observed in zebrafish following oral exposure for 14 days (Van Essen, Alcaraz, et al., 2021). Notably, the effects on MIH‐stimulated maturation occurred independently of plasma hormone levels in both in vitro and in vivo exposure scenarios (Raza et al., 2023; Van Essen, Alcaraz, et al., 2021), suggesting direct effects on the oocyte maturation machinery independent of systemic hormonal changes.

Developmental toxicity mechanisms, observed through waterborne embryonic exposure of fish, involve downregulation of ribosomal proteins, which affects protein biosynthesis and impairs embryo development and hatching pathways (Sun et al., 2016). Specific developmental processes appear to be targeted through altered expression of genes involved in eye development and phototransduction, including atoh7, col15a1b, recoverin and various opsin genes (Sun et al., 2016). Cardiac development and function are compromised through changes in myosin isoforms and cytochrome c oxidase expression, while calcium signalling pathways are disrupted through altered expression of genes such as cacng6a (Sun et al., 2016). These transcriptomics changes were associated with apical effects which are summarised above.

The hypothalamus‐pituitary‐gonadal‐liver axis in fish was affected in an organ‐specific and dose‐dependent manner following oral dietary exposure (Saunders et al., 2015). Lower doses particularly induced upregulation of oestrogen‐responsive genes including vitellogenin II, choriogenin H and ERα, while higher doses affected regulatory feedback mechanisms throughout the axis (Saunders et al., 2015).

In summary, there are few studies on mode of action of TBCO in mammalian systems. In fish, visual performance impairment represents the most sensitive endpoint investigated, occurring at just 10 μg/L through waterborne exposure during early development. Developmental abnormalities in zebrafish (mortality, spine curvature, uninflated swim bladders) showed significant effects at 20 μg/L waterborne exposure. Reduced fecundity in the same test system appeared at relatively low dietary doses of 40.6–58 μg/g feed, with reductions ranging from 18 to 56.8%. In terms of cellular effects, cytotoxicity in human neuroblastoma cells occurred at 0.5 μM, accompanied by membrane damage, apoptosis and oxidative stress. TBCO demonstrates multifaceted toxicity through disruption of steroidogenesis, affecting key enzyme expression (CYP11a, CYP19a, CYP17a, CYP21a) and showing both anti‐androgenic and anti‐oestrogenic activities. Developmental effects involve downregulation of ribosomal proteins and genes controlling eye development, cardiac function and calcium signalling. In fish, maternal transfer occurs, with reproductive impairment persisting through F2 generations before F3 recovery.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity in experimental mammalian species or epidemiological studies on TBCO.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for TBCO, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TBCO in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

No estimation of the exposure of breastfed infants was possible as the studies from European countries identified in the literature reported all values < LOD.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

5,6‐Dibromo‐1,10,11,12,13,13‐hexachloro‐11‐tricyclo[8.2.1.02,9]tridecane (DBHCTD) [CAS number 51936‐55‐1]

DBHCTD is a tricycloaliphatic compound with both bromine and chlorine substituents. It can form 10 stereoisomers among which six form pairs of enantiomers (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of DBHCTD are reported in Table 1. DBHCTD is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report any study on levels of DBHCTD in human samples. Since then, some studies have been identified in human milk and blood/serum from European countries, all reporting levels of DBHCTD below the LOD. These studies are briefly described below and details can be found in Appendix E (Table E.1).

Regarding human milk,

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) in which human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) were analysed, DBHCTD was not detected in any sample (LOD = 0.004 ng/g lipid).

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017) in which human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37) were analysed, DBHCTD was not detected in any sample (LOD = 0.004 ng/g lipid).

Regarding blood/serum samples,

Cequier et al. (2013) did not detect DBHCTD above the LOD in any of the 10 serum samples from Norway analysed (LOD = 5.4 pg/mL), or in any of the 46 serum samples from women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012 (Cequier et al., 2015).

Cequier et al. (2013) did not detect DBHCTD above the LOD in any of the 10 serum samples from Norway analysed (LOD = 5.4 pg/mL), or in any of the 46 serum samples from women from a Norwegian mother–child cohort established in 2012 (Cequier et al., 2015).

DBHCTD was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 63 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019), and in 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010 (LOD = 7.1 pg/25 μL, Sahlström et al., 2014).

DBHCTD was also reported below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 63 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019), and in 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010 (LOD = 7.1 pg/25 μL, Sahlström et al., 2014).

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since then, the comparative study of a number of BFRs conducted by the NTP has been published. DBHCTD was administered for 5 days via oral gavage to groups of 6 male Harlan Sprague–Dawley rats at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.54, 5.4, 54 and 541 mg/kg bw per day. After the 5‐day exposure, the dose levels of 54 and 541 mg/kg bw resulted in centrilobular hypertrophy and increased absolute liver weight (NTP, 2019b; Shockley et al., 2020a). Transcriptome changes associated with the Nrf2 pathway, metabolic and/or liver disease were reported at the top dose (Shockley et al., 2020a, 2020b).

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on DBHCTD (see Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD with ‘GOOD reliability’ (two models) and with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B).

Observations in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion. Since then, one publication was identified, pertaining to a population in China. In Appendix F an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion is shown, including the one on DBHCTD.

Guo et al. (2018) studied the association of serum DBHCTD levels with thyroid hormone levels in 10‐year‐old students living near a petrochemical complex in South China (for details see description in Section 3.2.4.2). Serum DBHCTD levels were statistically significantly associated with lower levels of serum T3, T4, FT4 and TSH.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified DBHCTD as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

No experimental data on genotoxicity were identified. Computational analysis indicates that DBHCTD does not induce mutations in S. Typhimurium, but no information is available on clastogenicity or aneugenicity. It is therefore not possible to conclude on genotoxicity overall.

One 5‐day‐study in rats indicates possible effects on the liver at doses of 54 mg/kg bw per day and higher. No other toxicity data were available.

In view of the absence of toxicological studies except for the single 5‐day study, no Reference Point could be identified.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on DBHCTD in food were submitted to EFSA. In the literature, one study included DBHCTD, but it was not detected above the LOD (Fernandes et al., 2016, see Section 3.1.2).

No exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

No estimation of the exposure of breastfed infants was possible as the studies from European countries identified in the literature reported all values < LOD.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

1,2,3,4,7,7‐Hexachloro‐5‐(2,3,4,5‐tetrabromophenyl)‐bicyclo[2.2.1]hept‐2‐ene (HCTBPH) [CAS number 34571‐16‐9]

HCTBPH belongs to the group of ‘dechlorane’ chemicals. It is a tricyclic compound with three chiral carbons, leading to the formation of three stereoisomers each one forming enantiomeric pairs. Dechloranes were widely used as substitutes for Mirex and used primarily as a pesticide but also as an additive flame retardant in plastics, rubber, paint, paper and electrical equipment (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of HCTBPH are reported in Table 1. HCTBPH is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on HCTBPH (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for HCTBPH with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (three models) and positive Ames test results with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Studies in other model systems and mode of action

Bajard et al. (2021) reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for HCTBPH, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model predicted medium probability of binding to the antagonist conformation of the AR.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on HCTBPH.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for HCTBPH, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on HCTBPH in food were submitted to EFSA. In the literature, one study included HCTBPH, but it was not detected above the LOD (Fernandes et al., 2016, see Section 3.1.2 ).

No exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Pentabromobenzyl acrylate (PBB‐Acr) [CAS number 59447‐55‐1]

PBB‐Acr is reactive flame retardant and as with other reactive BFRs is found at relatively lower levels in the environment. PBB‐Acr is a polar pentabromobenzyl alcohol derivative that is reactive, and suitable for polymerisation reactions, via the acrylate group (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of PBB‐Acr are reported in Table 1. PBB‐Acr is currently registered under REACH (100–1000 tonnes per annum) (see Table 2).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since then, one repeated dose toxicity study was described in the REACH dossier 37 , as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original study report. The REACH dossier reported that:

Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage for 28 days to 0, 15, 150 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of the technical product FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% PBB‐OH). Some animals from the control and highest dose groups were retained for a minimum two‐week recovery period. Dose‐related clinical signs were observed, e.g. increased salivation, alopecia and greasy fur. Animals had a lower bodyweight gain during the treatment period at 1000 mg/kg bw per day and recovered after. This reflects the lower food consumption. Increased water consumption was also seen in both sexes receiving 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Small disturbances in haematological parameters were recorded following treatment for male and female rats at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. For rats receiving 150 mg/kg bw per day, slightly lower haemoglobin content, lower total red blood cell counts and slightly shorter thrombotest times were seen for males only. Small electrolyte disturbances were seen at the highest dose in both sexes and triglyceride levels were notably increased and urea nitrogen level were decreased in females. At 150 mg/kg bw per day, most of these disturbances were also noted but to a lesser degree. Urinary volume was increased and the specific gravity was lower for rats of both sexes treated at 150 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Increased absolute kidney weights was noted for female rats at 150 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Macroscopic changes at 1000 mg/kg bw per day include enlarged kidneys for five females, irregular cortical scarring of the kidneys for one male and two females and alopecia for three females; two females treated at 150 mg/kg bw per day had enlarged kidneys. Renal tubular lesions consisting of cortical wedge and ray‐shaped tubular epithelial basophilia were noted at 1000 mg/kg bw per day in four males and five females. The CONTAM Panel identified a NOAEL of 15 mg/kg bw per day.

Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage for 28 days to 0, 15, 150 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of the technical product FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% PBB‐OH). Some animals from the control and highest dose groups were retained for a minimum two‐week recovery period. Dose‐related clinical signs were observed, e.g. increased salivation, alopecia and greasy fur. Animals had a lower bodyweight gain during the treatment period at 1000 mg/kg bw per day and recovered after. This reflects the lower food consumption. Increased water consumption was also seen in both sexes receiving 1000 mg/kg bw per day.
