Small electrolyte disturbances were seen at the highest dose in both sexes and triglyceride levels were notably increased and urea nitrogen level were decreased in females. At 150 mg/kg bw per day, most of these disturbances were also noted but to a lesser degree. Urinary volume was increased and the specific gravity was lower for rats of both sexes treated at 150 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Increased absolute kidney weights was noted for female rats at 150 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Macroscopic changes at 1000 mg/kg bw per day include enlarged kidneys for five females, irregular cortical scarring of the kidneys for one male and two females and alopecia for three females; two females treated at 150 mg/kg bw per day had enlarged kidneys. Renal tubular lesions consisting of cortical wedge and ray‐shaped tubular epithelial basophilia were noted at 1000 mg/kg bw per day in four males and five females. The CONTAM Panel identified a NOAEL of 15 mg/kg bw per day.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, information on genotoxicity studies was available in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) was reported to be negative in reverse mutation assays (plate incorporation assays according to OECD TG471) in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537, TA1538 and E. coli WP2 and WP2uvrA in the presence and absence of S9 mix from Aroclor‐induced rat liver.

FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) was reported to be negative in reverse mutation assays (plate incorporation assays according to OECD TG471) in S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537, TA1538 and E. coli WP2 and WP2uvrA in the presence and absence of S9 mix from Aroclor‐induced rat liver.

In an in vitro chromosome aberration test in CHO cells (OECD TG473), FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) was reported to be negative in the presence and absence of S9 mix.

In an in vitro chromosome aberration test in CHO cells (OECD TG473), FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) was reported to be negative in the presence and absence of S9 mix.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on PBB‐Acr (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for PBB‐Acr with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models) and with ‘LOW reliability’ (one model). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this negative prediction was consistent with the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Ezechiáš et al. (2012) used yeast reporter‐gene assays to determine the potential to interfere with oestrogenic and androgenic pathways, and reported that PBB‐Acr exhibited slight anti‐androgenic activity, as it was able to inhibit testosterone activation of AR with a nominal IC50 of 3.0 μM.

Bajard et al. (2021) reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for PBB‐Acr, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model predicted medium probability of binding to the antagonist conformation of the AR. Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified PBB‐Acr as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response

Changes in clinical signs, body weight gain, haematology, biochemical parameters and macroscopic and histopathological changes in the kidney were observed in rats exposed for 28 days to FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) at 150 mg/kg bw per day. This was the only toxicological study available.

According to the data reported in the REACH dossier, FR‐1025M (containing 95% PBB‐Acr and 5% pentabromobenzyl alcohol) was not mutagenic in bacteria and not clastogenic in CHO cells in the presence and absence of metabolic activation. The aneugenic potential was not investigated. Based on this in vitro information, the CONTAM Panel concluded that PBB‐Acr is not genotoxic.

In the absence of long‐term toxicity data, reproductive/developmental toxicity or neurodevelopmental toxicity studies, a Reference Point could not be identified.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data of PBB‐Acr in food were submitted to EFSA, but some studies in the scientific literature were identified (see Section 3.1.2 for an overview).

No exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the limited data on hazard, and lack of occurrence and dietary exposure data for PBB‐Acr, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the conclusion on genotoxicity of PBB‐Acr using the methods described in Section 2.8.

Information on genotoxicity was retrieved from the REACH dossier, reporting negative results for all the necessary studies. No sources of uncertainty were indicated, but the CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. Taking this into account, the CONTAM Panel judged with > 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that PBB‐Acr is not genotoxic.

Tribromoneopentyl alcohol (TBNPA) [CAS number 1522‐92‐5]

The previous Opinion (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b) reported that TBNPA is used together with tris(chloroalkyl) phosphate to form a base for the technical product SaFRon 6600 (Weil & Levchik, 2009). As many as 79 commercial sources of TBNPA have been indicated. It has a relatively strong resistance to biodegradation (0%–7% degradation after 28 days).

The physicochemical properties of TBNPA are reported in Table 1. TBNPA is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that TBNPA was tested in the Ames test using S. Typhimurium TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 in presence and absence of metabolic activation (10% Aroclor 1254 induced rat or hamster liver S9). TBNPA was not mutagenic in the absence and in the presence of rat liver S9; whereas it was positive in TA100 and TA1535 38 in the presence of hamster liver S9 (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b; Mortelmans et al., 1986).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since then, no studies were identified in the literature search, but the ECHA CLH Report (2019) and the ECHA RAC Opinion (2020) proposing harmonised classification and labelling at EU level of TBNPA, did report several studies. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The ECHA CLH Report and the ECHA RAC Opinion reported that:

−In a 30‐day oral study in Sprague–Dawley rats with TBNPA (purity 98%) at 0, 10, 30, 100 or 300 mg/kg bw per day (Anonymous, 1973), kidney damage and urinary bladder hyperplasia were observed in males from 100 mg/kg bw per day. Additional treatment‐related effects were: increase in serum urea nitrogen content in males receiving 300 mg/kg bw per day and decrease in serum glutamic‐pyruvic transaminase (SGPT) in males at the two top doses. No changes were noted in any of the females. The reported NOAELs were 30 mg/kg bw per day for males based on the kidney and urinary bladder effects and 300 mg/kg bw per day for females.

In a 30‐day oral study in Sprague–Dawley rats with TBNPA (purity 98%) at 0, 10, 30, 100 or 300 mg/kg bw per day (Anonymous, 1973), kidney damage and urinary bladder hyperplasia were observed in males from 100 mg/kg bw per day. Additional treatment‐related effects were: increase in serum urea nitrogen content in males receiving 300 mg/kg bw per day and decrease in serum glutamic‐pyruvic transaminase (SGPT) in males at the two top doses. No changes were noted in any of the females. The reported NOAELs were 30 mg/kg bw per day for males based on the kidney and urinary bladder effects and 300 mg/kg bw per day for females.

−In a 14‐day study, Crl:CD(SD) rats were administered TBNPA (purity 98.4%) by gavage at doses of 0, 100, 300 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Anonymous, 2011). Enlargement of the liver with associated dark areas was seen in one female at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Urine staining occurred in males and females in the top dose group. Males receiving the top dose were killed early on day 4 of treatment for animal welfare reasons. The reported NOAEL was 300 mg/kg bw per day.

In a 14‐day study, Crl:CD(SD) rats were administered TBNPA (purity 98.4%) by gavage at doses of 0, 100, 300 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day (Anonymous, 2011). Enlargement of the liver with associated dark areas was seen in one female at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Urine staining occurred in males and females in the top dose group. Males receiving the top dose were killed early on day 4 of treatment for animal welfare reasons. The reported NOAEL was 300 mg/kg bw per day.

−In a 28‐day study, Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to TBNPA (purity 97%) at 0, 30, 150 or 500 mg/kg bw per day and the animals were allowed to recover for 14 days (Anonymous, 2015). Increased liver weight (predominantly at ≥ 150 mg/kg bw per day), and slight minimal centrilobular hypertrophy were observed. Slightly higher kidney weights were observed in females in the low dose group and in males in the medium dose group. All findings showed full recovery, with the exception of kidney weights which remained slightly elevated at the end of the recovery period for males in the top dose group. A dose‐related increase in urinary volume and slightly high total protein and glucose output in males was seen. All of these findings showed full recovery. At the top dose group, transient slightly low sodium concentration and slightly high potassium concentration were noted. No treatment‐related changes in sperm count and motility were observed. Vaginal lavages which were taken early morning during the 3‐week period from all females, prior to termination of the animals showed no treatment‐related changes in the oestrus cycle. In addition, there were no dose‐related changes in weight of ovaries, seminal vesicles, testis, ureter, uterus or vagina in comparison to control animals. The reported NOAEL was 500 mg/kg bw per day.

In a 28‐day study, Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to TBNPA (purity 97%) at 0, 30, 150 or 500 mg/kg bw per day and the animals were allowed to recover for 14 days (Anonymous, 2015). Increased liver weight (predominantly at ≥ 150 mg/kg bw per day), and slight minimal centrilobular hypertrophy were observed. Slightly higher kidney weights were observed in females in the low dose group and in males in the medium dose group. All findings showed full recovery, with the exception of kidney weights which remained slightly elevated at the end of the recovery period for males in the top dose group. A dose‐related increase in urinary volume and slightly high total protein and glucose output in males was seen. All of these findings showed full recovery. At the top dose group, transient slightly low sodium concentration and slightly high potassium concentration were noted. No treatment‐related changes in sperm count and motility were observed. Vaginal lavages which were taken early morning during the 3‐week period from all females, prior to termination of the animals showed no treatment‐related changes in the oestrus cycle. In addition, there were no dose‐related changes in weight of ovaries, seminal vesicles, testis, ureter, uterus or vagina in comparison to control animals. The reported NOAEL was 500 mg/kg bw per day.

−In a 90‐day study, TBNPA (97% pure) was administered by gavage to Sprague–Dawley rats at doses of 0, 50, 150 or 450 mg/kg bw per day and the animals were allowed to recover for 28 days (Anonymous, 2020). There was an increase in blood urea nitrogen at 450 mg/kg bw per day and in creatinine at ≥ 150 mg/kg bw per day in males. This increase was associated with microscopic findings of increased eosinophilic droplets in the tubular epithelium consisting of tubular casts in kidneys. Microscopic changes were noted in kidneys and urinary bladder in males. In kidneys, increased eosinophilic droplets were noted in the tubular epithelium in the cortex of six males treated at 150 mg/kg bw per day and all males treated at 450 mg/kg bw per day but not in females. A single incidence of papillary necrosis was also observed at 450 mg/kg bw per day males. Diffuse epithelial hyperplasia was noted in urinary bladder of six males at 150 mg/kg bw per day and all males and one female at 450 mg/kg bw per day. All the changes reversed at the end of recovery period. The reported NOAEL was 50 mg/kg bw per day.

In a 90‐day study, TBNPA (97% pure) was administered by gavage to Sprague–Dawley rats at doses of 0, 50, 150 or 450 mg/kg bw per day and the animals were allowed to recover for 28 days (Anonymous, 2020). There was an increase in blood urea nitrogen at 450 mg/kg bw per day and in creatinine at ≥ 150 mg/kg bw per day in males. This increase was associated with microscopic findings of increased eosinophilic droplets in the tubular epithelium consisting of tubular casts in kidneys. Microscopic changes were noted in kidneys and urinary bladder in males. In kidneys, increased eosinophilic droplets were noted in the tubular epithelium in the cortex of six males treated at 150 mg/kg bw per day and all males treated at 450 mg/kg bw per day but not in females. A single incidence of papillary necrosis was also observed at 450 mg/kg bw per day males. Diffuse epithelial hyperplasia was noted in urinary bladder of six males at 150 mg/kg bw per day and all males and one female at 450 mg/kg bw per day. All the changes reversed at the end of recovery period. The reported NOAEL was 50 mg/kg bw per day.

The ECHA CLH Report (2019) also reported on a developmental toxicity study in Sprague–Dawley rats:

Pregnant females were exposed by gavage from GD6‐19 to TBNPA (FR‐513, purity 97.6%) at doses of 0, 100, 300, 500/1000 mg/kg bw per day. The highest dose was reduced from 1000 to 500 mg/kg bw per day due to post dosing toxicity. Two animals were killed due to animal welfare reasons. Gravid uterine weight in the dosed animals was lower. No clear findings of developmental toxicity were observed. Minor effects on ossification in the medium and high‐dose groups were within the range of the historical control data (Anonymous, 2016).

No multi‐generation reproductive toxicity study has been retrieved. However, in the 28‐day oral study in rats with a 14‐day recovery period described above, some relevant reproduction parameters were investigated but no indications of effects on sexual function and fertility have been identified (Anonymous, 2015; as cited in ECHA CLH Report, 2019).

No long‐term or carcinogenicity studies on TBPNA alone have been identified.

The CONTAM Panel noted that TBNPA was one of the impurities (about 7%) of the technical products containing DBNPG (see Section 3.2.6). The ECHA CLH Report (2019) included further information as follows: TBNPA and DBNPG are classified within the Small Brominated Linear and Branched Alkyl Alcohols group, comprising 61 compounds. These substances were identified by several (Q)SAR models, including the OECD QSAR Toolbox and exhibit highly similar chemical structures. In terms of chemical reactivity, TBNPA and DBNPG share several characteristics, such as comparable electrophilic properties, the presence of bromine and hydroxyl groups attached to primary carbon atoms and the potential for radical activation. Additionally, the aliphatic halogen moiety serves as a structural alert for both carcinogenicity and mutagenicity. Consequently, TBNPA and DBNPG were found to cluster within the same (Q)SAR‐based groupings associated with genotoxic and carcinogenic potential.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, the ECHA CLH Report (2019) and the ECHA RAC Opinion (2020) reported on several studies that are summarised below. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The ECHA CLH Report and the ECHA RAC Opinion (2020) reported that:

− TBNPA (FR‐513, purity 98%) was negative in a reverse mutation assay in S. Typhimurium TA1535, TA1537, TA 98 and TA 100 in the absence of S9 mix, but was positive in the presence of S9 mix (Aroclor‐1254 induced hamster liver S9 mix) in TA1535 and TA100 (Anonymous, 1996).

TBNPA (FR‐513, purity 98%) was negative in a reverse mutation assay in S. Typhimurium TA1535, TA1537, TA 98 and TA 100 in the absence of S9 mix, but was positive in the presence of S9 mix (Aroclor‐1254 induced hamster liver S9 mix) in TA1535 and TA100 (Anonymous, 1996).

− TBNPA (up to 500 μg/mL, FR‐513, purity 97%) was negative in an in vitro gene mutation test in mouse lymphoma L5178Y cells (TK locus) in the absence of S9 mix, whereas it was positive in the presence of S9 mix. Cytotoxicity was seen at 333 μg/mL and above with and without metabolic activation (Anonymous, 2004).

TBNPA (up to 500 μg/mL, FR‐513, purity 97%) was negative in an in vitro gene mutation test in mouse lymphoma L5178Y cells (TK locus) in the absence of S9 mix, whereas it was positive in the presence of S9 mix. Cytotoxicity was seen at 333 μg/mL and above with and without metabolic activation (Anonymous, 2004).

−The ability of TBNPA (100–2000 μg/mL, FR‐513, purity 97%) to induce chromosomal aberrations was tested in vitro in human peripheral lymphocytes in the absence and the presence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐1254 induced rat liver S9 mix). TBNPA was positive in the presence of S9 and in the absence of S9 at 1000 μg/mL. Cytotoxicity was observed as low as at 100 μg/mL, with S9 (Anonymous, 2004).

The ability of TBNPA (100–2000 μg/mL, FR‐513, purity 97%) to induce chromosomal aberrations was tested in vitro in human peripheral lymphocytes in the absence and the presence of metabolic activation (Aroclor‐1254 induced rat liver S9 mix). TBNPA was positive in the presence of S9 and in the absence of S9 at 1000 μg/mL. Cytotoxicity was observed as low as at 100 μg/mL, with S9 (Anonymous, 2004).

−In vivo, TBNPA (FR‐513, purity 98.1%) did not induce micronuclei in bone marrow cells of NMRI mice exposed by the oral route up to 300 mg/kg bw, however, it was not demonstrated that the bone marrow had been exposed (Anonymous, 2007).

In vivo, TBNPA (FR‐513, purity 98.1%) did not induce micronuclei in bone marrow cells of NMRI mice exposed by the oral route up to 300 mg/kg bw, however, it was not demonstrated that the bone marrow had been exposed (Anonymous, 2007).

− TBNPA (FR‐513, purity NR) was negative in vivo in a UDS test in rat hepatocytes exposed (2 or 16h) by gavage up to 2000 mg/kg bw (Anonymous, 2007).

TBNPA (FR‐513, purity NR) was negative in vivo in a UDS test in rat hepatocytes exposed (2 or 16h) by gavage up to 2000 mg/kg bw (Anonymous, 2007).

TBNPA has been classified by the ECHA's RAC as a ‘germ cell mutagen Category 2, H341: Suspected of causing genetic defects’. Based on read‐across from DBNPG, ECHA's RAC supported the classification of TBNPA as ‘Carc 1B, H350: May cause cancer’.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on TBNPA (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results for TBNPA with ‘GOOD reliability’ (four models). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B). The CONTAM Panel noted that this positive prediction was consistent with the available experimental data.

The QSAR Toolbox profilers provided the following information on the structural alerts and predicted mode of toxicity: TBNPA triggers the ‘Aliphatic halides’ alert according to the ‘DNA binding by OECD’ profiler, and the ‘Aliphatic halogens’ alert according to the ‘in vitro mutagenicity (Ames test) by ISS’ profiler because of the aliphatic Br atom (see Figure 2). In the QSAR Toolbox, this alert is associated with DNA alkylation by nucleophilic substitution. The QSAR Toolbox profiling results are provided in Annex B, while additional information on the alerts triggered can be consulted within the software.

Chemical structures of the other 21 BFRs included in the 2012 Opinion: Decabromodiphenyl ethane (DBDPE); 4‐(1,2‐Dibromoethyl)‐1,2‐dibromocyclohexane (DBE‐DBCH); 5,6‐Dibromo‐1,10,11,12,13,13‐hexachloro‐11‐tricyclo[8.2.1.02,9]tridecene (DBHCTD); 1,2‐Bis(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy)ethane (BTBPE); Hexabromobenzene (HBB); 1,2,3,4,7,7‐Hexachloro‐5‐(2,3,4,5‐tetrabromophenyl)‐bicyclo[2.2.1]hept‐2‐ene (HCTBPH); Pentabromobenzyl acrylate (PBB‐Acr); Pentabromoethylbenzene (PBEB); Pentabromotoluene (PBT); Tribromoneopentyl alcohol (TBNPA); 1,3,5‐Tris(2,3‐dibromopropyl)‐1,3,5‐triazine‐2,4,6‐trione (TDBP‐TAZTO); 1,2,5,6‐Tetrabromocyclooctane (TBCO); 2,3,5,6‐Tetrabromo‐p‐xylene (TBX); 1,3‐Bis(2,3‐dibromopropyl)‐5‐‐(2‐propen‐1‐yl)‐1,3,5‐triazine‐2,4,6(1H,3H,5H)‐trione (BDBP‐TAZTO); 1‐(2,3‐Dibromopropyl)‐3,5‐diallyl‐1,3,5‐triazine‐2,4,6(1H,3H,5H)‐trione (DBP‐TAZTO); Dibromostyrene (DBS); 2‐(2‐Hydroxyethoxy)ethyl 2‐hydroxypropyl 3,4,5,6‐tetrabromophthalate (HEEHP‐TEBP); Tetradecabromo‐1,4‐diphenoxybenzene (4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208); Tris(tribromoneopentyl) phosphate (TTBNPP); 2,4,6‐Tris(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy)‐1,3,5‐triazine (TTBP‐TAZ); Octabromotrimethylphenyl indane (OBTMPI).

The CONTAM Panel noted that the computational analysis supports the available experimental data.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified TBNPA as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'. The same authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that there are studies showing that TBNPA has some antagonistic activity towards the AR and PR, and agonistic activity towards AHR (Bajard et al., 2021).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

No toxicokinetic data were identified. A limited number of short‐term toxicity studies were available in the ECHA CLH Report (2019) and ECHA RAC Opinion (2020). Kidney damage and urinary bladder hyperplasia were observed in a 30‐day oral study in rats with TPNPA. In a 28‐day study in rats exposed by gavage to TBNPA increased liver weight and slight minimal centrilobular hypertrophy were observed as well as slightly higher kidneys. A dose‐related increase in urinary volume and slightly high total protein and glucose output. Administration of TBNPA to rats by gavage for 90 days induced an increase in blood urea nitrogen and in creatinine. This increase was associated with microscopic findings of increased eosinophilic droplets in the tubular epithelium consisting of tubular casts in kidneys and diffuse epithelial hyperplasia in the urinary bladder in males. The NOAEL was 50 mg/kg bw per day.

No clear findings of developmental toxicity were observed in a study in rats where pregnant females were exposed by gavage from GD6‐19 to TBNPA up to doses of 500/1000 mg/kg bw per day.

No multi‐generation reproductive toxicity study has been retrieved. However, in a 28‐day oral study in rats with a 14‐day recovery period, where some relevant reproduction parameters were investigated, no indications of effects on sexual function and fertility have been identified.

No long‐term or carcinogenicity studies on TBPNA have been identified.

According to the data reported in the ECHA CLH Report (2019) and the ECHA RAC Opinion (2020), TBNPA is mutagenic in vitro and clastogenic. Based on this information, and in the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, the CONTAM Panel concluded that it is likely that TBNPA is genotoxic. The carcinogenic potential of TBNPA is inferred from the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity, together with predictions based on structural similarity/read‐across considerations with DBNPG, which is genotoxic and carcinogenic (see Section 3.2.6).

The CONTAM Panel noted that the limited available data provide evidence of liver, kidney and urinary bladder toxicity but, particularly in the absence of carcinogenicity studies, the data are insufficient to identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TBNPA in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the lack of data on occurrence and exposure for TBNPA, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the conclusion on genotoxicity and carcinogenicity of TBNPA using the methods described in Section 2.8.

Based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity of TBNPA in the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, the CONTAM Panel concluded that it is likely that TBNPA is genotoxic and carcinogenic. Supporting this, the Panel noted the structural similarity with DBNPG which is genotoxic and carcinogenic.

Based on the overall evidence, and taking into account the associated uncertainties, the CONTAM Panel judged with 95%–99% certainty (i.e. probability) that TBNPA is genotoxic and carcinogenic.

2,3,5,6‐Tetrabromo‐p‐xylene (TBX) [CAS number 23488‐38‐2]

TBX is an environmentally stable aromatic compound with the potential to undergo reductive debromination (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). TBX is a non‐polymeric additive with a QSAR prediction to have the properties ‘(very)persistent’, ‘very bioaccumulative’ and ‘toxic’.28

The physicochemical properties of TBX are reported in Table 1. TBX is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

The previous Opinion did not report any study on levels of TBX in human samples. Since then, five studies have been identified in human milk and serum samples:

Regarding human milk,

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed 2 pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. TBX was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (LOD < 0.5 pg/g ww).

In Sweden, Sahlström et al. (2015) analysed 2 pooled human milk samples collected in 2009–2010. TBX was not detected in any of the two pooled samples (LOD < 0.5 pg/g ww).

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) and TBX was reported to be below the LOD (not reported).

Tao et al. (2017) analysed human milk samples collected in the UK in 2010 (n = 25) and 2014–2015 (n = 10) and TBX was reported to be below the LOD (not reported).

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), TBX was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37). It was not detected in samples from Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37, LOD = 0.001 ng/g lipid), while detection frequencies of 15% and 69% were reported for samples from Norway and the Netherlands, respectively, with mean and P95 concentrations across the two countries ranging from 0.019 to 0.035 ng/g lipid and from 0.038 to 0.128 ng/g lipid, respectively.

In the study by Čechová et al. (2017), TBX was analysed in human milk samples from Norway (2003–2006, n = 305), the Netherlands (2011–2014, n = 116) and Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37). It was not detected in samples from Slovakia (2011–2012, n = 37, LOD = 0.001 ng/g lipid), while detection frequencies of 15% and 69% were reported for samples from Norway and the Netherlands, respectively, with mean and P95 concentrations across the two countries ranging from 0.019 to 0.035 ng/g lipid and from 0.038 to 0.128 ng/g lipid, respectively.

Regarding levels in blood/serum,

TBX was not detected above the LOD (0.9 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010 (Sahlström et al., 2014).

TBX was not detected above the LOD (0.9 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010 (Sahlström et al., 2014).

Another study reported TBX below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 14 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Another study reported TBX below the LOD in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort (LOD = 14 pg/serum sample, volume not reported, Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on TBX (see Annex B).

VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for TBX with ‘GOOD reliability’ (three models). The Panel critically reviewed the predictions and considered them valid mainly due to the presence of relevant analogues known by the model with experimental and predicted values concordant with the prediction for the target compound (see Annex B).

Observations in humans

In the previous Opinion, no human studies on TBX were reported. Since then, three publications were identified, two pertaining to Chinese populations and one pertaining to a USA study population. Outcomes included thyroid hormone profiles (one study), type 2 diabetes (one study) and BMI (one study). In Appendix F, an overview of the epidemiological studies identified reporting on the BFRs considered in this Opinion, including those on TBX, is shown.

In the cross‐sectional study by Wang, Romanak, et al. (2019) in the USA (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), TBX concentrations were measured in wristbands in 101 adult study participants. TBX concentrations in the wristbands were not statistically significant associated with serum thyroid hormone profiles.

In the study by Zheng, Chen, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum TBX concentrations were not statistically significantly different between type 2 diabetes cases and controls in unadjusted comparisons (adjusted associations for single exposures were not reported) and TBX was not a main contributor to the mixture effects.

In the study by Zheng, Sun, et al. (2025) in China (for details see Section 3.2.4.2), serum TBX concentrations did not show statistically significant associations with BMI. Adjusted models are not presented.

Mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicity data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified TBX as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'. The same authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for TBX, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model predicted medium probability of binding to the antagonist conformation of the AR (Bajard et al., 2021).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

No experimental data on genotoxicity were identified. Computational analysis predicts that TBX does not induce mutations in S. Typhimurium, but no information is available on clastogenicity or aneugenicity. It is therefore not possible to conclude on genotoxicity overall.

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on TBX.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for TBX.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

Limited occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA (see Section 3.1.1) and no estimation of the dietary exposure based on these data was possible.

In the scientific literature, several studies were identified reporting on the occurrence of TBX and an overview is given in Section 3.1.2 and Appendix B.

One study was identified reporting estimates of dietary exposure to TBX. Sahlström et al. (2015) reported occurrence levels and exposure of TBX, among other BFRs from analysis in Swedish market basket food samples collected in 2010, food baskets comprising of fish, meat, vegetable oils, dairy products and eggs (n = 4 food baskets). TBX was detected only in fish (100% detection frequency) samples, at 20 pg/g ww, while concentration in other food categories were < LOD. The dietary exposure was based on measured concentrations and intake assumptions from questionnaire data about dietary habits of the participants. The estimated median dietary daily intake for both mothers and toddlers (n = 20) based on fish intake only, was 0.7 and 0.3 ng per day, respectively.

For breastfed infants, there were insufficient data in human milk from European countries reported in the literature to make robust exposure estimates. To provide an indication of the potential exposure, the CONTAM Panel made an estimation for average and high consumption of human milk (see Section 2.4) considering the available data reported in the literature. Only one study reported levels above the LOD (Čechová et al., 2017, see Section 3.3.13.1.2).

The exposure scenario based on average human milk consumption and the highest reported mean concentration in human milk across three European countries (Norway, the Netherlands, Slovakia) of 0.035 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.16 ng/kg bw per day.

The exposure scenario based on high human milk consumption and the highest reported P95 concentration of 0.128 ng/g lipid (Čechová et al., 2017), resulted in an estimate of 0.88 ng/kg bw per day.

It should be noted that these estimates are based on human milk samples from three European countries that were collected one or two decades ago. It is unknown whether this is representative of current exposure to this BFR.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible, due to the lack of a Reference Point and the limited data on dietary exposure.

1,3‐Bis(2,3‐dibromopropyl)‐5‐(2‐propen‐1‐yl)‐1,3,5‐triazine‐2,4,6(1H,3H,5H)‐trione (BDBP‐TAZTO) [CAS number 75795‐16‐3]

BDBP‐TAZTO is generally associated with TDBP‐TAZTO (see Section 3.3.7) which is used as an additive flame retardant. TDBP‐TAZTO is used as a mixture of the congeners substituted with one and two 2,3‐dibromopropyl groups (DBP‐TAZTO and BDBP‐TAZTO), respectively (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of BDBP‐TAZTO are reported in Table 1. BDBP‐TAZTO is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on BDBP‐TAZTO (see Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results with ‘LOW/MODERATE reliability’ (three models) and negative Ames test results with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model). The Panel assessed the validity of the positive predictions with moderate reliability according to VEGA, including the relevance of the analogues with experimental data identified by the software and the accuracy and consistency of their predictions. The Panel noted that the analogues were not sufficiently similar to the target compound to be confident in the reliability of the predictions, and thus these predictions were not further considered.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicological data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified BDBP‐TAZTO as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'. The same authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for BDBP‐TAZTO, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model predicted medium probability of binding to the antagonist conformation of the AR (Bajard et al., 2021).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on BDBP‐TAZTO.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for BDBP‐TAZTO, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on BDBP‐TAZTO in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Dibromostyrene (DBS) [CAS number 31780‐26‐4, 120359‐56‐0]

DBS is likely to occur as a mixture of isomers and is used for production of poly(dibromostyrene) (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). DBS is a reactive BFR with a QSAR prediction to be borderline ‘Persistent’ and borderline ‘Bioaccumulative’.28

The physicochemical properties of DBS are reported in Table 1. DBS is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

2‐(2‐Hydroxyethoxy)ethyl 2‐hydroxypropyl 3,4,5,6‐tetrabromophthalate (HEEHP‐TEBP) [CAS number 20566‐35‐2]

HEEHP‐TEBP is a highly polar tetrabromophthalic acid ester with one chiral carbon leading to formation of an enantiomeric pair. It is a phthalate ester with expected low environmental reactivity. Proposed uses are with production of foam for insulation (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b). HEEHP‐TEBP is a reactive BFR with a QSAR prediction to be ‘(very)persistent’ and ‘very mobille’.28

The physicochemical properties of HEEHP‐TEBP are reported in Table 1. HEEHP‐TEBP is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity of HEEHP‐TEBP in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion. The previous Opinion reported on one study with the technical product Saytex RB‐79 but no information on composition was reported. In that (range‐finding) study, the Saytex RB‐79 was given to female rats at a single gavage dose at 2000 mg/kg bw, and no effects were observed.

Regarding genotoxicity, the previous Opinion reported that the technical product Saytex RB‐79 (purity NR) tested in an Ames test with and without metabolic activation was not mutagenic. The technical product PHT4‐Diol (purity NR) was examined for mutagenic activity in S. Typhimurium and in Saccharomyces cerevisiae strain D4 with and without metabolic activation. No indication for mutagenicity was found (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

No new studies were identified since the previous Opinion.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on HEEHP‐TEBP (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity study in experimental animals, epidemiological studies or in vitro studies on HEEHP‐TEBP. One acute toxicity study and mutagenicity studies in bacteria and yeast conducted with the technical product Saytex RB‐79 and PHT4‐Diol did not show evidence of effects, but no information on the composition of these technical products was available.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for HEEHP‐TEBP, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on HEEHP‐TEBP in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and the lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Tetradecabromo‐1,4‐diphenoxybenzene (4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208) [CAS number CAS 58965‐66‐5]

4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 is a perbrominated compound that is expected to undergo photolysis in the environment. The high molecular mass may limit its bioavailability. It is used in high performance polyamide and linear polyester engineering resins and alloys (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 are reported in Table 1. 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results with ‘LOW/MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Hill et al. (2018) investigated the potential thyroidogenicity of 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 using an in vitro competitive protein binding assay with human thyroid hormone, transthyretin (hTTR) and albumin (hALB). The authors reported that 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 competed with T4 in binding to hTTR and hALB.

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicological data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208 as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'. The same authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model failed to predict due to the high molecular weight of this BFR (Bajard et al., 2021).

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, and it was not possible to conclude on its genotoxic potential.
