Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Tris(tribromoneopentyl) phosphate (TTBNPP) [CAS number 19186‐97‐1]

TTBNPP has been used in polypropylene products for uses in, e.g. carpets and stadium seats, and has potential for use in polyesters, polyamides, polystyrenes and polyolefins (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of TTBNPP are reported in Table 1. TTBNPP is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion.

Since then, toxicokinetics data have been added by the registrant(s) to the REACH dossier38. According to the REACH dossier, the technical TTBNPP product FR‐370 (purity 99.8%) was rapidly excreted in rats, primarily via faeces (81%–85%) and urine (2%) within 24 h of oral administration. This indicates very low oral bioavailability (2%) and no accumulation potential. The CONTAM Panel could not assess the data because it did not have access to the original study.

Levels in human samples

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, three studies were reported in the REACH dossier, 39 as submitted by the registrant(s), for the technical product FR‐370 or PB‐370. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original studies. The REACH dossier reported that:

In a 28‐day oral feeding study (OECD TG 407), male and female Sprague–Dawley CD rats were exposed to PB‐370 (purity NR) at 0, 4000, 8000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed. No effects were observed up to 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1361–1959 mg/kg bw per day for males and 1691–2081 mg/kg bw per day for females).

In a 28‐day oral feeding study (OECD TG 407), male and female Sprague–Dawley CD rats were exposed to PB‐370 (purity NR) at 0, 4000, 8000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed. No effects were observed up to 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1361–1959 mg/kg bw per day for males and 1691–2081 mg/kg bw per day for females).

In a 90‐day oral feeding study (OECD TG 408), male and female Sprague–Dawley CD rats were given PB‐370 (purity NR) at 0, 2000, 10,000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 158, 679 or 1358 mg/kg bw per day for males, and 0, 169, 843 or 1685 mg/kg bw per day for females). No effects were observed up to 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1358 mg/kg bw per day in males and 1685 mg/kg bw per day in females).

In a 90‐day oral feeding study (OECD TG 408), male and female Sprague–Dawley CD rats were given PB‐370 (purity NR) at 0, 2000, 10,000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 158, 679 or 1358 mg/kg bw per day for males, and 0, 169, 843 or 1685 mg/kg bw per day for females). No effects were observed up to 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1358 mg/kg bw per day in males and 1685 mg/kg bw per day in females).

In a developmental toxicity study (OECD TG 414), pregnant female Sprague–Dawley rats (CD (Crl:CDR (SD)IGS BR) were exposed by gavage from GD1–19 to PB‐370 (reported purity ‘99.1%’) at 0, 100, 300 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day. There were no adverse effects on the dams or on embryo‐fetal survival. Slightly reduced fetal weight and marginally increased placental weight were observed at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. No information on statistical significance was reported. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 1000 mg/kg bw per day. In the absence of the original data the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on a NOAEL on developmental toxicity.

In a developmental toxicity study (OECD TG 414), pregnant female Sprague–Dawley rats (CD (Crl:CDR (SD)IGS BR) were exposed by gavage from GD1–19 to PB‐370 (reported purity ‘99.1%’) at 0, 100, 300 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day. There were no adverse effects on the dams or on embryo‐fetal survival. Slightly reduced fetal weight and marginally increased placental weight were observed at 1000 mg/kg bw per day. No information on statistical significance was reported. The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 1000 mg/kg bw per day. In the absence of the original data the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on a NOAEL on developmental toxicity.

In a reproduction/developmental toxicity screening test (OECD TG 421), male and female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed orally via feed to FR‐370 (reported purity ‘99.8%’) at 0, 2000, 10,000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 160, 760 or 1590 mg/kg bw per day for males and 0, 400, 1780 or 4110 mg/kg bw per day for females). F0 animals were treated for 28 days before mating until termination either after all litters have been born for the males or after weaning of F1 generation for the females. F1 animals were treated from weaning until approximately 6 weeks of age. No adverse effects were observed in parental animals nor in F1 generation. The NOAEL for parental toxicity and reproduction was 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1590 mg/kg bw per day for males and 4110 mg/kg bw per day for females).

In a reproduction/developmental toxicity screening test (OECD TG 421), male and female Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed orally via feed to FR‐370 (reported purity ‘99.8%’) at 0, 2000, 10,000 or 20,000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 160, 760 or 1590 mg/kg bw per day for males and 0, 400, 1780 or 4110 mg/kg bw per day for females). F0 animals were treated for 28 days before mating until termination either after all litters have been born for the males or after weaning of F1 generation for the females. F1 animals were treated from weaning until approximately 6 weeks of age. No adverse effects were observed in parental animals nor in F1 generation. The NOAEL for parental toxicity and reproduction was 20,000 mg/kg feed (the highest dose tested, corresponding to 1590 mg/kg bw per day for males and 4110 mg/kg bw per day for females).

The first three studies were also reported in an US‐EPA Report on alternatives assessment for decabromodiphenyl ether (US‐EPA, 2014). The CONTAM Panel had no access to the original data.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, information on genotoxicity was retrieved from the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s) for the technical product FR‐370. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. The REACH dossier reported that:

FR‐370 (purity NR) was not mutagenic with and without metabolic activation in reverse mutation assays in S. Typhimurium (TA1535, TA1537, TA1538, TA98, TA100) or in the TK gene mutation assay in L5178Y mouse lymphoma cells (reported purity ‘99.6%’), and did not induce chromosomal aberrations in CHO Chinese hamster ovary cells with and without metabolic activation. Equivocal results for induction of numerical aberrations were also reported in CHO cells.

FR‐370 (purity NR) was not mutagenic with and without metabolic activation in reverse mutation assays in S. Typhimurium (TA1535, TA1537, TA1538, TA98, TA100) or in the TK gene mutation assay in L5178Y mouse lymphoma cells (reported purity ‘99.6%’), and did not induce chromosomal aberrations in CHO Chinese hamster ovary cells with and without metabolic activation. Equivocal results for induction of numerical aberrations were also reported in CHO cells.

The CONTAM Panel noted that the available information in the REACH dossier is insufficient to conclude on the genotoxic potential of the substance (cytotoxicity levels are often unclear, positive/equivocal results are scattered and not associated with a dose–response relationship, and precipitation of the test substance may have affected the reliability of the results). The same assays were reported in the US‐EPA Report on alternatives assessment for decabromodiphenyl ether (US‐EPA, 2014).

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for Ames test on TTBNPP (see above and Annex B).

VEGA models predicted positive Ames test results with ‘MODERATE reliability’ (one model) and negative Ames test results with ‘LOW/MODERATE reliability’ (three models). The CONTAM Panel assessed the validity of the positive predictions with moderate reliability according to VEGA, including the relevance of the analogues with experimental data identified by the software and the accuracy and consistency of their predictions. The Panel noted inconsistent data for different similar analogues, and thus these predictions were not further considered.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicological data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified TTBNPP as having ‘scarce toxicological data indicating lower toxicological concern'. In a later study, Bajard et al. (2023) reported that TTBNPP did not exhibit anti‐androgenic activity in an in vitro reporter gene assay.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

Several studies have been performed on PB‐370 or FR‐370. In these studies, no adverse effects were reported in 28‐day or 90‐day feeding toxicity studies in rats up to the highest dose tested 20,000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 1358 mg/kg bw per day in males and 1685 mg/kg bw per day in females for the 90‐day study) (purity NR). In a developmental toxicity study in rats exposed by gavage from GD1 to 19, there were no adverse effects on the dams or on embryo‐fetal survival up to the highest dose 1000 mg/kg bw per day. Slightly reduced fetal weight and marginally increased placental weight were reported at 1000 mg/kg bw per day (reported purity ‘99.1%’). No adverse effects were reported in the parental or F1 generation in a reproduction/developmental toxicity screening test in rats up to the highest dose tested 20,000 mg/kg feed (reported purity ‘99.8%’).

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, long‐term, neurodevelopmental or epidemiological studies on TTBNPP.

Based on the in vitro data reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel could not conclude on the genotoxicity of TTBNPP.

Based on the limited data available, and in the absence of access to the original study reports, the CONTAM Panel could not identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TTBNPP in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

2,4,6‐Tris(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy)‐1,3,5‐triazine (TTBP‐TAZ) [CAS number 25713‐60‐4]

TTBP‐TAZ is a non‐polymeric additive BFR. It has been assessed to be not PBT (‘persistent, bioaccumulative and toxic’) and not vPvB (‘very persistent and very bioaccumulative’). 40 TTBP‐TAZ may biotransform to the substance 2,4,6‐TBP, for which there is a concern for PMT (‘persistent, mobile, toxic’) or vPvM (‘very persistent, very mobile’) (Zheng et al., 2022). It is also possible that the hydrolysis of TTBP‐TAZ can form 2,4,6‐TBP.

TTBP‐TAZ occurs as technical products with different tradenames, such as SR 245 or FR‐245, that have been reported to be mono‐constituent substance (NICNAS, 2006).

The physicochemical properties of TTBP‐TAZ are reported in Table 1. TTBP‐TAZ is registered under REACH (1000–10,000 tonnes per annum) (see Table 2).

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion.

Since then, and based on the information in the REACH dossier,40 as submitted by the registrant(s), the technical TTBP‐TAZ product, FR‐245 (purity 99.9%), was reported to have very low oral bioavailability in rats (< 0.2%), and no accumulation potential has been found. The CONTAM Panel could not assess these data as it did not have access to the original study.

Levels in humans

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Toxicity in experimental animals

Studies considered in the previous EFSA assessment

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion.

Studies published since the previous EFSA assessment

Since the previous Opinion, studies with rats were summarised in the REACH dossier, 41 as submitted by the registrant(s) and in NICNAS (2006) for the technical products FR‐245 and SR‐245. The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original study reports. These sources reported that:

−An acute oral (gavage) toxicity study in rats with a single dose of the technical product SR‐245 (reported as 100% purity) of 2000 mg/kg bw was conducted and no mortalities were observed (LD50 > 2000 mg/kg bw).

An acute oral (gavage) toxicity study in rats with a single dose of the technical product SR‐245 (reported as 100% purity) of 2000 mg/kg bw was conducted and no mortalities were observed (LD50 > 2000 mg/kg bw).

−In a 28‐day oral (gavage) toxicity study (OECD TG 407) in Crj:CD(SD) rats, animals were given 0, 10, 50, 250 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of the technical product FR‐245 (reported as 100% purity). Two recovery groups were treated with the high dose (1000 mg/kg bw per day) or the vehicle for 28 days and then maintained without treatment for further 14 days. Clinical signs, body weight development, food and water consumption were monitored during the study. Haematology, blood chemistry and urinalysis were evaluated for all non‐recovery group animals at the end of the treatment period and for recovery group animals at the end of the recovery period. All animals were subjected to gross necropsy examination and histopathological evaluation of selected tissues from non‐recovery high dose and control animals. In females, decreased reticulocytes count in the 10 mg/kg bw per day and higher groups, decreased mean corpuscular haemoglobin in the 50 and 250 mg/kg per day groups were noted. However, these were deemed not to be dose related and were not present in the animals that were allowed to recover before sacrifice. The NOEL was reported to be > 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

In a 28‐day oral (gavage) toxicity study (OECD TG 407) in Crj:CD(SD) rats, animals were given 0, 10, 50, 250 or 1000 mg/kg bw per day of the technical product FR‐245 (reported as 100% purity). Two recovery groups were treated with the high dose (1000 mg/kg bw per day) or the vehicle for 28 days and then maintained without treatment for further 14 days. Clinical signs, body weight development, food and water consumption were monitored during the study. Haematology, blood chemistry and urinalysis were evaluated for all non‐recovery group animals at the end of the treatment period and for recovery group animals at the end of the recovery period. All animals were subjected to gross necropsy examination and histopathological evaluation of selected tissues from non‐recovery high dose and control animals. In females, decreased reticulocytes count in the 10 mg/kg bw per day and higher groups, decreased mean corpuscular haemoglobin in the 50 and 250 mg/kg per day groups were noted. However, these were deemed not to be dose related and were not present in the animals that were allowed to recover before sacrifice. The NOEL was reported to be > 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

−A 91‐day oral gavage toxicity study (OECD TG 408) in Crj:CD(SD) rats with a 28‐day recovery period was conducted using FR‐245 (reported as 99.7% purity). Three treatment groups (100, 350 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day) comprising 10 male and 10 female rats each received daily doses for 91 consecutive days, alongside a vehicle control group. Two recovery groups (five males and five females) received either the high dose (1000 mg/kg bw per day) or vehicle for 91 days, followed by a 28‐day treatment‐free period. The study assessed reproductive and neurotoxicity endpoints in addition to standard parameters. Daily oral gavage administration of FR‐245 to Sprague–Dawley rats for 91 consecutive days produced no adverse effects on body weight, food consumption, haematology, blood biochemistry, urinalysis, organ weight, or gross and histopathological findings throughout the in‐life phase, necropsy or histological assessment. No treatment‐related changes were observed in sperm count or motility. Vaginal lavages showed no treatment‐related alterations in the oestrous cycle. Furthermore, no dose‐related changes in ovarian, seminal vesicle, testicular, ureteral, uterine or vaginal organ weights were detected compared with controls. The NOEL was reported to be > 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

A 91‐day oral gavage toxicity study (OECD TG 408) in Crj:CD(SD) rats with a 28‐day recovery period was conducted using FR‐245 (reported as 99.7% purity). Three treatment groups (100, 350 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day) comprising 10 male and 10 female rats each received daily doses for 91 consecutive days, alongside a vehicle control group. Two recovery groups (five males and five females) received either the high dose (1000 mg/kg bw per day) or vehicle for 91 days, followed by a 28‐day treatment‐free period. The study assessed reproductive and neurotoxicity endpoints in addition to standard parameters. Daily oral gavage administration of FR‐245 to Sprague–Dawley rats for 91 consecutive days produced no adverse effects on body weight, food consumption, haematology, blood biochemistry, urinalysis, organ weight, or gross and histopathological findings throughout the in‐life phase, necropsy or histological assessment. No treatment‐related changes were observed in sperm count or motility. Vaginal lavages showed no treatment‐related alterations in the oestrous cycle. Furthermore, no dose‐related changes in ovarian, seminal vesicle, testicular, ureteral, uterine or vaginal organ weights were detected compared with controls. The NOEL was reported to be > 1000 mg/kg bw per day.

−A developmental toxicity study in Crl:CD(SD) rats was conducted in accordance with OECD TG 414 to evaluate the potential maternal and developmental toxicity of the technical product BTAC‐245 (purity NR). Doses of 250, 500 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day test substance were administered by gavage during GD5–19. No effects of test substance treatment were observed in maternal rats across any of the parameters examined. At caesarean section, no treatment‐related abnormalities were noted in mean gravid uterine weights, numbers of corpora lutea and implantations, percentage implantation index, or pre‐ and post‐implantation loss in any treated groups. Regarding fetuses, the number of live fetuses, percentage fetal deaths, fetal body weight, placental weights and sex ratio in treated groups were comparable to the control group. No treated groups demonstrated adverse effects of test substance treatment in external, visceral or skeletal examinations of live fetuses. Based on the absence of toxicity (maternal and fetal) and absence of teratogenic findings, the authors concluded that the dose level of 1000 mg/kg per day of TTBP‐TAZ represented the NOAEL for both maternal rats and fetuses.

A developmental toxicity study in Crl:CD(SD) rats was conducted in accordance with OECD TG 414 to evaluate the potential maternal and developmental toxicity of the technical product BTAC‐245 (purity NR). Doses of 250, 500 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day test substance were administered by gavage during GD5–19. No effects of test substance treatment were observed in maternal rats across any of the parameters examined. At caesarean section, no treatment‐related abnormalities were noted in mean gravid uterine weights, numbers of corpora lutea and implantations, percentage implantation index, or pre‐ and post‐implantation loss in any treated groups. Regarding fetuses, the number of live fetuses, percentage fetal deaths, fetal body weight, placental weights and sex ratio in treated groups were comparable to the control group. No treated groups demonstrated adverse effects of test substance treatment in external, visceral or skeletal examinations of live fetuses. Based on the absence of toxicity (maternal and fetal) and absence of teratogenic findings, the authors concluded that the dose level of 1000 mg/kg per day of TTBP‐TAZ represented the NOAEL for both maternal rats and fetuses.

In summary, from the scientific data identified it may be concluded that sub‐chronic exposure to the technical products SR 245, FR‐245 and BTAC‐245 containing TTBP‐TAZ did not have adverse effects on adult rats up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day. This dose did not cause systemic toxicity or developmental toxicity, and did not affect reproductive organs.

Genotoxicity

Since the previous Opinion, information on the same genotoxicity studies was available in the REACH dossier, as submitted by the registrant(s) and in NICNAS (2006). The CONTAM Panel did not have access to the original data. These sources reported that:

The technical products FR‐245 and SR‐245 (reported purity 100%) were not mutagenic with and without metabolic activation in reverse mutation assays in S. Typhimurium (TA1535, TA1537, TA98, TA100), and SR‐245 (purity NR) did not induce chromosomal aberrations in human lymphocytes with and without metabolic activation. The technical product BTAC‐245 (reported purity 99.8%) and FR‐245 were not mutagenic in the TK gene mutation assay in L5178Y mouse lymphoma cells with and without metabolic activation.

Based on the information reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel concluded that TTBP‐TAZ is not genotoxic.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox identified experimental data for the Ames test on TTBP‐TAZ (see above and Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results with ‘LOW/MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

No studies were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response analysis

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on TTBP‐TAZ.

Based on the limited data available identified, SR/FR‐245 did not show short‐term (28‐ and 90‐day) or developmental toxicity in rats at doses up to 1000 mg/kg bw per day, the highest dose tested.

No chronic/carcinogenicity, reproductive or neurodevelopmental toxicity studies were identified.

The technical product SR/FR‐245 was not mutagenic in bacteria or in mammalian cells, and did not induce chromosomal damage. Based on the information reported in the REACH dossier, the CONTAM Panel concluded that these in vitro data indicated that TTBP‐TAZ was not genotoxic.

The CONTAM Panel did not have information on the content of TTBP‐TAZ in the technical products tested.

Because of the limited data available, the CONTAM Panel concluded that it was not possible to identify a Reference Point.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on TTBP‐TAZ in food were submitted to EFSA, and no occurrence or exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Due to the limited data on hazard, occurrence and exposure for TTBP‐TAZ, uncertainty analysis was performed only for the conclusion on genotoxicity of TTBP‐TAZ using the methods described in Section 2.8 .

Information on genotoxicity was retrieved from the REACH dossier and NICNAS (2006), reporting negative results for all the necessary studies. No sources of uncertainty were indicated. Taking this into account, the CONTAM Panel judged with > 95% certainty (i.e. probability) that TTBP‐TAZ is not genotoxic.

Octabromotrimethylphenyl indane (OBTMPI) [CAS number 1084889‐51‐9, 1025956‐65‐3, 893843‐07‐7, 155613‐93‐7]

OBTMPI is a non‐polymeric additive BFR that has been assessed by QSAR to be borderline ‘Persistent’, ‘Bioaccumulative’, ‘Toxic’ (ECHA, 2024). OBTMPI contains eight bromine atoms on the two aromatic rings. It has one chiral carbon, leading to the formation of one enantiomeric pair (EFSA CONTAM Panel, 2012b).

The physicochemical properties of OBTMPI are reported in Table 1. OBTMPI is currently not registered under REACH (see Table 2), and thus its production volume and use within the EU should be lower than one tonne per year.

Hazard identification and characterisation

Toxicokinetics

No information on toxicokinetics was identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Levels in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion. Since then, two studies have been identified reporting levels in blood in European countries (see Appendix E, Table E.1):

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect OBTMPI above the LOD (18 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

Sahlström et al. (2014) did not detect OBTMPI above the LOD (18 pg/25 μL) in any of the 24 matched serum samples from mothers and their toddlers collected in Sweden between 2009 and 2010.

OBTMPI was also measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort with a low detection frequency of 2%, and a reported geometric mean of 1.3 ng/g lipid (Tay et al., 2019).

OBTMPI was also measured in 61 serum samples collected in 2013 from a Norwegian cohort with a low detection frequency of 2%, and a reported geometric mean of 1.3 ng/g lipid (Tay et al., 2019).

Toxicity in experimental animals

No data on toxicity in experimental mammalian species were identified in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Computational analysis

The QSAR Toolbox did not identify any experimental data for the Ames test on OBTMPI (see Annex B). VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results with ‘LOW/MODERATE reliability’ (four models). These predictions were not further considered due to their limited reliability.

Observations in humans

No studies were reported in the previous Opinion, nor in the current update.

Mode of action

Bajard et al. (2019) collected in vivo toxicological data on 62 FRs to establish prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern; the authors classified OBTMPI as having ‘insufficient toxicological data to evaluate the toxicological concern'. The same authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from published articles and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes; the authors reported that no data were retrieved for OBTMPI, and that the opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model failed to predict due to the high molecular weight of this BFR (Bajard et al., 2021).

Das et al. (2021) performed in silico toxicity predictions and reported that OBTMPI exhibited some oestrogen antagonist, androgen antagonist and oestrogen binding capability.

Consideration of critical effects and dose–response

The CONTAM Panel could not identify any toxicokinetic, toxicity or epidemiological studies on OBTMPI.

It was therefore not possible to identify a Reference Point for OBTMPI, and it was not possible conclude on its genotoxic potential.

Occurrence data and dietary exposure assessment

No occurrence data on OBTMPI in food were submitted to EFSA. In the literature, one study included OBTMPI but it was not detected above the LOD (Fernandes et al., 2016, see Section 3.1.2).

No exposure data were identified in the literature, either in the previous Opinion or in the current update. Therefore, no estimation of the dietary exposure was possible.

Risk characterisation

No risk characterisation was possible due to the lack of a Reference Point and lack of information on potential dietary exposure.

CONCLUSIONS

This assessment is an update of the EFSA CONTAM Panel Scientific Opinion on Emerging and Novel Brominated Flame Retardants (BFRs) in Food published by EFSA in 2012. The update focuses on the six BFRs considered in the 2012 Opinion and covered in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (TDBPP, EBTEBPI, HBCYD, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and DBNPG) and the remaining 21 BFRs considered in the 2012 Opinion (BTBPE, DBDPE, DBE‐DBCH, DBHCTD, HBB, HCTBPH, OBTMPI, PBB‐Acr, PBEB, PBT, TBNPA, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, TBX, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, TTBNPP, TTBP‐TAZ). It takes into account the occurrence data in food and biological samples submitted to EFSA after the publication of the previous Opinion, as well as the newly available scientific information of relevance to hazard identification and characterisation.

Hazard identification and characterisation

The information available on the toxicity of the BFRs included in the Terms of Reference varied greatly across compounds, but was in general scarce.

The information available on the toxicity of the BFRs included in the Terms of Reference varied greatly across compounds, but was in general scarce.

Regarding genotoxicity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that: −Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.− TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).− DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.− BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.−QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.−Insufficient (TTBNPP), or no data (HBCYD, EH‐TBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, OBTMPI) were identified to allow an assessment of the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs.

Regarding genotoxicity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that: −Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.− TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).− DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.− BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.−QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.−Insufficient (TTBNPP), or no data (HBCYD, EH‐TBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, OBTMPI) were identified to allow an assessment of the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs.

−Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.

Based on the information provided in the REACH dossier, EBTEBPI, DBDPE, PBB‐Acr and TTBP‐TAZ are not genotoxic.

− TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).

TDBPP is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (in kidney) and mice (in kidney, liver, forestomach, lung).

− DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).

DBNPG is genotoxic and carcinogenic in rats (e.g. in skin, subcutaneous tissue, mammary gland, Zymbal's gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small and large intestines, mesothelium, urinary bladder, lung and thyroid gland) and mice (e.g. in Harderian gland, lung, subcutaneous tissue, forestomach).

−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.

In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo genotoxicity data, TBNPA is considered genotoxic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity. TBNPA is predicted to be carcinogenic based on the positive in vitro mutagenicity and clastogenicity supported by structural similarity with the genotoxic and carcinogenic DBNPG.

−In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.

In the absence of conclusive negative results for in vivo mutagenicity, DBE‐DBCH is considered genotoxic based on positive in vitro mutagenicity in mammalian cells.

− BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB and PBT are not mutagenic, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

−QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

QSAR VEGA models predicted negative Ames test results for DBHCTD and TBX, but the lack of data on clastogenicity and aneugenicity did not allow the Panel to conclude on their genotoxic potential.

−Insufficient (TTBNPP), or no data (HBCYD, EH‐TBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBCO, HCTBPH, BDBP‐TAZTO, DBP‐TAZTO, DBS, HEEHP‐TEBP, 4’‐PeBPO‐BDE208, OBTMPI) were identified to allow an assessment of the genotoxic potential of the remaining BFRs.

Evidence of toxicity for reproduction or development in rodents was identified for DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and DBDPE.

Evidence of toxicity for reproduction or development in rodents was identified for DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and DBDPE.

Evidence of neurotoxicity or developmental neurotoxicity in rodents was identified for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO. Studies in zebrafish larvae indicated potential for developmental neurotoxicity for DBE‐DBCH.

Evidence of neurotoxicity or developmental neurotoxicity in rodents was identified for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO. Studies in zebrafish larvae indicated potential for developmental neurotoxicity for DBE‐DBCH.

The CONTAM Panel identified a Reference Point for TDBPP, DBNPG, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE and TDBP‐TAZTO: −For TDBPP, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 0.073 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on combined incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenoma and carcinoma in male rats.−For DBNPG, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.14 mg/kg bw per day for the technical product FR‐1138 (purity 78.6%), corresponding to 0.9 mg/kg bw per day for DBNPG was identified, based on increased incidence of mammary gland fibroadenoma, adenoma or carcinoma in female rats.−For BEH‐TEBP, a Reference Point corresponding to LOAEL of 1 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.−For EH‐TBB, a Reference Point corresponding to LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB.−For DBDPE, a Reference Point corresponding to a BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day was identified based on a neurobehavioural endpoint, an increase in total wheel running activity.−For TDBP‐TAZTO, a Reference Point corresponding to a LOAEL of 5 mg/kg bw per day was identified, based on depressive‐related behaviour in the forced swim test and the tail suspension test in rodents.

Occurrence in human samples

Occurrence data in human milk and/or blood/serum in European countries were identified for BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBDPE, BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX and OBTMPI.

The number of studies was limited and characterised by low detection frequencies, and relied on samples collected around a decade ago or more.

The number of studies was limited and characterised by low detection frequencies, and relied on samples collected around a decade ago or more.

No data on human samples in European countries was identified for the other BFRs included in the Terms of Reference.

Occurrence in food and dietary exposure for the European population

Occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA for two out of the six BFRs covered in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for six out of the remaining 21 BFRs covered in the 2012 Opinion (BTBPE, DBDPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX).

Occurrence data in food were submitted to EFSA for two out of the six BFRs covered in Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for six out of the remaining 21 BFRs covered in the 2012 Opinion (BTBPE, DBDPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX).

The occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA were provided by six European Countries and were derived from samples collected between 2010 and 2025.

The occurrence data in food submitted to EFSA were provided by six European Countries and were derived from samples collected between 2010 and 2025.

Risk characterisation

Risk characterisation could be performed for two out of the six BFRs covered by Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for one out of the remaining 21 BFRs assessed in the 2012 Opinion (DBDPE), making use of the limited information on dietary exposure estimates from one (EH‐TBB, DBDPE) or two (BEH‐TEBP) European countries reported in the scientific literature. The margin of exposure (MOE) approach was applied to assess possible health concerns. −For BEH‐TEBP, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 1 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.−For EH‐TBB, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to EH‐TBB does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.−For DBDPE, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day based on the increase of total wheel running activity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to DBDPE does not raise a health concern.

Risk characterisation could be performed for two out of the six BFRs covered by Commission Recommendation 2014/118/EU (BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB) and for one out of the remaining 21 BFRs assessed in the 2012 Opinion (DBDPE), making use of the limited information on dietary exposure estimates from one (EH‐TBB, DBDPE) or two (BEH‐TEBP) European countries reported in the scientific literature. The margin of exposure (MOE) approach was applied to assess possible health concerns. −For BEH‐TEBP, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 1 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.−For EH‐TBB, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 2 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to EH‐TBB does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.−For DBDPE, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the BMDL10 of 1.07 μg/kg bw per day based on the increase of total wheel running activity, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to DBDPE does not raise a health concern.

−For BEH‐TEBP, based on the limited dietary exposure estimates from the literature and the LOAEL of 1 mg/kg bw per day based on neurobehavioural and skeletal effects in a rat study with binary mixture of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the CONTAM Panel concluded that dietary exposure to BEH‐TEBP does not raise a health concern for non‐neoplastic effects.

No risk characterisation could be performed for any of the other BFRs included in the Terms of Reference, due to insufficient or lack of data on hazard characterisation and/or dietary exposure.

Uncertainty analysis

Uncertainties affecting the assessment were identified and their overall impact was quantified in those cases where the available data were sufficient to conclude on genotoxic potential, hazard characterisation or risk characterisation. The CONTAM Panel assessed the level of certainty (probability) to be 90% or more for all the reported conclusions, except for the risk characterisation for DBDPE, where the certainty of no health concern was lower and considered to fall within the range of 66%–90%.

Uncertainties affecting the assessment were identified and their overall impact was quantified in those cases where the available data were sufficient to conclude on genotoxic potential, hazard characterisation or risk characterisation. The CONTAM Panel assessed the level of certainty (probability) to be 90% or more for all the reported conclusions, except for the risk characterisation for DBDPE, where the certainty of no health concern was lower and considered to fall within the range of 66%–90%.

RECOMMENDATIONS

The CONTAM Panel made the following recommendations:

Occurrence data in food, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no occurrence data were submitted to EFSA. Priority should be given to: (i) BFRs for which a Reference Point is available and/or for those that the Panel has concluded to be genotoxic (TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, DBE‐DBCH, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBNPA), (ii) BFRs that have been detected in human samples but lack a Reference Point or conclusion on genotoxicity (BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX, OBTMPI).

Occurrence data in food, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no occurrence data were submitted to EFSA. Priority should be given to: (i) BFRs for which a Reference Point is available and/or for those that the Panel has concluded to be genotoxic (TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, DBE‐DBCH, TDBP‐TAZTO, TBNPA), (ii) BFRs that have been detected in human samples but lack a Reference Point or conclusion on genotoxicity (BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX, OBTMPI).

Biomonitoring data, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, should be generated. Priority should be given to TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, HBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, DBE‐DBCH and TBNPA, as these show potential for bioaccumulation, or have an identified Reference Point, or are concluded to be genotoxic.

Biomonitoring data, obtained using appropriate analytical methods, should be generated. Priority should be given to TDBPP, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG, DBDPE, HBB, TDBP‐TAZTO, DBE‐DBCH and TBNPA, as these show potential for bioaccumulation, or have an identified Reference Point, or are concluded to be genotoxic.

Genotoxicity data are needed, most importantly for the BFRs with inconclusive (BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX) or absent (EH‐TBB) genotoxicity information, and that are known to occur in food.

Genotoxicity data are needed, most importantly for the BFRs with inconclusive (BEH‐TEBP, BTBPE, HBB, PBEB, PBT, TBX) or absent (EH‐TBB) genotoxicity information, and that are known to occur in food.

Toxicological studies that would enable the identification of a Reference Point are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no studies were identified. Priority should be given to the BFRs for which evidence of potential dietary exposure was identified and/or they have been detected in human samples (BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX, OBTMPI).

Toxicological studies that would enable the identification of a Reference Point are needed for those BFRs for which insufficient or no studies were identified. Priority should be given to the BFRs for which evidence of potential dietary exposure was identified and/or they have been detected in human samples (BTBPE, HBB, DBE‐DBCH, PBEB, PBT, TBCO, DBHCTD, TBX, OBTMPI).

In the context of the One Substance One Assessment, and to ensure efficient and harmonised risk assessments, there is a need to further enable access to the original data underpinning regulatory submissions across relevant authorities.

In the context of the One Substance One Assessment, and to ensure efficient and harmonised risk assessments, there is a need to further enable access to the original data underpinning regulatory submissions across relevant authorities.

Toxicity studies in experimental animals

Table C.1Studies in experimental animals on the six BFRs covered in Commission recommendation 2014/118/EU (TDBPP, EBTEBPI, BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB, DBNPG) and on DBDPE Compound Purity Species Number of animals Age and sex Route of administration Exposure doses Study duration OutcomeNOAEL/LOAELReference TDBPP (Section 3.2.1 ) TDBPP Purity: NR Michigan Chemical Corporation, under the trade name Firemaster LV‐T23 Rats (F344) 55 M and 55 F per dose group 6 weeks old Oral exposure (feed) 0, 50, 100 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 2 and 4 mg/kg bw per day in M and 0, 2.5 and 5 mg/kg bw per day in F) Repeated exposure: 103 weeks + additional observation period of 1 or 2 weeks. No significant association between increased dosage and mortality. Kidney: ‐ Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma (M: 0%, 48%, 48%, respectively; F: 0, 7%, 19%, respectively). For M, significant positive association between incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma and TDBPP (6% in high‐dose M). ‐ Non‐neoplastic lesions in renal tubules: In 11% of high‐dose M and 65% of high‐dose F, a few tubular cells were slightly enlarged and showed nuclear dysplasia consisting of nuclear enlargement, chromatin clumping and parachromatin clearing. LOAEL = 2 mg/kg bw per day NTP ( 2014 ), Reznik et al. ( 1979 ) TDBPP Purity: NR Firemaster LV‐T23 (Michigan Chemical Corporation) Mice (F344) 50 M and 50 F per dose group 6 weeks old Oral exposure (feed) 0, 500, 1000 mg/kg feed (corresponding to 0, 60, 120 mg/kg bw per day in M, and to 0, 65, 130 mg/kg bw per day in F) Repeated exposure: 103 weeks + additional observation period of 1 or 2 weeks. No significant association between increased dosage and accelerated mortality. Kidney: – Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma (M: 0%, 6% and 18%, respectively; F: 0%, 4% and 4%, respectively). – Renal tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma observed in M (0%, 2% and 10%, respectively) but not in F. Variable amounts of haemorrhage, necrosis and calcification were associated with renal tubular‐cell carcinoma. – Renal tubular dysplasia observed in M (74% of low dose and 61% of high dose) and in F (2% of low dose and 26% of high dose). Forestomach: – Increased incidence of squamous‐cell papilloma (M: 0%, 21% and 23%, respectively; F: 2%, 21%, 41%, respectively). Carcinoma of the forestomach observed in 4% of the high‐dose M and 8% and 9% of the treated F. Chronic inflammation frequently found in the submucosa below the papillary lesion. The squamous‐cell carcinoma of the forestomach also often showed superficial hyperkeratinisation. – A basal cell carcinoma of the forestomach seen in a highest dose M and a leiomyosarcoma of the stomach wall in a highest dose F. There was one squamous‐cell carcinoma of the oesophagus in a high‐dose F. Liver: – Dose‐related increase in hepatocellular adenoma or carcinoma in F mice (11%, 46% and 71%, respectively). In M mice, no such dose‐related increase in hepatocellular neoplasms was apparent. Lung: – Bronchiolar/alveolar adenoma and carcinoma seen in M (22%, 41% and 50%, respectively) and in F (7%, 18% and 34%, respectively). Cystadenoma of the Harderian gland in F (0%, 8% and 4%, respectively) and in M (2%, 2% and 4%, respectively). LOAEL = 60 mg/kg bw per day NTP ( 2014 ), Reznik et al. ( 2020a ) BEH‐TEBP Purity: NR Mice (Balb/c) 8 mice per dose group 5‐week‐old M Repeated exposure Oral (gavage) 0, 200 mg/kg bw per day Vehicle: corn oil 28 days exposure BEH‐TEBP resulted in hepatocyte oedema, narrowing of the sinusoids and dilation of the central vein. NOAEL = 20 mg/kg bw per day (two doses tested) Zhang and Yin ( 2021 ) BEH‐TEBP Purity: 95% Rats (Harlan Sprague–Dawley) 12 rats per dose group 6‐week‐old M Repeated exposure Oral (gavage) 0, 5, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day Vehicle: corn oil 28 days exposure No changes in body weight, blood biochemical parameters and lipid metabolism. ( 2020a ) Binary mixtures BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB (Section 3.2.4. and 3.2.5 ) Binary mixture BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB (68.1% EH‐TBB and 31.9% BEH‐TEBP) Rats (Wistar) 8 per dose group c. 300g Pregnant F Repeated exposure Oral (on a wafer cookie) 0, 1000 ug/day Estimated mass fraction in FM‐550 was EB‐TBB 29.7% and BEH‐TEBP 13.9%, corresponding to EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP doses in the binary mixture experiment of 2.25 mg/kg bw per day EH‐TBB and 1.05 mg/kg bw per day BEH‐TEBP GD0–PND21 Vehicle: sesame oil 1M and 1F pup per litter randomly selected for behavioural tasks from PND65 to PND247‐261 Dams had more female pups that controls. Decreased bw of M and F pups at most time points. Increase relative liver and pituitary weight in M pups but not F pups. F offspring showed decreased latency to enter the centre of an open field and increased numbers of marbles buried. No significant effects in M pups. No significant effects of exposure on sociability with a stranger rat. Running wheel activity in the light period was elevated in M offspring. F offspring showed more deficits than M. – Witchey et al. DBNPG (Section 3.2.6 ) DBNPG Purity: technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG, 6.6% 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 6.9% 2,2‐bis(bromomethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 0.2% pentaerythritol and 7.7% dimers and structural isomers) Rats (F344/N) 60 M and 60 F per dose group 6 weeks old Oral exposure (feed) 0, 2500, 5000 or 10,000 mg/kg feed (Average daily doses of FR‐1138 were 0, 100, 200 or 430 mg/kg bw for M and 0, 115, 230 or 460 mg/kg bw for F) Repeated exposure 2 years Survival at 2 years of M and F rats in the two highest dose groups was significantly lower than controls. In both M and F, significantly increased incidences of neoplasms in various organs were observed, with M exhibiting a wider range of affected organs than F. In M: mammary gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small intestine, large intestine, skin neoplasms, subcutaneous tissues, all organs malignant mesothelioma, kidney, urinary bladder, lung, thyroid gland, pancreas, haematopoietic system and Zymbals’ gland. In F: mammary gland, oral cavity, oesophagus and thyroid gland. For more details See Table 14. Non‐neoplastic effects were observed: in M at several doses, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration, transitional epithelial hyperplasia (pelvis) and papillary epithelial hyperplasia), lung (alveolar/bronchiolar hyperplasia), thyroid gland (follicular cell hyperplasia), seminal vesicle (hyperplasia), pancreas (hyperplasia), urinary bladder (hyperplasia) and forestomach (mucosal hyperplasia) and in F, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration and papillary epithelial hyperplasia) at the highest dose only. LOAEL = 100 mg FR‐1138/kg bw per day NTP ( 1996 ) DBNPG Purity: technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG, 6.6% 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 6.9% 2,2‐bis(bromomethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 0.2% pentaerythritol and 7.7% dimers and structural isomers) Mice (B6C3F1) 60 M and 60 F per dose group 6 weeks old Oral exposure (feed) 0, 312, 625 or 1250 mg/kg feed (Average daily doses of FR‐1138 were 0, 35, 70 or 149 mg/kg bw for M and 0, 40, 80 or 170 mg/kg bw for F) Repeated exposure 2 years Survival in both M and F of the highest dose group was significantly lower than that of controls. In both males and females, significantly increased incidences of neoplasms in various organs were observed. In M: Harderian gland, lung, forestomach and kidney. In F: Harderian gland, lung, forestomach, mammary gland, circulatory system and subcutaneous tissue. See Table 15. LOAEL = 35 mg FR‐1138/kg bw per day NTP ( 1996 ) DBNPG Purity 87% (impurities: 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (6.7%), 1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (5.5%) and 3,3‐bis(bromomethyl)‐oxetane (0.5%), 0.3% water) Mice (CD‐1) Oral exposure (feed) 2G reproduction toxicity study 0, 141, 274 and 589 mg/kg bw per day of DBNPG Reduction postpartum BW and weight gain in M and F was at ≥ 274 mg/kg bw per day Dose‐dependent decrease in live pup weight. Significant reduction of the number of litters per pair and the number of live pups born per litter at 589 mg/kg bw per day. In the F1 generation, significant reduction of weight gain at the two highest dose levels in M and at the highest dose level in F. The number of live pups born per litter and adjusted live pup weight was decreased at 589 mg/kg bw per day. Cross‐breeding of F0 exposed M and F of the high‐dose group with F0 controls after 11 weeks of dosing resulted in a reduction of the number of live pups per litter and of pup weight only when exposed F were mated with control M. Histological examination revealed kidney lesions in treated M and F. Reduction of the number of follicles in the ovaries of cross‐bred F0 F in the high‐dose group. The same effect was found in F1 F of the two high‐dose groups. No effect on reproductive organ weights and oestrual cyclicity. LOAEL = 141 mg/kg bw per day Treinen et al. TDBPP

Purity: NR

Michigan Chemical Corporation, under the trade name Firemaster LV‐T23

Rats (F344)

55 M and 55 F per dose group

6 weeks old

Oral exposure (feed)

0, 50, 100 mg/kg feed

(corresponding to 0, 2 and 4 mg/kg bw per day in M and 0, 2.5 and 5 mg/kg bw per day in F)

Repeated exposure: 103 weeks + additional observation period of 1 or 2 weeks.

No significant association between increased dosage and mortality.

Kidney:

‐ Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma (M: 0%, 48%, 48%, respectively; F: 0, 7%, 19%, respectively). For M, significant positive association between incidence of kidney tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma and TDBPP (6% in high‐dose M).

‐ Non‐neoplastic lesions in renal tubules:

In 11% of high‐dose M and 65% of high‐dose F, a few tubular cells were slightly enlarged and showed nuclear dysplasia consisting of nuclear enlargement, chromatin clumping and parachromatin clearing.

TDBPP

Purity: NR

Firemaster LV‐T23 (Michigan Chemical Corporation)

Mice (F344)

50 M and 50 F per dose group

6 weeks old

Oral exposure (feed)

0, 500, 1000 mg/kg feed

(corresponding to 0, 60, 120 mg/kg bw per day in M, and to 0, 65, 130 mg/kg bw per day in F)

Repeated exposure: 103 weeks + additional observation period of 1 or 2 weeks.

No significant association between increased dosage and accelerated mortality.

Kidney:

– Increased incidence of tubular‐cell adenoma (M: 0%, 6% and 18%, respectively; F: 0%, 4% and 4%, respectively).

– Renal tubular‐cell adenocarcinoma observed in M (0%, 2% and 10%, respectively) but not in F. Variable amounts of haemorrhage, necrosis and calcification were associated with renal tubular‐cell carcinoma.

– Renal tubular dysplasia observed in M (74% of low dose and 61% of high dose) and in F (2% of low dose and 26% of high dose).

Forestomach:

– Increased incidence of squamous‐cell papilloma (M: 0%, 21% and 23%, respectively; F: 2%, 21%, 41%, respectively). Carcinoma of the forestomach observed in 4% of the high‐dose M and 8% and 9% of the treated F. Chronic inflammation frequently found in the submucosa below the papillary lesion. The squamous‐cell carcinoma of the forestomach also often showed superficial hyperkeratinisation.

– A basal cell carcinoma of the forestomach seen in a highest dose M and a leiomyosarcoma of the stomach wall in a highest dose F. There was one squamous‐cell carcinoma of the oesophagus in a high‐dose F.

Liver:

– Dose‐related increase in hepatocellular adenoma or carcinoma in F mice (11%, 46% and 71%, respectively). In M mice, no such dose‐related increase in hepatocellular neoplasms was apparent.

Lung:

– Bronchiolar/alveolar adenoma and carcinoma seen in M (22%, 41% and 50%, respectively) and in F (7%, 18% and 34%, respectively).

Cystadenoma of the Harderian gland in F (0%, 8% and 4%, respectively) and in M (2%, 2% and 4%, respectively).

BEH‐TEBP

Purity: NR

Mice (Balb/c)

8 mice per dose group

5‐week‐old

M

Repeated exposure

Oral (gavage)

0, 200 mg/kg bw per day

Vehicle: corn oil

28 days exposure

BEH‐TEBP resulted in hepatocyte oedema, narrowing of the sinusoids and dilation of the central vein.

Levels of ROS, MDA, 8‐OH‐dG and DNA‐protein cross‐links increased, and levels of GSH decreased.

Western blotting analysis showed upregulation of the endoplasmic reticulum stress related proteins GRP78, PERK and CHOP.

The effects of BEH‐TEBP were attenuated by co‐administration of 25 mg/kg bw per day curcumin, which was attributed to its antioxidant properties

BEH‐TEBP

Purity: 95%

Rats (Harlan Sprague–Dawley)

12 rats per dose group

6‐week‐old

M

Repeated exposure

Oral (gavage)

0, 5, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day

Vehicle: corn oil

28 days exposure

No changes in body weight, blood biochemical parameters and lipid metabolism.

Thyroids and relative thyroid weights in top dose animals were smaller. Also in the top dose rats, thyroid follicular epithelium was flatter and thinner and the follicular cavity was dilated.

TT4 and FT4 were significantly increased in the mid and top dose animals in a dose‐related manner. TSH was decreased at the top dose. No changes in TT3 or FT3.

GD0–PND21

Vehicle: sesame oil

1M and 1F pup per litter randomly selected for behavioural tasks from PND65 to PND247‐261

Dams had more female pups that controls.

Decreased bw of M and F pups at most time points. Increase relative liver and pituitary weight in M pups but not F pups.

F offspring showed decreased latency to enter the centre of an open field and increased numbers of marbles buried. No significant effects in M pups.

No significant effects of exposure on sociability with a stranger rat.

Running wheel activity in the light period was elevated in M offspring.

Authors concluded that developmental exposure to the binary mixture BEH‐TEBP+EH‐TBB affected socioemotional behaviour. Effects were exposure, sex‐ and task‐specific. F offspring showed more deficits than M.

DBNPG

Purity: technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG,

6.6% 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 6.9% 2,2‐bis(bromomethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 0.2% pentaerythritol and 7.7% dimers and structural isomers)

Rats (F344/N)

60 M and 60 F per dose group

6 weeks old

Oral exposure (feed)

0, 2500, 5000 or 10,000 mg/kg feed (Average daily doses of FR‐1138 were 0, 100, 200 or 430 mg/kg bw for M and 0, 115, 230 or 460 mg/kg bw for F)

Repeated exposure 2 years

Survival at 2 years of M and F rats in the two highest dose groups was significantly lower than controls.

In both M and F, significantly increased incidences of neoplasms in various organs were observed, with M exhibiting a wider range of affected organs than F.

In M: mammary gland, oral cavity, oesophagus, forestomach, small intestine, large intestine, skin neoplasms, subcutaneous tissues, all organs malignant mesothelioma, kidney, urinary bladder, lung, thyroid gland, pancreas, haematopoietic system and Zymbals’ gland.

In F: mammary gland, oral cavity, oesophagus and thyroid gland.

For more details See Table 14.

Non‐neoplastic effects were observed: in M at several doses, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration, transitional epithelial hyperplasia (pelvis) and papillary epithelial hyperplasia), lung (alveolar/bronchiolar hyperplasia), thyroid gland (follicular cell hyperplasia), seminal vesicle (hyperplasia), pancreas (hyperplasia), urinary bladder (hyperplasia) and forestomach (mucosal hyperplasia) and in F, in the kidney (focal atrophy, papillary degeneration and papillary epithelial hyperplasia) at the highest dose only.

DBNPG

Purity: technical product FR‐1138 (containing 78.6% DBNPG, 6.6% 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 6.9% 2,2‐bis(bromomethyl)‐l‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane, 0.2% pentaerythritol and 7.7% dimers and structural isomers)

6 weeks old

Repeated exposure 2 years

In both males and females, significantly increased incidences of neoplasms in various organs were observed.

DBNPG

Purity 87% (impurities: 2,2‐bis(hydroxymethyl)‐1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (6.7%), 1‐bromo‐3‐hydroxypropane (5.5%) and 3,3‐bis(bromomethyl)‐oxetane (0.5%), 0.3% water)

Oral exposure (feed)

2G reproduction toxicity study

0, 141, 274 and 589 mg/kg bw per day of DBNPG

Reduction postpartum BW and weight gain in M and F was at ≥ 274 mg/kg bw per day

Dose‐dependent decrease in live pup weight.

Significant reduction of the number of litters per pair and the number of live pups born per litter at 589 mg/kg bw per day.

In the F1 generation, significant reduction of weight gain at the two highest dose levels in M and at the highest dose level in F.

The number of live pups born per litter and adjusted live pup weight was decreased at 589 mg/kg bw per day.

Cross‐breeding of F0 exposed M and F of the high‐dose group with F0 controls after 11 weeks of dosing resulted in a reduction of the number of live pups per litter and of pup weight only when exposed F were mated with control M.

Histological examination revealed kidney lesions in treated M and F.

Reduction of the number of follicles in the ovaries of cross‐bred F0 F in the high‐dose group. The same effect was found in F1 F of the two high‐dose groups.

No effect on reproductive organ weights and oestrual cyclicity.

DBDPE

Purity: NR

Mice (Kunming)

14‐day exposure

Pregnant F

0, 5, 50, 500 μg/kg bw per day

After treatment, F mice were i.p. injected with PMSG and 48 h later with HCG and then mated with sexually mature M.

DBDPE inhibited preimplantation embryo development in a dose‐dependent manner by affecting the circadian rhythm (measured using a voluntary wheel running test) (from 50 μg/kg bw per day) and endogenous melatonin levels (decrease serum melatonin from 5 μg/kg bw per day in 2‐cell embryo and 8‐cell embryo). The highest dose significantly reduced the rate of 2‐cell embryos. The ratio of the 4‐cell embryo, 8‐cell embryo and morula dramatically decreased at 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day and even the lowest dose resulted in a significant decrease in the blastocyst rate.

See Table C.2 for developmental neurotoxicity outcomes.

Table C.2Developmental neurotoxicity studies on DBDPE in experimental animalsSpecies compound puritySpecies route of administration exposure doses study durationTest used to assess behavioural endpointsOutcomeReference DBDPE Purity: NR Mice (Kunming) F 0, 50 μg/kg per day Gavage For 30 days Exposed F mated with untreated M Pups tested on week 6 of age for behavioural performances. Open‐field test: (5–10 mice/group from 3 litters, free exploration for 5 min). Variables related to locomotor activity: total distance, average speed, running time, static time, walking time; variables related to anxiety‐related behaviour: corner zone duration, central zone duration. Dark/light transition test: (7 mice/group from 3 litters, 5 min). Divided into two boxes, a dark one connected with an illuminating one (50 ± 0.5 Lux). Number of transitions between the two boxes, time spent in each box (the longer the mouse stays in the light box, the lower the anxiety level of the animal is, contrary regarding the dark side). Novel object recognition test: to evaluate the preference for novelty and working memory (7 mice/group from 3 litters). Three sessions: habituation (5 min, any object inside the cage), training (10 min, two similar objects, exploring time of each object) and testing (10 min, one object similar to those used during the training session, the other is a new one, exploring time of each object). Three‐chamber test: (7 mice/group from 3 litters). 3 chambers with a cylinder cage in two of them, the right and the left ones whereas the central chamber remains empty. 3 phases: (i) habituation (5 min, free exploration of the complete maze), (ii) social preference test (5 min, a mouse is placed in one cylinder cage in one chamber, the other remains empty), exploring time of the mouse vs. the empty cage, (iii) social novelty test (5 min, one cage with a mouse that is the same as used for the social preference test, one new unknown mouse in the other cage), exploring time of the unknow mouse vs. the familiar one. Open‐field test: significant increases in the total distance travelled and the velocity only in DBDPE exposed females vs. controls. No effects on anxiety. Dark–light transition test: No significant variations of variables related to anxiety (time spent in each box) in both sexes. As observed in the OF, activity level of females significantly increased vs. controls (total distance travelled, velocity, walking time) Novel object recognition test: Significant increase in the time spent to explore the novel object in M and F control mice compared to the time spent to explore the ‘old’ one. No significant variations in M and F‐exposed mice. Significant increase in the activity level in F exposed to DBDPE vs. controls, not in M. Three‐chamber test: Social preference test: significant increases in the time spent to explore the mouse vs. the empty cage in control and DBDPE‐exposed M and control F. No difference in DBDPE‐exposed F. Social novelty test: same significant variations regarding the exploration of the unknow mouse vs. the familiar one in control and DBDPE‐exposed M and control F. No differences in DBDPE‐exposed F. Overall, DBDPE induced a significant increase in the activity level in F and not in M, and impaired the learning and memory performances of both DBDPE‐exposed M and F. F exposed to DBDPE were impaired for their social recognition (social memory) performances. Shi, Xu, et al. (2022) DBDPE Purity: NR Kunming mice (F) 0, 5, 50, 500 μg/kg per day Gavage For 14 days Exposed F mated with untreated M Voluntary wheel running test: To assess the locomotor activity according to the circadian rhythm Mice kept individually in cages within light‐tight chambers after 14 days of DBDPE exposure. First, the mice were exposed to a 12:12‐h light/dark (LD) cycle (lights on at 07:00, off at 19:00) for 4 days to adapt to the environment. Then, the mice were exposed to a 24‐h dark (DD) cycle for 4 days to demonstrate their own endogenous circadian rhythm. Clock gene expression was studied in the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the same animals before and during the test. In the LD cycle, mice in the four groups were active during the dark phase of the cycle and almost inactive during the light phase, showing an obvious circadian rhythm. After entering the DD cycle, the control group and 5 μg/kg bw/day DBDPE‐treated mice showed normal circadian rhythm behaviour. However, mice from the 50 and 500 μg/kg bw/day DBDPE groups showed active movement during the 12h‐period that corresponds to the light phase of the cycle, which indicated an endogenous circadian rhythm disorder. The daily activity of the mice was also analysed, and the results showed that the total wheel running activity and the wheel running activity during the dark phase of the cycle in the 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day DBDPE‐exposed groups were significantly increased in LD and DD, and the total rest bouts were significantly reduced, demonstrating the disorder of activity rhythm. Shi, Qiu, et al. (2022) DBDPE Purity: NR Rats (Sprague–Dawley) 0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day Gavage (P) M: 10 weeks before mating up to necropsy after litters were weaned. (P) F: 10 weeks before mating, up to 2 weeks during mating, during resulting pregnancies, up to necropsy on Day 23 of lactation. (F1) M and F: From weaning until termination of respective cohort. Cohort 2A: Developmental neurotoxicity testing (neurobehavioural testing followed by neuro‐histopathology assessment as adults) – treated from weaning up to approximately Day 75 of age. Cohort 2B: Developmental neurotoxicity testing – no direct treatment, assigned to neurohistopathology assessment at weaning. Neurobehavioural Screening – Cohort 2A Functional observational battery on all Cohort 2A animals on day 70 ± 1 of age In cage observations: Abnormal motor movements (e.g. fasciculation, convulsions, stereotypy, excessive sniffing, licking, grooming and twitches), Palpebral closure, Posture, Tremor In hand observations: Ease of removal from cage, Exophthalmos, Fur condition, Piloerection, Reactivity to handling, Salivation/lacrimation, Vocalisation on handling, Observation in the arena – 2‐min recording period:, Activity count, Arousal, Abnormal motor movements (e.g. fasciculation, convulsions, stereotypy, excessive sniffing, licking, grooming and twitches), Faecal count, Gait, Palpebral closure, Posture, Rearing count, Urination, Tremor Reactivity investigations: Approach response, Body temperature, Body weight, Grip strength: fore and hindlimb, Landing foot splay, Pupil closure reflex, Righting reflex, Auditory startle reflex, Tail pinch response, Touch response (pinna reflex), Proprioception F1 Cohort 2A: Auditory Startle Response Habituation: Habituation and latency to peak values. Developmental neurotoxicity studies on DBDPE in experimental animals

DBDPE

Purity: NR

Mice (Kunming)

F

0, 50 μg/kg per day

Gavage

For 30 days

Exposed F mated with untreated M

Pups tested on week 6 of age for behavioural performances.

Open‐field test: (5–10 mice/group from 3 litters, free exploration for 5 min). Variables related to locomotor activity: total distance, average speed, running time, static time, walking time; variables related to anxiety‐related behaviour: corner zone duration, central zone duration.

Dark/light transition test: (7 mice/group from 3 litters, 5 min). Divided into two boxes, a dark one connected with an illuminating one (50 ± 0.5 Lux). Number of transitions between the two boxes, time spent in each box (the longer the mouse stays in the light box, the lower the anxiety level of the animal is, contrary regarding the dark side).

Novel object recognition test: to evaluate the preference for novelty and working memory (7 mice/group from 3 litters). Three sessions: habituation (5 min, any object inside the cage), training (10 min, two similar objects, exploring time of each object) and testing (10 min, one object similar to those used during the training session, the other is a new one, exploring time of each object).

Three‐chamber test: (7 mice/group from 3 litters). 3 chambers with a cylinder cage in two of them, the right and the left ones whereas the central chamber remains empty. 3 phases: (i) habituation (5 min, free exploration of the complete maze), (ii) social preference test (5 min, a mouse is placed in one cylinder cage in one chamber, the other remains empty), exploring time of the mouse vs. the empty cage, (iii) social novelty test (5 min, one cage with a mouse that is the same as used for the social preference test, one new unknown mouse in the other cage), exploring time of the unknow mouse vs. the familiar one.

Open‐field test: significant increases in the total distance travelled and the velocity only in DBDPE exposed females vs. controls. No effects on anxiety.

Dark–light transition test: No significant variations of variables related to anxiety (time spent in each box) in both sexes. As observed in the OF, activity level of females significantly increased vs. controls (total distance travelled, velocity, walking time)

Novel object recognition test: Significant increase in the time spent to explore the novel object in M and F control mice compared to the time spent to explore the ‘old’ one. No significant variations in M and F‐exposed mice. Significant increase in the activity level in F exposed to DBDPE vs. controls, not in M.

Three‐chamber test: Social preference test: significant increases in the time spent to explore the mouse vs. the empty cage in control and DBDPE‐exposed M and control F. No difference in DBDPE‐exposed F. Social novelty test: same significant variations regarding the exploration of the unknow mouse vs. the familiar one in control and DBDPE‐exposed M and control F. No differences in DBDPE‐exposed F.

Overall, DBDPE induced a significant increase in the activity level in F and not in M, and impaired the learning and memory performances of both DBDPE‐exposed M and F. F exposed to DBDPE were impaired for their social recognition (social memory) performances.

DBDPE

Purity: NR

Kunming mice (F)

0, 5, 50, 500 μg/kg per day

Gavage

For 14 days

Exposed F mated with untreated M

Voluntary wheel running test:

To assess the locomotor activity according to the circadian rhythm

Mice kept individually in cages within light‐tight chambers after 14 days of DBDPE exposure. First, the mice were exposed to a 12:12‐h light/dark (LD) cycle (lights on at 07:00, off at 19:00) for 4 days to adapt to the environment. Then, the mice were exposed to a 24‐h dark (DD) cycle for 4 days to demonstrate their own endogenous circadian rhythm.

Clock gene expression was studied in the suprachiasmatic nucleus of the same animals before and during the test.

In the LD cycle, mice in the four groups were active during the dark phase of the cycle and almost inactive during the light phase, showing an obvious circadian rhythm. After entering the DD cycle, the control group and 5 μg/kg bw/day DBDPE‐treated mice showed normal circadian rhythm behaviour. However, mice from the 50 and 500 μg/kg bw/day DBDPE groups showed active movement during the 12h‐period that corresponds to the light phase of the cycle, which indicated an endogenous circadian rhythm disorder.

The daily activity of the mice was also analysed, and the results showed that the total wheel running activity and the wheel running activity during the dark phase of the cycle in the 50 and 500 μg/kg bw per day DBDPE‐exposed groups were significantly increased in LD and DD, and the total rest bouts were significantly reduced, demonstrating the disorder of activity rhythm.

DBDPE

Purity: NR

Rats (Sprague–Dawley)

0, 100, 300 and 1000 mg/kg bw per day

Gavage

(P) M: 10 weeks before mating up to necropsy after litters were weaned.

(P) F: 10 weeks before mating, up to 2 weeks during mating, during resulting pregnancies, up to necropsy on Day 23 of lactation.

(F1) M and F: From weaning until termination of respective cohort.

Cohort 2A: Developmental neurotoxicity testing (neurobehavioural testing followed by neuro‐histopathology assessment as adults) – treated from weaning up to approximately Day 75 of age.

Cohort 2B: Developmental neurotoxicity testing – no direct treatment, assigned to neurohistopathology assessment at weaning.

Neurobehavioural Screening – Cohort 2A

Functional observational battery on all Cohort 2A animals on day 70 ± 1 of age

In cage observations: Abnormal motor movements (e.g. fasciculation, convulsions, stereotypy, excessive sniffing, licking, grooming and twitches), Palpebral closure, Posture, Tremor

In hand observations: Ease of removal from cage, Exophthalmos, Fur condition, Piloerection, Reactivity to handling, Salivation/lacrimation, Vocalisation on handling, Observation in the arena – 2‐min recording period:, Activity count, Arousal, Abnormal motor movements (e.g. fasciculation, convulsions, stereotypy, excessive sniffing, licking, grooming and twitches), Faecal count, Gait, Palpebral closure, Posture, Rearing count, Urination, Tremor

Reactivity investigations: Approach response, Body temperature, Body weight, Grip strength: fore and hindlimb, Landing foot splay, Pupil closure reflex, Righting reflex, Auditory startle reflex, Tail pinch response, Touch response (pinna reflex), Proprioception

F1 Cohort 2A:

Auditory Startle Response Habituation: Habituation and latency to peak values.

Motor Activity: Motor activity including assessment for both ambulatory activity (low beam) and rearing activity (high beam)

In Cage Observations

In Hand Observations

Arena Observations

Brain morphometry

Auditory startle response habituation, reactivity investigations and motor activity (ambulatory and rearing) were unaffected by treatment.

There were no in cage or in hand observations that could be related to treatment and arena observations were limited to a slight increase in the incidence of females with elevated/slightly abnormal gait in the treated groups but with no evidence for a dose response.

All differences in brain morphometry parameters were considered incidental as they were inconsistent between sexes, inconsistent between cohorts, the magnitude was considered small, had no histopathological correlates, and/or had no significant mean brain weight differences.
