Studies informing the mode of action and studies on other model systems for the BFRs assessed in this Opinion

Table G.1Studies informing the mode of action and studies on other model systems of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB ReferenceComments Ezechiáš et al. ( 2012 ). Hormonal activities of new brominated flame retardantsReporter gene assays were used to assess the ability of BEH‐TEBP to activate or inhibit the transcriptional activity of human ER and AR. At a concentration of 14.1 μM BEH‐TEBP there was no oestrogenic, anti‐oestrogenic or androgenic activity. Saunders et al. ( 2013 ). In vitro endocrine disruption and TCDD‐like effects of three novel brominated flame retardants: TBPH, TBB, & TBCO. EH‐TBB, BEH‐TEBP and TBCO did not show AHR agonistic effects in two reporter gene assays. Maximal anti‐estrogenic effect of 62% was observed from EH‐TBB at 0.5 mg L−1 in the YES assay. BEH‐TEBP and TBCO had maximal anti‐androgenic effects in the YAS assay of 74% and 59% at 300 mg L−1 and 1500 mg L−1, respectively. Using the H295R assay, 0.05 mg L−1, 15 mg L−1 and 15 mg L−1 EH‐TBB, BEH‐TEBP and TBCO, respectively resulted in a 2.8‐fold, 5.4‐fold and 3.3‐fold increase in concentrations of E2. Mankidy et al. ( 2014 ). Effects of novel brominated flame retardants on steroidogenesis in primary porcine testicular cells.The effects of EH‐TBB, TBCO and BEH‐TEBP on steroidogenesis was studied in a porcine primary testicular cell model. TBCO and TBPH stimulated production of sex hormones testosterone and oestradiol was observed. Effects may have been mediated by changes in expression of genes encoding enzymes involved in the steroidogenesis pathway. Fic et al. ( 2014 ). Estrogenic and androgenic activities of TBBA and TBMEPH, metabolites of novel brominated flame retardants, and selected bisphenols, using the XenoScreen XL YES/YAS assay.In the XenoScreen XL YES/YAS assay, EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP, had no or very weak (anti)estrogenic or (anti)androgenic activities. Metabolites of TBBA and TBMEPH, showed anti‐oestrogenic (IC50 for TBBA = 31.75 uM and IC50 for TBMEPH = 0.265 uM) and anti‐androgenic (IC50 for TBBA = 73.95 uM and IC50 for TBMEPH = 2.92 uM) activities. Saunders et al. ( 2015 ). A mixture of the novel brominated flame retardants TBPH and TBB affects fecundity and transcript profiles of the HPGL‐axis in Japanese medaka. BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, the two main components of Firemaster 550, were investigated in a 21‐day fecundity study in Japanese medaka for their potential effects on reproductive endocrinology‐related genes and fecundity following dietary exposure. This study followed OECD test 229 for short‐term reproductivity testing in fish. Medaka were fed a diet containing either 138 μg BEH‐TEBP and 144 μg EH‐TBB/g feed (wet weight) or 1422 μg BEH‐TEBP and 1474 μg EH‐TBB/g feed (wet weight) in addition to an unexposed control. The fish were fed at a ration of 6% of the bw per day. There were no treatment‐related mortalities or changes in liver and gonad weights. Cumulative production of eggs was reduced by 32% in females exposed to the higher concentration of the mixture. No effects on fecundity was observed at the lower concentration. Both male and female medaka showed downregulation of the vast majority of genes along the hypothalamus‐pituitary‐gonadal‐liver axis. In males, key genes involved in steroidogenesis, such as CYP17, 3β‐HSD and HMGR, were significantly downregulated, suggesting a disruption in testosterone synthesis. In females, expression of erα was significantly lower in the liver, while genes associated with production of egg yolk protein (vitellogenin 2 and choriogenin h) were upregulated. Krivoshiev et al. ( 2016 ). Assessing in‐vitro estrogenic effects of currently‐used flame retardants.A number of flame retardants were tested for oestrogenic activity in the MCF‐7 proliferation assay. BEH‐TEBP did not exhibit significant activity in this assay. Klopcic et al. ( 2016 ). Comparison of in vitro hormone activities of novel flame retardants TBB, TBPH and their metabolites TBBA and TBMEPH using reporter gene assays.Anti‐androgenic, anti‐thyroid hormonal and anti‐glucocorticoid activities were tested in luciferase reporter gene assays in mammalian cell models. Both BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB exhibited anti‐glucocorticoid activity by direct competition with the glucocorticoid receptor. BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB, and their carboxylic acid metabolites also exhibited anti‐androgenic and anti‐thyroid hormonal activities, without agonistic activities on the respective receptors. BEH‐TEBP was the most active. Skledar et al. ( 2016 ). New brominated flame retardants and their metabolites as activators of the pregnane X receptor.Transactivation assays were used to investigate the effects of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB on nuclear receptor activities. Neither of the chemicals exhibited marked activity towards PPARa and PPARg. BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB both activated PXR in this system. BEH‐TEBP and its metabolite, mono(2‐ethylhexyl) tetrabromophthalate, showed PXR agonist activity, with EC50 values of 2.0 and 5.5 μM, respectively. BEH‐TEBP also showed PXR antagonist activity (IC50 = 13.9 μM). Molecular docking experiments indicated that similar binding of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB to PXR. Additionally, exposure to BEH‐TEBP, EH‐TBB and their metabolites upregulated CYP3A4 mRNA expression, a gene regulated by PXR. Usenko et al. ( 2016 ). Evaluation of Common Use Brominated Flame Retardant (BFR) Toxicity Using a Zebrafish Embryo ModelZebrafish embryos were exposed to a number of flame retardants at concentrations of 1.25–20 mg/L from 6 to 168 post‐fertilisation and assessed for toxicity, the rate of spontaneous movement and acetylcholinesterase and glutathione‐S‐transferase activities. EH‐TBB was moderately toxic with an LC50 value of 7 mg/L but did not exhibit effects on the other parameters assessed. BEH‐TEBP had no effect at any of the tested concentrations. Smythe et al. ( 2017 ). Impacts of Unregulated Novel Brominated Flame Retardants on Human Liver Thyroid Deiodination and Sulfotransferation.Human liver microsomal and cytosolic fractions were used to explore the potential inhibitory effects of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB on deiodinases (DIO) and sulfotransferases (SULT) following activation with T3 or T4. BEH‐TEBP inhibited DIO 3,3′‐T2 and T3 formation at the highest concentration tested (3.75 μM). There were no other effects on DIO and SULT activities. Xiang et al. ( 2017 ). Effects of novel brominated flame retardant TBPH and its metabolite TBMEHP on human vascular endothelial cells: Implication for human health risks. BEH‐TEBP and its metabolite mono‐(2‐ethyhexyl) tetrabromophthalate were tested separately for their effects and associated molecular mechanisms on human umbilical vein endothelial cells (0.02, 1, 2, 5, 10, 20 or 50 μg/mL). BEH‐TEBP induced early apoptosis after G2/M cell cycle arrest at 10 μg/mL. Its metabolite showed a higher toxicity (marked G0/G1 cell cycle arrest, high level of apoptosis and over expression of p53, cyclin‐dependent kinase inhibitors p21 and p27, underexpression cyclin D1, CDK2, CDK6, Bcl‐2). Tung et al. ( 2017 ). Firemaster 550 and its components isopropylated triphenyl phosphate and triphenyl phosphate enhance adipogenesis and transcriptional activity of peroxisome proliferator‐activated receptor (Ppargamma) on the adipocyte protein 2 (aP2) promoter.The authors investigated the adipogenic effects of Firemaster 550 and its components, focusing on the activation of the PPARγ receptor and the subsequent effects on adipogenesis. Firemaster 550 and some of its components including BEH‐TEBP induced lipid accumulation in murine 3T3‐L1 preadipocytes, but EH‐TBB did not. The adipogenic effect was dose‐dependent, showing significant lipid accumulation at 10 μM. Exposure to Firemaster 550 (100 uM), enhanced the transcriptional activity of the aP2 promoter via PPARγ. This was confirmed through reporter assays and chromatin immunoprecipitation (ChIP) experiments, showing PPARγ recruitment to the aP2 enhancer region. BEH‐TEBP (20 uM) did not cause PPARγ activation. EH‐TBB was not tested for this endpoint. In conclusion, Firemaster 550 promotes adipogenesis through PPARγ activation, but the main components causing this effect did not include BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB. Kollitz et al. ( 2018 ). Chemical Mixtures Isolated from House Dust Disrupt Thyroid Receptor β Signaling.The study tested 137 house dust samples and found that 41% of the extracts significantly antagonised TRβ signalling. Extracts contained 0.045–1266 μg EH‐TBB/mL and 0.566–564.4 μg BEH‐TEBP/mL. The concentrations of 12 flame retardants in the dust extracts were found to be significantly correlated with the observed TRβ antagonism with PBDE congeners showing the strongest correlations. However, when these flame retardants were tested individually up to concentrations of 10‐7 pg/mL (10‐16 g/L), they did not show any significant antagonistic activity. Ma et al. ( 2018 ). Multigenerational Effects and Demographic Responses of Zebrafish (Danio rerio) Exposed to Organo‐Bromine CompoundsExposure of zebrafish via feed at 3 levels of contamination of BEH‐TEBP (4.00, 15.95 and 63.80 μg/g) and EH‐TBB (3.00, 13.63 and 54.53 μg/g) for 21 days resulted in a dose‐related reduction of fertility in F0 females. The authors predicted extinction of the zebrafish population over multiple generations in 20 years. Pagé‐Larivière et al. ( 2018 ). Prioritization of 10 organic flame retardants using an avian hepatocyte toxicogenomic assay. BEH‐TEBP was exposed to hepatocytes isolated from chicken and double‐breasted cormorant hepatocytes for 24 h at concentrations of 0.1–300 uM. Cell viability, activity of 7‐ethoxyresorufin deethylase (EROD) and transcriptomics were assessed. EROD activity was induced indicating AHR activation. Zhang et al. ( 2019 ). Screening for neurotoxic potential of 15 flame retardants using freshwater planarians. BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB were tested for neurotoxicity using a planarian automated platform able to assess morphological and behavioural endpoints in adult or regenerating animals. BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB were tested at 5 different doses (0.01, 0.1, 1.0, 10, 100 μM). No effects of these two compounds were observed in regenerating and adult planarians. Shockley et al. (2020a). Comparative toxicity and liver transcriptomics of legacy and emerging brominated flame retardants following 5‐day exposure in the rat.Male Harlan SD rats were orally exposed to EH‐TBB or BEH‐TEBP for 5 days at doses ranging from 0.1 to 1000 μmole/kg bw per day. No treatment‐related liver histopathologic alterations were present with either of the test substances. EH‐TBB exposure for 5 days caused transcriptome changes in the rat liver with 2, 2 and 66 significantly regulated transcripts identified at doses of 0.55, 55 and 550 mg/kg bw per day, respectively. Several genes that are targets of the AHR (Cyp1a1, Cyp1a2, Gsta3, Nqo1 and Slc39a4) were upregulated. Of these, Cyp1a1 showed the strongest induction (162‐fold at 550 mg/kg bw per day). However, there was no corresponding increase in CYP1A1 activity, or indeed activities of several other xenobiotic metabolism enzymes analysed. The genes, Gsta3 and Nqo1, are also regulated by NRF2, which would be indicative of oxidative stress. The authors calculated a transcriptome BMDL for EH‐TBB, based on Gene Ontology Process categories, of 110.77 mg/kg per day. Other groups of rats were exposed to BEH‐TEBP at doses of 0.07, 0.71, 7.06, 70.6 or 706 mg/kg bw per day for 5 days. There were no significant liver disease‐related transcript changes following exposure to BEH‐TEBP up to the highest dose. Chen et al. ( 2020 ). Effects of novel brominated flame retardants and metabolites on cytotoxicity in human umbilical vein endothelial cells.Human umbilical vein endothelial cells were exposed to EH‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP, and their respective metabolites 2,3,4,5‐tetrabromo benzoic acid (TBBA) and mono‐(2‐ethyhexyl) tetrabromophthalate for 24h. Concentrations of 0.1, 1, 10, 50, 100 or 200 μg/mL were tested for cell viability, and of 0.8 and 8 μg/mL for LDH activity. Significant reduction of cell viability was observed with the two metabolites (50 or 10μM, respectively) whereas the parent compounds did not. Significant increases in LDH activity were measured with 8 μg/mL of BEH‐TEBP and the two metabolites. Significant gene overexpression (eNOS, VEGFR1) was reported with TBBA. Significant reductions in VEGFR1 expression and NO release were noted with BEH‐TEBP. Significant increases in adhesion molecule gene expression PECAM1 were observed with both metabolites 0.8 μg/mL whereas no changes were noted with the two parent compounds. In vivo, an acute oral administration of the two metabolites (0.8 μg/mL) induced a significant increase in platelet number. Metabolites caused higher toxicity and affected gene expression to a greater extent than their parent compounds EB‐TBB and BEH‐TEBP. Yin and Zhang et al. (2021). Effects of bis(2‐ethylhexyl)‐2,3,4,5‐tetrabromophthalate on liver injury in Balb/c mice.Levels of ROS, MDA, 8‐OH‐dG and DNA‐protein cross‐links were increased, and levels of GSH were decreased by BEH‐TEBP treatment. Western blotting analysis showed upregulation of the endoplasmic reticulum stress related proteins GRP78, PERK and CHOP by BEH‐TEBP. The effects of BEH‐TEBP were attenuated by co‐administration of 25 mg/kg bw per day curcumin, which was attributed to its antioxidant properties. Liu, Song, et al. (2022). Bis (2‐ethylhexyl)‐2, 3, 4, 5‐tetrabromophthalate showed poor penetrability but increased the permeability of blood brain barrier: Evidences from in vitro and in vivo studies BEH‐TEBP was tested for its toxicity on the blood–brain barrier (BBB) using hCMEC/D3 cells and in zebrafish models. In addition, behavioural toxicity of BEH‐TEBP in zebrafish was assessed. In vitro, BEH‐TEBP did not impair the cell viability and had a low level of penetrability through the BBB. In vivo measurement of BBB permeability using transgenic zebrafish exposed to BEH‐TEBP (0, 0.02, 0.2, 2 or 20 μM, 0 to 5 days post‐fertilisation) showed a significant increase in BBB permeability in fishes exposed to 2 and 20 μM BEH‐TEBP through modifications of the gene expression of tight junction proteins. Concomitant increases in the head content of BEH‐TEBP were measured at the two same doses in wild‐type zebrafish larvae. The assessment of neurotoxicity in wild‐type zebrafish larvae did not show any effects of the exposure to BEH‐TEBP (0, 0.02, 0.2, 2 or 20 μM, 0 to 5 days post‐fertilisation) on the neurodevelopment, neurotransmission and locomotor activity of the animals. Within the same experiment, the 2 and 20 μM exposed groups showed a significant decrease in the phototactic behaviour compared to the control group and a reduction in the expression of all the opsin genes measured in the same two groups. This study suggests a limited neurotoxicity of BEH‐TEBP regarding the neurodevelopment and neurotransmission of the brain, and the general activity of the animals. However, the alteration of phototactic response may be indicative of another form of neurotoxicity more related to the predator avoidance response. Maia et al. ( 2022 ). Impact of brominated flame retardants on lipid metabolism: An in vitro approach. EH‐TBB was studied for its ability to disturb lipid metabolism. Preadipocyte (3T3‐L1) cell line and hepatocyte (HepG2) cell line were exposed to EH‐TBB acutely (50, 100 or 250 nM, 24h) or chronically (250 nM, 4 weeks). Preadipocyte proliferation and differentiation were not affected by EH‐TBB (50‐250 nM, 24h) whereas the fatty acid composition has been changed with a short chain fatty acid decrease between day 3 and day 11 after exposure. In hepatocytes, 4‐week exposure to EH‐TBB 250 nM induced significant increases in lipid accumulation and long chain fatty acide concentration in culture medium. EH‐TBB reduced the expression of lipid metabolism‐related genes like PPARγ and PGC‐1α. Sousa et al. ( 2022 ). Brominated flame retardants effect in MCF‐7 cells Impact on vitamin D pathway. EH‐TBB was assessed for its toxicity on MCF‐7 breast cancer cells and its impact on vitamin D metabolism. Cells were incubated with EH‐TBB for 24 h in a range 10‐500 nM resulted only in a significant reduction of cell proliferation at a dose of 10 nM. The exposure of cells to 250 nM for 4 weeks induced early disturbances in cell migration after injury with values returning to control ones later whereas cell proliferation and MMP‐9 prtein expression remained unchanged. EH‐TBB 250 nM 4 weeks significantly reduced vitamin D gene expression in human hepatic cells (CYP2R1) and MCF‐7 cells (CYP24A1, RxRα), and impaired cell cycle response to vitamin D. Esplugas et al. ( 2023 ). In vitro neurotoxic potential of emerging flame retardants on neuroblastoma cells in an acute exposure scenarioThe acute neurotoxicity of BEH‐TEBP and EH‐TBB on neuroblastoma cells SH‐SY5Y was assessed at concentrations ranging 2.5–20 μM during 2–24 h. Cell viability, mitochondrial function, neural plasticity and development, promotion of oxidative stress and inflammation were not affected by BEH‐TEBP or EH‐TBB suggesting the absence of toxicity in this neural cell line. Witchey et al. ( 2023 ). Impacts of Gestational FireMaster 550 (FM 550) Exposure on the neonatal cortex are Sex Specific and largely Attributable to the Organophosphate Esters.PND1 siblings of the animals used for behavioural studies (Witchey et al., 2020) were assessed for transcriptomic and lipidomic differences in the cortex. Differentially expressed genes indicated multiple mechanisms included dysregulation of mitochondrial function and disruption of cholinergic and glutamatergic systems. Affected lipid classes included ceramides, sphingomyelins and triacyl glycerides. Comparison with organophosphate ester flame retardants (OPFR) suggested that OPFRs contribute more than the BFRs to the developmental neurotoxicity of Firemaster 550. Yuan et al. ( 2024a ). IL‐10‐TGTPO‐T4 axis, the target of bis (2‐ethylhexyl) tetrabromophthalate on thyroid function imbalance.Six‐weeks old Sprague–Dawley male rats were dosed with BEH‐TEBP (95% pure) control (corn oil), 5, 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days by gavage. The treatment altered the expression of thyroid‐specific proteins, including thyroglobulin (TG), thyroid peroxidase (TPO) and sodium iodide symporter (NIS). The mRNA and protein levels of TG and TPO were significantly elevated, indicating that BEH‐TBPH might stimulate thyroid hormone synthesis in rats. BEH‐TBPH exposure also affected the expression of cytokines, particularly IL‐6 and IL‐10. Both IL‐6 and IL‐10 were upregulated in thyroid tissues of rats treated with BEH‐TBPH. The elevated levels of IL‐10 were notably linked to increased expression of TG and TPO. The effects of BEH‐TBPH were further investigated in human thyroid follicular epithelial Nthy ori3–1 cells, where BEH‐TBPH treatment also increased the mRNA and protein levels of TG, TPO and IL‐10 at 100 nM, which was the highest concentration tested. Further experiments using RNA interference to knock down IL‐6 and IL‐10 showed that IL‐10 was involved in the upregulation of TG and TPO. Silencing IL‐10 diminished the effects of BEH‐TBPH on these thyroid proteins, suggesting that IL‐10 is a target through which BEH‐TBPH influences thyroid function. Fu, Zhu, et al. (2024). Bis(2‐ethylhexyl)‐tetrabromophthalate Poses a Higher Exposure Risk and Induces Gender‐Specific Metabolic Disruptions in Zebrafish Liver.Waterborne exposure to 2 uM BEH‐TEBP for 28 days resulted in predominant accumulation in the liver. The liver of males accumulated more BEH‐TEBP than that in females. Effects of BEH‐TEBP in the liver were studies after 2‐month exposure to 0, 0.2, 2, 20, 200 nM. Female zebrafish showed increased glycogen levels and reduced cholesterol, while male zebrafish exhibited altered lipid and glucose metabolism, with increased blood glucose, triglycerides and liver fat accumulation. There was dose‐dependent increase in visceral fat accumulation which was significant at 2 nM exposure in both sexes. Male zebrafish had higher visceral fat accumulation and more severe liver damage, including signs of non‐alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD), compared to females. Male liver transcriptome analysis indicated a significant upregulation of glycolysis and insulin‐signalling pathways, contributing to these metabolic changes. Histopathological analysis revealed liver damage in both sexes, with male zebrafish showing more severe inflammation and oxidative stress. Yuan et al. ( 2024b ). Effects of 1, 2‐bis (2,4, 6‐tribromophenoxy) ethane and bis (2‐ethylhexyl) tetrabromophthalate on serum metabolic and lipid profiles in male rats.Adult rats were orally exposed (gavage) to BEH‐TEBP at 500 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days and serum samples collected for metabolomics and lipidomics analysis. BEH‐TEHBP induced specific metabolic and lipidomic changes (19 metabolites and 70 lipids differentially affected). Reliable differential substances possibly related with changes in thyroid function (dehydroascorbic acid, acylcarnitine and diglyceride) were identified to be impacted by BEH‐TEBP. Cyclooxygenase‐2, an enzyme related to oxidative stress, was selected as a potent target for BEH‐TEBP. BEH‐TEBP was showed to impact the rat metabolism possibly related to changes in thyroid function. Fu, Hua, et al. (2024). Integrated Studies on Male Reproductive Toxicity of Bis(2‐ethylhexyl)‐tetrabromophthalate: in Silico, in Vitro, ex Vivo, and in Vivo. BEH‐TEBP was found to bind stably to the androgen receptor (AR) in in silico molecular docking studies. It exhibited antagonistic, but not agonistic, activity on AR signalling using an AR‐GRIP1 yeast assay system. Mouse Leydig cells were exposed to BEH‐TEBP at 0, 0.2, 2, 20 and 200 μM (equal to 0, 141, 1412, 14,122 and 141 228 μg/L). BEH‐TEBP inhibited the proliferation of Leydig cells by 0.8%, 3.1%, 7.3% and 22.5% at concentrations of 0.2, 2, 20 and 200 μM, respectively. Leydig cells showed inhibited testosterone production at 200 μM. In zebrafish, BEH‐TEBP exposure impaired sperm motility and movement patterns at all concentrations tested. Male zebrafish (F0) exposed to BEH‐TEBP displayed reduced mating behaviour when paired with unexposed females with significant changes observed at all concentrations starting at 0.002 μM. AR expression was specifically suppressed in the brain, providing possible mechanistic evidence for endocrine‐disrupting potential via central nervous system pathways. The offspring of exposed males showed dose‐dependent changes in behavioural locomotor patters with significance observed at concentrations of 0.02 μM and higher, indicating transgenerational effects.Abbreviation: NR, not reported.

Comparative tissue distribution, biotransformation and associated biological effects by decabromodiphenyl ethane and decabrominated diphenyl ether in male rats after a 90‐day oral exposure studyIn male rats orally exposed for 90 days to 100 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day. The CYP3A2 mRNA was slightly increased in liver tissue, but other Phase 1 biotransformation enzymes (CYP1A1, CYP2B1, CYP2B2) were not affected at the mRNA level. The authors suggested that DBDPE may induce PXR‐dependent gene expression (induction of CYP3A biotransformation enzymes), but no AHR and CAR‐dependent gene expression and consequent possible adverse effects. Cytotoxicity and apoptosis induction in human HepG2 hepatoma cells by decabromodiphenyl ethaneHepG2 cells were cultured in the presence of DBDPE at 0 (DMSO), 3.125, 6.25, 12.5, 25.0, 50.0, 100.0 μg/mL for 24, 48 and 72 h, respectively. DBDPE inhibited HepG2 viability (MTT test) in a time and dose‐dependent manner within a range of 12.5 to 100 mg/L and for 48 and 72 h. Induction of apoptosis was detected at 12.5–100 μg/mL at 48 and 72 h accompanied with overproduction of ROS. Moreover, N‐acetyl‐L‐cysteine (ROS scavenger), significantly reduced DBDPE‐induced ROS levels and increased HepG2 cells viability. Sun et al. ( 2014 ). Sub‐acute effect of decabromodiphenyl ethane on hepatotoxicity and hepatic enzyme activity in ratsMale and female Wistar rats were exposed by gavage for 28 days to DBDPE at 0, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000 mg/kg bw per day. No significant changes observed in liver weight (absolute or relative) following exposure. The Glu, TBA, ALT and AST levels following exposure to various doses of DBDPE were markedly higher. The Glu level was higher after exposure to ≥ 250 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE (≥ 250 mg/kg bw per day) increased UDPGT, 7‐pentoxyresorufin O‐depentylase (PROD) and luciferin benzylether debenzylase activities, and induced CYP2B2, CYP3A1 and CYP3A2 mRNA, CYP3A2 and CYP2B1/2 proteins. Increasing UDPGT activity with increasing dose suggest that oral DBDPE exposure induces drug‐metabolising enzymes in rats via the CAR/PXR signalling pathway. According to the study authors, the induction of CYPs and co‐regulated enzymes of phase II biotransformation may affect the homeostasis of endogenous substrates, including thyroid hormones, which may, in turn, alter glucose metabolism. Smythe et al. ( 2017 ). Impacts of unregulated novel brominated flame retardants on human liver thyroid deiodination and sulfotransferationThe inhibitory effects of DBDPE on DIO and SULT activity were investigated using human in vitro liver microsomal and cytosolic bioassays (concentrations: 0.13 to 130 nM or 260 nM). The potential inhibition of hepatic ORD and IRD activity in the presence of DBDPE was monitored by evaluating the production of T3 and rT3 from T4, respectively. There was almost complete inhibition of T3 and 3,3’‐T2 formation from T4 and 3,3’‐T2 formation from rT3 by DBDPE at the highest dose tested (260 nM). A concentration−response relationship was observed for formation of T3 from T4 IRD and for formation of 3,3’‐T2 from rT3 ORD. Significant inhibition of T2 formation from T4 ORD/IRD was only observed at the highest dose level. Inhibition of 3,3’‐T2 sulfation was investigated by monitoring sulfotransferase activity in the presence of DBDPE. No statistically significant inhibition of SULT activity was observed (up to 130 nM). Wang, Chen, et al. (2019). A comparison of the thyroid disruption induced by decabrominated diphenyl ethers (BDE‐209) and decabromodiphenyl ethane (DBDPE) in ratsMale Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE by gavage for 28 days to 0, 5, 50 or 500 mg/kg bw per day. Doses 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day could downregulate the expression of TSHR, NIS, TPO and TG genes in thyroid gland. Slight decreased TT4 and TT3 levels in serum after DBDPE exposure might be caused by impair of key components of TH biosynthesis, such as TSHR, NIS, TG and TPO. High levels of DBDPE exposure decreased the expression of Pax8 genes which might explain the decrease of TG, TPO, NIS and TSH receptor (TSHR) expression, eventually leading to reduced synthesis of T4 and T3. DBDPE exposure could up‐regulate the expression of TRHR gene in the pituitary compared to control. According to the authors, reduced thyroid hormone levels in serum had a feedback effect to the pituitary, and as a consequence, TSH synthesis was increased. Sun et al. ( 2020 ). Hepatotoxicity of decabromodiphenyl ethane (DBDPE) and decabromodiphenyl ether (BDE‐209) in 28‐day exposed Sprague‐Dawley ratsSprague–Dawley male rats were exposed by gavage to DBDPE (purity > 98%) for 28 days to 0, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day. BDE‐209 and DBDPE induce male reproductive toxicity through telomere‐related cell senescence and apoptosis in SD rat28‐day exposure of male Sprague–Dawley rats to DBDPE could damage the telomeric function by shortening telomere length and reducing telomerase activity at 500 mg/kg bw per day, which consequently caused cell senescence and apoptosis in testis of rat. According to the authors, these effects could contribute to the decline of sperm quality and quantity. Shi and Feng ( 2021 ). Decabromodiphenyl ethane exposure damaged the asymmetric division of mouse oocytes by inhibiting the inactivation of cyclin‐dependent kinase 1Three‐week‐old female mice were orally exposed to DBDPE at 0, 0.05, 0.5, 5, 50 μg/kg bw per day for 30 days. Exposure did not affect the first polar body extrusion of oocytes, but asymmetric division of oocytes was markedly impaired at 5 and 50 μg/kg bw per day, which resulted in oocytes with larger polar bodies. DBDPE exposure inhibited the spindle migration and membrane protrusion in oocytes during anaphase of meiosis I (anaphase I), thereby impairing asymmetric division. DBDPE exposure suppressed the inactivation of cyclin‐dependent kinase 1 (Cdk1), resulting in the decrease of cytoplasmic formin2 (FMN2)‐mediated F‐actin polymerisation in oocytes at the onset of anaphase I. Simultaneously, DBDPE exposure damaged the structural integrity of the spindle and the perpendicular relationship between spindle and cortex. These together led to the failure of spindle migration and membrane protrusion required for oocytes asymmetric division. Finally, DBDPE exposure injured the development of blastocysts, leading to blastocyst apoptosis. Shi, Qiu, et al. (2021). The toxic effects and possible mechanisms of decabromodiphenyl ethane on mouse oocyte DBDPE inhibited the rate of germinal vesicle breakdown (GVBD), first polar body extrusion (at 20–100 μM) and fertilisation of mouse oocytes (at 50 μM). After 14 h exposure, the hardness of zona pellucida metaphase II oocytes markedly increased and the spindle morphology was abnormal. DBDPE exposure‐induced abnormal mitochondrial distribution, mitochondrial dysfunction and ATP deficiency. It also downregulated the expression of antioxidant‐related genes (Sod2, Gpx1) and increased the level of ROS in oocytes. Autophagy occurred in treated oocytes with high expression of autophagy‐related protein (LC3) and genes (Lc3, Beclin1). In addition, DBDPE significantly upregulated the protein (Bax) and mRNA (Bax, Caspase3) levels of pro‐apoptosis genes. However, the protein and mRNA expression of anti‐apoptosis genes Bcl‐2 was downregulated in exposed oocytes. The authors concluded that DBDPE exposure impaired mitochondrial function, causing oxidative damage, autophagy and apoptosis in oocytes. Zheng et al. ( 2021 ). NLRP3 inflammasome‐mediated endothelial cells pyroptosis is involved in decabromodiphenyl ethane‐induced vascular endothelial injury Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE (purity ≥ 98%) at 0, 5, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day by gavage for 28 days. At the two highest doses there were increases in the protein levels of IL‐1b and IL‐18 in the abdominal aorta, demonstrating inflammatory reaction. Doses of 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day induced the activation of NLRP3 inflammasome, induced pyroptosis through activated caspase‐1 in abdominal aorta endothelium of rats and thus determined the important roles of NLRP3 inflammasome and caspase‐1 in cardiovascular diseases. In vitro, human vascular endothelial cells (HAECs) were treated for 24 h with 0 (DMSO), 6.25, 12.5, 25, 50, 100 mM DBDPE. DBDPE decreased viability, induced cytotoxicity (increase LDH activity) at concentrations ≥ 12.5 mM. It induced ROS generation and caused HAECs pyroptosis at 25 and 100 mM, which was evidenced by the elevated expression of Nod‐like receptor protein‐3 (NLRP3), ASC and caspase‐1 in DBDPE‐treated group. The authors constructed NLRP3 knockdown HAECs by lentivirus‐mediated short hairpin RNA (shRNA). It was shown that NLRP3 knockdown downregulated DBDPE‐induced increases of caspase‐1 activity and caspase‐1, ASC and NLRP3 mRNA and protein expression levels. The authors indicated that DBDPE caused HAECs pyroptosis by activating NLRP3 inflammasome and caspase‐1. Gao et al. ( 2022 ). Fat mass and obesity‐associated gene (FTO) hypermethylation induced by decabromodiphenyl ethane causing cardiac dysfunction via glucolipid metabolism disorder In the same male rats exposed for 28 days as in Sun et al. (2020), DBDPE induced significant disruption of myocardial systolic and diastolic function as shown by the decreased level of MYH6 and increased level of SERCA2 (the two key proteins involved in the maintenance of homeostasis during myocardial contractile and diastolic processes). Mitochondrial respiration‐related protein was measured, and the expressions of UCP2 decreased in all the treated groups in a dose‐dependent manner. The ATP level in heart tissue of rats was significantly reduced at 500 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE also increased the serum levels of glucose (at 500 mg/kg bw per day) and low‐density lipoprotein but decreased the content of high‐density lipoprotein (at the two high doses). The expressions of FTO in the 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day groups were reduced. Methylation level of the promoter region of the FTO gene were increased by DBDPE. The authors concluded that DBDPE exposure‐induced glycolipid metabolism disorder contributes to myocardial dysfunction through cardiomyocytes fibrosis and apoptosis via the mitochondrial‐mediated apoptotic pathway resulting from mitochondrial damage and dysfunction. FTO hypermethylation mediated this pathogenetic process via the PI3K/AKT/GLUT2, PPARγ/RXRα and mitochondrial apoptosis pathways. AC16 cells (Ventricular Cardiomyocyte Cell Line) were exposed to 0, 25, 50, 100 μM DBDPE, 0 μM DBDPE+12 μM 5‐aza and 100 μM DBDPE+12 μM 5‐aza for 24 h. The methylation rates were elevated critically in 100 μM DBDPE. FTO level significantly decreased in 50 and 100 μM groups, while 5‐aza, the inhibitor of DNA methylation, could significantly reverse the level induced by DBDPE. Cells viability gradually decreased with the increase of DBDPE concentration. DBDPE at 100 μM downregulate the level of MYH6. Additionally, the levels of SERCA2 increased in the 50 and 100 μM groups. In addition, the ROS level in 100 μM group was significantly increased when compared to that of control. DBDPE significantly decreased the level of ATP. 5‐aza could partially reverse the changed expressions of MYH6 and SERCA2 induced by DBDPE. Also, 5‐ aza could reduce the increase of ROS and antagonise the decrease of ATP level caused by DBDPE. The apoptotic rate of the cells increased at 100 μM. The UCP2 level was significantly decreased in all DBDPE exposure groups indicating the effect of DBDPE on mitochondria function. Hua et al. ( 2022 ). Decabromodiphenyl ethane induced hyperactivity in developing zebrafish at environmentally relevant concentrations.Zebrafish embryos were exposed to DBDPE (0, 2.91, 9.71, 29.14, 97.12 μg/L) from 2 to 120 h post‐fertilisation (hpf). Behavioural assessment of embryonic motor reflexes and larvae locomotor activity was performed at various times during the post‐fertilisation period. Morphological organisation of muscle fibres and motor neurons was assessed at 36 and 120 hpf. Whole‐body neurotransmitter content, cholinergic‐related enzyme activities (choline acyltransferase, ChAT and acetylcholine esterase, AchE) and specific genes expression related to brain development and nicotinic acetylcholine receptors were measured at 120 hpf. Embryonic exposure to DBDPE caused hyperactivity without affecting the development of secondary motoneuron axons and muscle fibres. However, further results implicated that DBDPE may affect the locomotor regulatory network via different mechanisms at lower and higher concentrations. Whole‐body neurotransmitter concentrations were significantly reduced at the lower dose of DBDPE whereas specific increases in acetylcholine content and ChAT activity, and concomitant reduction in AchE, were observed only at the two highest levels of dose. Molecular docking results showed direct interaction of DBDPE with ChAT. Results suggested that hyperactivity observed at higher concentrations of DBDPE was probably related to disruption of the cholinergic system with ChAT as a potential target. Jing et al. ( 2022 ). Oxidative stress and endoplasmic reticulum stress contributed to hepatotoxicity of decabromodiphenyl ethane (DBDPE) in L‐02 cellsHuman hepatocytes (L‐02) were exposed to 0 (DMSO), 6.25, 12.5, 25, 50 and 100 μM DBDPE. It caused L‐02 cell viability decrease (25–100 μM), increased lactate LDH (at 25 and 50 μM) and AST (concentration‐dependent) and ALT (at 25 and 50 μM) levels, ultrastructural damage and apoptosis. Moreover, DBDPE induced oxidative stress (i.e. increased ROS generation and MDA levels and decreased GSH content, SOD activity and mitochondrial membrane potential at 50 μM) and ER stress as evidenced by the elevated PERK and IRE‐1a expression levels. The CYP3A expression level was decreased in cells exposed o 50 μM. Pretreatment of L‐02 cells with antioxidant NAC and endoplasmic reticulum stress inhibitor 4‐PBA inhibited DBDPE‐induced oxidative stress, endoplasmic reticulum stress, CYP3A expression decrease and apoptosis. Shi, Xu, et al. (2022). Decabromodiphenyl ethane affects embryonic development by interfering with nuclear F‐Actin in zygotes and leads to cognitive and social disorders in offspring mice.Female Kunming mice were exposed for 30 days to 50 μg DBDPE/kg bw per day. After treatment, female mice were i.p. injected with PMSG and 48 h later, they were injected with HCG. Then, the females were mated with sexually matured males. DBDPE exposure did not affect spindle rotation in oocytes after fertilisation, but led to a decrease of pronuclei (PN) in zygotes. DBDPE interfered with the self‐assembly of F‐actin in pronuclei of embryo, resulting in their size reduction in zygotes. In addition, DBDPE reduced the expression of the Zscan4c transcription factor involved in maintaining genome stability during embryogenesis. The authors concluded that this early developmental instability may lead to metabolic and structural defects observed in later life, and that DBDPE exposure might affect embryonic development and result in behavioural defects in F1 mice related to learning and memory, spontaneous activity and social behaviour. The authors reported that DBDPE significantly increased γ‐H2AX expression in both male and female pronuclei (PN), suggesting that DBDPE exposure elevated DNA damage and that the accumulation of DNA damage was associated with reduced F‐actin levels. However, the Panel noted that increased γ‐H2AX expression in zygotes is not necessarily indicative of DNA damage. Instead, γ‐H2AX can also be involved in normal developmental processes, such as chromatin remodelling after fertilisation and genome reprogramming. Shi, Qiu, et al. (2022). Exogenous melatonin protects preimplantation embryo development from decabromodiphenyl ethane‐induced circadian rhythm disorder and endogenous melatonin reduction. DBDPE (5, 50, 500 μg/kg bw per day) exposure‐induced circadian rhythm disorder in adult female Kunming mice exposed by gavage for 14 days. It decreased also endogenous melatonin levels during pregnancy, which impaired preimplantation embryo development. DBDPE exposure impaired embryos by inducing oxidative stress, abnormal DNA methylation, mitochondrial dysfunction and mechanical property changes. DBDPE‐induced embryo damage led to adverse reproductive outcomes. DBDPE exposure led to endogenous rhythm disorder in mice because it reduced the expression of BMAL1. After pregnancy, DBDPE‐exposed mice with circadian rhythm disorders exhibited an inhibition of preimplantation embryo development. Therefore, DBDPE exposure‐induced circadian rhythm disorder resulting in embryo damage. The concentration of melatonin in the serum and oviduct of DBDPE‐exposed mice decreased significantly. The authors suggested that the circadian rhythm disorder and decrease in endogenous melatonin in DBDPE‐exposed mice inhibited preimplantation embryo development. It was also shown that DBDPE might affect epigenetic modifications in early embryos. Melatonin rescued the abnormal DNA methylation induced by DBDPE to improve preimplantation embryo development. The number of mitochondria dramatically increase to match the energy requirements of blastocyst formation, and mitochondrial dysfunction or deficiency induces developmental arrest of early embryos. It was shown that ATP levels in the blastocysts in DBDPE‐treated mice were reduced. This was related to the decrease in MMP and the number of mitochondria induced by DBDPE. The decline in the number of mitochondria may have been caused by the decreased expression of Drp1, which regulates mitochondrial division The authors reported that the number and functional disorder of mitochondria caused by DBDPE directly led to a reduction in melatonin synthesis, and that since mitochondria are also the location of melatonin functions, supplementation with melatonin rescued the mitochondrial dysfunction induced by DBDPE. Yan et al. ( 2022 ). In utero exposure to decabromodiphenyl ethane causes rapid growth in mice cubs by activating glycogenolysis and lipid synthesisPregnant mice were exposed to DBDPE at 0, 0.1, 10 mg/kg bw by oral gavage daily until the cubs were born. At PND1, the female to male ratio was counted. Then 8 cubs were randomly selected in each dam and continued to be fed (8 cubs each dam, 10 dam each group). Exposure to DBDPE during pregnancy had no significant effect on the body weight of pregnant mice. The litter size and the sex ratio were not significantly changed. However, DBDPE exposure in utero caused low birth weight in cubs and then the cubs grow rapidly during lactation. It was shown that in utero exposure to DBDPE affects the whole‐body metabolic profile of newborns; the cubs absorbed the energy quickly and underwent active glucose metabolism and lipid accumulation for rapid growth. After 25 days of rapid energy accumulation, cubs exposed to DBDPE were sufficient to alter metabolic profiles and body composition such as increased lipid content, triglyceride and cholesterol levels, and ultimately lead to early symptoms of obesity. The authors concluded that exposure to DBDPE may cause cubs to undergo active glycolysis pathway during which large amounts of pyruvate were produced, thereby promoting lipid accumulation and ultimately obesity. Xue et al. ( 2022 ). Decabromodiphenyl ethane induces male reproductive toxicity by glycolipid metabolism imbalance and meiotic failureMale Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to 0, 5, 50 and 500 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day. The meiosis regulator MILI (piwi‐like RNA‐mediated gene silencing 2) expression was downregulated in the cytoplasm of spermatocytes at 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE downregulated the expressions of spermatogenesis‐ and oogenesis‐specific helix‐loop‐helix transcription factor 1 (Sohlh1), and synaptonemal complex proteins 1 and 3 (SYCP1 and SYCP3) at these doses. The Cyclin A expression was reduced in all treated groups and the expression of cyclin‐dependent kinase 2 (CDK2) was downregulated only in the highest dose group. DBDPE activated the mitochondrial apoptotic pathway in the testes. The Bcl‐2/Bax ratio was found to be downregulated, while the expressions of Cytochrome C, Caspase‐9 and Caspase‐3 were upregulated in the 500 mg/kg bw per day group only. The expression of Cleaved Caspase‐ 9 and Cleaved Caspase‐3 increased in the 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day groups. So, DBDPE exposure result in meiotic failure leading to chromosomal association disorder in meiosis and spermatocyte cycle arrest. DBDPE produced mitochondrial damage and dysfunction and significantly increased serum glucose and low‐density lipoprotein cholesterol levels. Also, DBDPE caused meiotic failure through testicular DNA damage, reduced the expressions of meiotic regulatory factors (Sohlh1, MILI, CDK2 and CyclinA) and synaptonemal complex proteins SYCP1 and SYCP3. The authors concluded that DBDPE induced glycolipid metabolism disorder and promoted apoptosis of spermatogenic cells through the mitochondrial‐mediated apoptosis pathway, leading to reproductive toxicity by decreasing sperm quantity and quality. Dong, Wang, Zhang, et al. (2023). DBDPE and ZnO NPs synergistically induce neurotoxicity of SK‐N‐SH cells and activate mitochondrial apoptosis signaling pathway and Nrf2‐mediated antioxidant pathway.The present study assessed the neurotoxicity of DBDPE alone or in combination with Zinc oxide particles on human neuroblastoma SK‐N‐SH cells. Cells were exposed to DBDPE (0, 0.1, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0, 12.5 and 25 mg/L) for 12, 24, 36 and 48 h. DBDPE induced a significant reduction in cell viability after 24 h of exposure at doses of 5.0, 12.5 and 25 mg/L. Concomitant LDH activity increases were observed at the same doses. DBDPE 25 mg/L induced a significant increase in ROS production measured through the oxidation of the fluorescent probe DCFH‐DA, the MDA production, the SOD activity and the intracellular GSH content. DBDPE 25 mg/L induced mitochondrial damages and cell apoptosis reflected by significant reductions in mitochondrial membrane potential, and concomitant increases in caspase‐3 and ‐9 activities, and in the expression of apoptosis‐related genes and proteins (Bax, Bcl‐2, caspase‐3, Cyt C). Proportions of apoptotic cells were significantly increased after exposure to the same dose of DBDPE. The Nrf2/ARE signalling pathway was activated with DBDPE 12.5 and 25 mg/L to restore the redox stat in response to stimulation by DBDPE and induction of oxidative stress. The results suggest the neurotoxicity of DBDPE with the induction of oxidative stress and apoptosis as potential mechanisms of toxicity. Dong, Wang, Wang, et al. (2023). Investigating the adverse outcome pathways (AOP) of neurotoxicity induced by DBDPE with a combination of in vitro and in silico approaches.The neurotoxicity of DBDPE was investigated through a combination of in silico and in vitro approaches in human neuroblastoma SK‐N‐SH cells. Molecular docking simulation showed the ability of DBDPE to interact with PARP1 (poly (ADP‐ribose) polymerase‐1), an enzyme that participates to a caspase‐independent apoptosis pathway and to change the secondary structure of the protein. In SK‐n‐SH cells, DBDPE 12.5 and 25 mg/L induced significant increases in ROS production and MDA content, and reductions in SOD activity and GSH cell content. The same doses of DBDPE increased significantly the apoptosis rate measured through the staining of the Hoechst 33342 fluorescent probe. DBDPE 12.5 and 25 mg/L induced mitochondrial damages reflected by significant reductions in mitochondrial membrane potential and ATP production. The same doses of DBDPE increased PARP1 activity and AIF (apoptosis‐induced factor) translocation from mitochondria to the nucleus, leading to caspase‐independent apoptosis. Caspase‐3 and ‐9 activities and apoptosis‐related gene and protein (Bax, Bcl‐2, caspase‐3, Cyt C) expression were also increased after exposure to DBDPE 12.5 and 25 mg/L. Such results suggest the neurotoxicity of DBDPE and its ability to induce cell apoptosis by initiating key cellular events such as mitochondrial dysfunction, ROS production and DNA damage through caspase‐dependent and ‐independent pathways. Sun et al. ( 2023 ). Evaluation and Mechanistic Study of Transgenerational Neurotoxicity in Zebrafish upon Life Cycle Exposure to Decabromodiphenyl Ethane (DBDPE).F0 zebrafish embryos were exposed to DBDPE (0, 0.1, 1 and 10 nM) from fertilisation (2 h hpf) until 8 months old. Then, sexually matured F0 adults were paired to generate F1 embryos that were cultured until 8 months old. F2 embryos were obtained by pairing F1 adults and cultured until they were 8 months old. Contrary to F0, F1 and F2 generations were reared in clean water. Growth and behavioural performances (shoaling behaviour and social preference test) were studied in male and female adults of each generation (n = 8/sex/treatment/generation). Protein and neurotransmitter in eggs and brains from adult males and females in all treatment groups and generations were also measured. DNA damage (8‐OHdG), global DNA methylation (5‐mC) and transcriptomic sequencing were determined in male and female adult brains in all treatment groups and generations. Cell cycle and apoptosis were assessed in the brain of female zebrafish in all treatment groups and generations. The results showed that the effects on growth and shoaling behaviour of life cycle exposure to DBDPE were impaired in the female offspring whereas they remained quite unaffected in the male offspring. Sociability and recognition with the conspecifics were also impaired in DBDPE exposed F0 generation and the unexposed offspring (F1 and F2). The protein contents in the brain from both male and female adult brains were decreased in F0 generation, female F1 eggs and female F1 and F2 brains whereas the male eggs and adult brains (F1 and F2) were not affected. Specific neurotransmitter contents (Ache, dopamine, epinephrine, glutamate) were changed in F0 male and female brains, remained unaffected in F1 animals, and increased in F2 male and female fish. In addition, the disruption of neurotransmitters in lower dose groups in F2 females was stronger than that in the higher dose groups. In addition, the tested neurotransmitters were also altered in F1 and F2 eggs, suggesting a specific disturbance of the parental transfer of neurotransmitters to zygotes. A lack of changes in the F1 generation and opposite changing trends in the F0 and F2 generations were observed regarding the results of 8‐OHdG and 5‐mC brain levels and specific gene expression. In females, exposure of DBDPE led to cell cycle regulation disorders in the brain of all 3 generations, whereas apoptosis was observed in only F2 offspring from F0 animals exposed to DBDPE 10 nM. Taken together, such results indicated the ability of DBDPE exposure at environmentally relevant doses to induce transgenerational neurotoxicity in zebrafish.Yang, Zhu, et al. (2023). Mitochondrial Dysfunction Was Involved in Decabromodiphenyl Ethane‐Induced Glucolipid Metabolism Disorders and Neurotoxicity in Zebrafish Larvae.Zebrafish embryos were exposed to DBDPE (0, 50, 100, 200, 400 μg/L) from 2 h post‐fertilisation (hpf) to 120 hpf. Motor activity, whole‐body neurotransmitters and gene transcription measurements were done after 120 h of exposure. Mitochondrial respiration and oxidative stress measurements were also performed at the end of the period of exposure. In addition, a nontargeted metabolomic analysis was performed in control and 400 μg/L‐exposed larvae after 120 hpf. DBDPE 400 μg/L significantly increased in zebrafish larvae the malformation and heart beat rates, the locomotor activity during the dark–light transition period, and the whole‐body contents of various neurotransmitters like glutamate, epinephrine and norepinephrine, and acetylcholine whereas the content of GABA and the serotonin metabolite 5‐HIAA was significantly reduced. The untargeted metabolomic results showed different changes of metabolites upon DBDPE exposure, involved in glycolipid metabolism, oxidative phosphorylation and oxidative stress, and suggest a potential mechanism related with mitochondrial dysfunction for DBDPE toxicity. Exploration of mitochondria upon DBDPE exposure revealed inhibition in mitochondrial oxidative respiration, and reductions in respiratory chain complex activities, ATP content and mitochondrial membrane potential. All these results suggest the mitochondrial dysfunction is involved in DBDPE‐induced brain toxicity in zebrafish larvae. Li et al. ( 2024 ). Transcriptome‐based approach to identify mechanisms underlying locomotor abnormality induced by decabromodiphenyl ethane in zebrafish larvae.Zebrafish larvae were exposed to DBDPE (0, 3, 30, 300 μg/L) over the 5 days post‐fertilisation. Larvae were monitored for their locomotor activity at 120 hpf and transcriptomic sequencing performed at the same stage. Specific assessment of heart rate was performed in fishes of the 0, 30 and 300 μg/L ‐ exposed groups with adding 0.5 mg/L salbutamol. Results showed dose‐related increases in locomotor activity which was significant at the highest dose of DBDPE. Transcriptomic analyses indicated that pathways associated with cardiac muscle contraction and retinol metabolism. Locomotor hyperactivity was shown to be related to increasing heart rate linked to inhibition of Na+/K+ – ATPase activity whereas no histological alterations were identified. Larvae eye histology and retinoid contents remained unchanged in DBDPE‐exposed animals. Such results suggested the developmental toxicity of DBDPE in a zebrafish model of exposure. Xie et al. ( 2025 ). Decabromodiphenyl ethane exposure‐mediated mitochondrial dysfunction drives oxeiptosis in placental trophoblasts and induces fetal growth restrictionExposure of ICR mice to DBDPE during gestation caused placental damage, resulting in fetal growth restriction (FGR). DBDPE exposure induces oxeiptosis in placental trophoblasts via activating KEAP1/PGAM5/AIFM1 signalling. A significant reduction in the phosphorylation level of AIFM1 Ser116 in placental trophoblasts was observed, specifically correlating with the activation of oxeiptosis. Further investigations were performed in vitro in HTR8/SVneo cells. Metabolomic and transcriptomic analyses suggested that DBDPE exposure disrupts the oxidative phosphorylation (OXPHOS) pathway, thereby impairing mitochondrial function. Treatment with MitoQ, a mitochondria‐targeted antioxidant, effectively reversed DBDPE‐induced oxeiptosis in placental trophoblasts, alleviating the negative effects of DBDPE on placental damage and FGR. Mechanistically, the mitochondrial dysfunction induced by gestational DBDPE exposure initiates oxeiptosis in placental trophoblasts, exacerbating placental injury and ultimately leading to FGR. Li et al. ( 2025 ). Brominated Flame Retardants Exposure Promotes the Formation of Atherosclerotic Plaque in Aortic Roots of ApoE–/–Mice by Increasing Macrophage Recruitment and Foam Cell Formation.The authors investigate the effects of DBDPE on the development of atherosclerosis and explored the underlying mechanisms using an in vitro cell model and ApoE−/− mice. DBDPE (10 μmol/L, 24 h) significantly inhibited the viability of human aortic endothelial cells (HAECs) and induced the generation of ROS. Macrophages are the main immune cells involved in the formation of atherosclerosis, and the phagocytosis of Ox‐LDL by macrophages to form foam cells plays a very important role in the occurrence and development of atherosclerosis. DBDPE significantly enhanced the content of Ox‐LDL in THP‐1 macrophages, which promoted the formation of foam cells. In the in vivo study, DBDPE exposure significantly increased the plaque area and lipid content in the aortic root of mice. It also significantly increased the ROS level in plaques and promoted the expression level of adhesion molecule ICAM‐1, which enhanced the recruitment of macrophages. Transcriptome analysis showed that differentially expressed genes (DEGs) were significantly enriched in signalling pathways related to oxidative stress and lipid metabolism. Qi et al. ( 2025 ). The single and combined effects of decabromodiphenyl ethane and mixed microplastics on male mice reproductive toxicity.To investigate the mechanisms of reproductive toxicity of DBDPE, experiments using the mouse premeiotic germ cell line GC‐2 were performed. DBDPE exposure can lead to changes in thousands of differentially expressed genes (DGEs), in which the cell cycle, p53 signalling pathway, apoptosis, FoxO signalling pathway and TNF signalling pathway change significantly. The results also suggest that oxidative stress (increase of ROS level, increase of cellular MDA content and decrease of SOD level after 24 h exposure to 16 mg/L DBDPE) and apoptosis maybe potential target pathways of DBDPE induce reproductive toxicity. Protein expression levels of Nrf2 and Keap1 in mice testis were also examined by western blot analysis. Male mice were exposed by gavage to 0, 5 or 50 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day for 7 weeks. The protein expression levels of Nrf2 decreased with increasing doses of DBDPE. Conversely, Keap1 protein levels were increased dose‐dependently with DBDPE. The genes related to the p53 apoptosis pathway in mouse testes were investigated. The mRNA levels of p53 and Bax were both increased significantly following DBDPE exposure. The trend of Bcl‐2 was opposite to that of p53. The results showed that the toxicity of DBDPE is associated with oxidative stress and apoptosis. Results of transcriptomics from GC‐2 cells indicated that DBDPE lead to changes in oxidative stress, apoptosis, inflammation, proliferation and differentiation. Furthermore, DBDPE exposure inhibited the Kelch‐like ECH‐associated protein1‐NF‐E2‐related factor2 (Keap1‐Nrf2) signalling pathway. Cui et al. ( 2025 ). ARMMs modulate Notch signaling pathway in DBDPE‐induced liver lipid metabolism disorders: An in vivo and in vitro study.The impact of DBDPE on the liver in rats and L02 cells (immortalised hepatocyte line) was investigated. Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed by gavage to 0, 5, 50 or 500 mg DBDPE/kg bw per day for 8 weeks. L02 cells were exposed for 24 h to 0, 6.25, 12.5, 25 and 50 μM DBDPE. Low‐dose DBDPE exposure slightly promoted L02 cell proliferation. However, high‐dose DBDPE exposure significantly inhibited L02 cell proliferation, suggesting that DBDPE is also cytotoxic. It was shown that DBDPE exposure increased the number of lipid droplets in L02 cells through Nile Red staining and colorimetric assays, with significant elevations in TC, TG, HDL and LDL levels. The ultrastructural changes in L02 cells also supported this finding. TEM results showed that, compared with the control group, L02 cells exposed to DBDPE exhibited numerous lipid vacuoles. DBDPE exposure could induce lipid metabolism disorders, up‐regulate the expressions of Notch pathway genes and promote Arrestin domain containing protein 1‐ mediated microvesicles (ARMMs)‐derivation. Subsequently, siRNAs were used to inhibit the Notch pathway and the ARMMs‐derivation in L02 cells respectively. Inhibition of Notch or ARMMs‐derivation could reverse the lipid accumulation induced by DBDPE, and the inhibition of ARMMs‐derivation could suppress the activation of the Notch pathway. Extracellular vesicles from L02 cells exposed to DBDPE co‐culture with L02 cells confirmed that ARMMs can directly activate the Notch signalling pathway and induce lipid accumulation.Abbreviations: AchE, acetylcholine esterase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; CAR, constitutive androstane receptor; ChAT, choline acetyltransferase; DIO, thyroid hormone deiodinase; FGR, fetal growth restriction; GSH, glutathione; LDH, lactate dehydrogenase; MDA, malondialdehyde; NAC, N‐acetyl‐l‐cysteine; PARP1, poly(ADP‐ribose) polymerase‐1; PXR, pregnane X receptor; ROS, reactive oxygen species; SOD, superoxide dismutase; SULT, thyroid hormone sulfotransferase.

Studies informing the mode of action of DBDPE

Sprague–Dawley rats were exposed to DBDPE (purity ≥ 98%) at 0, 5, 50, 500 mg/kg bw per day by gavage for 28 days. At the two highest doses there were increases in the protein levels of IL‐1b and IL‐18 in the abdominal aorta, demonstrating inflammatory reaction. Doses of 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day induced the activation of NLRP3 inflammasome, induced pyroptosis through activated caspase‐1 in abdominal aorta endothelium of rats and thus determined the important roles of NLRP3 inflammasome and caspase‐1 in cardiovascular diseases.

In vitro, human vascular endothelial cells (HAECs) were treated for 24 h with 0 (DMSO), 6.25, 12.5, 25, 50, 100 mM DBDPE. DBDPE decreased viability, induced cytotoxicity (increase LDH activity) at concentrations ≥ 12.5 mM. It induced ROS generation and caused HAECs pyroptosis at 25 and 100 mM, which was evidenced by the elevated expression of Nod‐like receptor protein‐3 (NLRP3), ASC and caspase‐1 in DBDPE‐treated group. The authors constructed NLRP3 knockdown HAECs by lentivirus‐mediated short hairpin RNA (shRNA). It was shown that NLRP3 knockdown downregulated DBDPE‐induced increases of caspase‐1 activity and caspase‐1, ASC and NLRP3 mRNA and protein expression levels. The authors indicated that DBDPE caused HAECs pyroptosis by activating NLRP3 inflammasome and caspase‐1.

In the same male rats exposed for 28 days as in Sun et al. (2020), DBDPE induced significant disruption of myocardial systolic and diastolic function as shown by the decreased level of MYH6 and increased level of SERCA2 (the two key proteins involved in the maintenance of homeostasis during myocardial contractile and diastolic processes). Mitochondrial respiration‐related protein was measured, and the expressions of UCP2 decreased in all the treated groups in a dose‐dependent manner. The ATP level in heart tissue of rats was significantly reduced at 500 mg/kg bw per day. DBDPE also increased the serum levels of glucose (at 500 mg/kg bw per day) and low‐density lipoprotein but decreased the content of high‐density lipoprotein (at the two high doses). The expressions of FTO in the 50 and 500 mg/kg bw per day groups were reduced. Methylation level of the promoter region of the FTO gene were increased by DBDPE. The authors concluded that DBDPE exposure‐induced glycolipid metabolism disorder contributes to myocardial dysfunction through cardiomyocytes fibrosis and apoptosis via the mitochondrial‐mediated apoptotic pathway resulting from mitochondrial damage and dysfunction. FTO hypermethylation mediated this pathogenetic process via the PI3K/AKT/GLUT2, PPARγ/RXRα and mitochondrial apoptosis pathways.

AC16 cells (Ventricular Cardiomyocyte Cell Line) were exposed to 0, 25, 50, 100 μM DBDPE, 0 μM DBDPE+12 μM 5‐aza and 100 μM DBDPE+12 μM 5‐aza for 24 h. The methylation rates were elevated critically in 100 μM DBDPE. FTO level significantly decreased in 50 and 100 μM groups, while 5‐aza, the inhibitor of DNA methylation, could significantly reverse the level induced by DBDPE. Cells viability gradually decreased with the increase of DBDPE concentration. DBDPE at 100 μM downregulate the level of MYH6. Additionally, the levels of SERCA2 increased in the 50 and 100 μM groups. In addition, the ROS level in 100 μM group was significantly increased when compared to that of control. DBDPE significantly decreased the level of ATP. 5‐aza could partially reverse the changed expressions of MYH6 and SERCA2 induced by DBDPE. Also, 5‐ aza could reduce the increase of ROS and antagonise the decrease of ATP level caused by DBDPE. The apoptotic rate of the cells increased at 100 μM. The UCP2 level was significantly decreased in all DBDPE exposure groups indicating the effect of DBDPE on mitochondria function.

Abbreviations: AchE, acetylcholine esterase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; CAR, constitutive androstane receptor; ChAT, choline acetyltransferase; DIO, thyroid hormone deiodinase; FGR, fetal growth restriction; GSH, glutathione; LDH, lactate dehydrogenase; MDA, malondialdehyde; NAC, N‐acetyl‐l‐cysteine; PARP1, poly(ADP‐ribose) polymerase‐1; PXR, pregnane X receptor; ROS, reactive oxygen species; SOD, superoxide dismutase; SULT, thyroid hormone sulfotransferase.

HBCD, TBECH, and BTBPE exhibit cytotoxic effects in human vascular endothelial cells by regulating mitochondria function and ROS production.Human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs) were used to investigate cellular effects of BTBPE. Cell migration and tube formation were inhibited at concentrations of 10 μM and above. Cui, Xiao, Zhang, et al. (2024). 1,2‐bis(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy) ethane, a novel brominated flame retardant, disrupts intestinal barrier function via the IRX3/NOS2 axis in rat small intestine. BTBPE exposure significantly increased IRX3 and NOS2 expression in the jejunum and ileum, promoting inflammatory gene activation and necroptosis through RIP1, RIP3 and MLKL, including their phosphorylated forms. Immunohistochemistry confirmed increased NOS2 signals. BTBPE also reduced key tight junction proteins (ZO1, Occludin, Claudin1), weakening intestinal barrier integrity and causing mucus layer loss. These effects suggest compromised gut protection against harmful agents. Inhibition of IRX3 or NOS2 using siRNA or pharmacological inhibitors effectively reduced necroptosis and restored tight junctions, indicating IRX3/NOS2 activation as a major pathway in BTBPE‐induced intestinal damage. Zheng et al. ( 2024 ). Mechanistic Insights into 1,2‐bis(2,4,6‐tribromophenoxy)ethane‐Induced Male Reproductive Toxicity in Zebrafish.Adult male zebrafish were exposed to BTBPE via the water (0, 0.01, 0.1, 1 and 10 μg/L) for 28 days. BTBPE affected mating behaviour and reduced sperm quality and reproductive success. Proteomic and phosphoproteomic analysis revealed a predominant impact on mitochondrial functions and a widespread effect across cellular compartments on protein phosphorylation level. Ultrastructural damage, increased expression of apoptosis‐inducing factor and disordered respiratory chain confirmed the involvement of mitochondrial impairment in zebrafish testes.

Pentabromoethylbenzene Exposure Induces Transcriptome Aberration and Thyroid Dysfunction: In Vitro, in Silico, and in Vivo Investigations.5‐week‐old Sprague–Dawley rats (N = 10) were exposed daily by gavage to 0, 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day for 28 days. The mechanism underlying pentabromoethylbenzene‐induced adipogenesis and the obesogenic outcome in both cell and mouse model.Pregnant CD‐1 mice (N = 6) were orally administered with olive oil (control), 0.2, 2 and 20 mg/kg bw per day PBEB (dissolved in olive oil) until the day prior to delivery. Male, but not female, offspring exposed to PBEB exhibited significant liver injury and inflammation at 20 mg/kg bw per day. Elevated plasma markers such as ALT and AST, alongside histological liver damage, confirmed hepatotoxicity. Prioritization of hazards of novel flame retardants using the mechanistic toxicology information from ToxCast and Adverse Outcome Pathways.In vivo toxicological data were collected on 62 flame retardants to establish five prioritisation categories based on data availability and toxicological concern. Endocrine disrupting potential of replacement flame retardants‐Review of current knowledge for nuclear receptors associated with reproductive outcomes.The authors reviewed the in vitro activity of 52 BFRs towards five nuclear receptors (oestrogen, androgen, glucocorticoid, progesterone and Ah receptors) associated with reproductive outcomes, retrieving data from open literature and ToxCast/Tox21 programmes. No data was retrieved for PBT. The opensource platform Endocrine Disruptome model predicted medium probability of binding to the antagonist conformation of the AR. Sousa et al. ( 2022 ). Brominated flame retardants effect in MCF‐7 cells: Impact on vitamin D pathway.MCF‐7 cells were treated with PBT using short and long‐term (4 weeks) exposure protocols. Cell viability was not altered, however cell proliferation was reduced after 24 h or 4 weeks exposure. Changes in cell protein content can be indicative of cellular proliferation but also of other cellular events such as ribosomal impairment, nucleotide availability or DNA damage, a consequence of the prolonged exposure to BFRs. The authors reported that these findings were corroborated with the gene expression of MMP‐9 and the injury assay results. The authors reported that MMP‐9 is involved in the inflammatory response, angiogenesis, wound healing and cellular differentiation. An elevated expression of MMP‐9 has been associated with breast cancer development, angiogenesis, metastasis and invasion. PBT significantly reduced the expression of MMP‐9. Cell migration was diminished especially in the first 6 h after injury. When MCF‐7 cells were exposed to BFRs, cell cycle was arrested in phase G2/M, possibly due to DNA damage. Maia et al. ( 2022 ). Impact of brominated flame retardants on lipid metabolism: An in vitro approach.Pre‐dipocyte (3T3‐L1) cell line and a hepatocyte (HepG2) cell line were used to investigate the possible lipid metabolism disruption caused by PBT. PBT do not significantly affect preadipocytes proliferation, but increased lipid accumulation in hepatocytes and also display a significant increase of PPARγ gene expression. Lipid accumulation in the cells can occur by diverse mechanisms. Cells treated with PBT showed an increase in IL‐1β and a decrease in PGC‐1α gene expression, alongside with a tendency to a decrease in adiponectin gene expression. Moreover, PPARγ gene expression was increased after PBT treatment along with an increase in lipid accumulation.Abbreviation: PPARγ, peroxisome proliferator‐activated receptor γ.

Studies on the mode of action of PBT

Abbreviation: PPARγ, peroxisome proliferator‐activated receptor γ.
